# Sanpo — solo-first pet-care operations SaaS

Sanpo is the production brand and a vertical SaaS platform for independent
pet-care professionals, not a centralized dog-walking agency.

React PWA (Vite 8, React 19, TS strict, react-router-dom 7) + Supabase (Postgres 17, Auth, RLS, Realtime, Storage) + Deno edge functions + Stripe Billing + Mapbox (SVG fallback). Currency USD (integer cents — the *_pence column names are historical and hold cents). Timezone US Central — America/Chicago (UTC in DB).

Authoritative specs live in `docs/spec/`. `docs/phases/00–08` is the v1 build plan and is complete; everything after it (reskin, QC/hardening, ops, brand) is tracked in the status log below, not in phase files. Specs win over improvisation; if a spec is ambiguous, ask before deviating.

## Layout
- `app/` — frontend (Vite)
- `supabase/` — `migrations/`, `functions/`, `tests/`, `seed.sql`
- `docs/spec/` — specs (source of truth)
- `docs/phases/` — phase files, one per session

## Commands
- Frontend typecheck: `npm --prefix app exec tsc -- -b --force app`
  (**Not** `tsc --noEmit -p app`, which this file used to document and CI used
  to run: `app/tsconfig.json` is a solution file — `"files": []` plus project
  references — so `-p` checks *zero files* and exits 0 on a syntax error.
  Only build mode (`-b`) follows the references. Verified by planting a
  syntax error: `-p` exit 0, `-b` exit 2.)
- Frontend build: `npm --prefix app run build` (runs `verify:brand-assets` via `prebuild`)
- Frontend unit tests: `npm --prefix app test -- --run` (vitest)
- Frontend lint: `npm --prefix app run lint` (oxlint, `--deny-warnings`)
- E2E: `npm --prefix app run test:e2e` (Playwright; `test:e2e:install` first)
- Brand asset integrity: `npm --prefix app run verify:brand-assets`
- DB reset + migrate + seed: `supabase db reset`
- Would `db push` apply it: `./scripts/db-push-check.sh` (re-applies every
  migration from zero as a **non-superuser**, one transaction per file — the
  ordinary reset runs as the bootstrap superuser, which skips every privilege
  check a deploy performs)
- Smoke tests: `psql "$LOCAL_DB_URL" -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 -f supabase/tests/smoke.sql`
- Edge typecheck: `deno check supabase/functions/**/index.ts`
- Edge tests: `deno test --allow-read=supabase/migrations,supabase/functions ./supabase/functions/_tests/`
  (**Not** `-A`, which this file used to document. CI grants no permissions at
  all, so `-A` locally meant a test could pass here and fail there — and did.
  The two strings are now identical on purpose: keep them that way, and widen
  the permission only in the same commit as the test that needs it.)
- Full validation: `/validate`, or `scripts/validate.sh` (the same gates in the
  same order, runnable without Claude Code — review L22). Keep the three in
  lockstep: `ci.yml`, `SKILL.md`, `validate.sh`.

## Non-negotiable invariants
1. Credit balance mutations happen ONLY inside `SECURITY DEFINER` functions that take a per-client row lock (`SELECT … FOR UPDATE`). No code path ever `UPDATE`s `clients.credit_balance` or `INSERT`s into `credit_ledger` directly from an API role.
2. `access_credentials` ciphertext columns are unreadable by `anon` and `authenticated` (column-privilege REVOKE). Every read goes through the audited RPC + `credential-vault` edge function. Plaintext secrets are never logged.
3. Overage semantics: a walk is EITHER fully credit-funded OR fully charged at the rate snapshotted when it was created — `walks.overage_rate_pence` (plan client) or `walks.visit_price_pence` (pay-per-visit client, H32); the live `plans.overage_rate_pence` only for walks carrying NO snapshot (pre-0043 rows, and walks created with neither a plan nor a visit price whose client subscribed before completion) and only while the plan's subscription is live — a dead subscription's retained plan prices nothing. Never partial credit consumption, and for a client still on no plan a null snapshot means "refuse", never "free".
4. Rollover is single-lot carryover (v1 simplification, documented in `docs/spec/02-credit-engine.md`). Do not implement per-grant FIFO.
5. Every function touching credits or crossing tenants: `SECURITY DEFINER`, `SET search_path = public`, `REVOKE ALL … FROM PUBLIC, anon`, explicit `GRANT EXECUTE` only where required.
6. Migrations are append-only once applied. Never edit an existing file in `supabase/migrations/` — create a new migration.
7. Every tenant table carries `operator_id`; every RLS policy scopes on it.

## Conventions
- TS strict; named exports for lib/components; default export only for route screens.
- Money = integer minor units (cents; *_pence columns kept their names). Dates stored UTC (`timestamptz`), rendered US Central (America/Chicago) via `lib/format.ts`.
- Styling: the locked Indigo Emaki system in `docs/spec/05-design-system.md`
  and `docs/spec/07-indigo-emaki-visual-migration.md`. No Tailwind. Never
  restore PawTrail/Biscuit colors, Baloo, hard shadows, repeated card grids,
  decorative pet portraits, or a horizontal Sanpo logo.
- Color comes from CT-1 tokens (`--sanpo-color-*` in
  `styles/vendor/sanpo-product-color-tokens-r1.css`). Never write a raw hex in
  `components.css` — a literal bypasses the palette and its
  `prefers-contrast` overrides. Anything carrying state needs 3:1 against its
  background (WCAG 1.4.11); body text needs 4.5:1.
- Commit format: `area: summary` — e.g. `ops:`, `qc:`, `a11y:`, `docs:`,
  `brand:`, `prod:`. (`phase(NN):` applied to the v1 build plan only.)

## Workflow

The phase plan is complete, so work is no longer one-phase-per-session. For
any substantive change: read the relevant `docs/spec/` file first, make the
change, run `/validate`, commit, and append one line to the status log below.
Specs are updated in the same commit as the code they describe — a spec that
disagrees with shipped behavior is a bug in the spec.

Three files carry the rest, and a session should open all of them:
`docs/dev/backlog.md` is what to work on next; `docs/dev/session-notes.md` is
how to get a container running, which gates lie, and the traps this repository
has already paid for; `docs/dev/owner-actions.md` is what no file here can do.

## Ownership

Claude is the sole author of this repository. All file changes and all GitHub
interaction — branches, pull requests, reviews, merges — go through this
session. There is no external design handoff to import and no bundle to
restore; the repository is the source of truth. If a Git bundle or an
instruction to "restore" a branch arrives, check it against the current
remote before acting: replaying a stale snapshot can silently discard
reviewed work.

Design decisions that once went to an outside owner are made here now. The
Indigo Emaki system and the approved Today composition stay authoritative —
changing either is a deliberate act recorded in a commit, never a drive-by
edit. Preserve the full-screen Old Town Today composition, its vertical
responsive behavior, the schedule hierarchy, and the `END WALK` action.

`npm run verify:brand-assets` hash-guards `app/src/assets/brand`,
`app/src/assets/icons`, and the Today background. The guard catches
accidental churn; it does not freeze the files. When an asset changes on
purpose, update its hash in `app/scripts/verify-sanpo-assets.mjs` in the
same commit and say why in the message.

### Commits and attribution

Commits are authored **`AAnderson1817 <andyanderson1818@gmail.com>`** and carry
a `Co-Authored-By: Claude <model> <noreply@anthropic.com>` trailer naming the
model that co-authored it (`Claude Opus 5` through `78dd80b`, `Claude Fable
5.1` from the spec-drift PR on — the harness states the current one). The owner
owns the repository and the contribution history; Claude is the co-author, and
the trailer is where that is recorded.

This was wrong until now: the author was `Claude <noreply@anthropic.com>`,
which is not a GitHub account and therefore credits nobody. Squash merging
hid the problem, because GitHub re-authors the squashed commit to the pull
request's author — so `main` looked correct while every commit on every
working branch was uncredited.

**Merge with rebase, not squash.** Squash collapses a branch to one commit and
throws the rest away: the per-commit reasoning survives only in the pull
request, and a PR that took four commits to get right reads on `main` as
though it arrived whole. Rebase lands each commit individually, keeps the
history linear, and — now that authorship is right — credits each one.

Squash is still correct for one case: a branch whose intermediate commits are
noise rather than reasoning (a fix-up, a typo, a "wire it up" that only makes
sense with the commit after it). Judge by whether a reader a year from now
would want the steps or the summary.

### How work reaches `main`

`main` is a deploy trigger, not just a branch: `deploy-staging.yml` fires on
CI completing for `branches: [main]`, which deploys staging Supabase and then
runs the staging smoke suite against it. A one-line docs commit pushed
straight to `main` does all of that.

So:

- **Never push to `main` directly.** Every change goes through a pull
  request, docs included.
- **Batch.** One PR per unit of work, not per commit — each merge is a
  staging deploy, so accumulate related commits on the working branch and
  merge once.
- **Self-review on the PR.** Post findings there rather than only in chat;
  it is the durable artifact and it survives a lost session. Reviewing your
  own work is weaker than an independent reviewer — say so, and look hardest
  at the things tests do not catch.
- **Merge on green.** Merge authority sits in this session, including on the
  money and trust paths. The owner has asked for as little contact with the
  code as possible; escalating a routine decision back to them is a failure
  mode, not caution.
- **Raise the bar instead of raising a hand** on the money and trust paths —
  `supabase/migrations/`, credit/ledger/billing/Stripe code, the credential
  vault, RLS or tenancy, and the deploy workflows. These are where a mistake
  is expensive and hard to reverse, so they get: a written argument in the PR
  for why the change is safe, adversarial self-review aimed at the failure
  case rather than the happy path, and a test that would have failed before
  the change. The CODEOWNERS entries stay — they make the review request
  visible in the PR, which is the durable record.
- **Confirm with the owner only for what a merge cannot undo**: a production
  deploy, destructive or irreversible operations against live data, spending
  money, and anything that leaves the repository and reaches real users. A
  staging deploy is not one of these; it is what every merge already does.
- **`docs/dev/owner-actions.md` is the one list of things no file here can do**
  — dashboard settings, secrets only the owner holds, spending decisions. When
  a PR creates one, add it there in the same commit rather than only in the
  status log below; when one is done, delete it. Several of them are gaps the
  rest of the system currently papers over, and the log is not a to-do list.

- db(0019): PR 2 of the review sequence. `fn_book_walk` filtered `service_types ... and active`, a column that has never existed — Postgres resolves column refs at execution, not at CREATE FUNCTION time, so 0013 installed cleanly and then raised 42703 on every client self-booking since. 0019 is `create or replace` with that one predicate removed; the body diff is exactly one line and the signature, `security definer` and `set search_path` are byte-identical. Deliberately did NOT add an `active` column: nothing in the schema, API or UI has a concept of service deactivation and no surface could set it, so a permanent column to satisfy a typo would be worse than the fix. smoke.sql gains a `fn_book_walk (0019)` block — happy path plus five rejections — and each rejection asserts the SPECIFIC message rather than swallowing anything non-`FAIL:`, because the file's usual idiom would have passed against the broken function: a call dying of undefined_column is indistinguishable from one correctly refused. The unknown-service case is therefore the negative-path regression test. Row count is a delta, since an earlier fixture already books client A on `current_date`.

Twenty error messages were bare spans that announced nothing; they now go through one `FormError` region that mounts before its text arrives and collapses out of flow when empty (an out-of-flow child is not a flex item, so it costs no `gap`), with a CI grep keeping it the only path. `--sanpo-color-input-border` moves from Neutral Rule (1.47:1 on white) to Neutral Muted (5.07:1) at 2px, because Chrome floors 1.5px borders to 1px at DPR 1. Plus `<main>` in both shells, an `h1` on the three screens without one, per-route titles with a polite route announcer, `.sr-only`, skip links to content AND to nav (the operator rail is DOM-last but visually first), named non-interrupting timers, and `Sheet` marking its ancestors' siblings `inert` with `title` now required. The contract is written into `docs/spec/05`. Closes B7, H25, H26, M12, M13, L13, L14.
- ci(typecheck): found while verifying the above — the `Typecheck` step had never checked anything. `app/tsconfig.json` is a solution file (`"files": []` plus project references), so `tsc --noEmit -p .` resolves zero input files and exits 0; only build mode follows references. Proved by planting a syntax error: `-p` exit 0, `-b` exit 2. Now `npx tsc -b --force` (`--force` because `-b` is incremental and both referenced projects set `noEmit`). Type errors were still being caught, by `npm run build`'s `tsc -b` — so this was a false-assurance bug, not a coverage hole, and the same wrong command in CLAUDE.md is corrected too.
- today(field): PR 4 of the review sequence. Today was a poster, not a screen. The schedule sat absolutely positioned inside a fixed-ratio `overflow: hidden` box, so a day with more visits than the artwork had room for silently lost its afternoon — at 390×844, six rows put the last behind the nav, eight put one below the viewport, twelve lost five outright, and `scrollHeight === innerHeight` meant there was no scrollbar and no cue. The rows stayed in the DOM and were read by screen readers; they were invisible on screen only. The field is now a single-cell grid: everything stacks in one cell, so the field is as tall as the taller of the plate and the schedule — the `max()` CSS otherwise cannot express against a content-dependent value. `aspect-ratio` was the obvious first attempt and is wrong (measured: it CAPS, which is the same bug wearing a different hat), which is why the spec now forbids both it and `overflow: hidden` by name. The plate is pinned to the top at its intrinsic ratio with no `object-fit`, so the up-to-14.7% stretch is gone structurally rather than by a bounded media query that had to be kept honest; where the field is taller, `--emaki-paper` (sampled from the master's bottom edge) continues beneath it, which also removes the 147px bare-Cream discontinuity at 420×1010. Rows became links to the client record with `pet, time, route, state` labels — spec 05 had required "clickable rows" since before the screen shipped with six focusable elements and no way to reach a door code — with `END WALK` kept outside the link rather than nested in it. Empty days get an "Add a walk" action; Dashboard gets a 60 s tick and a `visibilitychange` refetch, both non-destructive so a dead-zone poll cannot replace a screen the operator is reading with a retry prompt. e2e goes 4 → 10 cases including 8- and 12-visit days; the five that matter were confirmed red against the old CSS before being made green. Nav clearance moved to whichever section is last, and the operator scrim now resolves toward the surface under it (4.83:1 on paper, sampled from the rendered bar — the token maths predicted 5.35:1).
- security(realtime): PR 5 of the review sequence. The live-GPS topic was public — `supabase.channel("walk:"+id)` with no options, and `private` defaults to FALSE in realtime-js (verified in the installed 2.110.0, RealtimeChannel.js:102), while the server helper set `private: false` outright. Supabase authorizes private channels only, and no `realtime.messages` policy existed in any of the 19 migrations, so while a walk was in progress the operator's device broadcast the live position of a named person at a named residential address to a topic any holder of the shipped anon key could join from any origin. The write side was worse: the same topic accepted `send`, and WalkDetail merges live points straight into the client's map, so the proof of service the product sells could be fabricated or terminated by a third party. The durable rows were always correct, which is exactly why the stream that bypasses them was missed. 0020 adds `fn_walk_channel_access` (SECURITY DEFINER, regex-guarded before the uuid cast so a malformed topic returns false rather than raising inside a policy on a shared platform table) plus receive/send policies mirroring `walk_gps_points`, with one asymmetry: clients never send. Both sides now declare `private: true`. The migration ASSERTS RLS is enabled on `realtime.messages` rather than setting it, because inert policies are worse than a failed deploy. The shim gains a `realtime` schema so the policies apply and are testable at all; smoke.sql asserts the matrix on the function and then through the policies themselves, and was confirmed to fail in both directions first — deny-all ("the walk's operator cannot read its own channel") and allow-all ("a foreign operator read 2 rows"). A new deno test pins the server's `private` flag and fails against the old value. **Not complete without one dashboard toggle**: policies govern private channels and do not stop a third party opening the same topic as a public one — that is "Allow public access" in Realtime settings, which no file here can set (no `config.toml` key, and neither workflow runs `config push` — review H2). `docs/dev/realtime-authorization.md` has the steps and both the positive and negative verifications.
- security(vault-key): PR 6 of the review sequence, and B2's core. The vault key had one copy, no identity and no rotation path: the blob was `iv‖tag‖ct` with no version and no key id, so nothing recorded which key wrote a row, two keys could never coexist, and rotation was not difficult but *impossible*. `importVaultKey` accepted any 32 bytes, so a wrong-but-well-formed key was cached and used to ENCRYPT — the only failure here that destroys data rather than denying it. v2 is `version(1)‖key_id(8)‖iv(12)‖ct‖tag` with the key id HKDF-derived from the key under a disjoint label, so it cannot disagree with the key it names; a 56-byte fixed-length AAD binds each blob to its credential and operator as raw uuid bytes, so a relocated ciphertext no longer decrypts and uuid casing is structurally irrelevant. Decryption routes strictly by id — never trial decryption — so `key_unknown` (recoverable: supply the key) is finally distinct from `decrypt_failed` (tampering). 0021 adds `key_id` as a GENERATED column that Postgres refuses to write for any role, a `vault_canary` pin verified through the LIVE function at deploy time, a compare-and-swap rewrap, and a four-number census. The census shape is the fix for the best adversarial finding: `count(*) where key_id <> current = 0` is satisfied both by "everything is rewrapped" and by "I cannot see any rows", so the retirement gate would have failed open and destroyed every secret. The rewrap has no journal on purpose — the work queue is the data, so it is idempotent and resumable, and a row whose key is missing today is reported rather than marked permanently dead. Runbooks stop generating the key in the SQL editor (it put the key into the very database it is meant to be independent of) and now say escrow-before-GitHub, because the escrow copy is the whole recovery story. Design came from a 14-agent workflow whose synthesis three independent adversaries then broke — the repo-global key pin they killed is why the pin is a per-environment canary row instead. 100 deno tests, 22 smoke blocks.
- fix(edge-errors): closes #25, a defect the ten-discipline review never found. `invokeEdge` threw the SDK's error and never read the response body — in `@supabase/functions-js` 2.110.0 any non-2xx throws `FunctionsHttpError` and the catch returns `data: null`, so the branch reading `data.error.message` was unreachable and all nine edge calls showed the SDK's fixed "Edge Function returned a non-2xx status code". A declined card, a walk that failed to bill and a vault reauth rejection were the same sentence. `invoke` also returns the raw `Response`, so the envelope is recoverable; the unwrapping moved into an exported pure function. The detail is captured inside the `try` and thrown OUTSIDE it — the first version of the fix threw inside, where its own catch swallowed it, which is byte-identical to the bug and reads as correct. Two of the eight new tests fail against that no-op, which is why they assert the message rather than merely that something was thrown.
- ci(concurrency): closes #28. Every PR commit ran the whole suite twice (`push` was unscoped as well as `pull_request`) and nothing bounded a stuck job, so a wedged runner left a permanently in-progress check that this session cannot clear — the token 403s on Actions writes and GitHub's job timeout is six hours. `push` scoped to main (the deploy gate needs that run), a concurrency group with `cancel-in-progress`, and a 12-minute ceiling on e2e. Deliberately NOT on the deploy workflows: cancelling a `db push` halfway leaves an environment nobody can describe.
- ci(apt): the e2e job then stalled a fifth time, on main, holding the vault deploy — proving the ceiling was a backstop and not a fix, since a bounded failure blocks a deploy exactly as a hang does. `playwright install --with-deps chromium` does two unrelated network things in one command, so five stalls said nothing about which half hung; splitting it named apt on the first outing. Dropping `--with-deps` takes the job from a multi-minute stall to 36 s green and the browser step from a 33 s median to 7 s, which is itself the evidence that ubuntu-latest already ships the libraries — a missing one is loud, Playwright refuses to launch and names the packages. Two claims were withdrawn before merge: that apt had stalled three consecutive times, and a sudo/root explanation for a 6m40s runtime that was actually 1m51s. Elapsed time read off intuition instead of the job record, then explained with a theory the evidence never required — the third instance this session of asserting a number I had not read.
- ops(deploy-order+dr): `sync-secrets` and `deploy-functions` both declared `needs: migrate`, so they RACED on the one run that matters most — the first deploy of a project, which the runbook says to do with `sync_secrets` ticked. A function winning that race is live without `VAULT_MASTER_KEY`, and production has never run, so its first execution IS that deploy, against real customer data. Now `gate → migrate → sync-secrets → deploy-functions` in both workflows; the explicit `if` is load-bearing, because adding an opt-in job to `needs` would otherwise skip its dependent on every ordinary push. Also `docs/dev/disaster-recovery.md`, which the repository had no equivalent of — zero occurrences of "restore", "rollback", "PITR" or "incident" against 21 append-only migrations. It states plainly that RTO/RPO are UNMEASURED and carries the rehearsal that would make them real, rather than implying a capability that has never been executed. Storage objects have no backup at any tier and `walk_photos` carries no checksum, so a restore desynchronises the photo evidence irrecoverably; that and the plan-tier/PITR decisions are listed as open, not quietly closed. Partially addresses B3. Also caught while confirming the vault deploy: the `Verify the vault key opens this project` step shipped in the vault PR ran for the first time on `5193e69` and **passed without verifying anything** — `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` is unset for the staging environment, so it warned and exited 0. A green deploy that verified nothing is indistinguishable from one that did, which is the same false assurance as the typecheck that checked zero files. Production now REFUSES to deploy without the secret; staging stays non-fatal (blocking every deploy on a secret only the owner can add would be worse than the gap) but says outright that it proved nothing, both runbooks list the secret, and issue #31 tracks it. Both workflows also gained an `assert-functions-deployed` job, because a skipped job does NOT fail its workflow — an expression mistake in the new ordering guard would have produced a run reporting success while deploying nothing, which is the same green-but-empty failure the rest of this entry is about. The guard asserts the outcome rather than trusting the expression that produces it.
- money(reversal): PR 7 of the review sequence, closing B4 and L6. The webhook subscribed to six events and none was a reversal, so a refund issued from the Stripe dashboard — the only way to issue one — returned the money and left the payments row reading `succeeded` with the cycle grant still in the ledger: the client kept credits they had been refunded for, and a disputed overage pulled funds plus a fee while Sanpo showed the walk paid forever. `payment_status` had carried `'refunded'` since 0001, the Money screen filtered for it and `status-treatment.ts` styled it, and no code path had ever written it. The blocking discovery was that reversal **could not be computed at all**: `credit_ledger` had no link to the payment that granted the credits, only a free-text note, so 0023 adds `stripe_invoice_id` (never backfilled — a best-effort parse would make guessed rows indistinguishable from traced ones, so untraceable payments set `reversal_needs_review` and claw back nothing rather than reconstructing a figure from a plan that may have changed since). Clawback is proportional and **floored at the balance**, because `credit_balance` carries a CHECK and `fn_ledger_apply` raises — a negative balance would breach invariant 1 — and the shortfall is surfaced as `credits_unrecovered` instead of being absorbed silently. Adversarial review found the real bug: `uq_payments_subscription_invoice` and `uq_overage_payment_per_walk` are partial indexes filtered on `status`, and `fn_apply_invoice_paid` tested idempotency the same way, so flipping a row to `refunded` dropped it out of its own uniqueness guarantee — Stripe redelivers `invoice.paid` for three days, and a redelivery after a refund granted a second cycle. Proved against the pre-0023 body (two `grant` rows for one invoice) before the assertion was written, and the assertion is on the LEDGER not the balance, because rollover `none` wipes the balance before each grant and the balance therefore reads identically whether or not the double grant happened. 118 vitest, 114 deno, 25 smoke blocks. Deliberately NOT included: an operator-facing "Refund this walk" button — dashboard-issued refunds now reconcile correctly, and an in-app refund is new product surface rather than a fix.
- money(connect): PR 8 of the review sequence, closing B5. `stripeClient()` read a single `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` with no `stripeAccount`, so every client payment for every operator landed in the PLATFORM's Stripe balance and no code path ever moved money to an operator — operator #2 had no way to be paid, and collecting funds for third parties is money transmission in most US states. The owner's decision that operators are the **merchant of record** determined the shape: Connect **Standard**, because Express and Custom put the platform in that seat with the chargeback liability. Production has never deployed (0 workflow runs), so there is no live customer, subscription or balance anywhere — which is why there is deliberately no dual-mode fallback to the platform account. A permanent legacy branch would be carried forever to serve data that does not exist, and Stripe cannot move customers between accounts anyway. `requireOperator` now returns the Connect state so a money path cannot forget to look it up, and `requireAccount`/`accountOf` split "takes money" from "reaches an existing object" — the billing portal deliberately does NOT require `charges_enabled`, because blocking a client from cancelling while Stripe reviews their walker would strand them. The hard part was the webhook: a Connect endpoint receives events for EVERY connected account, so `event.account` became an authorization input rather than a routing hint. Session metadata is attacker-controlled there — any operator can craft a Checkout Session in their own dashboard carrying another operator's `client_id` — so `updateClient` matches on client id AND operator id and a zero-row result is ignored, and the client and reversal lookups are scoped the same way. Two self-caught defects: a `connectedStripe` proxy that did nothing (deleted in favour of passing `{ stripeAccount }` explicitly at each call site, where a reader can see it in the same expression as the charge), and four CSS tokens written from memory that do not exist — the panel heading then measured 3.85:1 as `text-attention` on `surface-attention`, under the 4.5:1 floor, so both text colours are `text-primary` at 7.08:1 and kaki survives only on the decorative rule. 121 deno tests, 26 smoke blocks. Both runbooks now say **Listen to events on: Connected accounts** in bold, because an account-level endpoint receives none of the events that matter and the handler ignores accountless events — a misconfigured endpoint fails silently and completely.
- feat(settings): PR 9 of the review sequence, closing B6 and fixing a regression I had just introduced. A new operator could not create a plan, create or rename a service type, or change their business name or low-credit threshold after signup — the database had granted full CRUD on both tables since 0004 and `updateOperator` was written with zero importers, so this was purely a missing UI. The consequence was worse than "unconfigured": with no plan, every walk is marked overage at a zero balance and the overage path fired a `payment_failed` notification **at the pet owner** for every single walk, telling them to update a payment method they may not have. Notifications now split by fault — a card decline is the client's to fix and still reaches both, while a configuration fault (no plan, no billing profile, Stripe not connected) reaches the operator only, with wording that says what to fix. Confirmed failing against the old always-both behaviour first. The regression: PR #34 resolved the Connect account eagerly in complete-walk, so an operator who had not connected Stripe could complete NO walks at all — including fully credit-funded ones that never touch Stripe, which is exactly what a new operator does first. `resolveAccount` is now a thunk called at the moment of charge, so "not connected" is a billing failure on the walks that needed money rather than a wall in front of all of them. `create-plan` mints the Stripe Product and Price on the connected account and writes the row in one action, refusing before creating anything if Stripe is not connected. Settings is reached from Money, NOT the nav: the four-item operator navigation is a locked brand decision and adding a fifth tab is a deliberate act, not a side effect of shipping a screen. Also: a CI step asserting every `var(--x)` names a property something defines — two had shipped undefined, `--fs-13` (the scale is 12/14/16/20/24/32/44) and four colour tokens written from memory, and an undefined custom property makes the whole declaration invalid and silently inherits. Proved to fail in both directions before merge.
- ops(gate-noise): the staging deploy for `2edc6a5` went red at 07:21 with no push behind it. Cause: run 32087878822 (`b4697d1`, the vault merge) was the E2E job wedged on the apt stall before the timeout existed; GitHub's six-hour job ceiling killed it at 07:21:04, exactly 6h00m19s after it started, and its `completed` event fired `workflow_run` with conclusion `cancelled`. The gate refused correctly — that commit's work had already reached staging via `5193e69` — so nothing was broken and nothing needed doing. But the red is indistinguishable from a broken deploy at a glance, and it will now RECUR by design: CI carries `cancel-in-progress`, so two pushes to main close together cancel the first run and produce the same misleading red. The gate still refuses and still goes red (a skipped gate would let the run conclude success and fire the chained smoke against a commit that never deployed), but a cancelled conclusion now says so in its own first line and in a `::warning` annotation. Verified across all four cases — green deploys, failure refuses plainly, cancelled refuses with the explanation, a pull_request-triggered run refuses.
- fix(connect-routing): `stripe.customers.retrieve` was the one Stripe call in the tree still on the PLATFORM account after #34 — missed while the `paymentMethods.list` FOUR LINES BELOW it was routed. Every overage walk would have 500'd at END WALK: the customer exists only on the connected account, so the platform lookup raises `resource_missing`, which `isCardError` does not match, so it rethrows before `updateWalkCompleted`. Worse than a 500 — the pending claim is inserted BEFORE the Stripe call, so the operator's retry inside the 10-minute lease takes the `already_charged` branch and completes the walk carrying a payment that was never made. Found by a 13-agent revalidation of the remaining review findings against current HEAD, which is also the reason it was found at all: `overage_deps.ts` had ZERO coverage, because the overage suite injects pure `OverageDeps` mocks and so exercises the decision logic while never touching the wiring. The new test asserts EVERY Stripe call carries a `stripeAccount` rather than checking the one call that was wrong — a per-call test has to be remembered and extended, this one fails on its own for any call added unrouted. Red first, naming the offender.
- money(subscription): the first of the remaining review PRs, and it started with a 13-agent revalidation of the whole backlog against HEAD — worth it, because the review predated eight merged PRs and its top finding turned out to be a defect I had introduced myself afterwards (see `fix(connect-routing)`). It also told me what NOT to build: four findings were already fixed, one (`H22`) rested on a claim an adversary disproved by building with probe values, and one (`H10`) wanted a webhook test inverted that is correct as written — inverting it would delete credits a client paid for. Three real defects fixed here, all silent, all money. The materializer consulted exactly ONE subscription predicate (`<> 'paused'`) and `fn_book_walk` consulted NONE — it gated on `clients.status`, the lifecycle column, not the billing one — so a cancelled or past-due client kept having walks generated nightly and could still self-book. Both now share an allow-list (`active`, `none`) which also fails CLOSED: an enum value added later stops generating work until somebody decides what it means. `invoice.paid` was the only subscription arm not scoped to the client's bound subscription, while both siblings were and said why in comments; a customer can carry two live subscriptions whose invoice ids DIFFER, so `uq_payments_subscription_invoice` misses and the client gets two cycle grants and two rollovers — reachable because `ClientDetail` excluded `past_due` from `subscribed` and so offered a live checkout button to a client whose card had merely failed. And `fn_apply_invoice_paid` ran `fn_apply_rollover` unconditionally, so the FIRST invoice expired the whole balance on rollover `none`, destroying credit granted before billing started; #33 rewrote that function and did not close it. Owner decisions: `past_due` halts service until paid, and an overage rate of 0 is invalid (a floor at Stripe's minimum was offered and declined, so a sub-50c rate is still accepted here and refused by Stripe at charge time). 136 deno, 29 smoke blocks. Both database fixes confirmed failing first — the old predicate generated 7 walks for a cancelled client, and the old rollover expired a credit lot on the first invoice.
- money(overage-taxonomy): closes H13. A Stripe failure had two classes and needs three. `isCardError` matches `StripeCardError` and a fixed list of decline codes, and everything else was treated as TRANSIENT: rethrown, claim left `pending`, caller 500s. That is right for "Stripe is briefly unreachable" — the charge may yet have landed, so the claim has to keep blocking — and wrong for a failure retrying cannot fix. A customer that does not exist on this connected account raises `resource_missing`, so END WALK 500s, the id-less pending claim then short-circuits the reconcile branch, and the walk never completes at all: uncollected revenue showing as "Processing" forever with no action available. Permanent failures now resolve the claim to `failed` and notify the OPERATOR only, because a missing Stripe customer is not something the client can fix and telling them to update a payment method would be false. The message names the setting rather than repeating Stripe's developer-facing text. Two further defects found while building it, neither in the review: `payments` carries two PARTIAL unique indexes filtered on `status`, 0023 widened both so a reversal could not unlock a second charge, and the two edge-function reads that ask the same question kept the old narrower lists — so a walk whose overage had been refunded returned null from `getLiveOveragePayment`, fell through to the insert, hit the index, and reached the operator as the literal words "internal error", while a `failed` row from `invoice.payment_failed` made `hasPaymentForInvoice` true and suppressed the succeeded row for the same invoice (which a later refund then could not find, landing in `reversal_needs_review` instead of clawing back). Both sets now live in `_lib/payment_status.ts`, and `payment_status_test.ts` PARSES the last definition of each index out of `supabase/migrations/` and compares — asserting the literal list would only restate the code. Reading the LAST definition is load-bearing: 0012 created `uq_overage_payment_per_walk` with exactly the narrow list the code was still using, so a first-match parser would have confirmed the bug. Third: Stripe redelivers `invoice.payment_failed` on every dunning retry with a fresh event id, which `stripe_events` cannot dedupe, so the Money screen accrued one "Needs attention" row per retry for one unpaid invoice; the `past_due` write still happens on every delivery, since the later retries are when the account is most past due. Also removed a stale spec bullet #39 left behind that still described rollover running unconditionally. 147 deno tests. All three fixes confirmed red first, and the status-set test in BOTH directions — too narrow and too wide. The new test then failed in CI having passed locally, which was its own finding: CI ran `deno test` with NO permissions while this file documented `deno test -A`, so the local command was strictly more permissive than the gate. Both are now the same string, scoped to `--allow-read=supabase/migrations` and nothing else.
- fix(walk-durability): closes H8. Photos, care toggles and notes lived only in React state until the walk completed, and the resume path reseeded GPS and nothing else — so a reload, a back-swipe or the OS reclaiming the tab silently discarded every photo taken so far along with the notes and the potty/fed flags. `addPhotos` uploaded to Storage immediately but wrote no `walk_photos` row; those rows were created solely by complete-walk from the completion request, so an uploaded photo's only pointer was a React array and a remount stranded the file in the bucket with nothing referencing it. Rows are now written at upload time — `ignoreDuplicates` on `uq_walk_photos_path` (0013), the same conflict target complete-walk's upsert already used, so sending the path again at completion is a no-op rather than a second row. Resume is server-first (`listWalkPhotos` survives a different device and a cleared cache) unioned with the snapshot, which catches a photo uploaded offline whose row insert had nowhere to go. Toggles and notes have no column until completion, so the local snapshot carries them; notes prefer the snapshot because `walks.notes` is not written until the walk ends. The dangerous part is ordering: the screen mounts empty and restores asynchronously, so a writer that runs before the restore persists the empty starting state over the record it is about to read, turning the fix into the bug. `shouldPersistProgress` names that rule and is tested against losing the `hydrated` clause; `start()` no longer writes the snapshot itself, so there is exactly one writer that can never forget to pass the current progress. Second gap: the advertised exit guard is `beforeunload`, which does not cover the back button or an edge-swipe — a same-document history navigation fires no unload, so Walk Mode just unmounted and recording stopped with no confirmation at all. Guarded with a history sentinel rather than react-router's `useBlocker`, which needs a data router this app does not mount; verified in a real headless Chromium, where the log confirms the assumption the design rests on — popstate fires while the location is STILL the walk, so the confirm happens on screen and declining stays put. Also revokes the photo-strip object URLs on unmount, since the memory pressure they cause is one of the things that gets a tab reclaimed mid-walk. 137 vitest (19 new). Every rule confirmed red first against its plausible wrong implementation — server-only photo merge, DB-first notes, partial rather than total progress defaults, a snapshot that drops progress, and a writer without the hydration gate.
- fix(walk-gps): closes H7. `watchPosition` stops delivering when the page is backgrounded or the screen locks, and it stops SILENTLY — a suspended watch fires no error, so `geo.error` stayed null, nothing on screen changed, and the next fix was appended as though it were the next step of the walk. The route drew a straight line across the suspended interval and `distance_m` measured it, so a walk where the operator pocketed the phone could report a LONGER distance than one where they held it — on the number printed as proof of service. The product's entire mitigation was body copy asking the operator to keep the screen open, which is not a thing you can do with a phone in a pocket. Now: a screen wake lock re-requested on `visibilitychange` (the OS releases the sentinel whenever the page hides, so acquiring once survives exactly one app switch and then never returns), a visible "Recording paused" state when the last raw fix is older than `GPS_GAP_MS`, and the gap MARKED rather than drawn through — `pathDistanceM` skips the segment into it, `toSvgPath` opens a new subpath, Mapbox renders a MultiLineString, and `walk_gps_points.gap_before` (0027) carries it so a resumed or completed walk still shows the break. Under-reporting is the deliberate direction: leaving out a stretch nobody recorded is honest, inventing a straight line across it is not. The detection rule is the part worth getting right, and it is measured on RAW fixes only — those arrive ~1/s regardless of movement, while emitted points are throttled to >=5 s AND >=10 m, so an operator waiting at a crossing legitimately produces none for minutes and a time-gap rule applied to emitted points (or to stored `recorded_at`) would call that a suspension and delete real walking. That is also why 0027 adds a column rather than deriving the flag, and why nothing is backfilled: a guessed gap is indistinguishable from an observed one. `shouldEmitPoint` lets a gap fix through regardless of both thresholds, because the device can wake within 10 m of where it slept and a suppressed resume fix puts the mark somewhere further along or nowhere. The remount itself is marked too, so a mid-walk reload no longer draws across whatever happened while the page was loading. Also replaced the half-sentence hedge that had been in `docs/spec/00` since day one ("native fork option ... if background-GPS reliability demands it") with a stated platform limit, the locked-screen field test that has never been run, and the explicit position that live GPS is not sellable as an unqualified differentiator until it has. 153 vitest, 31 smoke blocks. All four gap rules confirmed red first against the shipped behaviour, and the smoke block against a dropped column. `tsc` caught a fixture-typing error the passing vitest run did not — build mode earning its place again.
- ops(nightly-cron): closes H15. The job that generates every walk on every calendar — and runs the daily credit-expiry sweep — was scheduled by hand-typing a Supabase dashboard entry with a pasted service_role bearer header. It lived in no migration, no workflow and no restorable form; nothing asserted it existed, a project restore did not recreate it (`disaster-recovery.md` listed it under "redo the wiring no file here can set"), and because the horizon is 14 days its absence was invisible for a fortnight — the operator finds out when a client asks why the calendar is empty. 0028 moves the schedule into a migration and, deliberately, NOT the way the review proposed: it wanted `pg_net` posting to the edge function with the key read from Supabase Vault, which is a faithful translation of the dashboard entry and more machinery than the problem needs. `fn_is_service_session()` (0003) already accepts `session_user = 'postgres'` and a pg_cron job runs as the role that scheduled it, so calling the SQL directly needs no key, no Vault secret, no HTTP hop and no pg_net at all. It also fixes detection instead of working around it: the dashboard cron marked a run "successful" once the HTTP call was DISPATCHED, so a 500 was indistinguishable from a good night, which is why the old runbook told you to read `net._http_response` by hand. The second defect was quieter and cost money: `if (!sweep.error) expired = …` swallowed the expiry sweep's error, so a permanently failing sweep read identically to a quiet night and clients kept credits they had been billed for while stopping paying overage. The sweep is still advisory — a failure must not cost the operator a calendar — but it is now captured, returned as `expiry_error`, logged, and marks the run not-ok. Third: `job_runs` gives "did it run last night?" an answer, and `fn_job_health` fails CLOSED — a job that has NEVER succeeded reads stale rather than unknown, because a fresh or restored project is exactly the case the check exists for. `.github/workflows/job-health.yml` asks daily at 06:00 UTC; it is described in its own header as a heartbeat check and not monitoring, since it is one cron watching another and shares their failure modes (H14 is the real thing and does not exist). Both migration guards confirmed to REFUSE rather than skip — no pg_cron and no stand-in, and a `cron.schedule` that returns an id while writing no row — because an inert schedule that deployed cleanly is the exact failure being fixed. The shim gains a pg_cron stand-in (stock postgres has no such extension, locally or in CI) that is honest about its boundary: it never runs a job, so what the local stack proves is that the migration installs the schedule and that the SQL it would run is correct. Two of my own test bugs found on the way, both the same shape: `now()` is TRANSACTION-constant, so `job_runs.finished_at = now()` made every duration zero and `order by started_at desc limit 1` picked an arbitrary row among runs in one transaction — the function now returns `run_id` and the tests select by it; and the service-role-only block passed without testing anything until it used `set local role`, because psql connects as postgres and `fn_is_service_session()` accepts that. 36 smoke blocks, 5 new. `job_runs` is deliberately not a tenant table (invariant 7 does not apply: the materializer runs across every operator at once), RLS on with no policies and no API-role grants.
- ops(frontend-gate): closes H16. Vercel's Git integration deployed `main` to production on push with no knowledge of GitHub Actions, so the change that reaches every user in ninety seconds was the one nothing checked, while the database half of the same commit needed a typed phrase and an environment approval. The runbook contradicted itself in a way that showed the gate had been assumed rather than built — one line called the automatic frontend deploy "safe, because the frontend is stateless", another claimed it "ships on every green main push", and nothing consulted CI. Worse than ungated, it was out of ORDER: `api.ts` is a direct PostgREST/RPC client, so stateless does not mean schema-independent, and a frontend ahead of its migrations means a client opening the portal to a screen that errors because the RPC it calls does not exist yet. The reverse is safe, because migrations are append-only and additive — that asymmetry is why database-first is a rule and not a preference. Both deploy workflows gain a `frontend` job after `deploy-functions`, and Vercel's production branch becomes a `release/*` ref that only that job advances. A release ref rather than the review's suggested deploy hook, because it rests on a git fact plus one dashboard field instead of on Vercel behaviour I cannot test from here (whether a hook build bypasses an ignored-build-step, whether auto-deploy can be cleanly disabled). The job is gated on `deploy-functions` DIRECTLY, not on the assert job, which exits 0 when migrate failed and would therefore have shipped a frontend onto a database that never migrated — the bug rebuilt one level up. Pushing a ref is fire-and-forget, and this repository has now found that shape four times (a typecheck checking zero files, a vault verification verifying nothing, a deploy able to skip its own function job, a cron marking dispatch as success), so `vite.config` stamps `dist/version.json` with the builder's commit and the job POLLS `APP_BASE_URL/version.json` until production serves it — production refuses, staging warns, the same split the vault-key step uses and for the same stated reason. `version.json` had to be excluded from the SPA rewrite by name rather than trusting Vercel's filesystem-before-rewrites ordering, and CI now asserts both that the build emits it stamped with the real SHA and that the rewrite still excludes it, since the only other place to discover otherwise is a production deploy after the database has already migrated. Staging is gated identically, not for symmetry but because the runbook's whole posture is that every change follows the staging path first — a staging frontend deploying off a push while its database waits for the workflow means staging never rehearses the ordering production uses. Rollback is Vercel's instant rollback, not a force-push: the job deliberately does not force, so a rejected push means the commit is not a descendant of what is live. All three CI assertions confirmed red first against the pre-fix state. Note the `frontend` job now holds `contents: write`, so branch protection on `main` matters slightly more than it did.
- ops(observability): closes the code half of H14; the log drain and error monitor remain owner actions and both runbooks now say so plainly. All 43 deliberate `HttpError(5xx)` throws discarded the underlying Postgres/Stripe error at the throw site — `HttpError` had no field to put one in — and `serveFunction`'s catch returned the envelope with NO logging whatsoever, so "completing the walk failed yesterday" had nothing to look at and the specific error had never been captured in the first place. `HttpError` now takes `(status, code, message, cause?, context?)`: `message` is ours and the only part a client sees, `cause` is the real error and never leaves the server, `context` carries `walk_id`/`client_id` and is the thing that actually answers the motivating question, since an id is what you search by. Two corrections to the review while wiring it: the count is 43 not 29 (it predates eight merged PRs), and `send-notification` does NOT bypass the shared wrapper — it uses `serveFunction` and merely added its own bespoke `console.error`; only `stripe-webhook` genuinely bypasses, and it now logs through the same helper so there is one format. The review's `HttpError` framing also missed 21 sites in `stripe-webhook`'s deps that throw bare `new Error("client lookup failed")` and drop the cause identically — the money path with the most moving parts — so `safeCause` follows `.cause` up to three levels, because otherwise the line would record our label and drop the finding. The dangerous half of this fix is what goes INTO the line: Postgres puts the offending VALUES in a unique violation's `details`, so `safeCause` projects to `name`/`code`/`message` and drops `details`, `hint`, `body` and `data` — no ciphertext unique constraint exists today, which is exactly why that is a tested rule rather than a remembered one. `causeCode()` is the stricter setting used at the four vault statements that carry ciphertext in the statement itself, giving up the message and keeping only the SQLSTATE; deliberately not the default, because elsewhere the message is the most useful thing on the line. `handleRequest` is extracted out of `Deno.serve` so the wrapper's behaviour is reachable from a test at all — it was not, which is how "no logging in the shared catch" survived unnoticed in the one place every function's failures pass through. 4xx logs nothing on purpose. A request id is minted or adopted from `x-request-id`, echoed on every response including successes, and carried in the error envelope so a failure a person is looking at ties to the line that recorded it; caller-supplied ids are normalised to an id-shaped subset, and while investigating that I found a literal newline cannot reach the code at all because `Headers` rejects it at construction — so the guard is defence in depth, and the test now says which. 173 deno tests (26 new). A new CI step parses every `new HttpError(5xx, …)` across line breaks and fails any with fewer than three arguments, confirmed red against a deliberately reverted site: the next throw site added would otherwise go back to dropping its cause invisibly. Two self-inflicted, both worth recording: `git checkout <file>` to undo a sabotage discarded the uncommitted fix in that file, since HEAD predated it; and CI's own secret-leak grep then caught the new test's fixtures, which used realistically-shaped `sk_live_…` literals to prove they get stripped, plus two cause strings naming `VAULT_MASTER_KEY`. The guard was right in both cases — a grep cannot tell a mention from a leak, and weakening it to allow one would let the other through — so the fixture is now a made-up canary and the cause strings describe the variable instead of naming it.
- ops(email-delivery): closes H17. `notifications` recorded whether the in-app bell had a row and nothing whatever about whether the EMAIL left. Delivery is a Supabase Database Webhook on INSERT, which is pg_net-based and does NOT retry a non-2xx, so a Resend outage, a rate limit, or the sending domain falling out of verification lost the email permanently — the function threw a 502, the webhook wrote a short-retention row in `net._http_response`, and the row said nothing. The bell still showed it, so the system looked healthy from the inside with the outside channel dead, for `payment_failed` and `walk_cancelled` among others. Worse than losing it silently: `if (!apiKey) return jsonOk({ skipped: true })` meant a deploy that forgot `RESEND_API_KEY` reported uniform success forever while sending zero email; that is now a 500 which, thanks to the H14 wrapper landing first, writes a log line naming what is missing — the dependency that made these two PRs sequential rather than one. 0029 adds an `email_delivery_status` enum and four columns. FOUR states rather than the review's bare `sent_at`, because "not sent" is three different things and a sweep that cannot tell them apart either retries forever or abandons real failures: `skipped` is TERMINAL (operator-only, or no client address) and must never be retried, `failed` is retryable, `pending` has not been looked at. Columns are prefixed `email_` against the review's suggestion, because the table already has `read_at` and a bare `sent_at` on `notifications` reads as "the notification was sent", which is true the moment the row exists. A skip does NOT increment `email_attempts`: it is a decision, not a try, and counting it would march terminal rows toward the give-up ceiling for nothing. `fn_notification_backlog` is deliberately NOT filtered by notification type — that list lives in `CLIENT_FACING` and a second copy in SQL would drift — so instead the function marks anything it will not send as `skipped` the first time it sees the row. `fn_expire_notification_backlog` gives up on purpose and countably, since without it the backlog grows forever and every future check reports the same dead rows; it is idempotent, so a row does not accrue a fresh give-up note every night. Rows predating the migration are backfilled to `skipped` with "unknown whether sent" rather than retried: we genuinely do not know, and a client receiving "your walk is complete" about last month is worse than an admitted gap — the same call 0023 made on untraceable payments. The retry itself is `POST {action:"drain"}`, service-role only (a client triggering a mass send is a mail-bomb and a way to burn the operator's Resend quota), carrying on past a failure because one bad recipient must not strand a backlog somebody is already owed, and answering 200 even with failures since each row records its own outcome and stays queued. `job-health.yml` becomes `Nightly ops check`: it drains daily and goes RED if a backlog survives the retry — a provider blip clears, a misconfiguration does not. Also split `handler.ts` out with injected deps, because the branching added here had zero coverage, which is the same shape as the defect being fixed; `recordPatch` lives there rather than in `index.ts` since importing `index.ts` executes `serveFunction` and binds a port. 189 deno tests (16 new), 42 smoke blocks (8 new). Every rule confirmed red first — a failure that records nothing, a skip that counts as an attempt, 429 classed permanent, a drain that throws on the first failure, and both a missing-column schema and a naive `sent_at is null` sweep. That last one caught a weakness in my own test: the skipped-is-terminal assertion initially passed against the naive sweep for the wrong reason, because the row reused from an earlier step still had `email_sent_at` set; it now uses a fresh row and fails on the assertion that matters. `tsc` again caught what vitest did not — three notification fixtures missing the new columns.
- security(vault-audit): closes H3, both halves. FIRST, the vault was protecting the less useful half of the secret. `key_location_hint` was an ordinary column with SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE granted to `authenticated`, rendered inline in the credential list with no re-auth, no audit row and no rate limit, and its placeholder read verbatim "Left of the porch, behind the planter" — so the field actively coached a means of entry into plaintext, sitting beside `properties.address_line1`. One `GET /rest/v1/properties` plus one `GET /rest/v1/access_credentials` with a borrowed session returned, for every client: full residential address, entry method, and where the key was hidden. DROPPED rather than encrypted in place: `label` already tells credentials apart and the secret field's own placeholder ("Code, key location, alarm sequence…") always covered key locations, so a second ciphertext column would add a reveal path, an audit event and a decision for the operator for nothing. Nothing real is lost — `deploy-production.yml` has ZERO workflow runs, checked against the Actions API rather than remembered, so no production project has ever existed and staging holds only seed fixtures; the migration reports the count it destroys so a future run somewhere unexpected says so in the deploy log. SECOND, the log recorded one event out of four: `credential_access_log` was written only inside `fn_read_credential` on a successful reveal, so create, rotate, revoke and a failed re-auth wrote nothing. A rotation left only `rotated_at`, which the NEXT rotation overwrote, so a door's code history was exactly one entry long and "who changed my garage code on the 14th" was unanswerable; a password attack against the vault left no trace at all. 0030 adds a `credential_action` enum (read/create/rotate/revoke/reauth_failed), IP and user agent on every row, and an optional `walk_id` validated against the operator AND the property — a mismatched walk would make the trail worse than empty by attributing an entry to a visit that was somewhere else. The purpose is typed by whoever is reading, which is the whole weakness of the original design; the walk is the half the system can vouch for. Writes go through definer functions so the row and its audit entry land in ONE transaction — two statements from the edge function could half-succeed and the surviving half would be the one that changes the door. A failed re-auth is recorded before the refusal and best-effort: if the audit write itself fails the caller is still refused, because a 500 there would tell an attacker they had found a way to turn the trail off. The log also had no mutation block at all, unlike `credit_ledger`, so the operator whose reads it records could rewrite or delete them; INSERT is now revoked too, since a forged `read` row attributing an entry to a time is worse than a missing one. And the client — the person whose door it is — had no read path whatsoever, which is what made the trail unable to answer its own question; they now select their own property's trail and credential metadata (never ciphertext, invariant 2) and see it as "Entry code activity" on the portal, with no IP or user agent, because a client does not need their walker's IP to know their door was opened. Specs 01, 03 and 04 all authorised the narrower behaviour and are corrected in the same commit. 195 deno, 164 vitest, 50 smoke blocks. Confirmed red first: no mutation block, no client read path, and a rotate that writes no audit row. Caught in my own work before shipping: the attention row put Neutral Muted on the Kaki tint at 3.71:1, under the 4.5:1 floor — measured by resolving the tokens to hex, which is the method a11y(emaki) and #34 both got wrong; Indigo on the same tint is 7.08:1, matching the figure #34 recorded for that identical pair. Also two of my own test-expectation errors: `renderToStaticMarkup` emits `dateTime` camelCase rather than the DOM's lowercase `datetime`, and `keypad` is not a value of `entry_method`.
- security(auth-posture): closes H2. Two findings. The STRUCTURAL one first: `supabase/config.toml` governs `supabase start` on a laptop and nothing else — neither deploy workflow runs `supabase config push` and that string appears in no workflow, so every auth decision in the file (signup policy, password rules, session lifetime, MFA, OTP expiry, rate limits) was fiction as far as staging and production were concerned, and the real settings lived in a dashboard no file recorded, no test asserted and no reviewer could see. The deployed auth posture was unknown from this repository. Hardened the values so pushing them would be an improvement rather than a regression — password floor 6 → 12 (this password is the only thing between a live session and every door code the operator holds), `password_requirements` empty → `lower_upper_letters_digits`, `secure_password_change` false → true, and the session `timebox`/`inactivity_timeout` uncommented at 12h/2h because both were commented out, so an exfiltrated token was good until its refresh token was revoked. `staging-smoke.yml` gains an `auth-posture` job that reads the live config back through the Management API, prints it into the run summary, and FAILS on the two settings that decide whether the vault's re-auth means anything at all. Printing it is most of the fix: "unknown from this repository" stops being true the moment a reader of the Actions log can see it. Deliberately did NOT add `config push` to either workflow, and `docs/dev/auth-posture.md` says why at length — it applies the auth block wholesale, and more importantly the Supabase CLI is not installed in this environment so I cannot confirm the subcommand exists in the pinned 2.109.1 or which keys it touches. Adding an unverifiable step to the production AUTH path, on a system holding other people's door codes, is the same shape as the typecheck that checked zero files, the vault verification that verified nothing and the cron that reported dispatch as success. SECOND finding: the vault's re-auth was defeatable by a session-only attacker, because with `secure_password_change` off `updateUser({password})` succeeds from a live session with no knowledge of the current password, and the vault check is then satisfied by the password just set — so the re-auth was ceremony and one compromised browser session meant every entry code for every client. Fixed in code and graduated: `sessionAssurance()` reads the request token's `aal` claim and `resolveAssurance()` returns `aal2` (allowed), `aal1_no_factor` (allowed — where the product is today) or `insufficient` (refused). Requiring aal2 unconditionally would lock every operator out, since MFA needs the Pro plan and TOTP enrolment is off, so this way ENROLLING A FACTOR is what closes the exploit with no further code change, and an attacker cannot manufacture aal2 because it needs the factor itself. A missing `aal` claim counts as aal1, never as strong — reading strength from an absent claim would be the gate failing open, and that has its own test. Only VERIFIED factors count: an abandoned half-finished TOTP enrolment must not lock an operator out of their own door codes. 202 deno tests. All three assurance rules confirmed red first — no gate at all, a missing claim assumed strong, and unverified factors counting. Specs 03 updated. Remaining and stated as an owner decision: Supabase Pro for MFA, which is the single highest-value security purchase here because the vault is the product.
- a11y(contrast-method): closes H23, H24, H27, and the half of H29 that is a decision rather than a defect. H25 and H26 were already closed by `a11y(vault+errors)` and were verified rather than rebuilt. The through-line is that every contrast number this repository had written down was **computed against a token**, and the surfaces they describe are not that token. Today's schedule sits on a painted watercolour that samples rgb(237,224,208), not Cream — so the "underway" stroke measured 2.70:1 against a comment claiming 3.27:1, the base track 1.13:1 against a claimed 1.37:1, and "On time"/"DONE" 4.15:1 against a 4.5:1 floor. Third round on that path, so the METHOD is the fix and it landed first: `e2e/today-contrast.spec.ts` takes the ink from computed style, hides it, screenshots the page and reads the pixels. Writing it found three defects in the measurement before any in the product — Chromium resolves `color-mix()` to `color(srgb 0.76 …)` with 0..1 channels, so an amber stroke read as near-black and reported 16.57:1; the "you are here" marker is a `<line>`, so a `path` selector never hid it and it measured against itself at a serene 1.00:1; and a single centre pixel is a guess on a raster, so it now takes the worst 5%-excluded pixel of the whole box. The masthead date needed the opposite care: it carries a four-way cream `text-shadow`, so the artwork behind it (1.04:1) is not its operative background and a naive sample would have produced a FALSE failure — outlined targets measure against the halo and assert the halo exists. Fixed with two Today-scoped variables (`--emaki-current` at 3.50:1, `--emaki-complete` at 4.72:1), 35% chosen over the 40% that also passes because a mark that clears by a hundredth is one artwork revision from failing again. The untravelled track stays exempt as a container, but is measured and printed every run, and an exemption now requires a written reason in the target. H24 was the same disease in the token layer: CT-1's roles clear 4.5:1 on Cream and White by 0.2–0.8 and every tint eats it, so `text-secondary` is 3.71:1 on the Kaki tint — and the tint surfaces ARE the attention states, so the palette made the most urgent information the least readable. Each role gains an `-on-tint` variant at 85% mixed with black (hue-preserving; a mix toward Indigo turns "attention" and "relationship" into two shades of blue), escalated once per SURFACE rather than once per component, because both live failures were components that had no idea what they were sitting on. Three tests: the role x surface matrix recomputed from the stylesheets, a parse asserting every tint-painting rule joined the escalation list, and a Playwright sweep of the rendered gallery. The third earned its place immediately — `.section-label` at 4.09:1 inside a rule that WAS escalating, because the legacy `--ink-500` alias resolved to the raw palette entry rather than to the role, and the role is what carries both the escalation and the `prefers-contrast` override. The nav's high-contrast override had therefore never reached the nav either. H27 was arithmetic: the plate is 875x1798, so a 640px field is 1315px tall and at 1440x900 END WALK was cut in half at 853-914 with the next visit off-screen, while at 768x1024 the last row ran under the bar. Fixed by bounding the field's width by the height available — every dimension is `cqw`, so the approved composition survives exactly, at a smaller size — with a 420px floor where the type clamps stop scaling. Both viewports now hold the whole day in one screen; I looked at the screenshots rather than only the numbers. On H29, spec 05 now names which divergences Today is permitted and their scope, and says plainly that harmonising the product TOWARD the illustrated surface is a separate, open piece of work rather than something to slip into an accessibility pass. Own errors worth recording, all the same shape: a token table that read every `--sanpo-color-*` declaration found a cycle, because component rules declare those names as scoped overrides and not definitions — no cycle in the cascade, one in the model; the rendered sweep first read tint tokens with `getPropertyValue`, got `#F2D7CC`, fed the hex to a number-matching parser and matched nothing, which only surfaced because of a guard asserting the gallery paints tints at all; and `?raw` for reading CSS in vitest is silently empty (`css: false`), so the whole suite would have passed for no reason. 170 vitest, 13 e2e. Every fix confirmed red first, and the CI custom-property check gained comment-stripping after failing on this work's own documentation.
- qa+docs(H18–H21): the four remaining unblocked review findings, in three commits. **H21** was the dangerous one: `CLAUDE.md` designates `docs/spec/` authoritative, and spec 06 still prescribed "stale-while-revalidate for GET API/Storage" — the exact design that caused the cross-account data leak. `qc(1–4)` fixed `sw.js` and never touched the spec, so for four hardening waves the authoritative document described the bug, and an engineer asked to make the worker match spec 06 would have reintroduced it with a written authority for doing so. That section is now a prohibition with a real test behind it: `scripts/service-worker.test.ts` evaluates `sw.js` with a stubbed worker global and drives the actual fetch handler, asserting `respondWith` is never called for the five Supabase path families or any mutation, and — the other direction — that the shell and navigations still are, so deleting the handler cannot satisfy it. Confirmed red by reinstating what the spec used to say. Also corrected: webhook idempotency (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` → the 0013 claim ledger with a LEASE, not a release-on-failure — my first draft got that wrong and a dead process cannot run its own cleanup), change-plan (0015 intent-first, webhook-applied), spec 02's renewal lifecycle (`fn_apply_invoice_paid` in one transaction), and contracts for `billing-portal` and `vault-rekey`, which had none. Spec 03's definer catalogue listed **11** of **48**; it is generated now (`scripts/gen-definer-catalog.py`) with a CI diff. I wrote 52 into the spec before checking, from a regex with a fixed window that was reading a NEIGHBOURING function's `security definer` — corrected, and the trap documented. `/validate` had the same disease in the file that gates every commit: its typecheck was `tsc --noEmit -p app`, which checks zero files, plus a bare `deno test` where CI passes a permission, no lint gate, no e2e gate, and the product still called PawTrail. **H19** was closed except branch protection, which this session cannot even read (403 on the branch and ruleset endpoints) — hence `docs/dev/owner-actions.md`, one list of the nine dashboard settings, secrets and spending decisions that no file here can perform, each saying what is true until it is done. **H20**: `supabase/tests/concurrency.sh` is the only suite that COMMITS, running two real backends. Two wrong drafts first, both tests that could not fail — one used `pg_advisory_xact_lock`, which serialises the sessions itself; the other asserted a balance, which is safe anyway because `fn_ledger_apply` writes `credit_balance = credit_balance + amount` inside one UPDATE. What `for update` protects is the DECISION (`if v_balance >= v_cost`), so the fixture is one credit and two walks: with the lock one debits and one falls to overage, without it both debit and the second hits `check (credit_balance >= 0)`. The "B is blocked" check is labelled a PRECONDITION, since it passes with the lock gone. The storage half found a live defect: both client pet-photo policies read `(storage.foldername(name))[2]` inside `exists (select 1 from pets p …)`, and `pets` HAS a `name` column, so Postgres bound it to `p.name` — the policy has been asking whether the second path segment of the string "Luna" is a pet id. Dead since 0008; the sibling walk-photo policy is identical in form and correct only because `walks` has no `name` column. Same shape as `fn_book_walk`'s `active`. Not a breach (a broken permissive policy denies), but a client has never been able to see or upload a pet photo — and it means 0012's cross-tenant fix was applied to a predicate that granted nothing. 0031 qualifies every reference as `storage.objects.name` and re-reads `pg_policies` to refuse if it did not take. **H18**: vitest becomes two projects, `node` for `lib/` and `scripts/` and `dom` (happy-dom + Testing Library) for components, screens and hooks — before this, every `.test.tsx` rendered through `renderToStaticMarkup`, so no effect, cleanup, subscription, handler or focus behaviour executed anywhere and tests for the vault timer or a route guard were unwritable rather than merely absent. RequireRole (7 cases, including the retry actually being clicked) and the vault reveal (30 s auto-clear, and the interval stopping on unmount — an interval that outlives its component holds a decrypted door code in a live closure) were both confirmed red by sabotage. `critical-flows.spec.mjs` skipped on `E2E_*` vars set in no workflow, so it skipped for everyone while the README called it coverage; it moves to `e2e/manual/`, is excluded by `testIgnore`, and THROWS on a missing variable — a skip reads as a pass. CI now fails a `test.skip(!…)` outside that directory and asserts the `dom` project matched files at all. 190 vitest, 202 deno, 13 e2e, 4 SQL suites. `tsc` caught a fixture error in four passing tests, again.
- fix(env)+security(0032,0033): H22 and the first medium batch — M1, M31, M34, L1. Six mediums were already closed by earlier work and were verified rather than rebuilt (M12, M13, M15, M22, M33, M40); M15 in particular was closed as a side effect of H24 repointing the `--ink-*` aliases at CT-1 roles. **H22**: spec 06 had claimed "build fails on missing required keys" since phase 02 and it did not — `env.ts` threw at first ACCESS from a getter that `supabase.ts` reads at module-evaluation time, so the throw happened inside the import graph before `main.tsx` ran and therefore before `ErrorBoundary` existed. Reproduced end to end first: build with no env vars, serve `dist/`, `#root.innerHTML.length` is 0 with one uncaught page error, and CI's frontend job built in exactly that state and was green. Now `scripts/verify-env.mjs` runs from `prebuild` and refuses (reading `process.env` AND Vite's `.env*` files, because failing a correctly-configured build is the false-positive direction that gets a gate disabled), CI sets placeholders so the gate is EXERCISED and asserts separately that it REFUSES without them, and nothing in `env.ts` throws any more so `main.tsx` can branch to a `ConfigError` panel — measured after: 954 bytes rendered, zero page errors. **M1**: `send-notification` called `requireOperator` and discarded the result, fetching the row by id alone through the service-role client — signup is open, so any operator could email another tenant's client, with four differentiated responses forming an existence oracle. The scope goes into the QUERY rather than a post-fetch check, and both 404s are identical. Plus a send-once guard keyed on `email_delivery_status = 'sent'` and deliberately NOT on `email_attempts > 0`, since a failed attempt must stay retryable — the attempts-based version is tested and fails. **M31**: `0004` enabled RLS from a literal array of the 18 tables that existed in `0002` and `smoke.sql` re-hardcoded the same array, so `plan_change_intents` and `vault_rate_limit_attempts` had no RLS at all and `job_runs` was never FORCED. No live exposure — one `revoke` was the whole protection, which is the point. 0032 fixes all three and both checks now derive from `pg_class`; the smoke assertion is the load-bearing half because a migration asserts once and a table added in 0033 would never be looked at again. Also `v_my_operator` gains `security_invoker`. Found while verifying: the anon sweep tests GRANTS, not RLS — a grantless table raises `insufficient_privilege` and the loop swallows it, so it stayed green on a deliberately unprotected table while the new assertion caught it. **M34**: the block labelled "client booked a walk for another client ... blocked by RLS" used client A's PROPERTY with A2's id, so the tenant-consistency trigger raised first (probed: P0001, "walk property must belong to client/operator") and RLS was never the control — confirmed by weakening the policy's `client_id` predicate, which the old test did not notice and the repointed one does. Seven other negative assertions caught `when others` and asserted nothing about why; pinning SQLSTATE alone would not help since every one raises P0001, so each pins its MESSAGE, every expected string read off a live probe. Proved by rewriting the cutoff guard to refuse for an unrelated reason. **L1**: the walk-photo READ policy checked only segment 2, so operator B could upload `{B}/{walk_of_A}/x.jpg` into their own folder — permitted, segment 1 is B's uid — and have A's client read it in their walk report.
