Nothing leaks OUT; B injects images INTO the proof of service, which is why it sat unnoticed while 0012 and 0031 fixed the two sibling policies. 0033 completes the family. 194 vitest, 205 deno, 13 e2e, 4 SQL suites; every fix confirmed red first against a live database.
- money(0034)+security(headers): M3 and M30. **M3**: `invoice.payment_failed` wrote a `failed` row and nothing resolved it, so a client who updated their card and paid left $90 in Collected AND $90 in Needs attention for one settled invoice, permanently. Correcting the review and my own first summary: the per-dunning-retry accrual it describes ($270 for one $90 invoice) is already fixed by `hasFailedPaymentForInvoice`; what remained is one stale row per invoice. Fixed with a nullable `superseded_at` column and NOT a `superseded` payment status — the enum is the obvious modelling and a trap this repository has paid for once, because `uq_payments_subscription_invoice` and `uq_overage_payment_per_walk` are partial indexes filtered on `status`, so a row moved outside that filter drops out of its own uniqueness guarantee (the 0023 double-grant hole in a new shape). A column also keeps more truth: the attempt did fail, and only whether anyone must act changes. An AFTER INSERT trigger rather than edits to `fn_apply_invoice_paid` plus the overage path, because both write succeeded rows and a third writer will exist eventually. The backfill IS safe here, unlike 0023's, because the link is already in the data rather than parsed out of a note. Frontend: the attention total excludes superseded rows and the retry button stops rendering for a walk since charged — clicking it found the succeeded row, moved no money, and said "Recovered $22.00" anyway. Confirmed red both ways at both layers: with no trigger a paid invoice leaves its failure open; with a trigger that supersedes every failure once anything succeeds, an unrelated unpaid invoice is marked settled — the opposite defect and worse, since it hides money owed. **M30**: `vercel.json` was a single rewrites array, so the deployed frontend had no CSP, HSTS, X-Content-Type-Options, Referrer-Policy or Permissions-Policy at all; the session token lives in localStorage, which is what makes H3 compound. `script-src 'self'` is the load-bearing directive and CI fails the build if it ever gains `unsafe-inline`/`unsafe-eval`. Verified in a browser against a server replaying the real headers rather than reasoned about — zero violations, connect-src/font-src/worker-src all exercised and permitted, and in the other direction an injected `<script>` refused and never executed and a foreign fetch refused. Detected via `securitypolicyviolation`, because a CSP-blocked fetch rejects with a generic TypeError my first check could not distinguish from DNS failure. Two loosenings written into spec 06 rather than hidden: `style-src 'unsafe-inline'` is forced by ~240 inline style objects (M38), and `img-src https:` is broad for signed photo URLs — measured residual: the foreign image was NOT blocked while the foreign fetch was.
- fix(0035): closes M2. `SignIn` offers a magic link, `signInWithOtp` creates the account, and no operator path anywhere sets a password — while every vault action gates on `verifyPassword`. So an operator could hold a valid session and be unable to open the vault at all, and the way they found out was a refusal reading "password verification failed" (a typo message for somebody with nothing to mistype) after `allowAttempt` had already burned a slot, so five tries returned 429 and locked them out on a doorstep with no in-product fix. GoTrue cannot make the distinction and should not: it returns the same `invalid_credentials` for "wrong password" and "no password set", deliberately, so sign-in is not an account oracle — `verifyPassword` collapsing errors to a boolean was the messenger, not the cause. `auth.users.encrypted_password` is the only honest signal and is unreachable through PostgREST, so 0035 adds a definer function whose BODY CHECK is the whole safety property: service role may ask about anyone, a signed-in user only about themselves, or it becomes the oracle GoTrue avoids being. The ordering is the actual fix — the limiter bounds password GUESSING, so it now sits directly in front of the guess and the shape/state checks come first and cost nothing. `password_not_set` is a 409 naming the fix and is deliberately NOT audited: no password was checked, so logging it as a failed re-auth would fill the trail with configuration noise in the one log that must make a real attack visible (H3), and it is not a blind spot because reading a credential still requires setting a password first, after which the reveal writes an ordinary audit row with the IP. Client-side, `ReauthSheet` checks on open and offers to SET a password, then continues straight to the action the operator was taking — in the sheet rather than at each `reauth()` call site because there are four and a fifth will exist, failing OPEN to the password form if the check errors (the server still refuses safely, and assuming "no password" on a lookup failure would push every operator through a reset), and surfacing a rejected `updateUser` rather than swallowing it since `secure_password_change` can require a recent sign-in. Confirmed red in every direction: three deno tests against the shipped ordering, and the smoke block against both a function with no caller check and one treating `''` as a password. One existing test changed — "rate limit rejects before any password attempt" asserted `calls.length === 0`, true only incidentally because the limiter ran first and the fixture did not record it; it now asserts the intent. Also found and fixed here: **a test file matching no vitest project runs nowhere, silently** — `node` includes `src/lib/**/*.test.ts` and `dom` covered only components/screens/hooks, so `src/lib/reauth-sheet.test.tsx` was claimed by neither and vitest reported "No test files found". Include widened, and CI now fails any orphaned test file (verified by planting one). Same class as the typecheck that checked zero files and `?raw` returning empty. 204 vitest, 218 deno.
- fix(0036)+feat(walk-bound): M28, in three parts, because "a walk can never finish" was three separate holes. **The sweep.** `complete-walk` is the only exit from `in_progress` and there was no maximum duration and no stale sweep, so a forgotten END WALK left a walk that never debited, never charged, never sent the client their report — and was INVISIBLE, because Today fetches `{ date: today }` and yesterday's abandoned walk is not today's. The last part is what made the other three permanent. 0036 stamps `walks.abandoned_at` on `in_progress` walks whose `started_at` is over six hours old and deliberately does NOT auto-complete them or guess an end time or a distance: completing means BILLING, and silently charging for a duration invented by a cron job is a worse failure than silently not charging, because the client sees it and the operator does not. The walk stays `in_progress` so it can still be finished properly. `started_at` not `scheduled_date` — a walk started at 23:50 is barely an hour old at midnight and a calendar-day sweep would flag it while the operator is on the doorstep. **The visibility.** `listAbandonedWalks()` is unfiltered by date on purpose and feeds an "Unfinished walks" section at the top of Today's follow-ups, linking to Walk Mode rather than the client record, since END WALK is the one action that finishes it. **The in-session bound.** `walkSessionBound` prompts at `duration_minutes + 30` ("Still walking?") and stops GPS emission 30 minutes later if nobody answered; answering restarts the clock FROM THE ANSWER, since measuring from `started_at` would re-prompt on the next tick and the button would visibly do nothing. Also fixed: `job_runs.error` now carries both advisory sweeps joined with ` | ` rather than `coalesce`, which looks harmless and rebuilds the 0028 swallow one level up — a permanently failing sweep stays invisible for as long as any other sweep is also failing; asserted with both sweeps broken at once. The real find was in my own change: the cap is the FIRST code path that ever deactivates a live watch and can reactivate it (`active` was one-way — a walk with a `result` is over), and the deactivate branch cleared `lastFix` with nothing left behind to say the run had ended. `isGapFix(null, t)` is false, so a resumed trail joined straight onto the fix before the stop and `pathDistanceM` measured the whole un-recorded stretch — H7's exact defect, reintroduced by the fix for M28, on the distance sold as proof of service. `useGeolocation` now marks the first fix of the next run, only if the ended run had produced one (or the trail opens with a break) and clears it once used (or every later point is a new subpath and the rest of the distance disappears). 231 vitest, 218 deno, 13 e2e, 44 smoke blocks. Every rule confirmed red first: five sabotages of the sweep (idempotence, status, age, `started_at`, threshold and caller checks), the `coalesce` swallow, a nightly run with the sweep deleted (caught on the OUTCOME — a flagged walk — not on the reporting key, which survives deletion because `v_stale_walks` initialises to 0), the watch left on `active`, both wrong snooze bases, a Dashboard that renders the section without fetching, and all three resume-gap rules. Two process notes: the migration guard correctly refused an Edit to an unapplied uncommitted 0036, so it was deleted and rewritten rather than the guard weakened; and `git checkout <file>` silently failed to restore two sabotages because the file was untracked, leaving both applied at once — the fourth time this session that command has bitten me.
- fix(0037): M32 — one lock order for `walks` and `clients`, and it is walks first. `fn_debit_walk` locked `clients` then `walks`; `fn_refund_cancelled_debit` is a BEFORE UPDATE trigger on `walks`, so its body runs with the walk tuple ALREADY locked by the UPDATE that fired it and only then reaches for `clients`. Two orders, one cycle. Reproduced deterministically first as `concurrency.sh` case 5, and the observed outcome was worse than the review predicted: the transaction Postgres chose to abort was the CANCEL, so the operator's cancellation silently failed and the walk stayed `in_progress` still claiming its credit — not just "END WALK sometimes fails". Deliberately NOT the fix the review proposed (a cancel RPC that takes the client lock first): that fixes the one path it rewrites and leaves the portal cancel, the pause-window sweep, schedule deactivation and whatever is written next free to invert it again, because the trigger fires for all of them. Walks-first is the order the trigger PHYSICALLY CANNOT violate, so a future path is compliant without knowing the rule exists. `fn_debit_walk` was the only violator in the tree — verified against every `for update` in 0001–0036 — and the reorder also deletes an unlocked pre-read of `walks` that existed only to find `client_id`, so there is no longer a window where the walk is read but not held. The race needs an interleave INSIDE `fn_debit_walk`, which the harness cannot do to a single RPC, so case 5 runs a copy generated from `pg_get_functiondef` with a barrier injected after the first `for update` — generated, so the copy provably carries the shipped order, and anchored on "the first lock" rather than on a table name, so the same test exercises both the old order and the new. The barrier is an advisory lock released by session A, not a sleep: a sleep too short reports "no deadlock", which is a FALSE PASS. It gates one session only and never stands between the two contenders, so it cannot serialise them the way this file's discarded first draft did. Two bugs found in my own test work, both the same shape as things this repo has already paid for. `wait_until_blocked` could pass VACUOUSLY: a needle containing a quote broke its SQL, `q` printed nothing under ON_ERROR_STOP, and `[ "" != "0" ]` read that as "blocked" — so the precondition in all four existing cases had never been required to look at `pg_stat_activity` at all. And the first draft of the smoke guard used `position('walks where id' …)`, which finds the first MENTION rather than the first LOCK — `fn_debit_walk` opened with an unlocked `from walks where id = p_walk`, so the live violation read as compliant; it now extracts the lock sequence per statement with a regex and is confirmed red against the shipped body. The guard checks every function taking both locks, not `fn_debit_walk` by name, because the next one to invert the order will be a new function. L6 was checked rather than rebuilt: 0023 §4b already narrowed the trigger to `old.status = 'in_progress'` and zeroes `credits_debited`, with smoke assertions in both directions. 231 vitest, 218 deno, 45 smoke blocks, 5 concurrency cases.
- fix(device-data): M7, M8, M36, M39 — four mediums, two of them about walk data that never leaves the device correctly. **M7**: `attempts` incremented on ANY failed send while `navigator.onLine` was true, and `onLine` is true on a captive portal, on one bar with no throughput, and on hotel wifi that resolves DNS and nothing else — so twelve attempts of exponential backoff, about nine minutes, silently DELETED a batch of route points with no log, no counter and no flag, while the screen said the walk was recording. Because `drainOnce` stops after the first failure, batches shed one at a time and the route stayed plausibly continuous rather than obviously broken. Three rules now: a transport failure backs off but does not COUNT (the give-up limit exists to identify a batch the server will never take, and a dead network is not evidence of that — backoff pressure moved to a per-instance counter so the delay still grows); a batch given up on is marked `dead` and KEPT, because those points are real observations that never reached the database and deleting them destroys the only remaining evidence the route has a hole in it; and drain/`pendingFor` order by the fix timestamps rather than by the store, because `makeIdbOutboxStore` keys on a random uuid so `getAll()` order is arbitrary — the existing "preserves enqueue order" test passed only because its fake store is a `Map`, and out-of-order points on a mid-walk resume draw a zigzag polyline and INFLATE `distance_m`, the number sold as proof of service. The indicator now asks "is anything still waiting?" rather than "does the OS think we have a network?": CURRENT only when online with an empty queue, SAVING when batches are pending, plus a "Route incomplete" panel naming the lost point count. **M8**: `signOut` cleared the session and three pieces of React state, so a shared device kept the previous operator's raw GPS coordinates, walk notes, care toggles and photo paths indefinitely — and because the outbox is constructed only inside Walk Mode, no drain loop existed to clear them until the NEXT operator opened Walk Mode, at which point they were POSTed under the new session. Both stores are deleted on sign-out, after the session is gone and without throwing (leaving someone signed in because a cleanup failed is worse than the leak), and the outbox gains an `owner` check so a foreign batch is marked dead rather than sent — RLS refusing it is a SERVER ANSWER, so under the new counting rule it would otherwise count as an attempt and the previous operator's route would be destroyed by the next operator merely opening the app. An unresolved operator id disables the check, since treating "not yet known" as "not yours" would destroy the operator's own data on every cold start; that has its own test, and the over-strict version fails 14 of them. **M36**: `format.ts` exposed only `distanceKm`, used by Walk Mode and by `ReportCard` — the report the pet owner receives — while Today converted inline to miles, so one walk read "7.2 mi" on the operator's home screen and "2.1 km" on the client's. Unified on miles (the currency is USD and the timezone is America/Chicago), spec 06 corrected in the same commit since it named the metric one, and a new `scripts/distance-formatter.test.ts` asserts the RULE — no `1609` outside the formatter, no `distanceKm(` anywhere — because fixing two call sites does not stop the third. That guard immediately caught my own doc comment explaining the old name; a grep cannot tell a mention from a use, so it narrowed to a call and exempted the one file allowed to discuss it rather than deleting the history. **M39**: `ClientDetail` rendered "Client not found" for EVERY failure with the raw error as the hint and no retry, so an operator on a weak connection outside a client's door was told the client did not exist, under a string like "JWT expired", with nothing to press. `isNotFound` pins PGRST116 — PostgREST's no-rows-from-`.single()`, the only error that means absence — and everything else goes to `LoadError` with a retry. 259 vitest (28 new), 218 deno, 13 e2e. Every rule confirmed red first: the pre-M7 counting rule, deletion instead of marking, no ownership guard, an over-strict guard, no ordering, a sweep that takes every localStorage key, the `onLine`-only banner, an unsurfaced loss count, and an `isNotFound` that says yes to everything.
- fix(sw): M6, the four halves the review named that were still live (its fifth, "`isNeverCache` is verified by nothing", was closed by H21's `service-worker.test.ts` and was verified rather than rebuilt). **The precache** was every `.js` in `dist/assets`, which swept in the code-split `mapbox-gl` chunk — 1.8 MB of a 3.0 MB precache, 59% of every install, re-downloaded on every deploy, executed only by the users who open a map. The rule is now STATIC REACHABILITY, computed in `generateBundle` where the module graph exists: walk the entry chunks through `imports`, and anything reachable only via `dynamicImports` is left to the worker's cache-first rule on first genuine use. A byte threshold was the obvious first attempt and the build refuted it on the first run — at 512 KiB it also excluded the app ENTRY at 580 KiB, the one chunk without which there is no offline shell at all; the comment I wrote for that threshold asserted it "sits above the app entry (~600 KB)", a number I had not measured and which was self-contradictory. Second bug in my own change: bundle keys are output paths (`assets/index-abc.js`) while the precache is built from `readdirSync`, which yields bare filenames, so matching the two forms produced an EMPTY js precache — silently, which is why the build now throws when no JavaScript survives rather than warning. Result 3.0 MB → 1.14 MB, mapbox runtime-cached. **The stamping** wrapped its whole body in a `try {} catch {}` whose comment claimed "no sw.js in this build" — but `sw.js` lives in `public/` and is always copied, so that catch could only ever swallow a real failure, and an unstamped worker fails twice over silently: `VERSION` stays the literal so the activate sweep never deletes a stale cache again, and `BUILD_ASSETS` stays a string so `Array.isArray` is false and no chunk is precached. Now it throws, and a CI step re-asserts the OUTCOME on the shipped `dist/sw.js` (stamped, has JavaScript, contains no lazy chunk), confirmed to refuse in all three directions. **The install** was `cache.addAll`, which is atomic: one failing asset — a CDN hiccup, a font 404, a chunk that rolled off after a fast redeploy — voided the whole install and left the user on whatever worker they had, or none. Per-URL under `Promise.allSettled` now, with `/index.html` REQUIRED, because best-effort must not mean installing a shell that cannot start. **The update path** did not exist: no `update()`, no `updatefound`, no `controllerchange`, and a browser only checks on navigation, which an installed PWA resumed from the app switcher does rarely or never — so a walker could run a weeks-old bundle against evolved edge contracts indefinitely. `skipWaiting()` is also gone from install, and that is the load-bearing half: it used to replace the controller under a running session, and `activate` then deleted the cache holding that session's chunks, so the next lazy import fetched a hashed file the new deploy no longer serves — a 404 mid-walk. The new worker waits, `UpdatePrompt` offers a reload, and only accepting it posts `SKIP_WAITING`. `hasWaitingUpdate` requires a controller so a FIRST install does not announce a new version to somebody opening the app for the first time, and the reload is gated on a flag because another tab accepting an update also fires `controllerchange` here. 276 vitest (22 new), 218 deno, 13 e2e. Every rule confirmed red first: atomic `addAll`, best-effort with no floor, a message handler that skips on any message, `skipWaiting` at install, and the three artifact-gate directions.
- a11y(marks+tabs+timer)+design(tokens): M37, M16, M19, M14. **M37**: `--hairline` was a raw hex, `#E6E0D8`, and `--mist` resolved to it — so the ledger row separators in ClientDetail and PortalBilling, the vault credential rows and the Roster chips were all drawn by a literal, measured at 1.22:1 on Cream and 1.31:1 on white. Faint is the smaller half; a literal is the one thing the `prefers-contrast: more` block CANNOT reach, so the billing ledger stayed invisible for exactly the users who asked for more contrast. Repointed at `--sanpo-color-border-subtle`, which carries that override — the same role-not-palette distinction H24 had to make for the ink aliases. 32 dead aliases deleted (the review said nine; the count grew as screens moved to CT-1 roles), including the ones whose names LIE: `--orange`, `--pink-ink`, `--mint-ink`, `--lilac-ink` and `--pine-600` all resolved to brand indigo, so a maintainer writing `var(--orange)` in good faith got blue. `scripts/no-raw-hex.test.ts` is the gate `CLAUDE.md` has been claiming since the emaki migration, with an allowlist BY NAME so the one real exemption (`--emaki-paper`, sampled from the artwork master) is a recorded decision rather than a marker anyone can paste. **M16**: both segmented controls declared `role="tablist"`/`role="tab"` with `aria-selected` and nothing else — no `aria-controls`, no tabpanel, no roving `tabIndex`, no key handling, no `aria-label` on ClientDetail's. A screen-reader user was told "tab, 5 of 5, selected" for the operator's main client workspace, whose fifth tab is the credential vault, reached for the arrow keys that announcement implies, and nothing happened. One `SegmentedTabs` + `TabPanel` for both, with MANUAL activation: the APG recommends selection-follows-focus only where panels have no noticeable latency, and ClientDetail's each mount a component that fetches, so arrowing across five would fire five requests. **M19**: the state marks were text glyphs and Nunito does not contain three of them. Verified in Chromium via `CSS.getPlatformFontsForNode` that U+2713, U+21A9 and U+26A0 render in DejaVu Sans while `…`, `!` and `—` come from Nunito — advance-width comparison, which is what the review used, was INCONCLUSIVE here because ✓ and ↩ happen to share a width. The review named two sites; there were six, including the check on the CLIENT'S OWN REPORT CARD. Five new masters on the approved 24x24 / 1.75px grid under the same hash guard (25 assets now), routed through `ApprovedIcon`; the bordered box went with the glyph, since it existed to give a text mark a consistent footprint across fonts that drew it at wildly different widths — a workaround, not a design element. **M14**: a 30 s reveal with no extend meant a timeout forced re-auth + purpose + reveal again, writing ANOTHER `credential_access_log` row — so the trail H3 built to make a real intrusion visible filled with repeated reads of the same door minutes apart, which is the shape a real intrusion has. "Keep showing" restores the window and deliberately writes no row (same person, same purpose, same door), capped at three extensions / 120 s total because an unlimited version is the timer removed with extra steps; `extendReveal` refuses past the cap even when the caller does not check. The limit is now in spec 03 rather than in a code comment citing a spec that never mentioned it. Two of my own changes were caught by this repo's existing gates: the H24 role-contrast check flagged the new photo placeholder for painting a tint surface it has no text on, and flagged three payment-mark selectors left in the escalation list that no longer paint a tint at all — both correct, both fixed. 301 vitest (33 new), 218 deno, 13 e2e, 25 hash-verified assets. Every rule confirmed red first, including the two guards in both directions.
- fix(queries+channel+touch): M9, M10, M11. **M9**: `api.ts` had 61 exported functions and two `.limit()` calls between them, and PostgREST caps an unbounded select at `max_rows` (1000) returning the first page WITHOUT SAYING SO — so an unbounded query does not fail, it quietly answers a different question. Two of those questions were money and service. `listPayments()` fed Today's "Needs attention" strip and Money's three totals ordered newest-first, so past the cap the NEWEST failures were the ones dropped, from the strip whose only job is to surface them; and `listWalksDetailed({})` in PortalHome ordered ASCENDING, so a client past the cap kept their first year and lost every recent walk including the "next walk" the screen exists to show. Seventeen queries were unbounded — the guard found them, I did not guess. Three bounds rather than one, because a route bounded at 200 points is a truncated route. Filtering moved into Postgres where a screen was fetching a haystack to show a needle (`listLowCreditClients`, `listAttentionPayments`, and two bounded PortalHome questions), with `newestFirst` load-bearing beside `limit` since "the last three reports" asked with a limit alone returns the OLDEST three. `LOW_CREDIT_SUBSCRIPTION_STATUSES` is declared once and shared between the selector and the query, because two copies of a predicate is the drift already paid for in the payment-status sets. `scripts/bounded-queries.test.ts` asserts the rule, follows the one bounding helper by name, and separately asserts that helper still bounds — otherwise the indirection is a hole. **M10**: `channel.subscribe()` took no status callback, and that matters more since 0020 made the topic private and authorization real — a rejected join looks exactly like a walk where nothing has happened yet, so the operator's screen said it was broadcasting and the client's portal showed a map that would never move. `channelState` reads anything unrecognised as still JOINING, never as live; claiming a connection on no evidence is what makes this come back. The remaining halves of M10 (the 60 s tick and the `visibilitychange` refetch) were already closed by `today(field)` and were verified rather than rebuilt. **M11**: HTML5 drag-and-drop fires no events at all on touch, yet the walk chip rendered `draggable` with a grab cursor on a phone — the phase-06 headline interaction advertising itself and doing nothing on the primary device. Gated on `(pointer: fine)`, live rather than read once (a tablet gains a pointer when a keyboard case is attached) and defaulting to FALSE where the query cannot run, since a device wrongly treated as touch keeps a working tap flow while the reverse gets the affordance that does nothing. Deliberately NOT a pointer-events rewrite: I cannot verify a touch drag without touch hardware, and a half-verified drag is worse than the tap path that already works — the chip is a button opening an action sheet wired to the same `reschedule()`, which is now asserted rather than assumed. 317 vitest (16 new), 218 deno, 13 e2e. Every rule confirmed red first — and one sabotage found a gap instead of a defect: flipping `channelState`'s fallback to "live" broke nothing, because I had added the function without a test. That test exists now and fails against it.
- security(email-consent): M29, and a defect in the smoke suite found on the way. `clients.email` is operator-typed and reconciled with nothing, there is no consent record anywhere, and the Resend payload carried NO headers — so no `List-Unsubscribe` pair. One typo in the Roster form therefore sends a stranger a recurring feed of `walk_complete` notices: when a named person's house is empty, several times a week, with no way to stop it and nobody to tell; and the `low_credit` body stated the client's credit balance in the message itself. 0038 adds an unguessable `unsubscribe_token`, an `email_suppressions` table, and a public `unsubscribe` edge function. **Suppression is keyed on the ADDRESS, not the client** — that is the whole design: the wrong recipient has no client row of their own, so suppressing "this client" would let the same address start receiving again the moment the operator corrects and re-enters it. It defaults to every operator and every type, because a stranger asking to stop is not asking to stop from one business they have never heard of. The endpoint answers IDENTICALLY for an unknown token, and a raise is an oracle too — the first version of that assertion only caught "reported success", so the sabotage aborted the suite with its own message instead of a `FAIL:` line, which reads as a broken suite rather than a broken rule. `send-notification` fails CLOSED on an unreadable suppression list (recorded retryable, never sent anyway — sending is the one outcome that cannot be taken back) while an actual suppression is TERMINAL, since recording it as `failed` would make the nightly drain retry every night against somebody who asked us to stop. Two of my own errors worth recording. The `create or replace` of `fn_notify_low_credit` was first written from memory and silently dropped the `fn_is_service_session()` guard, turned the unknown-client `raise` into a return, and changed the return type — caught only by reading `pg_get_functiondef` before trusting it, which is now how the migration says it was written. And `revoke select (unsubscribe_token) on clients` is a NO-OP against 0004's table-level grant: a column REVOKE cannot subtract from a table-level SELECT, so the token was still readable — and `listClients()` selects `*`, so it would have shipped to the browser for every client, where one XSS could mass-unsubscribe an operator's entire book. Replaced with an explicit column list, the same shape invariant 2 uses for the vault ciphertext, plus a smoke assertion naming any future column that is not granted (fail-closed, but as a sentence rather than a bare 42501). **The suite defect**: `reset role` does not undo `set local session authorization`, and the storage-policy block set one and never reset it — so every block appended after it ran as `authenticated`, silently, since RLS answers a service-role question with an empty result rather than an error. Found because a 0038 assertion failed confusingly; the danger is the assertions it would have passed vacuously. Fixed, and guarded with a block that asserts `session_user` is back. Also fixed: my own `Calendar.drag` test pinned an absolute fixture date, so it passed on the day it was written and started failing five days later on a change that had nothing to do with the calendar — a fixture that expires fails somebody else's PR. 317 vitest, 223 deno, 13 e2e, 48 smoke blocks. Every rule confirmed red first: per-client rather than per-address suppression, case-sensitive matching, an unknown token that reports failure, an unknown token that raises, the pre-fix token grant, and the balance back in the client-facing body. `NOTIFY_POSTAL_ADDRESS` is a new owner action — the footer says `[postal address not configured]` until it is set, deliberately, so an unset value looks unset in a test send rather than shipping a plausible wrong address.
- ops(deploy-verify)+ci(db-push): M4 and M5, plus a live defect the first of them found while it was still being designed. **M4**: `deploy-production.yml` ended at `deploy-functions` and asserted nothing. `supabase functions deploy` reports success once a bundle is uploaded and never runs it, so a module that throws while EVALUATING — a missing env var read at import, a bad specifier — deploys "successfully" and 500s on its first real call, which on the money paths is an operator standing at a client's door. `scripts/verify-deployment.sh` now runs between `deploy-functions` and `frontend` in both workflows (a frontend released onto functions that cannot boot is H16's ordering bug with a different dependency) and is dispatchable on its own, because `production-cutover.md` §9 is a genuine nine-step check that covers the first cutover once and is never run again. Two phases, because they fail differently: an inventory from the Management API — every function directory in the repo present and ACTIVE, which catches one that was never deployed at all — and then a GET to each, asserting its OWN answer. The function list comes from the filesystem, so a new function is probed automatically and gets the default contract; a bespoke one is a `contract_for` case, and a case naming a function the repo no longer ships is itself a failure, since a stale exception excuses a real probe forever. The half that makes it mean anything is the `x-request-id` requirement: only `serveFunction` sets it, so a 405 without one says the refusal came from something in front of our code rather than from our code. Read-only is argued rather than asserted — `handleRequest` returns before the handler for a non-POST, the webhook 405s before reading its body, and `unsubscribe` is probed with NO token, which a test pins as reaching no database. **The defect**: enumerating what each function answers is what exposed that `unsubscribe` answers 405 to everybody. It went out behind `serveFunction`'s POST-only gate, and the link lives in an email, so every recipient who clicked the one-click opt-out M29 exists to provide got a JSON envelope. Its tests drove the handler and never went through the gate; it had no test of its own at all. `ServeOptions.methods` makes widening opt-in and visible at the call site — the default is what stops a GET reaching a money path, where a charge would become prefetchable and linkable. **M5**: CI validated migrations against a hand-written shim and never against a deploy. `db-reset.sh` connects as the cluster's bootstrap SUPERUSER, which skips every ownership and privilege check there is, and applies each file statement-at-a-time; hosted Supabase's `postgres` is not a superuser and a deploy applies each file as a unit. `scripts/db-push-check.sh` re-applies all 38 from zero as a non-superuser holding only the privileges in the new `platform-roles.sql`, one transaction per file, checking the filename→version contract `db push` derives its ordering from. That privilege list was DISCOVERED, by taking privileges away until the migrations stopped applying, and two entries had never been written down anywhere: membership in the role owning `storage.objects` (the "must be owner of table objects" failure the runbook has described for a year without anyone being able to confirm it — it is real, and `staging-setup.md`'s advice to hand-create the policies in the SQL editor is now removed, because it leaves the project in a state no migration describes) and membership in the role owning `realtime.messages`, which 0020 needs and nothing mentioned. The sharpest finding is not about applying at all: 0004 puts `force row level security` on 23 tenant tables, FORCE subjects the OWNER to its own policies, and a `SECURITY DEFINER` function runs as its owner — so without `BYPASSRLS` all 53 definer functions read zero rows from tables they own and write nothing. The migrations still apply cleanly; the schema is simply inert. Invisible to every test here for the project's whole life, because a superuser bypasses RLS unconditionally, so CI has only ever exercised the one configuration in which the question cannot arise. The gate DEMONSTRATES it — a throwaway FORCE-RLS table and definer function owned by the deploy role, counted 1 then 0 with the attribute removed — rather than asserting it in a comment. What cannot be proved from here is whether a real project GRANTS that much, so that is `docs/dev/db-push-requirements.md`, one query the owner runs once (verified by running it against the modelled project, not by writing it out), and owner action #5. Transaction-per-file also catches something specific to this repo: six `alter type ... add value` statements live in their own migrations by hand, and adding an enum value and using it in the same file passes a statement-at-a-time apply and fails outright as one transaction — with a `-- db-push: no-transaction` escape hatch, because without one the first person needing `create index concurrently` deletes the gate. Ten verify-deployment cases and eight db-push sabotages, all confirmed red first. Two of my own bugs found on the way, both the shape this file keeps recording: the test harness ran the script with `execFileSync` while hosting the stub server in the same process, so the event loop was blocked and the first curl hung; and roles are CLUSTER-wide, so the first version of the privilege model kept passing on grants it no longer made — caught by sabotaging a grant and watching nothing happen.
- deps(M41): six npm advisories to zero, and a correction to the review on the way. The four HIGHs were all dev-only (`playwright`, and `postcss`/`nanoid` reached through `vite`) and went with ordinary bumps; the two that ship to users were `react-router`, an open redirect (CVE-2025-68470) plus its backslash bypass (GHSA-wrjc-x8rr-h8h6), and an SSR-hydration constructor injection. The review said remediation "requires 7.x". That was already wrong: the advisory range is `6.0.0 - 7.17.0`, so most of 7 is equally affected and the patched line is **>= 7.18.0** — an upgrade that stopped at "we are on 7" would have remediated nothing while looking like it had. Playwright was pinned to 1.55.1, the minimum the advisory needs, rather than latest: seven minors of unrelated change on the gate that guards the Today composition is not what a security fix should carry, and the jump to 1.62 also wanted a browser build with a different internal layout. The router moved in TWO commits on purpose — `v7_startTransition` and `v7_relativeSplatPath` are the only two behavioural changes 7 makes here and both are opt-in flags on 6, so they were switched on and the whole suite run green UNDER 6 before the version moved; a major upgrade carrying a behaviour change inside it gives a regression two places to have come from. Also established rather than assumed: the open redirect was **not reachable in this app**. Every `navigate()` and `<Link to>` target is a literal or a template whose first segment is a literal, so none can begin `//` or `\` whatever is interpolated — checked across every call site, not inferred. That was true by accident, which is the kind of property an edit removes without anyone noticing, so `lib/internal-path.ts` now makes it checkable and `deepLink` (the one target built from stored data) goes through it. Deliberately NOT paired with a CI grep over `navigate(` arguments: the single legitimate non-literal call site would have to be allow-listed by name, and a stale exception that excuses a real check is a failure mode already recorded twice in this log. Both sabotages red first — the pre-CVE rule that rejects only `//`, which is precisely the rule the bypass defeated, and a version with no control-character check. The control-character test itself changed shape mid-work: written as a regex it tripped oxlint's `no-control-regex`, and rather than suppress a rule that is right in general it became a code-point scan, because a suppression comment sits where a reader wants a reason. 334 vitest (7 new), 13 e2e, 0 advisories.
- a11y(contrast-modes)+security(csp): the design set's revalidation found two live 1.00:1 defects that the ten-discipline review never described, and both are mine. `M37` repointed `--mist` through `--hairline` at `--sanpo-color-border-subtle` — a BORDER role — and the vendor palette darkens border roles to Indigo under `prefers-contrast: more` while `--sanpo-color-text-primary` IS Indigo. Used as a rule that is the whole point: the boundary gets more visible. Used as a FILL it inverts, and two screens used it as a fill: the operator's client **invite link** (the one place an operator hands a client their account) and the client's Booking **cost card** on the DEFAULT credit-funded branch, i.e. the common case. Measured in a real browser under `contrast: more`, not computed from tokens: background `rgb(12,71,116)` on colour `rgb(12,71,116)`. Invisible. Gated on an OS setting rather than a viewport, so it hit exactly the users who asked for more contrast — the same failure shape M37 was written to fix. The rule now stated in `components.css` is *fill with a surface role, bound with a border role*: a surface role is untouched by the override, a border role is improved by it. Both sites are 9.03:1 in both modes. Booking's overage branch also changes colour, deliberately and visibly on a client money screen: `--amber` resolves to the Yamabuki **milestone** tint (spec 05:14 "Yamabuki marks positive milestones") on the panel telling a client they are about to be charged extra, while spec 05:99 says plainly "`overage` and `attention`: Kaki attention tint and border" and all four sibling surfaces already obey it. Booking was the sole outlier and only because an inline style reached for a legacy alias instead of a role. Third defect, same pass: `.today-emaki__empty` has failed AA at every viewport since it shipped — its `clamp(14px, 2.7cqw, 20px)` can never exceed 17.28px inside the 640px-capped field, so the 18.66px large-text threshold is unreachable and the floor is permanently 4.5:1 against text-secondary's 3.69:1 on `--emaki-paper`. `today-contrast.spec.ts` measures the progress strokes and row accents, so an empty day was the one Today state nothing looked at. **Why nothing caught any of it**: `role-contrast.test.ts` reads `components.css` and the vendor palette and never `tokens.css`, so the alias chain was not a chain it could follow — and the defects were in TSX, which it does not read at all. `scripts/inline-style-tokens.test.ts` closes that: it walks `var()` chains through all three stylesheets, collecting `prefers-contrast` declarations SEPARATELY because a role can be benign at `:root` and hostile under the override. Its own first version was wrong in a way only the sabotage found — it returned the chain's ENDPOINT, which is a hex literal, so "does this resolve to a border role?" matched nothing and the gate passed against the exact defect it exists for. It returns the chain now: two roles with opposite meanings can end at the same colour, which is how the inversion happens, so the question is what a value goes THROUGH. Three tint sites moved from TSX into CSS purely so the H24 escalation gate can see them — moving a tint out of a style object is what puts it under an existing check — and `--mist`, `--amber`, `--butter`, `--shadow-1` and `--sky-bright` are deleted now that nothing consumes them, along with `PlaceholderScreen.tsx`, dead code that was the sole thing keeping `--shadow-1` alive. `--ink-900` was NOT deleted: it has two real CSS uses, and the agent that proposed the list had it wrong. **Separately, spec 06 stated a falsehood I wrote**: that `style-src 'unsafe-inline'` "is required by ~240 inline `style={{…}}` objects" and "cannot be tightened without that refactor". React never writes a style ATTRIBUTE — it assigns through CSSOM (`node = node.style`), which CSP does not govern — so the exception bought nothing and recorded a permanent weakening as the price of a refactor nobody was going to do. Verified by serving the real built bundle under `style-src 'self'`: 0 violations, 0 console errors, every inline style applied including one resolving a `var()`; plus no `setAttribute("style")` or `cssText` anywhere in the bundle and no `style=`/`<style` in `dist/index.html`. Honest limit, written into the spec: only the signed-out route loads without a backend, so the authenticated screens were not rendered under the tightened header. CI's `unsafe-*` check now covers `style-src` as well as `script-src`. 338 vitest, 13 e2e. Every rule confirmed red first — a border role as a fill, a CT-1 tint inline, a raw hex — and the resolver bug was found by the sabotage rather than by review.
- brand(portal-nav)+today(week-width)+type(ramp): M18, M20 and M21, all three corrected by measuring rather than by repeating the review. **M18** is real and the fork had WIDENED since the review — 15 `[data-navigation-persona="operator"]` selectors, not 12 — but the fix is a DELETION, and smaller than it looks, because the Today-specific rules are keyed on `body:has(.today-emaki-page)` and not on the persona. The client never renders that page, so dropping the scope changes nothing about what the operator renders: the whole fork was redundant, which is the finding. The client had been carrying a Biscuit-era opaque white bar with an indigo-filled pill, byte-identical since phase(09) where `--brand` was `#FF6B35`, and no rail at any width. Two things this exposed that the review did not describe. The rail's `border-right` has NEVER rendered: the persona rule was (0,2,0) and set `border-color: transparent`, beating the (0,1,0) rail rule — so un-scoping makes them equal and source order would silently switch a border ON, on a locked operator surface, from a change meant to touch only the client. Zeroed explicitly. And the client's nav focus ring was drawn OUTSIDE the viewport: the operator rule carries `outline-offset: -3px` while the client fell through to the global `+3px` on an element at `bottom: 0` — measured at 390x800, ring outer edge at y=806 against an 800px viewport. That is the one genuine a11y defect in M18 and one treatment removes the class of it. `/portal/pets` was routed, specified at 06:63 and linked from NOWHERE — a 241-line screen reachable only by typing the URL, the same shape as `fn_book_walk`'s phantom `active` column — now linked from Home and made a rule by `route-reachability.test.ts`, deliberately not a fifth tab. **The regression that only measuring caught**: deleting `.bottom-nav .bottom-nav__item--active` as "duplicating the base" made the active item paint the deepened INACTIVE colour, because the base rule is (0,1,0) and `.bottom-nav .bottom-nav__item` is (0,2,0). Measured rgb(69,100,124) — the exact 70/30 mix — against Indigo's rgb(12,71,116), on the operator's locked navigation, before it was restored. Every operator dimension was then re-measured in a browser: transparent bar, 0 borders, 24x24 icons with no fill, Yamabuki marker at 34px / 3px on the rail, active Indigo at weight 900. **M20**: the cap is real — the week's seven days measured 86.84px each at BOTH 768x1024 and 1440x900, so widening the browser gained the grid exactly zero pixels — but three of the review's claims are wrong. "56% carrying nothing" is 49.4% (it counted the 88px navigation rail as nothing). "Cannot see next week" is FALSE at desktop, where `scrollWidth === clientWidth === 608`, and true on the PHONE, where 202px is hidden behind a horizontal scroll nested inside a vertically-scrolling page — the viewport the review does not mention. And Clients does not belong in the list at all: `.client-row` is a two-column reading row. `.page--wide` raises `--page-max` to `min(1120px, 100% - 88px)` at ≥1024 for the week view only, moving the custom property rather than `max-width` because the rail centring reads the same property; the `100% - 88px` term is load-bearing and that was proved, not asserted — with a bare `1120px` a 1024px viewport overflows the document by exactly 88px, the rail width. A second defect the review never described: the chip's text does not stop at its border. "11:30 AM Bartholomew" painted 6.16px past its own chip and 2.16px INTO TUESDAY at 1440x900, 13px / 9px at 390x844 — ordinary dog names, no unusual data. `.walk-card__route` already carried `overflow-wrap` and `.payment-row__pet` carried ellipsis; the calendar chip is the one row type that got neither. **M21**: the axis is live and that was measured rather than read off the `@font-face` — at 40px the advance widths rise monotonically 496/498/509/519/527/541 across 400-900 with no fallback face, so 500 is a real interpolated instance. Of 84 weight declarations NONE was below body's 600 and all fourteen rules pairing the de-emphasis colour with a weight sat at 700 or 800, so subordinate text rendered heavier than the body it is subordinate to while `--sanpo-color-text-secondary` sits 0.23 above the AA floor with nothing to spend. `--fw-quiet: 500` (not the review's 400 — the finding is the inversion, not absolute lightness) on twelve rules; the two CONTROLS in that set are exempt by name, because a segmented tab carries active/inactive separation in its weight and that is an affordance decision needing its own argument. Today is untouched: it already spans 1000→600 and that ramp is spec 07's. `type-ramp.test.ts` scans TSX as well as CSS, because the one offender outside the stylesheet was a 12px caption at weight 800 that a CSS-only check would have missed. Also new: a CI step failing any e2e spec with no step to run it — specs are invoked by filename, so one added without a step runs nowhere while looking like coverage. 346 vitest, 231 deno, 20 e2e. Every rule confirmed red first, and one e2e case failed for its own bug first — reading a bounding box straight after `goto` with a `?? 0` fallback, so an unrendered page reported a left edge of 0, which is a plausible number rather than an obvious absence.
- ops(deploy-gating): every staging deploy had failed since the frontend job was added, and the cause was three separate bugs of mine stacked on one job. **First**, `deploy-staging.yml` had the `verify-functions` and `frontend` conditions SWAPPED. `verify-functions` gated on `needs.verify-functions.result` — its OWN name, which is not in its `needs` and therefore evaluates empty and never equals `'success'` — so the job could never run and the M4 boot probe was **inert from the day it merged**: a verification step that verified nothing, which is precisely the failure it exists to prevent. `frontend` correspondingly lost the clause meant to gate it and released while verification was skipped. Production was correct, and the asymmetry explains the cause: the patch that added the gating replaced the FIRST matching `if` in each file with `count=1`, and by then staging already had a `verify-functions` job sitting above `frontend` while production did not. I validated the YAML and the `needs` graph afterwards — both were right — and never read the conditions back, which is the whole lesson. **Second**, the job pushes `release/staging` from a checkout with no `fetch-depth`, and `actions/checkout` defaults to 1: git cannot prove a fast-forward from a single-commit clone, so the server rejects an ordinary push as though history had diverged. The FIRST push created the ref and succeeded — creating a ref needs no ancestry check — and every push after it failed, which is why this looked like a sudden regression rather than something that never worked. Reproduced against a scratch remote before fixing: a depth-1 clone gives `! [rejected] (fetch first)`, a full clone fast-forwards, for a commit that is a descendant either way. **Third**, the job's own error message misdiagnosed it — "probably not a descendant of the commit currently released, re-run on the newest commit on main" — sending the reader after a rollback that was not happening, when the commit WAS a descendant. All three now say what they mean, and `scripts/verify-workflows.py` refuses the class: no job may gate on its own result; a job whose `if` uses a status function must re-state every `needs` whose implicit `success()` it dropped (`always()` exempt, since `assert-deployed` deliberately gates in its steps); and a job running `git push` must set `fetch-depth: 0`. All three rules confirmed red against the exact shipped text. Worth recording that the first attempt at that proof was itself broken — a bash-quoted sabotage silently failed to apply, so two rules "passed" while testing nothing, and the local reproduction of the shallow push initially used a local clone, where git ignores `--depth` and says so. `release/staging` is at `560e96a` and main is a descendant, so the next deploy fast-forwards with no ref surgery.
- security(auth-posture): the H2 gate ran for the first time on 2026-08-29 — it fires on a successful staging deploy and the four staging-smoke runs before it were all `skipped` — and went red correctly, but one of its two failures could not be acted on. `jq -r '.secure_password_change_enabled // false'` maps BOTH "returned false" and "no such key" to `"false"`, and those need opposite actions: a dashboard toggle, or a bug in the gate that no dashboard change can ever satisfy. The live response returned `null`, precisely the value the idiom could not classify. The checking moved to `scripts/check-auth-posture.sh` so it can be driven against fixtures at all — inline in YAML nothing could exercise it, which is why the defect survived from the day it was written. Presence is now asked separately from value, an absent key prints the response's own key names beside it, and a present-null is reported as present-and-unset. Only projected keys are printed; `/config/auth` carries SMTP and provider secrets. Three corrections, all the same direction — the docs were more confident than the facts. `secure_password_change` does NOT close the stolen-session chain (GoTrue demands reauthentication only once a session is older than 24h, so a lifted phone is inside the window); worse, a session timebox at or under 24h makes the setting permanently inert, so the two hardened values cancel out, and the gate now emits a `::notice` the moment that becomes true; and MFA does not need the Pro plan here — TOTP enrol and verify both read `true`, so the `aal2` path is reachable today for free, which three files and owner-action #9 denied. 11 tests, the three presence ones confirmed red against the shipped idiom.
- security(0039): closes H4. `fn_claim_invite` was one UPDATE with no expiry, no revocation, no binding to who was invited and no record that anyone tried; `fn_preview_invite` checked nothing either. Whoever opened the link first, from any account, became that client — address, door code, pet notes, every GPS trace terminating at the house. The operator could not fix it: `invite_token` is in no UPDATE grant, no RPC rotated it, and every child FK is `on delete restrict` so the row could not be deleted once it had a pet. 0039 adds expiry (14 days), revocation, reissue and a per-attempt log, checked in the preview as well as the claim. The structural decision, and the first draft got it wrong in a way that read as correct: **refusals are RETURNED, not raised** — a PL/pgSQL `raise` rolls back to the caller's savepoint and discards the audit row written beside it, so log-then-raise records only the attempts that SUCCEEDED. Measured, not reasoned about: four attempts written, one survived. Claiming binds to the invited address when the operator recorded one, because the claimant types their own address at signup, so confirmation otherwise verifies the address they chose rather than the one invited. Backfill measures expiry from `created_at`, not from now — `now() + 14 days` would extend the lifetime of exactly the credentials being bounded. Seven database sabotages and two frontend ones, all red first.
- privacy(0040): closes H5. No way to delete or export anything, and no retention job; a client who left kept their address, their encrypted door code and every GPS trace indefinitely. The shape is decided by the FK graph read off `pg_constraint`, and it is not what the review assumed: `credit_ledger.walk_id` and `payments.walk_id` are RESTRICT and the ledger must survive, so **walks cannot be deleted**; `walks.property_id` is RESTRICT and NOT NULL, so **properties cannot be deleted**; and `credential_access_log.credential_id` is RESTRICT into a table 0030 made immutable while `fn_write_credential` writes a `create` row for every credential, so **a credential can never be deleted** either. That last one is deliberate — H3 built the trail so an intrusion is visible, and letting a purge erase it hands the audited party a way to erase their own reads. **The destroyable thing is the secret, not the row**: the ciphertext is overwritten with a 37-byte sentinel that is not a v2 blob, so 0021's `key_id` resolves to NULL. Storage is two-phase and the order is load-bearing — SQL cannot delete a bucket object, so the paths are returned, the browser removes the objects, and only then are the rows dropped; the rows are the work queue, so it is resumable and an orphaned photo is impossible by construction. Retention sweeps COMPLETED walks past a per-operator window (default 365, 0 disables). Two defects caught in my own work, both tests that could not fail: a `create or replace` built from the 0028 body silently deleted 0029's and 0036's additions (existing smoke caught it), and the purge deleted `credential_access_log` first, which raises for **every client whose door code was ever stored** — the smoke fixture had inserted a credential directly, bypassing the definer that writes the audit row, so the hardest constraint in the design was never exercised.
- privacy(0041): closes H6. No notice, no terms, no consent record, and nowhere in the route table one could be linked from. **A timestamp alone is not a consent record** — it says somebody agreed to something and nothing about what, because the document can change underneath it. So each acceptance stores the document VERSION and a test hashes the text and fails if it changed without the version changing; that guard is the whole mechanism by which the stored version is evidence. The client's acceptance is written INSIDE `fn_claim_invite`, so a claimed account with no consent record is not a reachable state, and a claim supplying no version records no acceptance rather than asserting agreement to a document nobody can look up. Neither column carries an UPDATE grant for any API role. The documents describe what the code actually does, checked against call sites including exactly what each of the five subprocessors receives; a test fails the build if one is dropped. They are NOT legally reviewed and that is owner action #10 rather than a hedge inside the text. Landed after H5 deliberately: an honest notice has to describe deletion and retention, and until 0040 there was no deletion path to describe. Five sabotages red first.
- money(H11+H12): two money findings sharing a root — the system knew something about a client's money and neither acted on it nor said it. **H11**: `customer.subscription.updated` wrote `plan_id` ONLY inside `if (intent)`, so a price changed in the Stripe dashboard — which per B5/B6 is the only place a plan's price exists to be edited, making this the ordinary support action — left `clients.plan_id` on the old plan. It then SELF-PERPETUATED: `resolvePlan` short-circuited on the cached id, so every renewal granted the OLD plan's credits while Stripe collected the NEW price, forever, with nothing flagging it. Stripe is now the source of truth for what is billed: a price-derived plan that differs is written and the operator notified, the intent branch still wins when it matches, and a price with no local plan is reported but never clobbers `plan_id` (nulling it strands the client with no credits and there is nothing correct to point at). `resolvePlan`'s precedence is inverted — the invoice line's price decides, the cache is the fallback — which also makes `invoice.paid`'s own correction reachable; it was dead code, because resolvePlan returned the cached plan and the ids could never differ. The notification is self-limiting: it fires only when the ids disagree and the same event writes the fix. **H12**: `notifyFailure` shipped with no counterpart, so a SUCCESSFUL off-session charge told nobody — the client's only message was `walk_complete` with no amount and no mention that money moved. They were never quoted it either: Booking compared cost against the RAW balance, so two credits could carry three bookings with the confirmation shown on none, each being individually affordable at booking while billing happens at completion. `committedCredits` counts walks already booked and not started (`scheduled` only — an `in_progress` walk is already debited or flagged, and double-counting warns about a charge that will not come). Disclosure now sits at the Checkout mandate (on Stripe's record, naming the rate), at booking (committed vs available), and at `payment_taken` (amount + receipt), with the cost on the report card. Deliberately NOT built and recorded in spec 02: a sweep notifying that a scheduled walk became unfunded — per walk per night, reversed by the next cycle grant, and the kind of message that gets muted, after which the charge surprises them anyway. Both enum values had existed since 0025, whose header says it added them for H11/H12 and that "every reference lives in 0026"; 0026 fixed H9/H10 and wired neither, so this needed no migration. Six sabotages red first. 402 vitest, 247 deno.
- ops(smoke-identity): the diagnostic split from the previous commit answered its own question on the first run — `The lookup can see diag-claim-op@sanpo.test as e808132b…, so the DELETE is what failed`. Pagination was fine; cleanup was not. Cause: every FK in `0002_schema.sql` is `on delete restrict`, so the `operators` row survives while ANY dependent does, and `operators.id` is the auth user's id — so the auth user could not be deleted either, and the next run collided on 422. Two contributing bugs: `old_client_row` took `.[0].id`, so an operator that had accumulated more than one client kept the rest and blocked its own deletion; and every delete in `cleanup` ended `|| true`, so a refused delete was invisible and the leftovers accumulated silently until a run could no longer start. The real fix is structural rather than another round of cleanup repair: the fixture addresses are now **run-scoped** (`diag-claim-op-${GITHUB_RUN_ID}-${GITHUB_RUN_ATTEMPT}@sanpo.test`). A throwaway fixture has no reason to hold a stable identity, and a stable one makes cleanup a PRECONDITION — if the previous run failed to tidy up, this run cannot start. Run-scoping demotes it to housekeeping, which is what it should always have been. Cleanup is still fixed rather than abandoned: it deletes every client of the operator, and reports each refused delete as a `::warning` so accumulation is visible while it is cheap, without failing a run over it. Verified by extracting `del`/`cleanup` out of the YAML and driving them against a stub that 409s the operators delete: three client rows deleted, one warning, exit 0; and an empty cleanup silent. Worth recording that this took four rounds, and the ordering was the lesson — the two fixes that did not resolve it (pagination, then not discarding the response) are what made the fourth diagnosis a reading rather than a guess.
- ops(smoke-fixtures): I predicted the staging smoke would go green once the posture check moved out. It did not, and the reason was a second, unrelated failure the posture red had been hiding: `FAIL: fixture operator user`, from the invite-claim replay. Two defects, both pre-existing, neither caused by the split — verified by diffing the workflow, which shows my change was a pure deletion of the auth-posture job. FIRST, `user_id_for` read `?page=1&per_page=100` and stopped. Its job is to find THIS run's leftovers so they can be deleted before the fixtures are recreated, so once staging held more than 100 auth users they fell off page 1, cleanup found nothing, and the create then collided with the row it had failed to remove. It pages now — and my first attempt at that was defeated by the same class of mistake: it asked for `per_page=200` and treated a short page as the last one, but GoTrue CAPS the page size, so page 1 returned 100, `100 -lt 200` was true, and it stopped having read one page. Inferring the last page from the size REQUESTED rather than from what the server returned is the original bug wearing a different hat. It stops on an EMPTY page now, and carries a `seen` guard so an endpoint that ignores `page` is detected rather than rescanned to the bound and reported as absence. SECOND, and the reason I had to reason about the first instead of reading it: the create piped `curl` straight into `jq -r '.id'`, so the status and GoTrue's own message went in the bin and "already registered", "weak password" and "service key rejected" were one indistinguishable red. Same defect as review H14, in the workflow whose entire job is to say when staging is wrong; it now captures the body and prints `HTTP <code> — <message>`, projecting `error_description`/`msg`/`message` only rather than dumping a response. Verified by extracting the real function out of the YAML with a parser and running it against a stub that CAPS per_page the way GoTrue does: the target sits on page 3 of 350 users and is found, an absent address terminates empty, and a server that ignores `page` is caught by the `seen` guard in 0s rather than scanned 50 times. The diagnostic half paid for itself on the very next run, which said `HTTP 422 — A user with this email address has already been registered` instead of `FAIL: fixture operator user` — so the collision theory stopped being a theory. A 422 now also reports WHICH half is broken, by asking whether the lookup can see the leftover: if it can, the DELETE failed; if it cannot, the SEARCH did. Those were indistinguishable, and they have different fixes. Also removed a comment block orphaned above `onboard-repro` when the posture job moved out. The measured posture, now that the gate names keys that exist: `security_update_password_require_current_password=false` AND `security_update_password_require_reauthentication=false` — both off, so the previous entry's "off" was right in substance and had still never been measured; `password_min_length=6`; TOTP enrolment and verification both on.
- ops(deploy-retry): the staging deploy of `db741c8` went red with `failed to bundle function: exit 135` on `change-plan`. 135 is 128+7 — SIGBUS, the bundler process killed rather than reporting a diagnostic. Established before acting rather than assumed: `git diff --name-only df814295..db741c82` shows my two commits touched ZERO files under `supabase/`, the identical source bundled successfully in the deploy 45 minutes earlier, and `deno check supabase/functions/**/index.ts` passed on the same commit. So the input was not the problem. `rerun_failed_jobs` 403s (the token has no Actions write), so the retry had to come from a commit. Both deploy workflows now retry the function deploy up to three times with backoff. Safe here and NOT elsewhere, which is the whole argument: this step uploads bundles — it moves no money, migrates no data and mutates no customer row, and the artifact is a pure function of the source, so a second run cannot produce a state one run would not. `db push` gets no retry for exactly the inverse reason, and `ci(concurrency)` already refused `cancel-in-progress` on the deploy workflows on the same principle. Bounded and LOUD: every attempt is printed, a `::warning` names the retry count whenever one was needed, and three failures emit an `::error` saying outright that this is not transient and to read the bundler output before blaming infrastructure — a silent retry turns a recurring fault into a slightly slower green. Two bugs in my own change, both caught before pushing: the warning string originally contained `` `supabase functions deploy` `` inside double quotes, which is command substitution — it would have re-run the entire deploy inside an `echo`; and the first fix over-corrected to `\$attempt`, which in a YAML block scalar reaches bash as a literal `$` and prints the variable name instead of the number. Both found by extracting the step's real `run:` block with a YAML parser and executing it against a stub `supabase` that fails a configurable number of times — all three paths (clean, one retry, three failures) verified by running them, not by reading them.
- ops(auth-posture): the owner-actions pass, and the gate it depends on was broken. `check-auth-posture.sh` asked the Management API for `secure_password_change_enabled` — a key that API has never returned — so the check could not be satisfied by any dashboard change and `staging-smoke.yml` had been RED on every run since the job landed, hiding two passing regression jobs behind it. The presence-vs-value split built in PR #66 is what caught it: the first real run reported, in its own words, that "no dashboard change can satisfy the gate while it names a key that does not exist", and printed the response's real key names. The root cause is mine and it is the interesting part — `auth-posture.test.ts` had TWO fixtures, one captured from a live response and one I invented, and only the invented one exercised the PASS path. A test cannot catch an error it shares with the code, so the suite was green while the gate was permanently red. `compliant` is now derived from the captured key set (values overridden, names never invented), and a new test PARSES the script and asserts every key it names appears in a real response — six of thirteen cases fail when the original defect is reinstated. The check now accepts either `security_update_password_require_current_password` (closes the exploit — the attacker must know the current password) or `security_update_password_require_reauthentication` (narrows it — GoTrue only demands a nonce past 24h), naming which one it found, because the config.toml→API mapping cannot be resolved from here: the CLI is not installed and supabase.com is blocked by the egress proxy. Naming both is the honest answer to that rather than a guess. The posture check also moves to its own workflow: smoke asks "did the deploy break the product", posture asks "is the owner's configuration where we want it", and the second is EXPECTED to stay red until a person changes a setting — it must not spend the first one's credibility saying so (the `ops(gate-noise)` failure mode, second occurrence). Documentation corrected where it asserted things nobody had measured: `auth-posture.md` recorded `secure_password_change` as "came back null", which was an artifact of the bug rather than a reading, and `owner-actions.md` stated it as "off". Verified against live APIs rather than assumed: `main` is `protected: false` (so §7's "the token 403s, unreadable from here" is out of date for the branches endpoint, though rulesets remain unverifiable), `release/staging` tracks main at `df81429` so H16's ref-pushing half is confirmed working, `release/production` correctly does not exist, and staging's `password_min_length` is 6. `owner-actions.md` gains a three-item ordered front page and a table naming what tells you each item is still undone — two of the twelve, Realtime public access and the backup tier, have no automated signal at all, which is worth knowing about those two specifically.
- qc(lows): the 23 low findings, in three commits — 14 fixed, 7 verified already closed, 2 argued down to a recorded decision. Three earned their promotion. **L7** was parked on an explicit expiry ("becomes a real medium the day the settings screen ships") and B6 shipped it, so the client now agrees a price at booking and is charged whatever the mutable tables say at completion; 0043 snapshots both figures in a BEFORE INSERT trigger rather than at each of the three creators, backfills nothing (a guessed price is indistinguishable from a real one), and grants no API role UPDATE — a snapshot the operator can rewrite is not a snapshot. **L21** was fixed the way the review actually prescribed rather than the way that hides it: `InboxField` moves to `src/prototypes/` with its own stylesheet, because "dead CSS ships" and "a maintainer spends an afternoon looking for the messages table" are the same finding. 65.0 KB → 58.5 KB built stylesheet. My first attempt extracted the CSS with a depth-blind `re.split(r'(?<=\})\n')` that cut through `@media` blocks and left the file unbalanced; the second over-moved 71 classes because the H24 tint-escalation rule is one comma-grouped selector listing production AND prototype selectors, so matching "any class is a prototype class" took `.btn`, `.walk-card` and eighteen others with it. Third version splits comma groups and requires EVERY class in a group to be prototype-owned. `LiveWalkBanner` was deleted rather than moved — it is the pre-emaki current-moment banner superseded by `TodayIllustratedSchedule`'s own row, so it was not a prototype awaiting a backend but a retired component the DEV gallery still presented as current. **L16** had no recovery path at all: `resetPasswordForEmail` appeared nowhere, and the magic link is labelled as another way to sign IN. `/reset-password` watches for a session arriving AFTER the first `getSession()` — the URL parse is async, so the first answer is null for a link that is perfectly good, and without the subscription the screen tells somebody holding a working link that it expired (confirmed red by deleting the subscription). `describeResetOutcome` returns the same conditional sentence for success and for every unrecognised status, with the neutral branch as the DEFAULT rather than a list of known-safe codes, so a status this code has never seen cannot become a disclosure by omission. **L23**: the Stripe metadata keys are renamed outright with no dual-read — `deploy-production.yml` has 0 runs (checked against the Actions API) and no workflow, smoke suite or e2e spec invokes `change-plan`, so nothing carries the old keys — and both sides now import the names from one module, because the writer and the reader never call each other and a halfway rename would not fail to compile, would not fail either side's mocked test, and would silently stop applying plan changes people had paid for. Seed data moved London → Chicago, `@pawtrail.dev` → `@sanpo.test` (RFC 2606 reserved, so a fixture that reaches a mailer cannot email a stranger) and `+44 7700 9000xx` → the `+1 555-01xx` block. The device storage keys keep `pawtrail` deliberately and spec 06 says why: they are addresses, not labels, and renaming one orphans an in-progress walk's photos (H8) or a queue of undelivered GPS points (M7). **L8** was split: the billing address is collected now, because Stripe Tax cannot be enabled retroactively for customers whose address was never captured, while `automatic_tax` is not — it needs overage restructured from a PaymentIntent, which cannot carry tax at all, into an invoice. `customer_update.address` is the half that is easy to lose, and its absence collects the address for the payment and never writes it to the Customer; the test pins the two against each other and needed the deno read permission widened to `supabase/functions`, done in the same commit across all four places that string lives. **L17** is the review's motion spec minus one wrong assumption: the Today stroke is drawn by CLIPPING the path, not by `stroke-dashoffset`, so what animates is the clip rect's geometry — verified tweening in a real Chromium (270px → 810.6px at 120ms → 900px) rather than asserted from the computed style. The three new `:active` tint states were caught by this repo's own H24 escalation check on the commit that added them. **L9**'s runbook corrects the finding twice: H15 removed one of the two pasted service-role keys, and H17's backlog means the surviving one now delays email rather than losing it. **L22** deletes `HANDOFF.md` (a dead machine path, 93 tests where there are now 436, and a final paragraph contradicting the current working agreement), adds a root README, and adds `scripts/validate.sh` so the gate `CLAUDE.md` names is runnable without Claude Code — it found the stale definer catalogue on its first run. Deliberate non-actions, recorded rather than silently skipped: no `automatic_tax` (above), and no rename of the four device storage keys (spec 06). Own errors worth recording: a case-sensitive `sed` over `@pawtrail.dev` missed `H4-A@PawTrail.dev`, which is the deliberately case-mangled address proving invite binding is case-insensitive — smoke caught it; and `/validate`'s file had TWO gates numbered 11, one of them the CSS-token check added earlier this session. SignIn had no test at all, which is how it acquired a third mode with nothing checking the third mode is reachable; six now cover the affordance, the oracle, the rate limit and — the reason to write them — that adding a branch to a chain of ternaries did not stop the other two rendering. 451 vitest, 250 deno, 20 e2e, 3 SQL suites, 5 concurrency cases.
- money(pay-per-visit): H32 — a client with no plan is now chargeable, and `payments.type='topup'` stops being the enum value nothing had ever written. Three pieces. **The price.** `service_types.visit_price_pence` (null = not offered; 0 refused, the 0026 rule) snapshots into `walks.visit_price_pence` at INSERT via the 0043 trigger, and the charge resolves snapshot-first: plan rate, else visit price, else live plan rate for walks with no snapshot at all. The blocking discovery was that 0043's money-side snapshot had ZERO readers — `getClientBilling` selected the live `plans(overage_rate_pence)` and spec 02 claimed "snapshotted at creation, not read at completion" while spec 04 documented the live read, so the Settings-edit re-pricing L7 was recorded as fixing was still live for the dollar amount, and the review's "it just needs a price source other than `plan.overage_rate_pence`" named a line that was ALREADY the defect. A price edit also stamps unpriced `scheduled` walks of NO-PLAN clients (fill-only, never rewrite, plan clients skipped, id-ordered locks — each proven red first), because the materializer's 14-day queue would otherwise refuse to bill for a fortnight on exactly the adoption path H32 is about. **The card.** The review said the off-session machinery exists and it does — but a no-plan client has no saved card (only subscription checkout ever collected one), so the path dead-ends at "no payment method on file" for the very client it serves. create-checkout therefore grew three shapes behind pure param builders and ONE `sessions.create` call: subscription (unchanged), `topup` (payment mode, `setup_future_usage='off_session'` — one top-up makes a cash client fully chargeable), and `setup` (card on file under a mandate naming each priced service, refused `visit_price_missing` when nothing is priced — a card saved under no stated terms is H12's surprise waiting to happen). The old checkout test regexed index.ts for one literal object and could never have covered three; it now asserts the built objects per kind plus the single-call wiring. **The top-up.** `fn_apply_topup` is the `fn_apply_invoice_paid` shape keyed on the PaymentIntent, with `refunded`/`disputed` in both the function's check and `uq_topup_payment_per_intent` from day one (the 0023 lesson, avoided rather than re-fixed), and the PI id stamped into both `stripe_invoice_id` columns — that column is the grant↔money trace, `in_…`/`pi_…` cannot collide — so a dashboard refund claws the credits back through `fn_reverse_payment` with zero topup-specific code; the smoke block proves grant → refund-clawback → redelivery-grants-nothing end to end.
