The webhook resolves the client through the session's CUSTOMER scoped to the event's account, never the metadata (forgeable in any operator's dashboard), validates the credit count, and re-announces nothing on redelivery. Corrected in the review's favour: its headline harm — `payment_failed` dunning the pet owner per walk — was already closed by B6/H13, verified rather than rebuilt. Deliberately NOT built: a client-side portal top-up (operator-initiated matches every existing checkout; self-serve is product surface, not a fix), a `visit` payment type (the claim index, statuses and reconcile all key on `type='overage'`, and a parallel type re-opens the exact status-set drift M3/H13 were), a live visit-price fallback (old no-plan rows refused before and refuse still — honest beats retroactive), and the repositioning half (the owner's marketing call). Own errors worth recording: one sabotage silently failed to apply — a sed anchor missed a trailing semicolon, so the suite "passed" while testing nothing, the exact class ops(deploy-gating) recorded — caught by demanding a non-empty diff before believing any red; and the first draft of the subscription param builder wrote `plan_id: undefined` into metadata, which the object-level test caught before it shipped where the source regex never would have. Also: deno was absent from this container and `validate.sh` SKIPS gate 6 when it is — installed 2.9.1 to match CI's pin rather than trusting a gate that would have gone green by not running. Before the PR opened, a 29-agent adversarial review (five hostile dimensions, every finding independently verified) confirmed 23 findings against this very work, and the serious ones were mine: the top-up arm granted credits on `checkout.session.completed` WITHOUT checking `payment_status` — a delayed-notification method (ACH, one Standard-dashboard toggle away) completes `unpaid` and the async events fell to `default: ignored`, so a bounced debit left spendable credits, a `succeeded` row and no reversal path (the 0044 comment "an unpaid session never completes" recorded the exact false assumption); the top-up was offered to SUBSCRIBED clients whose next renewal sweeps the balance via `fn_apply_rollover` — selling credits the machinery is scheduled to destroy (now refused, 409 `client_subscribed`, UI card hidden; `fn_adjust_credits` stays the operator-judgment path, and invariant 4 forbids the per-lot tracking that would let purchased credit survive); the stamping trigger re-priced pre-0043 PLAN-client walks onto the cash price (no plan predicate — my own "the ORDER is what keeps a plan client off the cash price" comment defeated one branch down); a mandate-less top-up still saved the card `off_session`, the exact state the setup branch refuses (now: no mandate, no card save); Booking let a client tick "I understand" against NO figure for a charge the backfill could price afterwards (now blocked, `overageBookingGate`); a lease-expired claim retry replayed the same Stripe idempotency key with a RE-RESOLVED amount (idempotency_error until the key expires, then a charge at the drifted figure — the retry now replays the claim's recorded amount); a zero-amount top-up would have been unrefundable by construction (`fn_reverse_payment` raises before its escape hatches); and "pre-snapshot rows only" overstated the live-rate fallback in five places — it also covers post-0044 walks created with neither plan nor price whose client then subscribed, now stated as the deliberate fallback it is. Plus the runbooks' webhook lists gained the async events, ci.yml's "migrations directory and nothing else" comment stopped denying the permission its own suite runs on, Settings got functional updates and per-row price errors with revert, ClientDetail got its own error/busy state and a persistent link (window.open with noopener returns null even on success, so popup-blocked and opened are indistinguishable), and this entry's own review quote is corrected to the review's actual text. A Codex review on the PR then found three more, all real and all fixed with red-first proofs: a DEAD subscription's retained `plan_id` still priced new walks (the deletion webhook keeps it, so a cancelled pay-per-visit client's walks stamped a rate whose mandate died with the subscription — the plan now prices only while its subscription is live, in the INSERT snapshot, the backfill and the charge fallback alike); an unbounded credit count (a >2^31 value passes `Number.isInteger`, Checkout collects the money, and the granting RPC can never encode `p_credits int` — bounded at 10,000 on both sides of the handshake); and the six-service mandate cap hid figures that could still charge off-session ("and N more" was partial disclosure wearing a summary's clothes — the mandate now names every priced service or the card is not saved, with setup refusing outright). Codex's literal fix for the first ("restrict pay-per-visit to `none` + no plan") was declined with the reasoning on the thread: cancelled clients are exactly the churn-to-pay-per-visit cohort H32 exists for, and the actual harm was the pricing, not the eligibility. Left standing, stated: the topup notifications sit outside the idempotency boundary (a crash between grant and bell drops the announcement — the invoice.paid arm has the same shape) and the INSERT/backfill race is documented and self-healing rather than serialized away. 33 sabotages red-first across SQL, edge and frontend. 463 vitest, 278 deno, two new smoke blocks (76 DO blocks total — the "48" this log last recorded matches no way of counting the file I can find, so the absolute is stated in a unit that is checkable: `grep -cE '^do \$\$' smoke.sql`), 20 e2e.
- money(h31): Sanpo charges the operator — $49/month after a 14-day trial (owner's figures), and signup finally closable. The review's structural finding held: ClaimInvite ran on public `signUp`, the magic link CREATED accounts (`signInWithOtp` with no options — `shouldCreateUser` defaults true), and `operators_self_insert` let any authenticated user become an operator, so the GoTrue toggle could never be flipped without killing client invites. The claim's ACCOUNT-creation moved server-side: public `claim-signup` (verify_jwt=false) asks the new service-role-only `fn_invite_signup_check` — which answers exactly what `fn_claim_invite` would, smoke-pinned for parity by driving BOTH functions across every outcome — BEFORE `auth.admin.createUser`, because an account created first is public signup wearing an invite's clothes. Refusals log to `invite_claim_attempts` with a null `attempted_by` (its NOT NULL dropped: a pre-account probe is exactly what the H4 trail must show) and only refusals, or every legitimate signup double-logs; a passing check is followed by the authenticated claim, which still carries the notice version, so consent (H6) never moved. An already-registered address collapses into the same success rather than becoming an account-existence oracle — the frontend's sign-in is the rate-limited place that distinction already lives, and an unconfirmed admin-created account (createUser sends no email) gets `auth.resend` on the sign-in refusal. 0045's other half closed a hole the review never named: 0004's INSERT grant on operators was TABLE-LEVEL, so an operator could self-insert `trial_ends_at = 2099` or `platform_subscription_status = 'active'` at onboarding — and, pre-existing, forge `stripe_charges_enabled` (0024 revoked UPDATE only). The grant is now an explicit column list (the 0038 shape), proven live as `authenticated` rather than by asking `has_column_privilege` twice. The money: `operator-billing` mints subscription checkout/portal on the PLATFORM account — the one money path whose Stripe calls must carry NO `stripeAccount`, asserted over every recorded call as the overage guard's inverse — with the customer persisted before the link (connect-onboarding idiom), the price resolved by Stripe lookup key (`sanpo_operator_monthly_4900`, amount in the key so a price change mints a new key; no config table, Stripe is the platform book of record) and remaining trial passed only above Stripe's 48h floor. `platform-webhook` is stripe-webhook's mirror: own secret (`STRIPE_PLATFORM_WEBHOOK_SECRET`, in both sync-secrets lists), listens on "Your account", ignores any event WITH an `account`, shares the `stripe_events` claim ledger; `trialing` maps to active, `incomplete` decides nothing, an unpaid completed session binds ids but grants no status (H32's lesson pre-applied), and the past_due/cancelled bells are transition-gated so dunning redeliveries never ring twice (H13's). No payments rows for operator money — payments is tenant-scoped, and a parallel book here would re-open the status-set drift M3/H13 were. The gate: `operatorAccess` is pure — active→full, past_due→GRACE (a banner while Stripe duns, never a wall), trial-over with none/cancelled/paused→locked, and every unreadable input fails OPEN, because locking a paying operator out on bad data is the M39 stranding class with money attached. It sits in RequireRole AFTER role resolution, operator persona only (the same component guards portal routes — a pet owner is never locked out over their walker's bill), reads billing fetched IN the role query so it cannot see "no data" a failure would have surfaced as roleError, and renders BillingLocked in place (the LoadError precedent — no route, no redirect loop). `/signup` is deliberately the ONE public `signUp` left — that is what makes the toggle mean something (owner-action 1b: it now governs operator acquisition alone) — and `/pricing` states the price from the same constant the checkout charges, pinned across runtimes (app constant, edge constant, lookup key, 0045 trial default) by scripts/platform-price.test.ts. GoTrue's "Signups not allowed for otp" for unknown magic-link addresses is collapsed into the same check-your-email confirmation, the describeResetOutcome rule one mode over. The staging-smoke claim replay now exercises claim-signup itself (dead token refuses as not_found and creates NO account, live token creates one the paginated lookup must find; admin email-confirm stands in for the link CI cannot click) — verified by extracting the step and driving it against a stub in both failure directions before it ever runs against staging. Found on the way: the 0043 smoke block picked op 1's service with an unordered `limit 1`, and op 1 owns a weekend-surcharged service, so on a Sat/Sun run the block went red whenever the scan returned that row first — observed live on Sunday 2026-08-30, green on the next identical run; it uses a dedicated service now (the 0044 lesson, one block up). Fifteen sabotages red-first across the three layers: four SQL (grant reopened, allowed column revoked, ladder rung dropped — "check said claimed, claim said revoked" — and a pass-logging check, which first surfaced through the wrong assertion, so the targeted one now runs before the count), six edge (create-before-check, a stripeAccount on a platform call, the 48h floor dropped, Connect events processed, an un-gated bell, active-on-unpaid), five frontend (gate unscoped from the persona, fail-open inverted, past_due walled, shouldCreateUser dropped, sign-in-before-check). 491 vitest, 313 deno, 78 smoke DO blocks, 16 edge functions, migrations through 0045.
- money(h31-review): the pre-PR adversarial pass over the H31 diff itself — five hostile dimensions, each finding independently verified, with a wrinkle worth recording: the auth and DB reviewers were flagged twice by the serving model's safety layer for the "attack it" framing (legitimate defensive review, wrong phrasing), so those two dimensions re-ran with defensive wording, one on a different model. Sixteen confirmed findings, all fixed red-first. The BLOCKER was a resurrection: `subscription.deleted` kept `platform_subscription_id` (deliberately) but nothing made `cancelled` terminal, and Stripe delivers dunning-exhaustion's `invoice.payment_failed` and `deleted` in either order with distinct event ids the claim ledger cannot relate — deleted-first meant the late failure flipped the row back to past_due, which is GRACE: free access forever, with the honest resubscribe then refused as `already_subscribed`. Two structural rules now govern platform-webhook: cancelled is terminal for the id it died with (the kept id IS the tombstone the guard reads), and a NEW subscription may replace a null-or-dead binding — never a live one (the checkout arm rang up a silent $49/month duplicate by clobbering; it refuses and rings a duplicate-subscription bell instead). The whole class was invisible to the existing tests because every one fed canned returns to isolated events; the new sequence rules are pinned by a STATEFUL deps double. Also: the payment_failed arm read the pre-Basil `invoice.subscription` field — DEAD on any Dashboard-created endpoint, whose payload shape follows the account's API version, not the SDK pin — while stripe-webhook already carried the dual-shape read, so the helper moved to `_lib/stripe_shapes.ts` where the two endpoints cannot disagree; the dunning bell now rides the status TRANSITION in both racing arms (whichever wins rings once); operator-billing asks Stripe at mint time (a live subscription there refuses checkout even when the DB is behind — the webhook-window pay-twice path — and stale open sessions are expired so back-out-and-reclick cannot leave two completable sessions); and the 48h trial floor gained a margin, because exactly-48h is under the floor by the time the request lands and Stripe rejects the whole session for precisely the operators closest to needing it. Frontend: the gate re-renders on every auth event, so a trial ending MID-WALK swapped Walk Mode for the wall — GPS stopped silently and END WALK, the billing moment, became unreachable, converting the operator's lapse into the client's harm; operatorBare defers both the lock and the banner (a router link above a live walk is an unguarded exit, the fix(walk-durability) class). BillingLocked was a dead end wearing a wall's clothes: billing rides the role query and is fetched once per session, so a PAYING subscriber whose tab outlived their trial hit a wall whose Subscribe 409'd, whose Manage billing rendered only for cancelled, and whose check-again hid behind a payLink that the 409 never set — it now refreshes on arrival, offers check-again unconditionally, treats already_subscribed as the good news it is, and covers paused/billing-bound states via hasBilling. Settings stops promising "your trial days are kept" inside the window where the checkout drops them (the floor pinned cross-runtime). The nastiest UX find: an invited client's resent confirmation email landed on site_url, which routes a role-less user to the OPERATOR onboarding form, and completing it dead-ends the invite permanently — the resend now redirects to `inviteUrlFor(token)` with `/claim/*` allowlisted (config.toml + owner-actions), and Onboard warns the misdirected. 0045 was rewritten in place (unmerged, the 0036 precedent): a foreign_key_violation handler in the pre-flight (unlocked read racing a client DELETE now answers not_found — true by then — instead of 500ing a public endpoint), the trial-default comment corrected to the real mechanism (now() is STABLE — one evaluation, attmissingval, no rewrite; the outcome promised was right, the mechanism wasn't), and the refuse block checks all seven Onboard columns after the reviewer showed dropping `phone` passed every gate. Smoke gained the one refuse-vs-admit parity cell it couldn't see (NULL-email invites must admit any address — red first against the exact guard drift) and staging-smoke's `user_id_for` now distinguishes absent from lookup-failed, after the reviewer showed a 401 body read as absence passed the dead-token SECURITY assertion while checking nothing — the stub now proves that direction red too. Stated residuals, recorded not buried: claim-signup has no rate limit (the 0016 shape if ever needed), the signup toggle bars strangers but an invite-holder can still technically reach an operator trial, and two-cancellations-inside-the-redelivery-window can theoretically rebind a dead id. Eight sabotages red-first this round (four webhook rules, the mint-time guards, deferLock, the wall's mount-refresh and 409 recovery). 501 vitest, 324 deno, 78 smoke blocks.
- security(h31-auth): the safety-flagged auth dimension, re-run defensively on a different model — seven findings, all fixed, and the important negatives verified: the check/claim normalization parity holds end to end (both ladders lowercase both operands; Postgres `lower()` under C.UTF-8 is per-character and cannot disagree with Go's), all three GoTrue error-code matches are what auth-js 2.110.0 actually returns, and no path lets a password reach a log line. The fixes: `passwordMeetsPolicy` now enforces the FULL declared posture (length AND lower_upper_letters_digits — the mirror only carried the length, so invited clients could hold weaker passwords than every operator, on the account nearest the door codes; both forms now advertise both halves and the test fixtures stop encoding a password the declared policy rejects); GoTrue's own 4xx refusals (weak_password, address validation) surface as 400s with GoTrue's message instead of collapsing into an unactionable 500; the resend's error is no longer discarded (it is the ONLY thing that ever puts a confirmation in an invited client's inbox, and a rate-limit with the check-email screen showing meant waiting forever on an empty inbox); a signed-in client holding a LIVE foreign invite gets an honest dead-end instead of the silent /portal bounce that left the operator staring at an invite that never claims — with the 23505 race mapped to `AccountAlreadyBoundError` rather than "check your connection" for a permanent condition; the email is normalized ONCE and used on both sides of the create/sign-in handshake (the input sanitises ASCII whitespace but not NBSP — a paste from a rich-text invite email could create an account under one string and sign in with another); the createUser-sees-the-checked-email invariant gained its recorder assertion; and Signup's retry got SignIn's busy treatment, making its dead `disabled` prop load-bearing. Process note, the FIFTH bite from the same command family: the sabotage round used `git restore` for recovery while this round's fixes were still uncommitted, silently reverting two files to the pre-fix commit — caught because the next full run was green in a way that couldn't be trusted, re-applied, re-verified. The reds it produced were, by accident, the honest kind: failures against the true pre-fix code. 504 vitest, 324 deno.
- money(codex-77): the Codex review on PR #77 confirmed three findings against the H31 diff — two P1, one P2, all real, all fixed red-first before merge. **The webhook TOCTOU (P1)**: every platform-webhook rule was enforced by reading the row and deciding in memory, so a `subscription.deleted` landing in the gap between another arm's read and its write resurrected a cancelled row — `payment_failed`'s predicate excluded only its own target status and the generic `updated` write had none, which is the h31-review resurrection blocker rebuilt one level down, in the race the sequence tests could not see (every canned and stateful test ran events one at a time). The rules now ride IN the write: `OperatorWriteGuard` (`whileBoundTo` — the write belongs to this binding; `bindableTo` — the row may take this binding: null, dead-and-different, or same-and-live; `unlessStatusIn` — terminal + bell gates), with `guardAdmits` as the one executable specification the stateful double evaluates and the PostgREST or-filter translation is source-pinned against, ids validated before they enter the filter string. Fixing it surfaced two neighbours Codex did not name: a LATE `deleted` for a replaced subscription could kill a fresh rebind (its write was pinned to the operator, not to the binding it belonged to — `whileBoundTo` closes it), and `updated` carrying `canceled`/`incomplete_expired` wrote the terminal status silently, after which the real deleted arm's gated bell could never ring — it now gets the deleted arm's write and bell. Four race tests via an `afterFirstFind` seam in the stateful double, all red first. **The checkout mint race (P1)**: two concurrent Subscribe requests both passed the open-session sweep before either created — the sweep only ever protected against sequential re-clicks — minting two completable sessions, i.e. two $49/month subscriptions with a manual refund as the only exit; the fresh-customer path skipped every guard outright. `operators.checkout_mint_claimed_at` is now a per-operator lease claimed by ONE conditional UPDATE (the claimCustomerId idiom, with expiry) BEFORE the customer is created, so the first-checkout double-customer race sits inside it too; the loser 409s `checkout_in_progress` without touching Stripe, release runs on success and failure alike (the retry's own sweep handles a maybe-created session), and a crashed mint lapses after 2 minutes. Red first: the concurrency test observed two `sessions.create` against the shipped handler. **The unbound-invite mint (P2)**: the h31 spec had RECORDED as a residual that a live token whose client has no email admits every address — Codex correctly priced that as unlimited account minting from one leaked link. `fn_invite_signup_check` now RESERVES the first admitted address with one atomic `update … where email is null`, so a token is worth at most one account and the check/claim parity holds THROUGH the reservation by construction (the claim then enforces the reserved address like an operator-typed one); a lost race re-reads and re-decides as the ladder would, a mistyped first address is recoverable by the operator editing the roster email, and the refused second address logs its pre-account row. 0045 rewritten in place again (unmerged, third time), smoke's null-email cell inverted from "admits any address, both sides" to the reservation matrix — red first against the shipped function. 336 deno (12 new), 504 vitest, 78 smoke DO blocks, db-push-check over all 45.
- security(mfa-ui): the aal2 path gets its missing half — TOTP enrolment and step-up shipped, closing the gap the "what next" survey ranked as the top code item: `resolveAssurance` (H2) has refused password-only sessions on factor-holding accounts since it landed, owner-actions ranked "enrol a TOTP factor" #1, and no `supabase.auth.mfa.*` call existed anywhere in the tree — the posture the gate was built for was unreachable. Two surfaces, one module (`lib/mfa.ts`), with the decision logic pure and exported. Settings → Two-factor authentication runs enroll → scan → challengeAndVerify, which upgrades the CURRENT session to aal2 in place, so the vault works the moment the code is accepted; an abandoned enrolment (closed mid-scan) is cleaned up and re-QR'd on the next attempt, because GoTrue cannot re-show a secret and only VERIFIED factors carry weight — the sweep is pinned to never touch a verified one, since deleting it would turn two-factor OFF as a side effect of opening the setup sheet. ReauthSheet asks for the code exactly when the server would refuse `insufficient` (`resolveMfaGate` mirrors resolveAssurance, aal2 short-circuit included) and steps the session up BEFORE the vault call — the M2 shape one rung up: the doomed request is never made, no rate slot is spent, and magic-link or pre-enrolment sessions upgrade in place instead of being told to sign in again. In the set-password flow the step-up precedes the password write, so a factor-holding account's password is only ever changed by an aal2 session — the exact chain the gate exists to contain. The client check fails OPEN to password-only on any transport error (the server still refuses safely; failing closed would wall the vault off on a flaky connection — the accountHasPassword precedent), and turning the factor off requires a current code, which leaves the honest edge stated rather than papered over: a lost authenticator is removable only in the dashboard, written into owner-actions §9 (now a one-minute scan instead of "impossible until a UI ships") and auth-posture.md. config.toml's [auth.mfa.totp] flips to true for local parity (deployed projects already measured true; H2 — the file governs supabase start only). Deliberately NOT built: step-up at SignIn — the vault is the only aal2 consumer, so a sign-in gate would tax every session for one screen's benefit and still miss magic-link arrivals; the sheet covers all session origins at the moment of need. Seven sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs: the aal2 short-circuit dropped, unverified factors accepted (both directions), fetchMfaGate failing closed, settle-despite-refused-code, updateUser-before-step-up, removal-without-code, and the cleanup sweeping verified factors. 524 vitest (20 new).
- security(mfa-review): the pre-PR adversarial pass over the MFA commit — five defensive dimensions, 28 agents, every finding independently verified against the installed library: 22 confirmed, 1 refuted, all fixed red-first. The headline was mine and subtle: `resolveMfaGate` gated on `nextLevel`, which auth-js computes from the CACHED session's `user.factors` with NO network call — while `listFactors`, sitting in the very same `Promise.all`, is a fresh `GET /user`. A session minted before enrolment on another device therefore reported `nextLevel aal1` for up to a token lifetime, the sheet stayed silent, and the doomed password-only request was made exactly when the factor was newest — the precise behavior the commit's own comments claimed to eliminate, verified line-by-line in GoTrueClient.js by the reviewer AND the refuter. The fix inverts the trust: only `currentLevel === "aal2"` suppresses (it comes from the same JWT the vault will see), the FRESH factor list decides everything else, and the fail-open narrowed to the one input whose absence must fail open — an unreadable factor LIST is password-only (server backstops), an unreadable levels read still prompts, since an operator who provably holds the app pays at worst one extra field. Pinned by the stale-session cell `{aal1,aal1}+[VERIFIED] → gate`, red before the fix. The rest: auth-js returns API failures as ENVELOPES and rethrows only non-AuthErrors, so the enrolment sweep's unchecked `unenroll` silently skipped and let the enroll fail one step deeper with a misattributing message (each step now names its own failure, and the sweep is TOTP-filtered so another factor type mid-enrolment elsewhere is not this surface's to sweep), and a thrown `challengeAndVerify` would have stranded the form on a permanent busy spinner (wrapped, message returned); a factor deleted since the sheet opened re-challenged a dead id forever (factor-not-found now clears the stale gate with an honest sentence and the next press proceeds password-only — which the server now accepts, the factor being gone); the server's `second_factor_required` message told people to "sign in again and complete the second step", a flow this commit deliberately did not build (reworded to the real remedy); and the "turning it off requires a code" property is now HONESTLY scoped in spec 03 and auth-posture as client-side — GoTrue unenrolls for any aal2 session, and a token stolen at aal2 is already past the factor, which is why the timebox bounds that window, not this control. A11y: a failed "Turn on two-factor" click stored an error the off view had nowhere to render (one always-mounted FormError now serves the whole section — which also fixed the unavailable branch mounting its live region WITH its text), view transitions dropped keyboard focus to body at the bottom of a long page (heading takes it, the role=status notice announces), and the manual key gained `WebkitUserSelect` plus the `otpauth://` link that IS the same-device path — a phone cannot scan its own screen. Test-honesty closed five gaps the sabotage round had missed: the loading gate (a form shown before the MFA check answers is the doomed request back), gate-NOT-cleared-on-refusal, a PASSED step-up never demanded twice across a failed password write, the enrolment cancel path, and fetchVerifiedFactor's throw-on-error contract (unavailable and off must stay distinct). Deliberately recorded, not built: the non-TOTP factor mirror — the server counts verified factors type-blind, but phone/webauthn are off everywhere with no enrolment surface, so the state is unreachable and the filter carries a pin naming the config that keeps it so. Nine red-first proofs this round (the stale-nextLevel cell before its fix, then eight sabotages behind verified diffs). tsc caught the fixture missing the new `uri` field in four passing tests — again. Codex on the PR then found the finding my own round half-fixed: `removeTotpFactor` had the SAME unwrapped-throw hole the round fixed in `stepUpWithCode` four lines up — a non-AuthError thrown after the successful step-up escaped `MfaSection.remove()` past its `setBusy(false)`, stranding the removal form on a permanent spinner; wrapped identically, red first. A fix applied to one site and not its sibling is the recorded per-call-test disease, caught by the reviewer whose job is the diff. 539 vitest, 336 deno.
- fix(client-columns)+feat(client-edit): the operator's Clients tab has been returning 42501 since `0038`, and the fix for it is what unblocked the edit surface this PR was actually for. **The defect.** PostgREST does not narrow `select=*` to the columns the caller may read — it emits `SELECT clients.*` and Postgres refuses the whole statement if any column in that expansion is ungranted, so one withheld column does not hide a field, it takes every row of every query on the table with it. `0038` replaced the table-level SELECT grant on `clients` with a column list so `unsubscribe_token` could not reach the browser; `0043` §2 withheld three more. Six `api.ts` functions were still asking for `*`: `listClients`, `getClient`, `createClient`, `updateClient`, `getMyClient`, `listLowCreditClients` — the Clients tab, the client record, adding a client, the low-credit strip, and every screen of the client portal. Measured, not reasoned about: a real PostgREST 12.2.3 against the local database at migration head, as a real operator JWT with RLS live, answers `401 {"code":"42501"}` to the shipped `listClients()` and `200` with the operator's two clients to the explicit list; the PATCH that `updateClient` issues fails the same way and writes nothing. The break was bisected to `0038` by replaying the migrations one at a time (after `0038` the hidden set is `unsubscribe_token`; after `0043` it is four columns), and `.select()` with no argument is the same wildcard — postgrest-js 2.110.0, `src/PostgrestQueryBuilder.ts:935`, `const cleanedColumns = (columns ?? '*')`. **Why nothing caught it.** The rule was written down TWICE and connected to nothing: `api.ts` states it above `CRED_META` for `access_credentials` alone ("never select * … a wildcard select would be denied"), and `smoke.sql` states it in the comment above the withheld-set check — "`select("*")` starts failing with a bare 42501 from PostgREST" — a check that then exempts by name the four columns that trigger it. Both halves were true; nothing executable joined them. No gate could see it either: smoke runs through psql, which simulates the personas with `set local role` and never goes through PostgREST, and no e2e spec signs in against a backend. `0043`'s own header comment also states something that has not been true since `0038` — that `select("*")` was still RETURNING the private columns. **The fix** is frontend, because the grants are right and the client was asking for more than it may read: `CLIENT_COLUMNS` (one `as const` literal — a `+`-concatenation widens to `string`, which postgrest-js cannot parse at the type level, and every caller silently degrades to `GenericStringError`), and `ClientRecord = Omit<Clients, …>` so the grant is enforced by the compiler rather than by a comment. All four withheld columns had zero readers in `app/src`, which is what made the narrowing free. `app/scripts/column-grants.test.ts` replays the grant statements out of the migrations in order and fails on a wildcard against any column-restricted table, or on `CLIENT_COLUMNS` drifting from the grants in either direction; it parses STATEMENTS rather than lines, because `0038`'s grant spans several of them and a line-oriented parser reads it as no grant at all — which is the check passing by seeing nothing. **The feature.** Client records were create-only: `updateClient` shipped with zero importers, so a mistyped email could not be corrected and a client who moved house needed database surgery — while spec 04 already told the operator that a wrongly-reserved invite address "is recoverable the ordinary way: the operator edits the client's email", and `fn_unbind_invite` (0042) deliberately does NOT clear `email`, so releasing a wrongly-claimed account reissues a token still bound to the wrong person. Both recoveries were documented and unreachable. Now: *Edit details* on the header (name, email, phone) and per-property *Edit* on the Access tab. The part that needed care is that on an UNCLAIMED client `clients.email` is not contact detail — it is the last rung of the claim ladder in `fn_claim_invite` and `fn_invite_signup_check` alike, and the only ladder input an operator can change with a plain UPDATE rather than a definer function — so the form says which decision is being made: adding an address narrows the invite to it, changing one transfers it, CLEARING it re-opens the invite to anyone holding the link. Capitalisation raises nothing, because both ladders compare `lower(trim(...))` and a warning there would train the operator to ignore the one that matters. `status` and `notes` are deliberately absent though both sit in the same UPDATE grant: `status` is written by three invite-lifecycle functions, and `notes` has had no SELECT grant since `0043`, so a notes field could be written and never read back. The patch carries only what changed, so saving an untouched form does not rewrite the column the ladder reads. Editing is withheld from a purged client — `fn_purge_client` redacts the row but the UPDATE grant still covers those columns, so nothing in the database stops an edit re-personalising an erasure carried out on request; stated in spec 03 as the UI-only guard it is. The first version guarded only the header, which the Codex review on PR #79 correctly called a P1: the purge REDACTS the property and the credential and keeps both rows (retained walks reference them) and DELETES the pets, so *Edit property*, *Add property*, *Add secret* and *Add pet* each re-personalise the record exactly as editing the client would — a rule stated in a spec and false two tabs over. All five now read one `editable` decision, asserted per surface so that fixing one and not its siblings fails; that is the same "applied to one site and not its sibling" shape the MFA entry above records, found this time by the reviewer rather than by me. An adversarial pass over the same diff then found five more, all real and all fixed here. Three were the guard failing at its own job: `.select("*, pets(name)")` — the compound form this file already uses four times — passed an exact string match, so a wildcard is now parsed out of the argument with embeds dropped; a grant statement the parser could not read was silently SKIPPED rather than reported, so the drift check derived its expectation from a stale model; and `CLIENT_COLUMNS`' own comment named a gate file that does not exist. The second of those is worth recording twice, because the first fix for it could not fail either — the filter asked for `/ select /` with spaces on both sides, which skips every `grant select, insert, …`, i.e. exactly the multi-privilege statements most likely to defeat the parser. It took a sabotage to notice, and that sabotage is the test now. The other two were product: `emailEditEffect` ignored the invite LIFECYCLE, so an operator who had just withdrawn a forwarded link was told "anyone who has the invite link can claim this client's account" and to "withdraw the invite first" — contradicting the Withdrawn badge on the panel directly above, since the ladder refuses a revoked or expired invite at an earlier rung than email; there is a `dormant-invite` sentence now, and the state is PASSED IN rather than derived, because `inviteState` lives in `api.ts` and this module is claimed by the node project. And the purge guard stopped at the client row: a purged client still got "Send a new invite", which mints a live 14-day token on a tombstone whose `email` is NULL — the rung that admits ANY address, with the pre-flight then reserving the first that arrives, so one click made an erased record claimable again. `InvitePanel` returns null on `purged_at`; the database half is a real asymmetry left open and recorded, since `fn_unbind_invite` carries a `purged_at` guard and `fn_rotate_invite`/`fn_revoke_invite` do not (read, not assumed). Separately, a stray probe file left in `app/src/components/` by that review found a defect nothing else would have: the edit sheet is mounted for the life of the screen, so keyed on the row alone an operator who typed a wrong address and pressed Escape to abandon it reopened to find it still there, still showing the consequence note, one Save from being written — `PropertySheet` was already immune because its key falls to "closed". The note region also now always mounts, `:empty` and out of flow, for the reason `FormError` does: a live region that appears together with its text is announced far less reliably. Own process error in the fix: the sabotage round restored from a snapshot taken BEFORE the guards, silently reverting them — the sixth instance of this family in the log, and the correct discipline is to snapshot the FIXED state and restore to that. Own gap found by the sabotage round rather than by review: the effect ladder's tests passed with the claimed check moved BELOW the invite cases, because every claimed fixture had a non-null email — and `fn_claim_invite` writes `auth_user_id`, `status` and the notice columns and never touches `email`, so a client claimed through `/claim/:token` with no address is reachable and would have been told they were "binding an invite" on an account that already exists. Two cases added; the sabotage is red now. Recorded rather than built, both in spec 04: an email edit does not rotate `clients.unsubscribe_token`, so a stranger holding a mistyped email's one-click link would suppress the CORRECTED address (the column carries no UPDATE grant for any API role by design, so rotating it needs a definer function); and editing TO an already-suppressed address silently and terminally stops every future client-facing email. `address_line2` stays uncollected in both the add and edit property forms, so the two agree about what a property is — the create form has never collected one. An earlier draft of this entry and of the code comment justified that with "it is the one address column with no UPDATE grant", which is FALSE: `authenticated` may update it. The claim came from `information_schema.column_privileges`, which filters by currently-enabled role and is misleading read as superuser — I replaced that source with `has_column_privilege` when it hid `notes` too, and never went back to re-check the conclusion I had already drawn from it. The adversarial reviewer caught it; the symmetry reason was always the real one and stands alone. The task queue that had been carried in session working notes is now `docs/dev/backlog.md`, where it can be maintained in the open. Nine more findings from that same pass, in a second round: the guard still missed an EMBEDDED `clients(*)` (its `.select(` capture was a `[^)]*` regex that truncates at the first `)` — which is exactly where an embed opens, so a balanced scan replaced it) and never checked an explicit list for a WITHHELD column at all (`previewInvite` is the one `clients` read outside `CLIENT_COLUMNS`, and its `Pick<Clients, …>` is keyed off the un-narrowed type, so widening it would compile); `PutCredentialSheet` was the one sheet in the rewritten Access tab with no remount key, and it holds a DOOR CODE — two actions per card made moving between properties the ordinary flow this change created, so an interrupted operator opened the next property's Add secret onto the previous one's secret; a save dismissed mid-flight reported nothing either way; "Add secret" repeated per property with no distinguishing name beside a sibling that had just been given one; the tab-level error state the rewrite dropped made a failed post-save refresh leave a stale address on screen; and the note was not `aria-describedby`-linked from the field it describes. Two of this round's own sabotages passed first time and were fixes to the TESTS, not the code: the embed sabotage was swallowed by that same truncating capture, and the dismissal test rendered with a no-op `onClose`, so the sheet never closed whatever the guard did. A third round from the same pass caught six more, four of them the class this file keeps recording: "`0038`'s grant spans five lines" is six (stated three times, never counted — the number is simply gone now, since the argument never needed it); the sabotage enumeration below names twelve while claiming twenty-eight, so it says "among them"; "the handoff document" named a provenance no reader can check; and `.client-relationship-header__actions` claimed the ghost button "inherits the header's already-escalated roles", which is NOT the mechanism — `.btn--ghost` paints its own opaque fill from `--sanpo-color-button-secondary-*` declared on `:root`, and substitution happens where a property is declared, so an ancestor re-pointing text roles never reaches it. The two real ones: a second action in the property row left the address — the thing an operator reads at a door — in about a hundred pixels at 375px, so the identity column gets `min-width: 0` (a flex item will not shrink below its content without it) and the actions wrap beneath rather than compress it; and `.form-note` painted the Kaki attention rule on reassurance as well as on the three notes that change who may claim the account, which teaches the operator the rule means nothing in particular. The rendered width at 375px is NOT measured — no e2e spec signs in, so this one rests on the CSS rather than on a screenshot, which is weaker than this repo's usual bar and is said rather than implied. 597 vitest (58 new), 29 sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs, among them — six wildcard call sites, the two drift directions, a renamed constant the regex must not silently miss, a line-oriented parser, blank-clears-to-empty-string, a patch that sends every field, a case-sensitive comparison, the reordered claim check, `isEditable` ignoring the tombstone, the screen not consulting it, and the property sheet losing its remount key.
- security(0046): the two migration-gated items PR #79 recorded as residuals rather than smuggling into a frontend-only change. **The first is a real hole and it was proven before it was fixed.** `fn_purge_client` (H5) leaves a tombstone safe AT REST — `email` nulled, a fresh `invite_token` nobody holds, and `invite_revoked_at` set. `fn_rotate_invite` then sets `invite_revoked_at = null` and stamps a fresh 14-day expiry, and because the purge also NULLs `auth_user_id` the tombstone looks exactly like an unclaimed client to its `auth_user_id is null` predicate. So one press of "Send a new invite" undid the purge's own revocation. Measured on this schema as the owning operator, before the fix: `after purge: revoked_at set = t, email is null = t` / `after rotate: revoked_at = NULL, expires in future = t, purged_at still set = t` / `fn_invite_signup_check(<new token>, 'stranger@example.test') -> claimed`. That last line is the finding — a NULL email is precisely the ladder rung that admits ANY address, so the erased record became claimable by a stranger, and the signup pre-flight would then RESERVE that stranger's address onto the tombstone, writing personal data back into a record erased on request. Two routes to undoing H5 in one call. `fn_unbind_invite` has carried `purged_at is null` since 0042; this is its sibling acquiring the same predicate — the "applied to one site and not its sibling" shape recorded twice already in this log. The body was built from `pg_get_functiondef` of the LIVE function rather than from the 0039 text (the 0040 lesson: a `create or replace` written from an older body silently deletes what a later migration added) and verified identical apart from the one predicate. 0046 also RE-REVOKES any purged client already carrying a live invite and reports the count rather than repairing silently, because a non-zero number there is a record somebody erased and a token somebody could still have used. **`fn_revoke_invite` is deliberately NOT given the predicate**, and the asymmetry is the point rather than an oversight: rotate MINTS a token, revoke KILLS one, so on a tombstone revoke is a no-op in effect, moves toward safety, and is the only in-product remedy for a row that already carried a live invite — refusing it would strand exactly the rows the repair exists for. A guard that blocks the safe direction is worse than no guard, and the sabotage that adds it is one of the five. **The second is the unsubscribe link outliving the address it was sent to.** `clients.unsubscribe_token` is a bearer credential and `fn_unsubscribe_by_token` suppresses whatever address the row holds AT CLICK TIME, so the M29 recovery had a sting in it: the stranger who received mail at a typo'd address still held a live one-click link, and clicking it suppressed the CORRECTED one — silently and terminally, since a suppression is recorded `skipped` and the nightly drain never retries it. A BEFORE UPDATE trigger rather than a definer RPC, and that choice is the whole design: the column carries no UPDATE grant for any API role, so a caller CANNOT do this for itself, and every writer of `clients.email` — the operator's edit form, the client persona's own contact edit, the 0045 signup reservation, and whatever writes it next — gets the rotation without knowing the rule exists. Verified rather than assumed, on a throwaway table, that a BEFORE trigger may set a column the CALLER has no UPDATE grant on; that is what makes it reachable from `authenticated` at all. Compared NORMALISED (`lower(trim(...))`, matching both ladders) so a capitalisation fix keeps its link, and `is distinct from` so setting and clearing both count. Not SECURITY DEFINER and not revoked from the API roles, matching `fn_default_walk_origin`, its nearest analogue: a trigger function cannot be usefully invoked directly (Postgres refuses), so a revoke would be ceremony rather than a control — stated as a decision so it does not read as an omission. Five sabotages red-first, each injected after smoke's own `begin;` so the whole run rolls back and no reset is needed between them: the shipped rotate body, the trigger dropped, a trigger rotating on EVERY update, a case-SENSITIVE comparison, and revoke wrongly guarded too. The last of those first went red as a raw `ERROR` rather than a named sentence, because the call was bare — the 0038 lesson about an assertion that aborts the suite with the function's own message reading as a broken suite rather than a broken rule — so it is wrapped now. The frontend needed no change, which is what choosing a trigger bought; `InvitePanel`'s comment claiming the database "does not refuse this" is corrected, and its guard kept as defence in depth, because a panel offering an action the server will refuse is a worse surface than one that does not offer it. My own adversarial pass over the diff then found two things in it, both the shape this file keeps recording. The deploy-time assertion matched the RAW `pg_get_functiondef`, so a future edit could satisfy it with a `--` comment while deleting the predicate — demonstrated on this schema, a function whose only mention of `purged_at is null` was in a comment matched a naive `position()`; comments are stripped before the match now, and the assertion says outright that it is a structural backstop while the behavioural proof lives in smoke. And the trigger was `before update OF email`, which is evaluated against the columns the STATEMENT names rather than what the row image ends up holding — measured: with a second BEFORE trigger assigning `new.email`, an `OF email` trigger does not fire at all when the statement never mentioned the column. No trigger assigns `new.email` today, so that was defence in depth rather than a live fix, but the whole argument for using a trigger is that the next writer should not have to know the rule exists, and an `OF` clause quietly reintroduces exactly that requirement one level down. The clause is gone; the function's own comparison costs one comparison per update. Two false alarms on the way to that last check, worth recording because both looked like findings: a `prosrc ~ 'new\.email'` sweep returned 1 against leftover probe functions from my own boundary test that survived `drop table ... cascade`, and then returned 1 again on a fresh database against `fn_rotate_unsubscribe_token` itself, which READS `new.email` in its comparison and does not assign it. The conclusion survived both. The Codex re-review — requested because the first review ran on a commit my own pass had since changed — then found BOTH of those fixes incomplete, and both were right. Stripping only `--` left `/* purged_at is null */` satisfying the assertion, the same defect one layer down in the fix for it; both comment forms are stripped now. And dropping the `OF` clause narrowed the trigger hole without closing it: BEFORE ROW triggers fire in NAME order and a BEFORE trigger sees only the row image as it stands when it runs, so a trigger sorting AFTER this one that assigns `new.email` changes the address after the comparison and the token never rotates — measured, with a `z_late` trigger, token rotated = f. That cannot be closed by construction without an AFTER trigger issuing a second UPDATE on every address change forever, to defend against a trigger nobody has written, so the precondition is EXECUTABLE instead: smoke fails if any BEFORE UPDATE trigger on `clients` other than the rotation one assigns `new.email`. It lives in smoke rather than in 0046's own assertions because a deploy-time check runs once and would never see the trigger a later migration adds. Honest about being a `prosrc` heuristic over the assignment forms PL/pgSQL offers rather than a proof, and proven in both directions — it fires on a trigger that assigns and stays silent on one that only reads. Worth recording that the two rounds found different things: my pass caught what the first review missed, the second review caught what my pass missed, and the second review only happened because I asked for one on the head that had changed. 597 vitest, 336 deno, 81 smoke DO blocks (3 new), migrations through 0046.
- docs(session-notes): the operational half of a cold start moved out of an uploaded handoff document and into the repository, where a session that is never handed a file can still find it. `CLAUDE.md` carries the invariants and the working agreement and `docs/spec/` carries behaviour, but neither said how to get a container running — and the one file that did was an upload. Measured rather than assumed before writing: `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_PATH` appeared NOWHERE in the tree, so the e2e gate was unrunnable here for anyone without that upload, while the `git restore` rule and the two-vitest-projects trap existed only as narrative inside status-log entries — recorded as things that had bitten somebody, not as instructions anybody would find before repeating them. That is the same shape as this session's headline defect: a rule written down and connected to nothing. `docs/dev/session-notes.md` states them as instructions, cross-linked from the Workflow section above, from `docs/dev/backlog.md`, and from `docs/dev/local-stack.md` (which is the database half and now says so). New facts this session paid for and the old handoff did not have: deno.land is blocked by the egress proxy and `npm install -g deno@2.9.1` carries the same binary; a real PostgREST run against the local database is the only way to see the class of defect `smoke.sql` structurally cannot, since it goes through psql and no e2e spec signs in (verified — the four specs CI runs authenticate nothing); a sabotage injected after smoke's own `begin;` at line 11 rolls back with the suite, so no reset is needed between proofs; background agents can mutate the working tree mid-validate, and `git status` settles it in one command where theorising does not; and the snapshot discipline is to copy the FIXED state and restore to that, which is the correction the sixth `git restore` bite produced. Two corrections to the old handoff rather than transcription: the committer is NOT worth setting, because GitHub's rebase merge rewrites it to the merging account and nothing survives onto main (checked against `git log --format='%cn'`, which reads `AAnderson1817` for every commit including the five merged today); and Codex reviews the commit a PR opened with and does not re-review on push, so a diff changed afterwards needs an explicit `@codex review` — which on PR #80 found two defects in fixes that had themselves come out of an adversarial pass. Every file:line and count in the new file was checked against the tree, including the six recorded instances of the `git restore` family, `playwright.config.mjs:26`, smoke's `begin;` line, the CI deno pin, and the two gates that exist only in CI.
- perf(today-plate): M17, and the review's own prescription was half impossible. One `875x1798` WebP went to every device, so a DPR-1 laptop painting a `438px` field downloaded 875 pixels of width to show 438 — and 438 is not a hypothetical, it is the field at `1440x900`, the desktop viewport spec 07 names for testing (`900 x 875/1798 = 438.0`). The plate now ships as four candidates behind one `srcset`, and the widths are MEASURED field widths rather than a `1x/2x` ladder: 438 (that laptop), 640 (`--page-max`, so no device ever needs more), 750 (`375` CSS px at DPR 2), 875 (the master). Measured across a device matrix, a DPR-1 laptop transfers 90 KiB instead of 437 and a DPR-2 phone at 375 CSS px 218 instead of 437. **The other half — "ship a 2x master" — cannot be done from inside this repository**, and establishing that was most of the value: `875x1798` is every pixel that exists anywhere in the tree OR its history, and `docs/reference/sanpo-today-locked-composition.png` — the one file that looks like a master, being the same dimensions — is not one: it is the composition mockup, artwork PLUS UI, measured 18.57 dB PSNR against the plate, with its README saying it must not be embedded. The real lossless PNG master does exist, at `d313486` where this file already says it is, and the variants are still encoded from the SHIPPED WebP: measured against the lossless master downscaled to the same size, the second-generation encode scores 35.15/35.46/36.01 dB at 438/640/750w against 35.73/36.63/37.36 from the PNG — 0.6-1.35 dB for 2-3% more bytes, invisible on a watercolour, and pinning to the shipped plate is the stronger guarantee that a variant is the artwork the app actually serves. So DPR-3 phones still upscale 10-47%, exactly as before; that is owner action #13 and it is new artwork, not code. Said plainly in spec 07 rather than left for a reader to infer from a fix that did not fix it. **The trap is the service worker.** `SHELL_ASSET_EXTENSIONS` precaches every `.webp` in `dist/assets`, so four candidates would have put ~923 KiB of artwork into every install to serve the ~180 KiB one device picks — re-inflating the exact budget `perf(today-field)` reclaimed. Precaching none is worse, and that is measured rather than assumed: an `<img srcset>` does NOT fall back to another candidate when the picked one fails, it renders NOTHING (`naturalWidth` 0, verified in Chromium). So one plate is precached — the install list is byte-for-byte what it was, 5 files and 1207 KiB — and the worker substitutes it for any candidate it cannot produce, which is seamless because every candidate is the same composition at the same ratio and the layout is CSS-driven (serving the master for a `438w` URL renders at ratio 2.0548 against the plate's 2.0549). Matching is by a build-STAMPED stem: an exact URL list cannot answer for a page still running the previous build, which asks for the previous hashed variant and is the request most likely to 404, and a stem written into `sw.js` would silently match nothing the day the asset is renamed. A non-ok response falls back too, since for this asset a 404 and an offline failure look identical on screen. `sizes` restates `--page-max` because it resolves before layout; `clamp()`, `calc()`, `min()` and `dvh` are all legal there and Chromium honours them — verified against the real page, where every pick matched the width actually rendered. Two guards make that duplication safe: a test reading both expressions out of `components.css`, and an e2e spec checking the browser's pick against the width it painted across 7 viewports x 3 device pixel ratios. Fidelity was measured, not asserted: at the display widths each candidate serves, a variant differs from the master by 33.2-38.9 dB PSNR, the same neighbourhood as the 38.8 dB the original PNG-to-WebP re-encode shipped at; and `today-contrast.spec.ts`, which samples rendered pixels, moved every ratio UPWARD by 0.02-0.09 with none crossing a floor, because a high-quality downscale averages out the artwork's darkest speckles. Variants are generated by a committed script that REFUSES unless the master hashes to the approved value, so a variant can only ever be the approved artwork made smaller; its bytes are the encoder's, so regenerating on a different Chromium is a deliberate act with a hash update, stated rather than hidden. Fourteen sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs: five worker rules (no fallback offline, no fallback on 404, fallback before the cache, fallback unscoped, stem match dropped), six drift guards (`sizes` as a naive `100vw`, a CSS cap moved without it, a candidate dropped from `srcset`, a variant losing its hash-guard entry, the media clauses reordered so 768 swallows every desktop, the generator and the app disagreeing), and the e2e spec against `sizes="100vw"`, which fails exactly the four DPR-1 cases the change exists for. **Stated rather than papered over**: the worker fallback is a backstop, not a fix for an observed failure — the mechanism is proven by the real fetch handler under test, but several attempts to reproduce a blank plate end-to-end in a real browser all rendered anyway, because Chromium's own HTTP cache answered first in every arrangement tried. Own errors worth recording, all four the same shape — a conclusion drawn from something I had not actually read: I told six investigating agents the reference PNG was the lossless master, which measurement then refuted, and a fidelity path built on it would have replaced the plate with the mockup; I also told them `d313486` was not in this clone, on the strength of one `git cat-file` run BEFORE the fetch, which is how the real PNG master nearly went unexamined; the first `sizes` guard used `split(", ")`, which cuts straight through `clamp(420px, calc(...), 640px)` and reported a correct attribute as malformed; the first candidate-width parser matched numeric literals only and silently read three candidates where four ship, missing the master because its entry names a constant; and the first worker sabotage replaced only the non-ok branch, so two "distinct" proofs were the same one until the catch branch was sabotaged separately. `tsc` again caught what vitest did not, twice — a config import needing an explicit extension, and a test whose module could not be typechecked in the project it sat in. And the SEVENTH instance of the restore family, a new variant of it: the discipline was followed — snapshot the fixed file, sabotage, verify the diff, restore — but the whole sequence was one `cd app && SNAP=… && cp …` chain whose `cd` failed because the shell was already in `app`, so every later link including the restore was skipped in silence, leaving the sabotage applied under a `validate.sh` that was running at the time. Issuing a restore is not the same as having restored; `docs/dev/session-notes.md` now says to assert the file is back before believing any green run. Codex on the PR then found the one defect neither my own adversarial pass nor the gates could: `sourceVariantCount` filtered `src/assets/illustrations` on `-<N>w.webp` ALONE while `todayPlateFamily` counted only files carrying the plate's basename, so a second responsive illustration in that shared directory — the exact thing a future M17-shaped change adds — would make the two disagree by one and fail EVERY production build while every Today candidate was present and correct. The worst shape available for a gate: not one that fails to fire, but one that fires on a healthy tree, which is how a gate gets deleted by whoever is trying to ship something unrelated. Scoped to the stem, red-first against the flagged filter (`expected 4 to be 3`) — and Codex's SECOND round then caught that I had fixed one site and not its sibling, the shape this log keeps recording: `today-plate.test.ts` scanned the same shared directory with its own identical unscoped regex, so the suite still went red on a healthy tree. There is one exported implementation now, so there is no sibling to forget; reproduced Codex's exact experiment before and after (a planted `some-other-scene-320w.webp` turns all three candidate-set assertions red on the old scan and none on the new one). 622 vitest (25 new), 336 deno, 43 e2e in 5 files, 28 hash-verified assets, 16 validate gates with none skipped.
- db(0047): the photo integrity record, and two false premises corrected on the way in. `walk_photos` recorded WHERE an object lives and nothing about what it is, so for any surviving object there was no way to ask whether it is still the one that was uploaded. 0047 adds nullable `sha256` (lower-case hex, CHECK-pinned to `^[0-9a-f]{64}$` because every consumer compares against `sha256sum` output) and `byte_size` (CHECK > 0 — a zero-byte photo is a failed upload wearing a successful one's clothes), written by the browser at upload and never backfilled. **The item's own justification was wrong twice, and finding that was most of the work.** `disaster-recovery.md` §5 said verification was "impossible even in principle"; it is not, and never was — Supabase Storage records a server-side `size` and a content-derived `eTag` per object in `storage.objects.metadata`, ONE JOIN from `storage_path`, visible in the installed `storage-js` type contract, and nothing in this repository has ever read it. What that copy cannot do is answer this question, because it is REGENERATED: replace an object and its metadata is rewritten to describe the new bytes, so a row written once and never updated is the only operand that still describes the past. That is the whole value here and it is far narrower than "impossible" implied — the one scenario where it changes an outcome is verifying a copy restored from a storage mirror, which is DR open item 4 and does not exist. Second and more dangerous: both §5 and review B3 sold this as evidence for a chargeback dispute. **It cannot be that.** `authenticated` holds DELETE alongside INSERT on `walk_photos` (0004:328), so anyone wanting a different photo on a report deletes the row, uploads new bytes and inserts a fresh row whose digest matches perfectly — a match proves nothing about authenticity and a mismatch is not evidence of misconduct. Shipping under that framing was the actual harm on offer, because mismatches are near zero by construction (a fresh `randomUUID` path per upload, `upsert: false` measured on the wire, no read-path re-encoding), so the first one anyone sees looks like a signal when the likeliest cause is a bug of ours — a false accusation against a named person about a named house. The digest is therefore documented, in the migration and in spec 01 and in the script's own header, as detecting STORAGE DIVERGENCE and explicitly not tampering. **Write-once is enforced by the grant rather than asserted**: there is no UPDATE grant on this table for any API role, so neither column can be rewritten by the browser that wrote it, and the migration REFUSES if one ever appears. The hash is taken inside `uploadWalkPhoto`, in the same expression that uploads the bytes, and that placement is the fix for the defect this design exists to prevent: `addPhotos` holds `file` and `compressed` on adjacent lines, `compressImage` re-encodes to JPEG, and `sha256(file)` reads perfectly naturally while matching nothing on every photo forever — with no reader it would ship green and poison the corpus. A caller that cannot see the bytes cannot make that mistake, so the function returns `{path, sha256, byteSize}` rather than a path. The availability guard is `typeof crypto?.subtle?.digest === "function"` and deliberately NOT `isSecureContext`, which is `undefined` in BOTH vitest projects — the wrong guard makes five tests return null and the hashing path is then tested nowhere while looking covered; `crypto.randomUUID` is gated by the same secure-context rule and `uploadWalkPhoto` already calls it, so hashing adds no platform requirement the upload did not already have. NULL is a third state meaning "not recorded", never "failed": rows predating 0047, rows `complete-walk` won the race for (it replays paths from the completion request and has no bytes), and any runtime that cannot hash. That makes `ignoreDuplicates: true` load-bearing for INTEGRITY rather than row counts — measured, a DO NOTHING replay preserves the digest and a DO UPDATE replay erases it, with no grant to put it back. **`scripts/verify-photo-integrity.sh` ships in the same commit and is the reason this is not a written-and-never-read column** — the shape this repository has now shipped six times under other names. It reports three states and never two, exits non-zero ONLY on a genuine mismatch (a check that goes red on a healthy tree is one that gets deleted — the M17 lesson, one PR old), and prints COVERAGE on every run including a clean one, because a digest path that silently writes nothing otherwise reads exactly like success. Driven against a stub with a match, a mismatch and a missing object: 75% coverage, 1 verified, 1 diverged, 1 gone, exit 1. Ten sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs: four frontend (the digest taken over a different blob than the one uploaded, the `isSecureContext` guard, upper-case hex the CHECK would refuse, a hashing failure that throws and loses the row), three SQL in smoke (a completion replay with DO UPDATE, an UPDATE grant appearing, the hex CHECK dropped), and three in the migration's own assertions (the grant narrowed to a column list, an UPDATE grant, plus the control showing both stay silent on the real schema). Own error worth recording: the first constraint probe invented a `walks` fixture with a `duration_minutes` column that does not exist, so the checks aborted before testing anything — the fixture was wrong, not the migration, and hanging them off a seeded walk was the fix. `tsc` again caught what vitest did not: the new columns typed as `never` until `gen-types.py` regenerated `types.ts`. Deliberately NOT done: read-path verification in the product (the report card shows the photo, not its digest — the DR rehearsal is the consumer), and no backfill of any kind. Recorded as owner action #14: whether the `storage` schema is in Supabase's own backup is unverifiable from here and decides whether 0047 is a second opinion or the only surviving record. Codex on the PR then found TWO more of the same class, both in the verification script itself — which is the part of this worth recording, because that script's entire reason for existing is that a check reporting success having verified nothing is this repository's most-repeated defect, and I shipped two of those inside it. The credential header was bearer-only where every other hosted call here sends `apikey` alongside it (staging-smoke.yml:99, job-health.yml:75); a gateway answers 401, the loop counted that as GONE for every row, and the run exited 0 — reproduced exactly against a stub demanding both headers: `GONE p/good.jpg (HTTP 401)`, `verified: 0`, exit 0, against `verified: 1` after. 401/403 and an unreachable host are now fatal and distinguished from a missing object, since every row would fail identically and a page of false GONEs is worse than one honest refusal. And the work-list query ran inside `< <(...)`, where a failure is NOT propagated — measured directly: the loop sees zero lines, prints 0/0/0 and exits 0. It reads into a file with `ON_ERROR_STOP=1` now, checks the status, and cross-checks the row count against the coverage figure taken moments earlier so a silently short read cannot pass as a clean run either. One own error worth recording alongside: the commit message claimed 624 vitest, a number I recalled rather than read — the session notes say counts must be read, and this is what that rule is for. 631 vitest (9 new), 82 smoke DO blocks, db-push-check replays all 47 migrations as a non-superuser, 16 validate gates (10b red only on the documented false-red of an uncommitted `types.ts`, confirmed byte-identical after regenerating against the reset database). A THIRD review round then caught that my fix for the second one was an enumeration rather than an inversion: I listed the failures I had thought of (401, 403, unreachable) and let the catch-all swallow everything else, so a 429 or a 502 during a rehearsal would still have reported every object GONE — a storage-divergence finding somebody would act on — and could exit 0. Only 404, the API's actual not-found, means missing now; every other status is a failure to verify and is fatal. Proved across the matrix before and after: 429/503/302 went from `GONE` + exit 0 to `FAIL` + exit 2, while 404 still reports GONE and 200 still verifies. Enumerating the ways a check can be wrong is itself how a check ends up reporting success having verified nothing, which is the defect this whole script exists to prevent — three rounds on one file, all the same class, and each of my first two fixes closed an instance rather than the class.
- security(0048): a rate limit for `claim-signup`, the one genuinely public endpoint that creates accounts — and the honest justification is not the one the backlog gave. This is a **regression, not a gap**: before H31 the same flow ran on `supabase.auth.signUp`, which GoTrue rate-limits (`sign_in_sign_ups = 30` per 5 minutes per IP); H31 moved account creation behind `verify_jwt = false` and `auth.admin.createUser` with the service role, and either way that lands the control is gone — if GoTrue exempts `/admin/users` there is no limit at all, and if it does not, the only IP GoTrue can see is the edge function's egress address, so one bucket is shared by every claimant in the world and one attacker exhausts it for everybody. Which is true cannot be established from here (no GoTrue, supabase.com blocked by the egress proxy) and is owner action 15, because it sizes the harm rather than deciding whether the fix is right. **Two of spec 04's reasons for accepting the absence were false.** It discounted the address oracle as "the same fact `fn_preview_invite` gives any authenticated holder, minus the sign-in" — read from `pg_proc`, `fn_preview_invite(p_token uuid)` takes NO email and returns none, so it cannot confirm or deny an address at any rate, and unlike this endpoint it needs an account at all. And it deferred the fix "if either is ever observed in the trail" while nothing reads `invite_claim_attempts`: no `api.ts` function, no view, no screen — its only consumers are `smoke.sql` and the migrations, so the trigger condition was unsatisfiable, a permanent deferral wearing a trigger's clothes. Also worse than stated: `fn_claim_invite` and `fn_revoke_invite` BOTH retain `invite_token`, so the log write handle is not "a REAL token" but every invite ever issued, claimed and revoked ones included, permanently. **The key is the CLIENT, resolved from the token, not the caller** — the backlog said "keyed for an unauthenticated caller", i.e. the IP, and that is wrong three ways: the attack requires a live token, which is a stable server-known identifier IP rotation cannot change; an IP key locks out carrier-grade NAT, an office, a household, none of whom are the attacker; and 0016, the precedent, keys on the SUBJECT being protected rather than on the caller. Keying on the client also closes a growth vector the token key would have opened, since the token is caller-supplied and random uuids are free. Resolving the token leaks nothing, because token existence is already public — `not_found` is the answer on request one, by design — so a 429 on the token-matches-a-client path tells an attacker nothing the first 409 did not, and a token matching no client is therefore **allowed and records nothing**: refusing it would make the limiter the token-existence oracle this ordering exists to avoid. Placement is 0035's rule — after the shape checks, before the check — so an exhausted budget computes NO outcome and writes no `invite_claim_attempts` row, and every request reaching the limiter counts INCLUDING one that will succeed, because a limiter counting only refusals leaves the correct address unlimited while every wrong one is refused and the attacker reads the answer straight off the status at no budget cost: the bug wearing the fix's clothes. `rate_limited` is deliberately not one of the 0039 outcome codes — `claimSignup` remaps those onto `InviteClaimError` and `ClaimInvite` renders that as a TERMINAL dead-end, and waiting fixes a 429. The RPC answer is read fail-CLOSED, extracted as `rpcAllowsAttempt` purely so the rule is reachable from a test at all (importing `index.ts` binds a port). No retention sweep, and that is a property rather than an omission: the function prunes the key's expired rows before counting and inserts only under the limit, so at most 10 rows per client exist at any instant — asserted by crossing a window and re-counting, which is also the only assertion a missing prune fails, since the count filter alone keeps the LIMIT correct while the table grows forever. The ledger is infrastructure, not a tenant table (no `operator_id`, invariant 7 does not apply — the `job_runs` precedent), with RLS enabled AND forced, no policies, no API grants: 0032 exists because `vault_rate_limit_attempts`, the table this one is modelled on, shipped with a REVOKE and no RLS at all. The body's service-role guard is tested by GRANTing execute inside the rolled-back suite, which is the only way to reach the body as a non-service caller and therefore the only way that defence-in-depth is anything but decoration. Thirteen red-first proofs behind verified non-empty diffs: five edge (limiter after the check, above the shape checks, counting only refusals, the code colliding with an outcome, failing open), seven SQL (unknown token refused, no null-client branch so the endpoint 500s, an off-by-one ceiling, the insert before the ceiling check, no prune, the body guard dropped, the budget shared across clients), and one frontend (every failure rendering as a dead end). Own error worth recording, and the sabotage found it rather than review: my new "shape refusals never reach the limiter" test used a bare `assertRejects`, which passes for ANY rejection — including the plain `Error` thrown by the untouchable deps when the sabotage moved the limiter in front of them — so it went green against the exact defect it exists to catch. It asserts `HttpError` and status 400 now, and the TypeScript analogue of smoke's "assert on a named sentence" rule is written into `session-notes.md`. Also corrected: `config.toml` called `unsubscribe` "the ONLY public endpoint in this project", untrue since H31 added this one. The Codex review on PR #84 then found two P2s against this work, both real, both reproduced against a live database before being fixed and 0048 rewritten in place (unmerged — the 0036/0045 precedent). **The first was my own stated mitigation being false**: this file's header listed "the operator can reissue the invite" as one of three mitigations for a token holder burning the legitimate claimant's budget, and rotation mints a token on the SAME client row while the budget is keyed on the client — measured, the operator reissues and the brand-new token is refused with all ten rows intact, so the documented remedy did not work for the rest of the hour. Fixed with an AFTER UPDATE trigger on `clients` rather than an edit to `fn_rotate_invite`, because `invite_token` has five writers across 0039-0046 (`fn_rotate_invite`, `fn_unbind_invite`, and three successive replacements of `fn_purge_client`) and fixing the one the reviewer named while leaving its siblings is the shape this log keeps recording. AFTER with a WHEN clause rather than `before update of invite_token`, which is 0046's own Codex finding applied a migration later: a BEFORE trigger sees only the row image as it stands when it runs and an `OF` clause is evaluated against the columns the STATEMENT names, while an AFTER trigger's WHEN clause reads the final image. The trigger also closes a privacy gap neither the review nor I had named — the rows carry an `ip`, the purge rotates the token, and a purged client receives no further attempts, so the prune (which only runs for the key being attempted) would never reach them: they were personal data surviving an erasure request indefinitely, and spec 03's purge inventory now says so. **The second**: the token lookup is unlocked, so a client deleted between it and the insert leaves the FK to raise, and an unhandled raise on a PUBLIC endpoint is a 500 for a request the check would have answered `not_found` — reproduced at exactly that point with a BEFORE INSERT trigger, SQLSTATE 23503 propagating out. It catches `foreign_key_violation` and ALLOWS, so the caller reaches the honest answer; `fn_invite_signup_check` already handled the same permitted race the same way (0045), so this is that precedent applied to the function in front of it. Deliberately NOT closed by locking the client row, which would let an unauthenticated caller hold a lock on `clients` — trading a rare 500 for a denial-of-service primitive on the table the whole product reads. Five more sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and a verified restore: no trigger, a trigger that clears nothing, a reset scoped to the wrong client, no FK handler, and a vanished client refused rather than allowed through. A SECOND Codex round then found the deeper version of the first one, and it was right: the token is resolved BEFORE the advisory lock and never re-validated, so a request bearing the old token can queue behind the lock while the operator rotates or purges and then insert against the client that still exists — undoing the reset for the reissued invite, and on the purge path re-creating an `ip` row for a client erased on request.
