Reproduced as `concurrency.sh` case 6, using case 5's apparatus for the same reason (the interleave is inside one RPC, so the function is copied from `pg_get_functiondef` with a barrier injected at the point the finding names): the reissue cleared the burned budget and the stale attempt put a row straight back. Fixed by re-reading the client under the lock and holding it with `for no key update` until the insert; a stale token is ALLOWED and records nothing, exactly as an unknown one is. Case 6b then demonstrates the mechanism rather than asserting it — that the row lock genuinely blocks a rotation, and that advisory→clients→attempts against the rotation's clients→attempts cannot cycle (the 0037 rule, and this is the first time this function takes a lock on `clients` at all). That reverses the position the previous round stated, and the reversal is recorded rather than made quietly: "an unauthenticated caller holding a lock on `clients`" was overstated, because the lock lives for the remainder of one autocommit statement the caller cannot prolong, and callers on the same token already serialize on the advisory lock. Three own errors worth recording, all in the VERIFICATION rather than the fix. Restoring the sabotaged migration FILE does not restore the database, so a "passing" run was still executing the sabotaged body — and the query I used to refute that theory was defeated by my own prose, because `for no key update` appears three times in the live definition and once in the code, the mention-versus-use defect 0046's Codex round had already found in a migration assertion. Then the first sabotage of the lock mode (`for key share`) did not go red, which turned out to be the finding: `clients_invite_token_key` makes `invite_token` UNIQUE, so rotating it is a KEY update taking `for update`, which conflicts with every mode — the comment claiming the rotation "takes the conflicting `for no key update` itself" was simply false, and is corrected to the reason that survives a future migration dropping that index. All three are now in `session-notes.md`. 632 vitest, 342 deno, 83 smoke DO blocks, 7 concurrency cases, 67 definer functions, migrations through 0048.
- feat(push): M27, the backlog's largest item — Web Push end to end, and a defect found on the way in that mattered more than the feature. **The crypto came first because it decided the shape.** RFC 8291 payload encryption that "did not throw" is indistinguishable from encryption producing a body no browser can open: the push service returns 201 either way, so the failure is invisible from the sending side. Rather than an npm package this session cannot exercise under the edge runtime — every primitive is already used in `_lib/crypto.ts` and `_lib/stripe.ts` — it is written against `crypto.subtle` and pinned BYTE-FOR-BYTE to `http_ece`, the reference implementation `web-push` itself delegates to, with the salt and ephemeral key fixed so the output is deterministic. The RFC is blocked by the egress proxy and a test written from the same reading of the spec as the code shares its misunderstandings, so the oracle had to be somebody else's implementation; `scripts/gen-webpush-vector.mjs` regenerates the vector so its provenance is reproducible. Eleven sabotages red-first across the two halves: public keys swapped in `key_info`, salt and auth-secret roles swapped, padding delimiter `0x01`, record size little-endian, HKDF info missing its trailing NUL, the CEK derived from the ECDH secret, `aud` as the full endpoint, `exp` in milliseconds, a signature covering only the payload, `k=` advertising a key that did not sign, and the subject check dropped. The raw r‖s assertion earns its place: WebCrypto emits the pair JWS wants while Node's default is DER, so an implementation ported from a Node example fails at the push service with nothing on our side to look at. **0049** is a tenant table plus the `push_*` delivery quartet mirroring H17's email one, and registration is a definer RPC rather than an INSERT grant for one reason: a push endpoint identifies a BROWSER, not a person. On a shared phone two people present the same endpoint and whichever row survives decides whose walk reports land on that lock screen — the qc(1-4) leak in a new shape, on a channel that puts a client's name on a lock screen — so the upsert REASSIGNS ownership. Erasure is closed by construction rather than left to be found: the purge REDACTS the client row so the FK cascade never fires, which is exactly the gap Codex found in 0048 for `invite_signup_attempts`, fixed here before it could become the same defect. Eight sabotages red-first, and one of them — the shared-device reassignment, the one that matters most — first exited non-zero with NO `FAIL:` line because the sabotage duplicated a column assignment and the function was never replaced: it looked red while proving nothing. `session-notes.md` now says a non-zero exit is not proof, your own sentence has to appear. The send path is a push arm ALONGSIDE H17's email arm in the same function, because a second function needs a second Database Webhook — a dashboard step only the owner can perform. Push runs first (the email arm throws on a missing `RESEND_API_KEY`, so push after it would be silently skipped) and never fails the request; one aggregate outcome per notification, because the question is "was I told", not "did device 3 of 4 accept it". Eight more sabotages. The worker's rules are that every push ends in a VISIBLE notification — Chrome permits a few silent ones, then shows "This site has been updated in the background" itself and revokes the permission from repeat offenders — and that a payload path is not a trusted destination, `safePath` mirroring M41's `internal-path.ts` at a sink that is a real navigation; six more sabotages. The opt-in has five states rather than a boolean because "off" has four causes and three cannot be fixed by a switch, and sign-out removes the device BEFORE clearing the session, the opposite of the M8 cleanups beside it, because the removal RPC is scoped to the caller. **The defect found on the way**: `send-notification` selected `email_delivery_status` — the name of the ENUM TYPE 0029 created, not the column, which is `email_status` — so real PostgREST 12.2.3 answers 42703 and every lookup 500'd. No email has been sent since `security(0032)`. Nothing could see it: smoke goes through psql and never builds a select list, the edge tests inject mocked deps, and the fixtures asserted the invented shape rather than a captured one — the `check-auth-posture.sh` defect again. The nightly drain is green because the backlog is empty on staging, not because delivery works. `deploy-production.yml` has 0 runs (checked, not recalled), so no real client has missed anything. Being the SECOND instance of the class after `fix(client-columns)`, the response is a gate: `select-columns.test.ts` replays the migrations into a schema model and checks every column named in every `.select()` across app AND edge functions, resolving `.select(CONST)` — load-bearing, since without it the gate would have skipped the very query it exists for and passed for the wrong reason. It found two bugs in itself on its first run (column names may contain digits, so `p256dh` was dropped from the model; and a non-literal argument read as a column list). Proven red-first in both directions. VAPID keys are owner action §17 and everything degrades to `skipped` without them. The Codex review then found FIVE, all real, all fixed red-first. Two P1: the sign-out cleanup called the removal RPC BEFORE `sub.unsubscribe()`, so a network failure skipped the local half entirely — and since that path swallows errors by design, a shared device kept receiving the PREVIOUS account's notifications, the exact hazard 0049's reassigning upsert exists for, reached from the other side; the order is reversed now, and the reasoning it shipped with was wrong in both directions, since a stale server row SELF-HEALS on the 410 the send path already handles while a browser left subscribed does not. And the nightly drain never retried a failed push: `drainBacklog` ignored the push deps and `fn_notification_backlog` filtered on `email_status` alone, so a notification whose email succeeded and whose push failed was never selected again — "retryable" written down and connected to nothing, which is the shape this repository keeps finding. Three P2: the `RESEND_API_KEY` check ran as a precondition for the whole request, so on a deployment with push configured and email not, execution never reached the push call at all and even OPERATOR-ONLY rows, which skip email by definition, could not be pushed — H17's loud 500 now fires inside `sendEmail`, where email is actually attempted, so it still fails loudly for anything that needed the provider and only for those; a failed server registration left the browser subscribed, so the UI reported `on` against no row and, because `on` offers only the OFF action, stayed falsely enabled until somebody toggled twice (the missing-keys branch four lines up already rolled back, which is the inconsistency that made it wrong); and a database error in `getSubscriptions`/`recordPush` rejected at the call site and stopped the email from ever going out, so the optional channel gated the one that is not. Nine more sabotages red-first. Widening the backlog also broke three existing smoke assertions, correctly: a row whose email is sent but whose push was never attempted IS still owed something, so those fixtures now settle both channels and say why — the alternative was an assertion that no longer meant what it claimed. A SECOND Codex round then found three more, all real. **P1**: the sign-out fix covered only the GRACEFUL path — a session ending without it (a failed refresh token, cleared auth storage, a tab killed mid-session) leaves the browser subscribed while the row still belongs to the previous account, so the next person's UI reports `on`, offers only the OFF action, and nothing ever re-registers: the device keeps receiving the PREVIOUS account's notifications and never receives the current one's. `reclaimPushDevice()` re-registers on every resolved session, which needs no new machinery because the upsert-reassign the shared-device case required is exactly the primitive that repairs this; idempotent and best-effort, since the function refuses a caller who is not yet an operator or a client. **P2, and it contradicted my own reasoning**: the reassignment was UNCONDITIONAL, while `fn_remove_push_subscription` is scoped to its caller precisely because an endpoint is not secret enough to authorize acting on it — so any authenticated caller who learned an endpoint could claim that row, stopping the victim's notifications and delivering their own onto the victim's device. It now requires the endpoint's existing key material, which is the right discriminator because the genuine shared-device case presents endpoint and keys TOGETHER (`pushManager.subscribe()` returns the existing subscription object); the residual — a browser recycling an endpoint under fresh keys would also be refused — is written into spec 01 rather than guessed at, and such a row is already undeliverable anyway. **P2**: `data.json()` SUCCEEDS for the literal `null`, so the parse guard did not fire and the first field read threw, rejecting `waitUntil` and showing nothing — the silent push the handler exists to prevent, arrived at through the one input that parses cleanly and is not an object. Three more sabotages red-first. A THIRD round found three further P2s, two fixed and one argued down. **Fixed**: the widened backlog excluded a push past the ceiling while `fn_expire_notification_backlog` touched only the email fields, so such a row simply VANISHED from the drain — no give-up note, no abandoned count, `push_status` left `failed` forever with nothing that would look at it again, which is the asymmetry of having mirrored H17's four states and its predicate but not its expiry. And nothing bounded the device count per recipient: the endpoint check accepts any https url, so an authenticated caller could register unbounded fabricated endpoints, and every later notification POSTs to each of them SEQUENTIALLY before the email arm runs — unbounded outbound work on a shared runtime that also delays the sender's own email. A quota of ten evicts the oldest, and `id <> v_id` is the load-bearing clause rather than belt-and-braces: two registrations in one transaction share a `last_seen_at` because `now()` is transaction-constant (the 0028 lesson), so without it the row just registered is as likely to be evicted as any other — which is exactly what the first version did, caught by its own smoke assertion. **Argued down and recorded rather than built**: send-once is a read-then-act, so two concurrent invocations can both pass the guard and both send. Real, but it is PRE-EXISTING in the email arm since M1 — `if (row.email_status === "sent")` has the identical shape — so fixing push alone would imply email is safe when it is not, and a correct fix is the 0013 claim-ledger-with-lease, which is machinery this does not earn: the worker sets `tag` to the notification type, so a duplicate push COLLAPSES on the lock screen rather than stacking, while a duplicate email does not. It is a backlog item covering both channels. One own error worth recording: the first sabotage for the expiry fix replaced `push_status = 'failed'` with `push_status = push_status`, which still wrote the note and still counted rows — SMOKE PASS, proving nothing. The rule from 0049's earlier round held: a sabotage that does not go red is a broken proof, not a passing one. A FOURTH round found three more, and two of them were defects in the THIRD round's own fixes — the point at which the convergence question is worth asking rather than assuming. Judged on merit and all three were real and cheap, so they were fixed rather than stood down on. **P1**: the device quota is a count-then-delete, and under READ COMMITTED each concurrent transaction sees only committed rows plus its own insert — so with nine devices present, any number of simultaneous calls each see ten, each delete nothing, and all commit. The quota bounded nothing against exactly the caller it exists to bound, who can trivially issue concurrent requests; an advisory lock on the recipient now serializes the read-modify-write, the 0016/0048 instrument. Proven as `concurrency.sh` case 7 — two real backends, because a single transaction cannot observe its own isolation level — which reported 11 rows without the lock and 10 with it. **P2**: the expiry summed the per-channel counts, so a row abandoned on BOTH was reported twice and inflated the nightly figure; the comment beside it described the intent while the arithmetic contradicted it. **P2**: the payload tagged by notification TYPE, so two distinct `walk_complete` events collapsed into one tray entry and the second silently replaced the first — losing a notification is worse than a fuller tray. Tagging by the row id still collapses a duplicate delivery of the SAME row, which is what a tag is for here, so this strengthens the send-once deferral rather than weakening it. Three own errors, all found by running rather than reading: `returning id into <uuid[]>` takes ONE row and raises on more, and the migration applied cleanly anyway because PL/pgSQL resolves a body at EXECUTION — the `fn_book_walk` phantom-column lesson, in my own code this time; a bare `wait` in the concurrency harness also waits for session A's FIFO psql, which does not exit until the end of the file, so case 7 hung until killed (case 5 collects explicit PIDs for exactly this reason); and the first sabotage of the quota eviction was too weak to prove anything. A FIFTH round found four more — and unlike the fourth, all four were in ORIGINAL code rather than in a previous round's fix, so the reviewer was still finding new ground. **P1**: the push `fetch` had no deadline, and devices are awaited SEQUENTIALLY ahead of the email arm — so an endpoint that accepts the connection and then stalls holds the invocation until the runtime kills it, costing the recipient their email and every later device. That is the channel isolation this function claims, defeated by a socket rather than an exception, and registered endpoints are attacker-influenced because the shape check accepts any https url. Ten-second `AbortSignal.timeout` per request. **P2**: `dropSubscription` awaited a supabase-js delete without reading `error` — failures arrive in the RESOLVED result, not by rejecting — so a failed delete counted as "gone" anyway and `deliverPush` could record the terminal `skipped` while the dead endpoint stayed in the table for every future notification to POST again. **P2**: the header declares `rs = 4096` while a single record was emitted regardless of size, so a long title or body (both unconstrained text, and operator notifications embed a client name) produced framing contradicting its own header. Clamped at the caller — a truncated sentence on a lock screen beats a notification that does not arrive — with the crypto layer refusing oversize input as a backstop rather than mis-framing it. **P2**: on an upgrade from a pre-M27 worker the ACTIVE worker has no `push` handler, and `getRegistration()` returns it regardless, so subscribing produced deliveries nothing displays; a `stale-worker` state now reports that ahead of `on`, because a device already subscribed under a stale worker is the case that matters — saying `on` there claims notifications work while none can appear. Four sabotages, and the fourth PASSED on its first run: the `enablePush` guard had no test, which is an untested rule rather than a proven one, so the test was written and the sabotage re-run red. 672 vitest, 364 deno, 84 smoke DO blocks, 8 concurrency cases, 70 definer functions, migrations through 0049.
- security(push-ssrf): the sixth Codex round on PR #85, and its P1 is the most serious finding of the PR. **Registration accepted any https url and `send-notification` then POSTed to it**, so every authenticated caller held a server-side request forgery primitive against whatever the edge runtime can reach — with an oracle attached, because `notifications.push_last_error` carried 300 characters of the response body and `0004` grants `select` on that table to `authenticated`. Register an endpoint, trigger a notification addressed to yourself, read the answer. 0049 shipped a device QUOTA instead and said in its own comment that a stricter host allowlist would be brittle across providers; that priced the cost of having one and not the cost of not having one, since a quota bounds how MUCH outbound work a caller can cause and says nothing whatever about WHERE it goes. Both are kept now — they answer different questions — and the comment is corrected rather than left to mislead. The allowlist is four services, each **read rather than recalled** and cited in spec 01: `fcm.googleapis.com`, `updates.push.services.mozilla.com`, `web.push.apple.com`, `*.notify.windows.com`, plus the regional siblings. Samsung Internet is the known unknown and nothing was added on a guess — its Android push hosts are documented, whether its WEB endpoints use them or FCM is not, and inventing a hostname is the class of error this file keeps recording. Enforced TWICE, and that is not redundancy: `fn_is_push_service_endpoint` refuses at registration so the person toggling notifications on gets a sentence naming their push service instead of a switch that reads "on" and never delivers, and `isPushServiceEndpoint` refuses at SEND, which is the check that actually stops the `fetch` — it covers a row predating the rule and any future write path that forgets. One list in two runtimes is the `payment_status` drift this repository has already paid for, so `app/scripts/push-service-hosts.test.ts` parses the SQL array literals and compares them, and FAILS rather than returning nothing when it cannot find them (a parser that answers "no hosts" reports drift as agreement — `column-grants.test.ts` had to be fixed for exactly that). The leading dot on each suffix is load-bearing and is asserted on its own: without it `notify.windows.com` also admits `evilnotify.windows.com`, which anyone can register. Two more layers. `redirect: "manual"`, because the allowlist decides which host this function contacts and a followed redirect hands that decision to the response one hop later, at a host nothing checked — measured in Deno rather than assumed, where it returns the real 302 (`type: "basic"`, `ok: false`) and makes no second request, so a 3xx arrives as an ordinary non-2xx and is recorded retryable. And the oracle closed structurally rather than by filtering: `sendPush` returns the STATUS and nothing else, and the body is read and logged in the same expression, so it never becomes a value anything downstream can forward. What a failure records is now ours — `the push service answered 502` — with the body on one server-side line carrying the subscription id and the endpoint's HOST, never the endpoint, whose path segment is the device's bearer credential. Splitting `push_deps.ts` out of `index.ts` is what made any of that testable: importing `index.ts` executes `serveFunction` and binds a port, so every push test to date injected a pure `sendPush` and NOTHING had ever exercised the request options — the `overage_deps.ts` blind spot exactly, and both defects lived precisely there. The redirect assertion runs over EVERY recorded call rather than the one call site, so a request added later without the option fails on its own. **The P2 was the stale-worker guard I had shipped one round earlier being neither necessary nor sufficient.** It asked `registration.waiting != null`, and during an upgrade from the pre-M27 worker the new one spends its whole install in `installing` — `waiting` is null while the ACTIVE worker, the one a delivery reaches, has no `push` handler at all; meanwhile a deploy that changes nothing about push leaves a worker waiting while the active one works perfectly, which that check called broken. The page ASKS now: `PUSH_CAPABLE?` on a `MessagePort`, and silence is a NO — a worker from before M27 receives the message, matches none of its own cases and returns without replying, so the only way to hear yes is from a worker running the current `sw.js`. Both `readPushEnvironment` and `enablePush` ask, because the first decides what the screen SAYS about a device already subscribed and the second whether to subscribe at all; a guard on one leaves the switch reading `on` under a worker that can display nothing. Both waits are bounded, since `serviceWorker.ready` never rejects and an unanswered port never settles, so a worker that never activates yields `stale-worker` rather than a spinner where a switch belongs. Twelve sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and verified restores — six SQL (predicate always true, suffixes without their dots, a userinfo-blind host parse, a case-sensitive compare, registration not consulting the predicate at all, and the predicate neutered) and six code (no endpoint guard in `deliverPush`, redirects followed, the body returned, the upstream exception text recorded, the pre-fix `waiting` rule, and a timeout that answers yes) — plus three targeted probes showing each near-miss rule is individually load-bearing rather than incidentally covered by the aggregate. Two own errors worth recording: the first `pg_get_functiondef`-derived sabotage was injected without a terminating semicolon, so the run died on a syntax error at line 260 and read as red while proving nothing — the trap this file recorded one PR ago, caught only by checking WHICH sentence came back; and the first userinfo sabotage dropped the suffix list as well, so it went red on "a real push service endpoint was refused" instead of the rule under test, which is red for the wrong reason. `tsc` again caught what vitest did not, and `PushSection.test.tsx`'s fixture went red on its own for the right reason. **Then the adversarial re-read of my own diff found the thing the review had not**: a rule written twice can disagree, and these two did. Measured rather than reasoned about — `URL` normalises an uppercase scheme and an explicit `:443` away and the POSIX regex did not, so `HTTPS://fcm.googleapis.com/…` and `https://fcm.googleapis.com:443/…` were ACCEPTED by the sender and REFUSED at registration. Neither was a hole, because registration is the stricter side and no row could carry one; but a security control whose two implementations differ is one nobody can reason about, and "one list, one rule" was the entire claim the two halves made together. The SQL now lowercases the whole endpoint before extracting the authority (the scheme is case-insensitive too and that regex was not) and strips a trailing `:443` and only `:443`, since an explicit 8080 would open every internal service reachable on an allowlisted name. The fix for the CLASS is `scripts/check-push-endpoint-parity.sh`: one case list, both implementations, the ANSWERS compared — where `push-service-hosts.test.ts` compares the two LISTS, which is what drifts when somebody adds a provider, this catches what drifts when somebody edits either parser. It is its own script because no test runner here has a database AND deno, so the CI database job gains `setup-deno`, and `ci.yml` / `SKILL.md` / `validate.sh` moved in lockstep. Proven in all four directions it can fail: the real pre-fix SQL body (named both divergences), a userinfo-blind rewrite of the TS side, a case whose expectation drifted away from both implementations, and an empty case list — which exits 2 saying so, because a parser that sees nothing reports agreement (the `column-grants.test.ts` lesson). **Codex round seven then found the one thing both my pass and the previous six had missed**, and it is in the round-five design rather than in any round's fix — so the rounds were still finding new ground, which is the question worth asking by seven. `reclaimPushDevice` repairs a subscription that survived an ungraceful session end, but only at the NEXT sign-in, and push delivery is entirely independent of the page's auth state: between a failed refresh token (or cleared storage, or a killed tab) and whenever somebody next signs in, the push service keeps delivering and the worker keeps DISPLAYING the previous account's notifications on a device that is signed out and possibly already in somebody else's hands. Client names on a stranger's lock screen is the hazard 0049's whole shared-device design exists for, reached from the one direction the reclaim structurally cannot cover. `forgetPushDeviceOnSignedOut` now unsubscribes the moment a resolution yields no session — local only, and that is not a compromise: `fn_remove_push_subscription` is scoped to its caller and there is none, so the row self-heals on the 404/410 the unsubscribe guarantees, which is the path `deliverPush` already implements. The cost is stated rather than hidden: a session ending for any reason now costs this device its registration, which is already true of every deliberate sign-out and is the safe direction. Both repairs are `void`-ed fire-and-forget calls that nothing downstream observes — the "written down and connected to nothing" shape this file keeps recording — so `auth-context-push.test.tsx` renders the provider and asserts each fires on its own transition and NOT on the other's. Three more sabotages red-first: the call deleted, an unsubscribe that does nothing, and a cleanup that throws into the auth transition. **And then reading the CI job log rather than its green tick found a vacuous pass in my own work.** The allowlist refuses anything but a real push service, and `concurrency.sh` case 7 registers ten devices at `https://push.example/race-N` — so all ten ERRORED, nothing was inserted, and the case still printed `ok (got 9)`, because its detector was `total <= 10` and the nine seed rows satisfy that on their own. Green locally and green in CI, twice, proving nothing about the quota, the advisory lock or the race. The postgres SERVICE CONTAINER log at the very end of the job carried ten `is not a push service` lines the job itself never surfaced; the fixtures are real FCM endpoints now, and the case gained the precondition it never had — all ten registrations must have LANDED, checked first and labelled as a precondition the way cases 1, 5 and 6 already do. The general rule, now in `session-notes.md`: an assertion with a floor is vacuous without a precondition, and tightening a validation rule silently invalidates fixtures in every suite whose detector can be satisfied by doing nothing. `push_delivery_test.ts` went red on the same change and was fixed within the minute; this one could not, which is the whole difference. **Codex round eight then found a purge race in the registration RPC, and it was real** — reproduced as `concurrency.sh` case 8 BEFORE the fix, where a device row survived an erasure request. `fn_register_push_subscription` resolved the caller with `my_client_id()` and then read `clients.operator_id` unlocked and with no `purged_at` predicate, so a registration that began before an erasure inserted its row after `fn_purge_client` had tombstoned the client and this file's own trigger had deleted every device it knew about: an endpoint identifying a browser, outliving the request H5 exists to honour. Nothing sequential can reach it — the purge NULLs `auth_user_id`, so afterwards `my_client_id()` returns null and the client branch is never entered — which is why only an interleave finds it and why the barrier apparatus was needed at all. The lookup is now `for no key update` with `purged_at is null`, and **both halves are load-bearing, which the sabotages established rather than assumed**: dropping the predicate leaves case 8 red (the purge commits first, the registration re-reads a tombstone it does not check), dropping the LOCK leaves case 8 green and case 8b red (the purge is still in flight, the read sees a live row, and a device survives) — two different interleaves, one fix each. The advisory lock also moved BEFORE the clients read and is now keyed on the client alone rather than the operator:client pair: the pair cannot be computed until `operator_id` has been read, which would force clients-then-advisory here against 0048's advisory-then-clients, and two functions taking the same two locks in opposite orders is the 0037 cycle — so the key is derived exactly as 0048's is. Case 8b demonstrates the lock rather than asserting it (the erasure genuinely blocks, neither side aborts, no deadlock) on the 6b precedent. One own error, caught by the sabotage: 8b's barrier was anchored on `purged_at is null`, so removing that predicate made the case ABORT instead of failing — the exact trap 6b's own comment warns about, one case over; it anchors on the assignment now. **Round nine: two P2s, both real, and one of them wrong about WHY.** First, `skipped` was documented as TERMINAL in both delivery arms and neither guard enforced it — both tested `status === "sent"` alone, so the widened backlog (which selects a row when EITHER channel is owed) brought back a notification whose push was skipped for want of a device and whose email had failed, and pushed it retroactively once the recipient registered one: "your walk is complete" about a walk two days ago, which is the harm H17's backfill decision named arriving by another route. The reviewer named the push arm; the EMAIL arm had the identical hole one function over, so `isSettled` states the rule once — only `pending` and `failed` are retryable, the complement of `fn_notification_backlog`'s own predicate — and both call it. Second, `db_cleanup` never deleted `push_subscriptions`, and the reviewer's mechanism (the `on delete restrict` FK refusing the operator delete) is FALSE: `session_replication_role = replica` two lines above disables FK triggers, measured on this schema, so the delete succeeds and silently ORPHANS the rows. That is worse than a refusal — `endpoint` is UNIQUE, so the next run's seed insert dies on `uq_push_subscriptions_endpoint`, a message saying nothing about the previous run, and the suite cannot start; the `ops(smoke-identity)` failure exactly. Reproduced end to end before fixing: cleanup exit 0, one survivor, then the collision. Cleanup now deletes them and drops the case-8 barrier function, and the run ASSERTS its own namespace is empty before starting, so a cleanup failure says so where it happens. Three sabotages red-first, and the third found a defect in my own comment: removing the push delete surfaced `on: command not found` on stderr, because I had written backticks in a SQL comment inside a double-quoted shell string, where they are command substitution — bash was running the words and splicing the empty result into the SQL. Harmless by luck. `ops(deploy-retry)` recorded that trap twice in one string; this is its third instance, it is now in `session-notes.md`, and a passing run hides it because the stderr only shows when something else goes wrong. **Round ten: two more P2s, both real, both in code the earlier rounds had already touched.** The endpoint-takeover fix from round three checked `p256dh` and nothing else — which is the ECDH PUBLIC key, semantically not a secret — while the update replaced `auth` as well, so anyone holding an endpoint and its public half could take the row AND overwrite the secret one, silencing the victim. Checking the public half of a keypair and calling it proof is checking the wrong half; both halves are required now, and the legitimate case is unaffected because `pushManager.subscribe()` hands back endpoint, p256dh and auth together. Worth recording that the existing smoke block PASSED against the fix, because it presented a wrong p256dh — the new case presents the RIGHT one with the wrong auth, which is the finding, and it is red against the pre-fix predicate. Second: `dropSubscription` threw on a database error (round five's own fix), which aborted the device fanout mid-loop — the recipient's remaining healthy devices were never tried, no aggregate outcome was recorded at all, and a device that had already accepted the push got it AGAIN when the still-pending row came back through the drain. A blip on one dead row is not a reason to drop a live notification, so the drop ANSWERS instead of throwing: the failure goes to the log (round five's actual requirement — it must not be swallowed) and a row that did not go is NOT counted `gone`, so the aggregate stays retryable rather than resolving to the terminal `skipped` over an endpoint still in the table. Both directions tested, since "always retry" would satisfy the first half and lose the second. **Round eleven: two P2s, and the first is a regression I introduced in round five with a comment asserting the opposite.** Moving the `RESEND_API_KEY` check into `sendEmail` (to stop it blocking push) put it INSIDE the try that records any throw as a retryable delivery failure — so H17's deliberately loud 500 became `resend unreachable` on the row, a 502 to the webhook and a 200 from the drain: a deploy that forgot the secret reporting success, which is the exact defect H17 exists to prevent, rebuilt by its own fix. The comment in `index.ts` claimed it "still fails loudly for anything that genuinely needed the provider", which is worse than the bug. It is `deps.assertEmailConfigured()` now, called after every TERMINAL skip (an operator-only row or a client with no address is better recorded than 500'd — none of them changes when the key appears) and OUTSIDE the try, which is the `fix(edge-errors)` lesson restated. `drainBacklog` gains a try around the email arm so a throw cannot abort the run and take the remaining rows' PUSH with it — the rule its own comment already stated for the push arm — and stays loud by the surviving backlog instead. Second, the two push repairs were both fire-and-forget and could run CONCURRENTLY; they are opposites and both begin by reading `pushManager.getSubscription()`, so either completion order loses the new account's subscription. `createSerialRunner` gives them one-at-a-time plus latest-wins. Two own errors, both caught by running rather than reading: the first overlap test PASSED against a runner with no serialisation at all, because rendering twice does not produce two transitions (the provider subscribes once and a rerender re-runs nothing) — it drives the captured `onAuthStateChange` listener now; and the second asserted a property the design does not have, since `applyRole` is async so a sign-out's repair always starts before a following sign-in's is queued. That is why the rules live in `serial-repair.test.ts` and the provider test pins only the wiring: a rule that cannot be exercised where it is asserted is a rule with nothing behind it. **Round twelve found the same defect on the PUSH side, one round after the email one — and again my comment claimed the opposite.** Everything before the `fetch` is ours: the missing VAPID keys, and a payload that will not encrypt. Both THREW, and `deliverPush`'s catch flattened them to `status: 0`, recorded as "the request to the push service did not complete" — false, since no request was made — while `sendPush`'s own logging ran only AFTER the fetch, so the fault was written down nowhere at all. A deployment whose keys were removed while devices existed reported an ordinary transient failure forever, and the catch's comment said "the error itself is logged by the implementation". `PushAttempt.blocked` carries the class now (`not_configured` / `payload` / `transport`), each gets its own recorded sentence, and the implementation logs at the point it classifies. All three stay retryable, because a restored key, a shorter body or a re-registered device fixes them. Deliberately NOT the email arm's 500: push must not fail the request — that is the channel isolation round five was about — so the log is where it is loud. Six sabotages red-first across the two rounds. That is two consecutive rounds finding the same class in sibling code, which is the argument for looking at the sibling every time rather than the site the reviewer names. **Round thirteen named a limitation I had documented as deliberate, and was right that it is the defect.** The serial runner supersedes a repair that has not STARTED, and `applyRole` awaits a database query before queueing the reclaim — so a sign-out's cleanup has ALWAYS started by then and the pre-start check can never reach it. It unsubscribed the browser, the reclaim then found nothing to register, and the newly signed-in account was left with push silently off: the account-switch case 0049's reassigning upsert exists for, lost to the repair meant to protect it. My own comment claimed the opposite — third round running where the comment overclaims — and the honest account is that I had written that test, watched it fail, and reworded the design rather than fixing it, because I could not see how to. A running repair is still never INTERRUPTED (a half-applied unsubscribe is worse than a completed one); it is TOLD. The runner hands each repair a `superseded()` predicate, and both check it immediately before the irreversible step — the unsubscribe, the RPC — rather than at the top, which is what the runner already does. Four sabotages red-first, including a signal wired to a constant false and a cleanup that ignores it; the provider test captures the predicate the cleanup was handed and evaluates it AFTER the sign-in, which is the end-to-end form of the finding and is not visible from call counts. `tsc` caught what vitest did not, again — `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` on the captured predicate. **Round fourteen found round twelve's fix incomplete in exactly the way this file has recorded before**: I classified the pre-fetch steps I thought of — the missing keys, the encryption — and left `vapidAuthorization` where it was, inside the `fetch` options object, so a malformed `VAPID_SUBJECT` or an unimportable private key threw past the classification, was flattened to `transport`, recorded the false network sentence and logged nothing. The same defect, surviving in the sibling operation, one round after its own fix. `verify-photo-integrity.sh` needed three rounds to learn the same thing and this file already says it: enumerating the ways something can be wrong is how the next one is missed. So the rule is structural now — the `fetch` call takes only precomputed values and nothing inside its argument can throw — and it is asserted as an INVARIANT rather than a case: `blocked: "transport"` may not be claimed when no request was made, which fails for any future pre-fetch step added outside the classification, with the other direction pinned too (a real transport failure must reach the network). The helper that turns a throw into a value is there for the reason smoke.sql wraps its calls: without it the red sentence is the library's and reads as a broken suite rather than a broken rule. **Round fifteen, a P1: the stand-down added in round thirteen was inert in production, and had introduced a leak.** Both because the version was minted where a repair is SCHEDULED rather than where the transition ARRIVES. The reclaim is scheduled only after `resolveRole` returns — a database round trip, while a repair is two service-worker lookups — so a running cleanup was never told in time and unsubscribed anyway; and a role lookup begun BEFORE a sign-out could finish after it and queue its reclaim as the newest repair, making the sign-out's cleanup stand down while the previous account's subscription stayed live. That last is the shared-device leak the cleanup exists to close, reintroduced by the stand-down meant to protect the account switch. The reviewer also named why my test passed: the mocked role query resolved immediately, hiding the window. It takes 60ms now, and both new cases fail against the old versioning. The version is the caller's and is bumped on arrival; unmounting bumps it too, since nothing a torn-down provider scheduled is current — which was found by a test failing for that reason and is a real rule, not a test accommodation. Four sabotages red-first. Two own errors, both found by running: a provider test read the MOUNT's stale predicate and passed for an unrelated reason, and its premise could no longer be produced by two synchronous listener calls, because rule 2 now DROPS the cleanup rather than running it — the better outcome, and one my test had been written before. `tsc` caught what vitest did not for the third time this PR: `delete PUSH.superseded.forget` narrows the property to `never` and the later read stops compiling. **Round sixteen: two P2s, and the first is the SIBLING I had told myself to check and did not.** `noteFailure` destructured `const { data }` and ignored both errors, so an unreadable row became a zero and the next write RESET a device's `failure_count` to 1 — health data destroyed by the call that exists to keep it. That is `dropSubscription`'s tenth-round defect, in the method directly beside it, four rounds later. A failed read now skips the write entirely, because skipping beats guessing on an advisory counter, and a failed write is logged and swallowed for the reason the tenth round established: losing a live notification over bookkeeping is the same defect facing the other way. Second: `signOut` awaited the push cleanup unbounded. `forgetPushDeviceBeforeSignOut` catches rejections, but a promise that never SETTLES is not a rejection — a stalled `unsubscribe()` or hung RPC left the await pending forever, so the button did nothing, said nothing, and somebody walked away from a shared device still signed in: the exact hazard the cleanup exists to prevent, caused by the cleanup. Bounded at three seconds, and losing the cleanup is survivable because the SIGNED_OUT transition retries the local half on this load and every one after. `withTimeout` moved to its own module rather than being exported from `push.ts` — the mock coupling that surfaced when the auth test could not stub it was the factoring telling on itself, since a general bound has nothing to do with push. Four sabotages red-first; the unbounded sign-out one takes the full 10s test timeout, which IS the finding. **Round seventeen, another P1, and a consequence of round fifteen's own fix**: the transition version bumped on EVERY auth event, and supabase-js re-emits `SIGNED_IN` for an already-signed-in user (a refocused tab) and `TOKEN_REFRESHED` on a timer, both with the same session. Each of those superseded a reclaim that was between its service-worker lookups and its RPC, and the `resolvedFor.current === uid` early return then scheduled no replacement — so on a shared device the surviving subscription stayed owned by the PREVIOUS account while the UI reported push as on. The leak again, reached through the third distinct door. The version answers "whose session is this", so it bumps only when the IDENTITY changes; a token refresh does not change the answer. Both directions tested, because "never supersede" satisfies the new case and restores the account-switch leak — the sabotage that never bumps turns four other cases red, which is the shape worth having. Rounds fifteen, sixteen and seventeen were each a consequence of the one before, which is the point at which convergence is worth asking about — but each named a DIFFERENT reachable state with a real user-visible outcome rather than a refinement of the previous argument, so each was fixed rather than argued down. **Round eighteen: two P2s, and the interesting part is that two of my own SABOTAGES stayed green.** First finding: the sign-out deadline stops AWAITING and does not cancel, so a stalled `unsubscribe()` settling after somebody else signs in falls through to `fn_remove_push_subscription` under the NEW session — caller-scoped, so it deletes the row that account just reclaimed, leaving their UI saying `on` over nothing. The cleanup is abandonment-aware now, one timer doing both jobs; the late unsubscribe itself cannot be recalled and that residual is stated rather than implied. Second: `noteFailure` was a client-side read-modify-write, so two notifications delivered concurrently to one failing device both read the old count and both wrote the same value. Fixed with `fn_note_push_failure`, added to the UNMERGED 0049 rather than a new migration, which also deletes the read and with it round sixteen's branch about what an unreadable count means — no read, no wrong answer. **Both smoke sabotages passed, and each was a real defect in my own test.** The atomicity assertion cannot see a read-modify-write at all, because two SEQUENTIAL calls reach +2 under one too — so the claim moved to `concurrency.sh` case 9 (ten concurrent recordings; the read-modify-write body loses some) and the smoke comment now says outright what that block does and does not prove. And the service-role assertion passed on the missing GRANT rather than the body guard, so the block was green without ever reaching the code it names; EXECUTE is granted inside the rolled-back suite first, the 0048 precedent. That one then failed AGAIN for a third reason: `fn_is_service_session()` accepts `session_user = 'postgres'` and `set local role` does not change session_user, which is the trap the 0028 entry already records — it needs `set local session authorization`, and `reset role` does not undo that (the 0038 lesson). Three separate ways for one assertion to mean nothing, all found by insisting the sabotage go red. 709 vitest, 396 deno, 84 smoke DO blocks, 11 concurrency cases, 43 e2e, 71 definer functions, 17 validate gates.
- security(0050): the nineteenth Codex round on PR #85, and the finding is a privilege this schema was never actually granting itself. `0004`'s header states the rule the whole matrix rests on — "explicit grants per the spec 03 matrix (no reliance on platform default privileges)" — and its loop does BOTH halves for each of the original 18 tables: `revoke all ... from public, anon, authenticated` AND `grant all on table %I to service_role`. Only the first half is load-bearing on a live project, which is exactly what makes the second easy to drop: Supabase's bootstrap sets `alter default privileges in schema public grant all on tables/functions to ... service_role`, so anything `postgres` creates in `public` reaches the sender whether a migration says so or not. A migration that revokes and forgets to grant therefore WORKS, and nothing on the project can tell the two apart — an inherited grant and an explicit one both read as `service_role=arwdDxt/postgres` in `relacl`, and smoke cannot see it either because it runs against a database that has the default ACL. Codex named two objects: `push_subscriptions` (the sender selects it and deletes retired endpoints) and `fn_note_push_failure`. Both real. The useful half was refusing to fix only what was pointed at: deriving the sender's WHOLE object set from the `.from(...)`/`.rpc(...)` calls in `supabase/functions/` and re-measuring found two more of the identical defect in MERGED migrations — `vault_canary` (0021:94), read by vault-rekey, and `fn_unsubscribe_by_token` (0038:148), called by the public unsubscribe endpoint, i.e. the one-click opt-out link in every client-facing email and the canary that decides whether a vault key opens this project. Four members, two named, and fixing two of four is the "applied to one site and not its sibling" shape this log keeps recording. 0049 is unmerged and was rewritten in place; 0021 and 0038 are merged, so their fix is `0050`. The grants are additive and non-widening BY CONSTRUCTION and that is the safety argument rather than a hope: on a real project service_role already holds ALL on all four through the default ACL, so these statements change nothing there, and a grant cannot subtract, so naming a narrower privilege does not take the broader one away. `push_subscriptions` is `select, delete` following 0028 rather than `grant all` — the sender reads it (including `p256dh`/`auth`, which `authenticated` may not) and deletes endpoints the push service has retired; it never inserts or updates, both of which go through definer RPCs that run as the owner. **The gate is `db-push-check.sh`, because it is the only place that can ask the question**: it replays every migration as `sb_deploy`, which holds no default privileges, so an object is reachable there only if a migration said so out loud. It measured all four unreachable while still exiting 0 — a check reporting success having verified nothing, which is this repository's single most repeated defect and the reason this is a gate rather than a comment. The object set is DERIVED rather than enumerated, so a table added to a handler is covered without anyone remembering, and it refuses outright when the derivation returns nothing. Its blind spot is stated in spec 03 rather than left to be discovered: a `.from()` built from a variable is invisible to that grep, so the rule stays a review concern as well. Found in my own work by the third sabotage, and it is the same disease one level down: `set -euo pipefail` plus a grep that exits 1 on no match killed the script AT the assignment, so the empty-derivation guard below it was UNREACHABLE — the run came back as an unexplained exit 1 after the BYPASSRLS block instead of the sentence naming the cause. Caught only by reading WHICH line came back rather than trusting the exit code; the `|| true` is load-bearing and the comment says so. Also caught by the discipline rather than by luck: a sabotage whose Python heredoc had an escaping error silently failed to apply, and the "SABOTAGE DID NOT APPLY" guard refused to let the run be believed. Three sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and verified restores — the shipped 0049 (names `push_subscriptions` + `fn_note_push_failure`), 0050 removed (names `vault_canary` + `fn_unsubscribe_by_token`), and a derivation that matches nothing (names itself) — plus the privileges re-measured after the fix in a database built with the default ACL stripped: select/delete/execute true, insert still false, anon still false, and `authenticated` still unable to read the whole row or the key material. Found while checking my own claim that this IS a gate: `ci.yml:673` runs `db-push-check.sh` and `SKILL.md` documents it as gate 7b, but `validate.sh` never ran it — so the file `CLAUDE.md` describes as "the same gates in the same order" was missing the one gate that can see this defect, and a contributor adding a table would have had a green local run and a red CI. It is gate 7b there now, which is why the count moves 17 -> 18. Gate 10a's red on the way through was the documented false-red of an uncommitted spec file, confirmed by regenerating and reading the diff: 38 insertions, 0 deletions, no generator churn. **Codex round twenty then found two P2s against this very work, both real.** FIRST, the gate asked only for `select` — object VISIBILITY, not the operations the sender performs — so narrowing `push_subscriptions` to `select` alone would have passed it while `dropSubscription()`'s DELETE failed on any deployment without the default ACL: a check verifying less than it claims, which is the defect it exists to catch, in the fix for it. It derives the PRIVILEGES now, mapping each supabase-js method to the SQL privilege it needs (`.upsert(` counts as both), bounding each `.from(`'s window by the NEXT one so an operation is never attributed to the wrong table, and FAILING LOUDLY on a `.from()` whose operation cannot be read — quietly checking less is the one outcome that looks like checking more. Red-first against Codex's exact example: `table push_subscriptions needs DELETE`. SECOND, and subtler: `fn_register_push_subscription` applied its OWN case-sensitive `p_endpoint !~ '^https://'` BEFORE consulting `fn_is_push_service_endpoint`, which lowercases first — so `HTTPS://fcm.googleapis.com/...` was refused at registration while the predicate AND the edge library both accepted it, and `check-push-endpoint-parity.sh`, which compares only those two, reported agreement while the RPC that actually guards registration disagreed with both. A gate testing something other than the effective gatekeeper. The redundant check is gone (one rule, one place — the null guard stays, because the predicate returns NULL for NULL and `if not null` is not true) and the error message's host extraction is case-folded to match. The existing smoke assertion only proved registration REFUSES what the predicate refuses, which refusing everything also satisfies; the accept direction is asserted now and is red against the shipped body with `registration refused an endpoint the predicate accepts`. Also corrected: the empty-derivation message blamed the grep, which misdiagnoses an unparsed `.from()` — a red that misdescribes itself is the `ops(deploy-gating)` failure. Three more sabotages red-first. **Round twenty-one then found two more ways the same gate under-reported, both mine.** A mutation followed by `.select()` makes PostgREST use `RETURNING`, so the caller needs the write privilege AND select — and the extractor kept only the FIRST recognised method, so a table used solely through `.insert(...).select(...)` could hold write grants alone and pass. It reads the whole CHAIN now, bounded by the chain itself rather than by the next `.from(`, because over-attributing an unrelated later call is the opposite error and equally wrong. Writing that scanner immediately caught a case the loose one had absorbed silently: `connect-onboarding/index.ts:37` puts a `//` comment BETWEEN `.from("operators")` and `.select(...)`, so a whitespace-only skip ends the chain and loses every op after it — found by the UNPARSED guard rather than by review, which is that guard earning its place. Second: `to_regclass(...) is not null` SILENTLY DISCARDED a derived name with no matching object, so a misspelled `.from("walkz")` — a handler that fails on every invocation — would have been dropped and the gate would report every sender object granted. That is the `fn_book_walk` phantom-column class rebuilt inside the gate written to prevent it; unknown names are reported now, for RPCs too. The sibling the reviewer named was checked rather than assumed: `select-columns.test.ts` skips unknown tables for a DIFFERENT and legitimate reason — it checks column names, where an unknown table is not evidence of a bad column (the M17 rule about gates that go red on a healthy tree) — so it is correct as written and was left alone. Verified before changing anything that no table is mis-derived TODAY (all four mutation-then-select chains — payments, walks, clients, operators — already carry SELECT from other reads), so the fix closes a future gap without moving any present expectation, and that `_tests/` contributes no fabricated table names, which is what makes unknown-is-missing safe. Three more sabotages red-first: a misspelled table (`table walkz does not exist`), the chain rule isolated on a table nothing else touches (first-op-only derives `pets=INSERT`, chain-accurate derives `pets=INSERT,SELECT`), and the comment skip removed (`UNPARSED ... connect-onboarding/index.ts:37`). The FAIL header now describes both row kinds, since "not granted" misdescribes a missing object — the same self-misdiagnosing red corrected one round earlier. **Round twenty-two found a FOURTH way, and it is the round where the stopping rule got tested.** One P2: an RPC was matched by NAME alone, so `.rpc("fn_note_push_failure", {p_eror: ...})` found the function and passed the privilege check while PostgREST rejects the call at runtime — the round-21 existence hole one level down, since PostgREST resolves overloads by argument NAME and a bad key names a function that does not exist. I had written on the PR one round earlier that a fourth way to under-report should probably mean STOPPING rather than sharpening the heuristic further, so the question was judged rather than reflexed: all 26 `.rpc` call sites pass literal objects (measured — no spread, no variable), which makes the parse low-risk, and the rule chosen is deliberately NARROW — every key supplied must be an IN parameter of some overload, and NOT that required arguments are present or typed, which is a typechecker's job and not this gate's. That line is now written into spec 03 alongside the existing blind spot. Own defect in the same edit, caught before it shipped: changing the derived format to `name=key,key;...` left the EXECUTABILITY arm splitting on commas, so it read fragments rather than names — it would have passed by matching nothing, the failure this gate exists to prevent, in the commit adding a check for it. Proved fixed rather than assumed: sabotage H removes the 0049 execute grant and the arm still names `fn_note_push_failure(p_id uuid, p_error text)`. Two sabotages red-first (`has no argument p_eror`, and the executability arm). **Round twenty-three was the fifth finding in the same gate, and it was DECLINED** — the first time this run a review finding was argued down rather than fixed. It is correct in the abstract: with `fn_x(p_a)` and `fn_x(p_b)`, a call passing both keys satisfies each from a different `pg_proc` row while PostgREST resolves neither, because the check asks about ANY overload and the extractor unions keys by name. Both halves were MEASURED unreachable rather than argued away: no `fn_` function in this schema is overloaded at all (the only overloads in `public` are pgcrypto's `digest`/`hmac`/`pgp_*`, which no handler `.rpc()`s), and no RPC is called from more than one site, so there is nothing to union. Closing it properly needs per-call key sets and overload-level grouping — machinery for a state that cannot occur — which is precisely the epicycle the stopping rule stated one round earlier was for. So the PRECONDITION is pinned instead of the check being grown: `db-push-check.sh` now fails the day an `fn_` name gains a second signature, naming what the check would then need, and whoever adds the overload decides. That is the distinction drawn and worth keeping — refusing to extend a heuristic is not the same as leaving its assumption implicit, and this repository's most-repeated defect is a rule written down and connected to nothing. Red-first: adding a real `fn_note_push_failure(p_id uuid)` overload makes the gate say so. **Round twenty-four then found that guard mis-scoped, and it was fixed rather than declined** — the distinction being that it makes the code SIMPLER and removes an assumption, where round 23 wanted machinery for an unreachable state. The guard matched `proname like 'fn\_%'` while the extractor accepts any lowercase RPC name, so it did not cover its own subject: one rule with two scopes, which is the disagreement `check-push-endpoint-parity.sh` exists for one file over, reproduced in the commit that added it. Scoped to the names actually DERIVED now, which also deletes the pgcrypto carve-out — `digest`/`hmac`/`pgp_*` are overloaded and simply are not called, and a handler calling one is exactly when this should fire. Proved in BOTH directions, and the second is what shows the gap was real rather than theoretical: an `fn_` overload is still caught (no regression), and pointing a handler at pgcrypto's `digest` now fires where the old guard stayed green. **Round twenty-five was the same shape again and was also fixed**: both extractor regexes matched only DOUBLE-quoted literals, so `.from('x')` derived nothing — and the emptiness guard cannot see that, because the other call sites keep the aggregate non-empty. Reachable rather than hypothetical, and measured: nothing in this repo enforces a quote style (no prettier, no editorconfig, no oxlint quote rule), so such a call lints and typechecks clean while being invisible to the gate. Both styles accepted now, with a BACKREFERENCE so `.from("x')` still matches nothing. Red-first on a table that genuinely has no service_role grant — a single-quoted `.from('invite_signup_attempts')` now yields `needs SELECT` where the old regex derived nothing at all. Seven consecutive rounds landed on this one gate; six were fixed and one declined, and the line held is the useful record — GROW NOTHING for unreachable states (round 23 wanted per-call key sets and overload grouping for a state no schema here can reach), but NEVER LEAVE A GUARD WHOSE SCOPE DISAGREES WITH WHAT IT GUARDS (rounds 24 and 25 were both that, and both fixes made the code simpler rather than larger). **Round twenty-six moved off the gate and into the PRODUCT, which is where the review effort was worth more**: a `PushSubscription` records the application-server key it was created under and the push service refuses a payload signed by any other, so after a VAPID rotation — or in the deploy window where the frontend and the edge function carry different keys — an existing subscription stays non-null and is completely DEAD. `subscribed: existing !== null` reported it as `on`, which offers only the OFF action, so the device was never prompted to re-opt in while every send was rejected; `reclaimPushDevice` re-registered it, keeping a row alive that nothing could reach. `subscriptionUsesKey()` compares the bound key, and it is consumed in THREE places, because a helper the call sites ignore fixes nothing — including one the review did not name: `pushManager.subscribe()` rejects with `InvalidStateError` when a subscription exists under different options, so reporting `off` alone would have offered an action that throws, leaving the device permanently unfixable. It FAILS OPEN, which is the load-bearing part: `options.applicationServerKey` is not readable everywhere, and answering "stale" because we could not look would unsubscribe working devices on that browser. Own error worth recording, and it is lesson (e) again: the first sabotage — the shipped `subscribed: existing !== null` — stayed GREEN, because the new tests exercised only the pure helper and none of the three call sites that consume it. A broken proof, not a passing one. Behavioural tests at each call site were written, and a second sabotage then also stayed green, exposing that the reclaim skip had no test at all. All three are red-first now. **Round twenty-seven found the fail-open rule applied to only ONE side of the comparison**, which is the same asymmetry one level in: an unreadable BOUND key failed open, while a malformed CONFIGURED key failed closed. A VAPID setting truncated in a dashboard is still valid base64url, so it decodes without throwing and then differs from every real subscription — condemning WORKING devices over a typo, and `enablePush` would unsubscribe one before `subscribe()` refused the bad key: a configuration mistake converted into lost notifications while the edge function still held the good key. An application-server key is an uncompressed P-256 point (65 bytes, leading `0x04`), and anything else is now no evidence rather than evidence of a mismatch — a SHAPE check, not a curve check, since a 65-byte non-point still reaches `subscribe()` and is refused loudly there. The finding also exposed my own FIXTURE: the tests mocked `vapidPublicKey` as `"BHYK"`, three bytes, which no browser could ever have subscribed under, so the comparison was being exercised against something structurally impossible. It is a realistically shaped 65-byte key now (via `vi.hoisted`, since `vi.mock` is hoisted above ordinary consts). Red-first against the shipped round-26 body. **Round twenty-eight then pressed on the very thing round 27 had DECLINED — full point validation — and was right, because my stated reason for declining was wrong.** I had argued a 65-byte non-point "still reaches `subscribe()`, which refuses it loudly"; that is about DETECTION, and misses that `enablePush` unsubscribes the working device BEFORE `subscribe()` is called. The destructive step happens first, so a `0x04`-prefixed off-curve value was accepted as comparison evidence and would have destroyed a live subscription. `crypto.subtle.importKey("raw", …, P-256)` validates the point and was MEASURED rejecting exactly that case with `DataError`; the helper is async now and all three call sites await it. The fixture was wrong for the THIRD round running, and this time in a way that would have made the whole rule vacuous: the mocked key was `0x04` plus invented bytes, so under real validation it fails open and every mismatch assertion would have passed for the wrong reason — both fixtures are now genuine generated P-256 points, and the mismatch case uses a second real key rather than three bytes. The off-curve case the review named as uncovered is covered. Two sabotages red-first: shape-check-only (`expected false to be true` on the off-curve case) and treating an unimportable key as a mismatch instead of as no evidence. 717 vitest, 396 deno, 84 smoke DO blocks, 11 concurrency cases, 43 e2e, 71 definer functions, 18 gates, migrations through 0050.
- money(send-once): backlog item 1 — send-once becomes ATOMIC, on both channels. `deliverNotification` and `deliverPush` each read the channel's status through `isSettled` and write the outcome later, so the guard was a READ-THEN-ACT: two invocations both pass it and both deliver. Three ways to get two, all reachable: the INSERT database webhook racing the nightly drain, a drain run overlapping its predecessor, and the endpoint accepting a `notification_id` directly (M1), which an operator can POST in a loop. **The harm is asymmetric, and that is why this covers both arms rather than the push one Codex named on #85**: the service worker tags by notification id, so two deliveries of one row COLLAPSE into a single lock-screen entry, while a duplicate email is a second email in somebody's inbox — and the email arm has had this shape since M1. `0051` adds `fn_claim_notification_send(id, channel, lease)`: the mutual exclusion is the single conditional UPDATE, not the read in front of it, so a second contender blocks on the row lock, re-evaluates its `WHERE` against the row the winner just wrote, and updates zero rows (the 0016/0048 instrument). A LEASE, because a claim that never expires turns a crashed sender into permanent loss — the row neither sent nor reclaimable, which is worse than the duplicate it prevents; that is 0013's `stripe_events` shape. A timestamp column rather than a `sending` enum value, for a mechanical reason already paid for here: `alter type ... add value` cannot be used in the transaction that adds it and `db-push-check.sh` applies one transaction per file, so the enum modelling would need two migrations to say one thing — and `*_status` keeps meaning only the OUTCOME. `fn_notification_backlog` stops handing out a row under a live claim (rebuilt from `pg_get_functiondef` of the LIVE function, not 0029's text, because 0049 widened it for push and a body written from the older file would silently delete that). **What proves it is `concurrency.sh` case 10, because nothing else can**: ten contending backends produce exactly one winner and nine refusals, and the read-then-act body — demonstrated, not assumed — passes the smoke block unchanged, so only contending backends distinguish the fix from the defect. Own errors, and both are the lessons #85 spent eleven rounds teaching, arriving immediately: the first semantic probe ran `select id from notifications limit 1` against an EMPTY table, so every claim hit a null id and every answer was false including the ones that must be true (a fixture that cannot be real proves nothing); and the first read-then-act sabotage STAYED GREEN because I left the conditional `WHERE` on the UPDATE and only added a redundant pre-read — the sabotage was wrong, not the code, and the genuine one makes the write unconditional. The service-role assertion also failed honestly at first: `fn_is_service_session()` is `auth.role() = 'service_role' OR session_user = 'postgres'`, and switching only the session authorization left the suite's earlier `{"role":"service_role"}` claim satisfying it — both inputs are switched now. Eight red-first proofs: two edge call sites (email and push, each reverted to the shipped read-then-act), three SQL rules (no lease, claim ignoring status, backlog ignoring live claims), the genuine read-then-act under concurrency, and both directions of the claim at each arm so a guard wired to refuse everything cannot pass. `select-columns.test.ts`'s pinned model and three notification fixtures needed the two new columns — `tsc` caught the fixtures the passing vitest run did not, again. **Then a 21-agent adversarial pass over this very diff, every finding independently verified, confirmed 11 — and the P1 was mine and it disabled an alarm.** Four dimensions found it independently: nothing RELEASED the claim, so a sender that had fully finished still held it for the lease, and `fn_notification_backlog` now hides claimed rows. `job-health.yml` — H17's ONLY signal that client-facing email is not being delivered — drains the backlog and re-reads it seconds later, exiting 1 if anything survives. So the re-read was always 0 and the check could never go red again: a dead `RESEND_API_KEY`, an unverified sending domain or a provider outage would all have reported green, which is this repository's most-repeated defect (a check reporting success having verified nothing) rebuilt by the fix for a different one. Reproduced against the live database before fixing — `undelivered row visible to the ops re-read: 0 (H17 needs 1)`, and 1 once the lease lapsed — and the verifier added that with a recorded failure the attempts counter climbs nightly until the row ages out of the backlog entirely, so even the `before` read goes quiet without a single red ever raised. `recordPatch`/`pushRecordPatch` now write `*_claimed_at = null` on every kind: the lease is for a sender that CRASHED, and one that recorded an outcome did neither. It also restores the only immediate retry path there is — an operator POSTing `notification_id` after noticing a blip — which had been answering `skipped` and blaming a sender that had already exited. **The second confirmed defect was the headline proof itself.** Case 10 launched ten backends and hoped they would overlap; measured against the read-then-act body it went red 3 times in 12 — a 25% detection rate, so the single red I had generalised from was luck, and a test unreliable in the direction of passing when the bug is present is the worst shape available. The race is FORCED now: a holder session takes the row with `SELECT … FOR UPDATE`, all ten contenders are OBSERVED queued on that one lock, and only then does the holder commit — after which the read-then-act gives ten winners (its unlocked read is not blocked by a row lock, so all ten decide to claim and all ten write) and the real function gives one. 5/5 in both directions, and the failure now reads `expected 1, got 10`. Three more from the same pass, each a rule with nothing behind it: the push arm's channel argument was pinned by nothing, so the whole suite stayed green with `deliverPush` claiming `"email"` (which would wire both arms to one claim); the backlog's push-channel gate could be deleted entirely and smoke still reported "per-channel … OK"; and NEITHER real `claimSend` had a test, because the email copy sat in `index.ts`, which binds a port on import — so the rule their comments call load-bearing (throw on a database error, never answer false, since reading a transient failure as "already claimed" silently drops the delivery) was enforced by nothing. That one is fixed structurally rather than with a second copy of a test: there is ONE `claimNotificationSend` in `handler.ts` now, called by both arms, typed structurally so a stub drives it. The lease got the same treatment — `fn_notification_claim_lease()` is the single definition the claim's default AND the backlog's predicate both read, because drift between two copies is silent in both directions (a shorter backlog window hands out rows the claim then refuses, work the drain reports as done and did not do; a longer one hides a row whose sender died), and smoke MOVES that one definition and asserts both consumers move with it — behavioural, since a structural check is satisfied by a mention in a comment (the 0046 lesson). Granting execute on it inside the rolled-back suite is load-bearing for the same reason the claim's grant is: the parameter default is evaluated in the CALLER's context, so without it the non-service call dies on `permission denied for function` before the body guard runs, and that block would report a guard working while never reaching it. Deliberately NOT fixed here and now backlog item 2: `getClient`/`getOperator` discard the supabase-js `error`, so a transient blip yields `client === null` and records the TERMINAL skip "client has no email address", permanently cancelling a `payment_failed` email — real, confirmed, and byte-identical on `main`, so it is a pre-existing defect in a function this diff does not touch rather than something to smuggle into a money-path PR. Seven further red-first proofs this round, each behind a verified non-empty diff and a verified restore: the release removed from all three outcome kinds in each arm, the push channel argument switched to `"email"`, the shared claim answering false instead of throwing, the read-then-act body under the forced race, and the lease given back to each consumer separately (both directions). One own error worth recording: the holder's `pg_stat_activity` needle was written as a trailing `-- comment` AFTER the semicolon, so it belonged to the next statement and the probe timed out for ten seconds every run while the lock it was looking for was in fact held — the contenders blocking on it is what showed the mechanism worked and only the probe was wrong. Also fixed, pre-existing and flagged rather than smuggled: `db-push-check.sh` carried backticks in SQL comments inside an UNQUOTED heredoc, so bash was running `.from("walkz")` and `fn_x(p_a)` as commands and splicing the empty results in — the FOURTH instance of a trap this file already records three times, and it was writing errors to stderr on every run of the gate whose output this PR cites as evidence. **The Codex review then found the same defect one door along, and it was mine again**: the release lived in `recordPatch`, which covers every way the two arms RETURN and none of the ways they THROW. `assertEmailConfigured()` — H17's deliberate 500 for a missing `RESEND_API_KEY` — throws AFTER the claim, `drainBacklog` catches it and still answers 200, so the row kept its claim and the ops re-read came back empty: H17's alarm dead again, through the throw path instead of the record path, in the commit that fixed the record path. `deliverPush` had the identical shape when `getSubscriptions` throws, which Codex named too — the sibling worth checking every time rather than only the site the reviewer points at. Fixed STRUCTURALLY rather than by deferring the claim (which would undo the "claimed before the terminal checks" rule my own test pins) and rather than by patching the two throws named: each arm now claims and wraps its whole remaining body, so anything added between the claim and the send is covered without its author knowing the rule exists. Enumerating the pre-send steps is how the next one is missed — `verify-photo-integrity.sh` took three rounds to learn that and the push arm's pre-fetch classification took two. The release is a plain UPDATE through the existing `notifications` grant, so no migration moved; it is best-effort and its own failure is CONTAINED, because replacing "RESEND_API_KEY is unset" with "db unreachable" tells the operator the wrong thing about why nothing was sent (the `fix(edge-errors)` defect), and the lease is what makes swallowing it affordable. One residual is now stated in spec 04 rather than implied away: the claim excludes CONCURRENT senders and does not make delivery exactly-once across a crash between Resend accepting a message and us recording it — `record` throwing after a successful send leaves the row `pending` and the drain returns it. Releasing does not introduce that; the row is retryable either way and the lease only decides whether the duplicate arrives in five minutes or on the next drain. That case has its own test so the behaviour is pinned rather than accidental. Also corrected while writing them: `assertRejects` here takes a MESSAGE, not an error class, and returns the error — a bare call passes for any rejection, which is the 0048 trap, so each new test pins the type and the message. Three more sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and verified restores: the email release deleted (three tests red), the push release deleted, and the release's own failure left uncontained. **Codex round 2 then found the deeper version of it, and was right**: a lease recovers a crashed sender by ASSUMING a slow one has died, and cannot tell them apart. A sender still running when its lease lapses is replaced, both are live, and the first one's outcome write was unconditional — so it cleared the REPLACEMENT's claim and recorded over it, marking the row sent while the replacement was still delivering.
