Codex offered two remedies; they close DIFFERENT halves and neither is sufficient alone, so both are here. **Fencing** protects the write: `fn_claim_notification_send` returns a uuid token instead of a boolean and every later write by that sender is conditioned on it, so a fenced-out sender updates zero rows. **A deadline** stops the second SEND, which fencing cannot: the Resend request now carries `AbortSignal.timeout`, which the push arm has had since PR #85 and this one did not — the sibling asymmetry this file keeps recording, load-bearing here because a request with no bound can outlive its five-minute lease. The token is a DEDICATED column rather than a reuse of `claimed_at`, and that was my first design, refuted by a probe rather than by review: `now()` is TRANSACTION-constant, so two claims in one transaction share a timestamp exactly — unreachable in production, where every sender is its own transaction, but it makes the property unassertable in `smoke.sql`, which runs in one transaction, and a token that collides under the very conditions a test uses is one nobody can check. Testing the deadline needed `deps.ts`: `makeDeps` sat in `index.ts`, which binds a port on import, so nothing in it could be driven by a test — the blind spot that has now hidden TWO defects in this one file (the `claimSend` rule and this missing deadline), the `overage_deps.ts` shape twice over. Split out mirroring `push_deps.ts`, with the env reads hoisted into `index.ts` so the module takes values and stays constructible under `deno test` with no permissions. One own error on the way, the depth-blind extraction trap the L21 CSS split already paid for: the first `deps.ts` was carved by LINE RANGES and cut through a comment block, so it would not parse — redone with text anchors and asserted non-empty at each boundary. Five more sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and verified restores: unfenced outcome writes (three tests red), a token minted per ROW instead of per claim, an unfenced write at the SQL level, the deadline removed, and the stamp replaced by a constant. `tsc` caught the three notification fixtures the passing vitest run did not — the same three files, for the second time in this PR. **CI then went red on a gate `validate.sh` does not run**, which is the more useful half: changing the RPC's return from boolean to uuid silently invalidated `concurrency.sh` case 10, whose detectors count `t`/`f` lines — all three assertions fell to zero INCLUDING the precondition, so it failed loudly rather than passing vacuously, which is the only reason it was legible. The call asks `is not null` now (the question the case actually cares about; token distinctness is smoke's assertion), and the deeper fix is that **`concurrency.sh` is a validate gate at last** — it had been CI-only, so an 18/18 local run could not see it, and this file describes `validate.sh` as "the same gates in the same order". Second instance of exactly that gap: `db-push-check.sh` was the first, added as 7b one PR ago. Same shape as the push-service allowlist silently invalidating case 7's fixtures in #85 — tightening or retyping a rule invalidates every fixture built on the old one, and only a suite that actually runs can say so. 717 vitest, 419 deno, 85 smoke DO blocks, 12 concurrency cases, 43 e2e, 72 definer functions, 19 gates with none skipped, migrations through 0051.
- docs(handoff): a readiness pass over the three files a fresh session opens, and the two things it found were both **a rule written down and connected to nothing** — this file's most-recorded defect, in the documentation that records it. FIRST, the Phase status paragraph said migrations run through `0050` while the tree carried `0051`, and its own parenthetical said the number had gone stale twice before and that "nothing enforces these two counts". A handoff reads that paragraph as fact about the tree. It is gate 10d now (`scripts/check-status-counters.py`, in `ci.yml` and `SKILL.md` too), and the gate FAILS when it cannot find the sentence carrying a count rather than reporting agreement — the `column-grants.test.ts` and `db-push-check.sh` lesson. Proved red against the LIVE defect rather than a simulated one, which is the strongest form available: the first run named `0050` vs `0051` unprompted. Two more directions proved behind verified non-empty diffs and verified restores — both sentences deleted (names itself, does not pass), and the edge-function count moved to 15 (names the mismatch and lists all 16). SECOND, `session-notes.md` asserted that `db-push-check.sh` "is not part of `validate.sh`", which stopped being true when `security(0050)` made it gate 7b, and `SKILL.md` numbered its 10-series differently from `validate.sh` and carried the concurrency suite inside gate 8 rather than as 8b — so the three files this file calls a lockstep were not one. Both corrected, with the rule stated where a reader will hit it: a gate added to `ci.yml` moves all three in the same commit. Also recorded, from `money(send-once)`: extract a module with TEXT ANCHORS and never line ranges (the `deps.ts` split cut through a comment block and would not parse — the L21 CSS split's defect again), a deps module reading `Deno.env` at construction cannot be constructed under `deno test`, which CI runs with no permissions, and retyping a function's return silently invalidates every detector built on the old type. New backlog item: the Supabase CLI pin is at 2.109.1 against v2.116.0, read off the `24c74bd` deploy log rather than recalled, with the pg-delta certificate stack trace it prints on every push — established as an optional *catalog cache* failing behind a `Warning` prefix with `db push` reporting `Finished`, i.e. log noise rather than a failed migration, which is the distinction worth writing down before somebody reads it as a broken deploy. Verified rather than asserted while checking: `main` == `origin/main` == `release/staging` at `24c74bd`, all seven jobs of the staging deploy green with `0051` applied, no open PRs, and the designated branch reset off `main` because its pull request had merged (it held the pre-rebase copies — identical trees, zero diff). **Codex then found the new gate committing the very defect it was written against**, and was right: `NOT_A_FUNCTION = {"_lib", "_tests"}` is an ENUMERATION where `scripts/verify-deployment.sh`'s `repo_functions()` — the inventory the deploy probe compares against the Management API, and therefore what "N edge functions" actually means — is a PREDICATE: skip a leading-underscore directory, require `index.ts`. So the next `_shared/` helper or a scaffold directory with no entrypoint would be counted, and the gate would go RED ON A HEALTHY TREE, which is the M17 failure mode this log already records as the worst shape available because it is how a gate gets deleted by whoever is trying to ship something unrelated. Measured rather than argued: with `_shared/` and `scaffold-no-entry/` planted, the shipped gate reported "the tree has 18". The fix uses the same predicate and makes the code simpler, which is the criterion PR #85's rounds 24 and 25 settled — never leave a guard whose scope disagrees with what it guards — and both files now name the other so an editor of either sees the sibling. Proven in three directions with the cleanup verified by `git status`: the two planted non-functions now ignored (red before, green after), a REAL planted function still counted (`the tree has 17`, so the gate did not become vacuous — the round-26 lesson about a fix that passes for the wrong reason), and the real tree green. **Codex round two then refuted the argument I had just written on the PR.** I had declined a parity harness for the two implementations on the grounds that a two-line directory predicate has "no input space to disagree over", and the reviewer BUILT the input: `Path.iterdir()` yields dot-directories and the shell glob `*/` does not, so a `.fixtures/index.ts` counted in Python and not in bash — reproduced exactly as described, 17 against 16. Two divergences in two rounds on a pair I had claimed could not diverge, so the response is neither the one-line fix nor the harness I declined: `scripts/repo-functions.sh` is the ONE definition now and both callers ask it, which is the same structural answer the M17 entry records — "there is one exported implementation, so there is no sibling to forget". The dot-directory exclusion is written into it EXPLICITLY rather than left to the glob, so the rule does not depend on one language's directory listing happening to hide them. Proven with `.review-hidden/index.ts` still planted (gate green where it was red), a real planted function still counted (`the tree has 17`), and both helper failure modes — missing and non-zero exit — now answering with a named `FAIL:` sentence rather than a Python traceback, which is `session-notes.md`'s own rule about a broken gate reading as a broken rule. `verify-deployment.test.ts` passes through the delegation (10), the resolution was checked from a different cwd, and cleanup was verified with `git status`. **Round three then found the failure surface that refactor introduced, in the half I had not hardened.** The Python caller checks the subprocess status before touching its stdout; `verify-deployment.sh` did not — it runs `set -uo pipefail` WITHOUT `-e`, so `expected=$(repo_functions)` keeps whatever the helper printed before dying and the `[ -z "$expected" ]` below it resets `$?`. A helper that printed three names and exited 9 therefore left the deploy verifier probing a PARTIAL set, reporting the rest as "deployed but no longer in this repository", and printing `DEPLOYMENT VERIFY PASS` — every unprinted function silently unverified, in the script whose entire purpose is to catch exactly that. Status is checked before the output is consumed now, and `REPO_FUNCTIONS` is injectable so the case is testable at all. The proof is worth recording because the FIRST version of it went red for the wrong reason: a truncated list that omits a bespoke-contract name trips `contract_for`'s stale-exception check, which is an incidental catch rather than the guard under test — the corrected fixture keeps all three bespoke names in the partial list and omits an ordinary one, and against the unfixed script it exits **0** with `complete-walk` never probed. 11 verify-deployment tests. The sibling substitutions were audited rather than assumed: `status=$(mgmt …)` IS its own check, and a partial `deployed=$(jq … | sort)` makes the script report functions as undeployed, i.e. it fails in the safe direction — pre-existing and left alone rather than widening the PR.
- docs(spec-drift): an audit of this log and `docs/spec/` against HEAD — seven verification passes across every era, with every mechanism that could be executed executed (12 concurrency cases, 85 smoke blocks, 419 deno, 718 vitest, 43 e2e, the deploy-role replay, the parity check, invariant 1, the counter gate) and every external claim re-read from the API rather than recalled (`main` unprotected, `release/staging` at `main`, no `release/production`, zero production deploys, no open PRs). The log held: every enforcement claim that names a gate has a gate behind it, and every mechanism claim that could be probed probed true, including "a BEFORE trigger may set a column the caller cannot update" and "a column REVOKE is a no-op against a table-level grant". What had drifted was the AUTHORITATIVE half, and two of those would have caused harm if an engineer had made the code match the spec — the failure the spec-06 service-worker entry records, this time on the money path and the vault. **Spec 04:59 prescribed the billing order `qc(1–4)` removed**: mark completed, then `fn_debit_walk`. The code has debited first since that fix, with a comment saying why, and `complete_walk_test.ts` fails if it is reverted; the spec said the opposite for every hardening wave since. **Spec 01:32 stated `credential_access_log.credential_id` as `on delete cascade`** while the schema has been RESTRICT since 0002 and spec 03:114 builds the purge design on RESTRICT — a migration written to match spec 01 would have let deleting a credential erase its own audit trail, and nothing asserted the rule. Smoke does now, across the four RESTRICTs the erasure argument rests on, confirmed red against a cascade sabotage on a scratch copy. **Spec 02:3 ordered the ledger chain by `created_at, id`**, which `0002:341` refutes in its own comment and `smoke.sql` never used. **Spec 01's enum block was a hand list under a heading saying "migration 0001"**: missing `disputed` (in every partial-unique-index predicate spec 04 says the code must agree with), `card_saved`, and four whole enums. It is generated now — `scripts/gen-enum-catalog.py`, the definer catalogue's shape, gate 10e in `ci.yml`, `SKILL.md` and `validate.sh` together — and proven in three directions on scratch copies of the migrations: a dropped `add value` changes the block, an `alter type` the parser cannot read refuses by name rather than modelling it, and zero enums refuses rather than reporting agreement. The rest of the stale prose: spec 03's "nine" storage policies (seven), its "There are 48" above a generated block saying 72, and its purge inventory missing `push_subscriptions`; spec 05 naming the live-walk banner L21 deleted; spec 00's Postgres 16 and Netlify; spec 01's `integer pence`, column lists four migrations behind, an understated audit-log grant and a third withheld push column; this file's own header saying `react-router-dom 6`. Six comments a reader would consult first and be misled by: the vault handler still claiming MFA needs the Pro plan (the third of the three sites the `ops(auth-posture)` correction said it fixed), `unsubscribe` calling itself the only public endpoint (there are four), `sw.js` and its test fixture saying the push tag is the TYPE a round after the server switched to the row id, the push-state comment counting five of six states, and `verify-deployment.sh`'s "12 of the 13 functions" (16, with two bare `Deno.serve`s) — which now says outright not to state a count there. Recorded in `docs/dev/backlog.md` for the next PRs rather than smuggled in here: `service-worker.test.ts` passes for the wrong reason (every Supabase fixture is cross-origin, so the origin gate alone satisfies all five assertions and deleting `isNeverCache` stays green); the FormError CI grep matches one literal class; the invariant-1 catalogue regex misses `update public.clients` and an alias; `session-notes.md` names two CI-only gates where there are seven; four definer trigger functions carry PUBLIC EXECUTE and the catalogue generator, reading only grants, prints **none** for them; and `create-plan` accepts a zero overage rate the 0026 CHECK then refuses after the Stripe Price is minted. Also corrected: the Commits section named one model as though the co-author were fixed. Codex on the PR then found the generator committing a defect its own docstring warns against: it processed every `create type`, then every `alter type`, then every `drop type` within a migration rather than in statement order, so `drop type if exists mood; create type mood as enum (…)` — a valid migration — became create-then-drop in memory and the catalogue would have omitted a live enum while CI reported agreement. Every matched statement is applied as one position-ordered stream now; proven against Codex's exact case (the shipped collector omits `mood`, the ordered one keeps it), its mirror (create-then-drop still removes it), a `before` anchor in the same file, and the three earlier sabotages still red. Round two found the sibling: the `drop type` regex demanded the semicolon straight after the name, so `drop type payment_status cascade;` matched nothing and the dropped enum stayed in the catalogue with CI reporting agreement — the parser-that-sees-nothing defect in the fix for the parser-that-sees-nothing defect. The strict shape (a comma list, optional CASCADE/RESTRICT) is parsed now, and every `drop type` the strict regex does NOT match is refused by name rather than ignored, the rule the `alter type` branch already had. Proven: `cascade` and `restrict` drops remove the type where the shipped regex kept it, a two-name drop removes both, and a drop the parser cannot read exits 1. Round three found two more of the class in the same file: a quoted identifier (`create type public."delivery_status" as enum (…)`) matched no regex and was skipped with fifteen enums already keeping the parser-saw-nothing guard quiet, and the comment stripper cut `add value 'client--reminder'` at the marker inside the literal, so the statement no longer matched and the value went missing silently. The stripper is a small state machine now (comments removed only outside string literals and quoted identifiers), and every `create type … as enum` and `alter type` the strict regexes do not read is refused by name, the rule the drop branch got one round earlier. Proven: the quoted create and a quoted alter exit 1 naming the statement, the `--` label survives, an apostrophe inside a comment still strips, and the committed block is byte-identical. One claim in the first draft of this sentence was false and my own proof said so: an escaped quote inside a label (`'it''s'`) was REFUSED, not kept — honest, but not what I had written, and the commit went out because the proof's exit status was not gating the push. Labels are read as SQL literals now, doubled quotes and all, and the proof set refuses to commit on any red. Round four, two more in the same file and the same class: block comments NEST in PostgreSQL, so the stripper left at an inner `*/` and read the rest of the outer comment as SQL (a `create type ghost` inside a comment became a catalogued enum); and an escape-string label (`E'…'` with a backslash-escaped quote) was half-read with both create scans still matching. Depth is tracked now, and a `create type … as enum` body that is not a comma list of plain `'…'` literals is refused by name — decoding every literal form PostgreSQL has is more parser than fifteen enums earn, and refusing keeps the gate honest. Four rounds, six findings, one file, one class (a parser accepting less than SQL allows and saying nothing); each was fixed rather than declined because each was a way for CI to bless a wrong authoritative catalogue, and each fix made the parser stricter rather than larger. Round five, two more: DDL-shaped text inside a dollar-quoted VALUE (`comment on type … is $doc$create type ghost …$doc$`) was scanned as a statement, and a block comment between two tokens (`create/*gap*/type`) was removed without the whitespace PostgreSQL treats it as, so the statement vanished from both scans. Dollar-quoted regions are blanked now (their own comments stripped first, and any `create/alter/drop type` still inside refused by name — top-level statements are all this generator reads), and a removed block comment leaves a space. Round six, two more: `select 'drop type payment_status;'` — a VALUE — dropped the enum from the catalogue, and a label containing `;` truncated its own statement so the gate refused a valid migration. One structural fix for both: the stripper now also emits a same-length SKELETON with every literal's contents masked, the statement scans run on the skeleton (a value cannot open, close or name a statement), and labels are read from the intact text at the same spans. Round seven, two more, both in the fix for round six: a quoted IDENTIFIER's contents were copied into the skeleton unmasked, so `select 1 as "drop type payment_status;"` — an alias — matched both DROP scans and removed a live enum; identifiers are masked like literals now, with a doubled quote inside one read as content. And `alter type` actions were whitespace-collapsed BEFORE parsing, so `add value 'needs  review'` recorded one space, a BEFORE/AFTER anchor carrying that label was not found and the value was appended instead of placed, and a `rename value` of it silently did nothing — the action is parsed from the intact span now and collapsed only for the failure message. Round eight, two more, and both are about what a migration can EXECUTE rather than what it says: a top-level `set search_path = private, public` before an unqualified `create type` puts the type in `private`, and the generator recorded it over the public enum of the same name — a change of search_path is refused now unless `public` is its first schema (`set`, `set_config`, `alter database/role`; `reset` passes), because tracking the path is more parser than fifteen enums earn and the fix is one `public.` prefix. And a DO body written as an ordinary single-quoted string (`DO 'BEGIN EXECUTE ''CREATE TYPE …''; END'`) was masked whole, so both scans saw nothing — and its dollar-quoted sibling `do $$ begin execute 'create type …'; end $$` was equally invisible, because round six checked a body's SKELETON and an EXECUTE carries its DDL inside a string. A DO body runs at migration time, so its clean text may not mention enum DDL or `search_path` at all; that reverses round six's allowance for prose inside one (`raise notice 'create type …'` is refused now, a function body still is not), and single-quoted bodies of DO and function statements get the same check as their dollar-quoted forms. Round nine, one more, and it is the round-eight fix's own scope disagreeing with what it guards: `set schema 'private'` is PostgreSQL's alias for `set search_path to private`, one word the regex did not know, so the gate passed a migration that moved every unqualified statement into `private` — the alias is refused on the same public-first rule now, while `alter table … set schema`, a move rather than a path change, still passes. Round ten, one more in the same guard: `set_config('search_path', concat('private', ',public'), false)` moves the path with a value no regex can read, and the round-eight pattern recognised a literal second argument and matched NOTHING otherwise — enumerating the forms I knew, which is how the next one is missed (the `verify-photo-integrity.sh` lesson, in a gate written after it). The rule is inverted: a `set_config` whose name or value is not a plain literal is refused outright, and only a literal `'search_path'` with a literal public-first value passes; an unrelated GUC with a computed value still does. Round eleven, two more, both in the round-ten fix and in opposite directions: it scanned the INTACT statement for `set_config`, so `select 'set_config(foo)'` — inert data — was refused as a computed call, a gate red on a healthy tree, which is the worst shape available; and a quoted `pg_catalog."set_config"(…)` was invisible, because the skeleton masks identifiers and the scan did not look through them. Calls are located on the skeleton now and their arguments decoded from the intact text at the same spans, a quoted identifier is resolved to the name it carries, and the same rule reaches `set "search_path"` and `alter role … set "search_path"`. Round twelve, one more, and it removes a distinction round eight drew: a function body got the SKELETON check on the argument that it runs later, but later can be the very next statement — `create function mk() … execute 'create type public.mood …'` followed by `select mk()` created the enum at migration time with the DDL masked inside the string, and the catalogue was blessed without it. One rule for every procedural body now: DO, function and procedure alike may not mention enum DDL or `search_path` in their clean text, whichever way they are quoted and whenever they are called; a COMMENT ON value keeps the skeleton check, since a value cannot execute. The code got simpler, which is the criterion this file's stopping rule uses. Round thirteen, two more: a final statement with no `;` runs in PostgreSQL and matched no scan (every regex keyed on the terminator), so EOF is one now; and `execute 'create ' || 'type …'` carries no contiguous keyword for the body check to see, so the rule is inverted for EXECUTE too — the command text must be a plain literal or a `format()` of one, the only two forms this repository's 51 migrations use, and a variable, `||`, `concat()` or any other expression is refused as unreadable. Also written into the generator's docstring now, because thirteen rounds is the point to say what the gate is FOR: catching mistakes in migrations written in good faith, refusing what it cannot read rather than modelling it, and NOT defending against SQL assembled to evade it (a `format('%s type …', 'create')` template supplies the verb through a placeholder and is not detected; that residual is stated rather than chased). Round fourteen, two more. The EXECUTE rule scanned a body with nested dollar quotes unrecognised (the round-five decision for VALUES, where `$sign$` is prose), so `perform length($msg$please execute this later$msg$)` in a function body was refused as an EXECUTE — a gate red on a healthy tree; inside a BODY a nested `$tag$…$tag$` is a string literal now, kept intact in the clean text and masked in the skeleton, so `execute $q$create type …$q$` still carries its DDL into the check while inert prose does not. And labels were written into the Markdown VERBATIM, so one legal label `'two · labels'` rendered exactly as two values and a label carrying a newline plus `- ` injected a list item; every value is a code span now, fenced one backtick longer than any run inside it and padded where CommonMark would strip, and a label with a control character is refused rather than escaped — which reverses round seven's "newline inside a label preserved", because a value the catalogue cannot show on one line is not one it should claim to. The regenerated block carries the same fifteen value lists, checked by parsing both. Round fifteen, three more: `set search_path = "PUBLIC", public` was case-folded to `public` and accepted while PostgreSQL resolves the quoted name as a DIFFERENT schema (a quoted name keeps its case now, an unquoted one folds, a single-quoted list is unwrapped first); a label made only of spaces was padded into a code span CommonMark does not strip, so the catalogue showed three spaces for one (all-whitespace and empty labels are refused beside control characters); and `$` is legal INSIDE an identifier, so two aliases `first$tag$` and `second$tag$` were paired as one dollar region and the enum statement between them refused — an opener is recognised only at a token boundary now, while a closer needs none because the lexer ends the string at its first occurrence. Twice this round the fix commit went out without this sentence, because the script that appends it sat below the proof guard in a run a stale proof had failed; the sentence is written before the proofs run now. Round sixteen, two more, and the first is the reviewer right about the defect and wrong about the mechanism: it asked the spec to restore `purpose text not null check (length(trim(purpose)) > 0)` from 0002, but 0030 DROPPED that NOT NULL and replaced the check with `credential_access_log_purpose_required_for_read` — a purpose is required and non-blank only when `action = 'read'`, because a revoke has no purpose and a failed re-auth has no credential the caller was entitled to name — so the bare `purpose text` this PR wrote was incomplete rather than weaker than the schema, and restoring 0002's constraint would have written the exact drift the audit exists to remove back into the authoritative file. The spec states the live constraint now, read off `pg_constraint` after a reset rather than off the migration the reviewer cited, and the two siblings in the same sentence gain the `not null` they have carried since 0002. The second: `create type phase as enum ()` is legal PostgreSQL — measured, zero labels after the create and `add value` populates it — and the body regex demanded at least one literal, so the create-then-add-value workflow could not pass the gate: a gate red on a healthy tree, the worst shape available. An empty body is accepted now and rendered as `(no values)`, which no code-span value can be mistaken for. Round seventeen, one more, and it is a SETTING rather than a statement: with `standard_conforming_strings = off` PostgreSQL reads a backslash in an ordinary literal as an escape, so `create type mood as enum ('line\nfeed')` stores a real newline where the generator recorded a backslash and an `n` — a catalogue blessed with a value the live enum does not hold, and the control-character rule bypassed on the way. Measured before fixing, and the measurement had its own trap: a `psql -c` string is ONE query message, lexed whole before any of it runs, so the `set` in the same string had no effect on the literal beside it and the first probe refuted the finding falsely; sent as separate statements, the way `psql -f` and `db push` apply a file, the label is nine characters with a newline inside. Refused rather than modelled, on the search_path rule: `set`/`set local`/`set session`, `set_config` and `alter database|role|user|system … set` may not move `standard_conforming_strings` to anything but an explicit `on` (any boolean spelling PostgreSQL accepts; `default` and `reset` return to the cluster default, `on` since 9.1), and a procedural body may not mention it at all. `backslash_quote` was checked rather than assumed and needs no guard: it applies only while this setting is off. Round eighteen, one more, back in the spec: the `walks` entry's last sentence still named the materializer's idempotency key as `(schedule_id, scheduled_date)` — the index 0002 created and 0012 DROPPED, because a drag-rescheduled walk left its original day free to be materialized again and billed twice — while the same paragraph, two sentences earlier, explains `origin_date` as the key that stops exactly that. The entry contradicted itself and the audit read past it, which is the finding worth recording: a sentence that is wrong beside one that is right survives a pass that checks each column list against the schema, because neither sentence is a column. Read off `pg_indexes` after a reset, `uq_walks_schedule_origin (schedule_id, origin_date) where schedule_id is not null` is the only unique index on the table besides the primary key, and the `on conflict` target has matched it since 0012 (rebuilt identically in 0013 and 0026). The four other index claims in spec 01 (`clients.auth_user_id`, `walk_gps_points (walk_id, recorded_at)`, `credit_ledger (client_id, created_at desc)`, `push_subscriptions.endpoint`) were checked against the same catalogue rather than assumed, and hold. Round nineteen, one more, in round eight's guard: `set search_path to default` is `reset search_path` spelled the other way — measured, `current_setting` reads the same value after each — and `first_schema` read the keyword as a schema named `default` and refused a healthy migration, a gate red on a healthy tree three rounds after round sixteen's. The bare keyword is a reset in both session guards now, while a quoted `'default'` or `"default"` stays a schema of that name and is still refused; `standard_conforming_strings` had accepted the quoted spelling one round earlier, which PostgreSQL rejects, so that guard tightened to the same keyword rule. Round twenty, one more, the same keyword one clause over: `alter role … set search_path to default` and `alter role … reset search_path` REMOVE a role's stored setting — measured, `rolconfig` goes back to null — and the round-eight `alter database|role|user` branch refused any statement that so much as named the setting, a mention rule that is right for a value and wrong for a reset. The reset forms (`set … to default`, `reset <setting>`, `reset all`) pass for both settings now; a value, or `from current`, which copies a session state the generator cannot see, is still refused. Round twenty-one, one more, and it is the assumption underneath round eight's whole guard: the default path is `"$user", public`, and `$user` follows the CURRENT role, so `set role authenticated` before an unqualified `create type` lands the type in a schema named `authenticated` when one exists while the catalogue records it as public — measured, `pg_type` shows `mood` under that schema. Nothing read `set role` or `set session authorization`, both are GUCs `set_config` reaches, and `alter role … set role` is accepted and stored (measured too). Refused now on the same allow-list shape — `reset role`, `set role none`, `reset session authorization` and `set session authorization default` pass, any other switch is refused at top level and inside a body, and the ALTER branch reads the parameter a `set` names rather than grepping for a word, since `alter role` itself contains one — and the first version of that parser regressed round eleven, silently: a masked identifier ends in a quote and `\b` needs a word character beside it, so `alter role … set "search_path" = private` was no longer refused, which the existing proof caught and review did not. No migration here switches role, so the real tree is untouched. Nine rounds have now landed in this one guard and each was the same move — one more session state the generator's stated assumption rests on, made an explicit precondition instead of an implicit one — so the docstring names the whole set (search_path, standard_conforming_strings, role, session authorization) rather than leaving the next reader to discover it a round at a time. Round twenty-two, one more, in round twenty-one's own fix: `role` and `session_authorization` are GUCs as well as special syntax, so `set "role" = authenticated` and `set session_authorization = authenticated` switch the role through the generic form — measured — and the two new patterns matched only the bare `set role` and `set session authorization` spellings, while the two settings beside them had carried the quoted and `=`/`to` forms since round eleven. One shape for all four now, at top level and inside a body; the fix was modelled on the special syntax instead of on the sibling regex one line up, which is the per-site disease this log keeps recording. Round twenty-three, one more, the round-ten door inside a body: `perform set_config('role', 'authenticated', false)` in a DO block carries no `set role` phrase for the body's word rule to see, and the `set_config` reader ran only at top level, so a role switch executed at migration time was invisible and the unqualified create after it catalogued as public — measured, the switch persists past the block and the type lands in the role's schema. The other three settings were already caught in a body by their own names; `role` is the one word too common to ban from a body (`auth.role()`, a column called role), which is exactly why it needed the call-shaped rule the top level had since round ten. A body may not call `set_config` on a guarded name at all now, whatever the value, and a `set_config` whose name is not a plain literal is refused as unreadable — inside an EXECUTE'd literal and a `format()` template too, since the check runs on the clean text where those still read as calls. Round twenty-four, one more, and it is round ten's inversion applied incompletely: `set_config('ro' || 'le', 'authenticated', false)` begins with a literal that is not a guarded name and continues into an expression, and both the body rule from round twenty-three and — checked as its sibling — the top-level reader from round ten took the first literal for the whole name and read on; measured, PostgreSQL evaluates it to `role` and switches. The name must be the ENTIRE first argument now, one plain literal followed by the comma, in both places, and anything else is unreadable and refused. Round twenty-five, one more, the `$user` schema from the other side: `create schema authorization current_user` brings into existence the schema the default path already names first, so the next unqualified `create type` lands in it — measured, in `postgres` here and it would be `sb_deploy` in the deploy-role replay — with no role switch for any guard to see, and a rename to the role's name does the same. The deploy role is not knowable from a file, so the rule is structural rather than a list of role names: once a migration creates or renames a schema (other than `public` itself), every later enum statement — in that file and every file after it — must be qualified with `public.` or is refused with a sentence saying so, and a body may not create or rename a schema at all. No migration here does either, so the tree is untouched, and the precondition joins the docstring beside the four session states. Round twenty-six, one more, and it is round fifteen's lesson forgotten one round after it was used: `$` is legal inside an unquoted identifier and Python's `\b` treats it as a boundary, so `create schema public$deploy` — legal, measured — matched the `public` exemption and was waved through, a schema of exactly the shape a deploy role's name takes. The exemption reads the whole identifier now, `public` and `"public"` only. Round twenty-seven, one more, the same exemption one alphabet wider: PostgreSQL's lexer treats every non-ASCII byte as an identifier letter, so `create schema publicé` is legal — measured — and an ASCII-only lookahead exempted it. The lookahead refuses any identifier character now, non-ASCII included, and the other patterns were checked for the same shape: each fails in the safe direction, refusing a name it cannot read rather than exempting it. Round twenty-eight, one more, a body with no quotes around it: PostgreSQL 14 added SQL-standard routine bodies — `create procedure … language sql begin atomic create type ghost as enum ('x'); end` — and the body's statements sit at top level for every scan here, its own semicolons even splitting it into statements, so the generator catalogued `ghost` as created by the migration. The reviewer's scenario could not be reproduced: PostgreSQL 16, the server this container has, refuses `create`, `alter` and `drop type` inside an unquoted body at definition time (`not yet supported in unquoted SQL function body`, measured for all three), and whether 17 — the configured target, and a stated limit of this audit — lifts that could not be measured from here. Refused by name regardless, since a body the generator cannot read is refused whether or not the server would run it; `set_config` inside such a body was already caught by the top-level reader, which sees the unquoted text. No migration here uses the form. Round twenty-nine, one more, round twenty-seven's alphabet applied to the one place it cuts the other way: a dollar-quote tag follows identifier rules, so `$é$` opens a body — measured, the DO block runs and its concatenated EXECUTE creates the type — and the tag pattern knew ASCII only, which made the body invisible AS a body: its text was scanned as top-level statements, the EXECUTE it could not read was never asked about, and the gate passed while recording nothing. Everywhere else in this file an unreadable name fails safe by being refused; here it failed open by not being a region at all. Tags accept every identifier character now. Round thirty, two more, both a gate red on a healthy tree — the worst shape, and the direction four rounds of widening were always going to produce somewhere: `€` is an identifier character to PostgreSQL's lexer and not to Python's `isalnum()`, so `first€$tag$` opened a region where round fifteen's boundary rule should have seen an alias, and the enum between two such aliases was refused; and `app.set_config('role', …)` is a different function from the built-in, which the reader matched after any dot — both measured. The predecessor rule uses the lexer's definition now (any non-ASCII character continues an identifier), and only an unqualified, `pg_catalog.` or `public.` `set_config` is treated as the built-in, at top level and inside a body. Round thirty-one, one more, and the third time one boundary has bitten this file: Python's `\b` stops at `$` and at any non-ASCII character that is not a letter, while PostgreSQL's lexer continues an identifier through both — so `custom$set_config('role', …)`, a call of a DIFFERENT function that switches nothing (measured), was read as the built-in and the gate refused a healthy migration, at top level and inside a body alike. Rounds twenty-six and twenty-seven had fixed the same boundary at one site each; this time it is one definition, the lexer's rule, used by every keyword and name scan in the file, which also removed false refusals nobody had named — a PL/pgSQL variable called `v$search_path` or `search_path$1`, an alias called `my$execute`, all legal (measured) — and one miss: `alter role my$set set search_path = private` stores the path on the role (measured) and the scan read its target as `set`, off the `$`, and passed it. Stated rather than chased: the body scan reads clean text on purpose, so an EXECUTE'd `set_config(''role'', …)` is still seen, and the price is that a quoted function name ending in ` set_config` is refused. The proof set caught the fix over-reaching once before it shipped: a nested dollar string's closing tag is not an identifier character either, so `$q$create type$q$` inside a body had stopped being seen — the tags are blanked in the body's clean text now, the content kept at the same offsets. Round thirty-two, two more, both a gate red on a healthy tree. `create function f(x text default 'create type') … as $$ select x $$` is legal — measured, the default comes back as data — and EVERY literal in a DO or CREATE FUNCTION statement was checked as the routine's body, so a parameter default, a SET clause value or a C function's link symbol carrying a guarded phrase was refused as procedural code; only the literal in body position is a body now — the one after AS, or a DO block's code after its optional LANGUAGE clause — and the rest are values, masked as any value is (a dollar-quoted default carrying DDL text is still refused, as a dollar-quoted VALUE, the round-five rule). And a custom setting whose first component is a guarded name — `alter role deploy set search_path.custom = 'value'`, legal and stored as its own entry (measured) — was read as `search_path`, because the reader stopped at the dot; the whole dotted name is read now, quoted components resolved and lower-cased (PostgreSQL folds a setting's name — measured, `set "SEARCH_PATH"` moves the path). The review named the ALTER reader; the four top-level SET readers had the same stop and were checked as its siblings, where `set search_path.custom = 'x'` was refused as a moved path and `set role.custom = 'r'` as a role switch — measured, both leave the real setting untouched. The proof set caught the fix twice before it shipped: an escape string's `E` sits between AS and the quote, so `as E'…'` had dropped out of body position; and the SET readers spelt a quoted name as an optional quote pair, so the engine backtracked to the unclosed `"search_path` when the dot rule refused the closed one and `set "search_path".custom` was still refused — the quoted spelling is one alternative now. Round thirty-three, one more, and a MISS rather than a refusal: PostgreSQL parses a dynamic command as SQL, comments and all, so `execute 'create /*gap*/ type public.ghost as enum (''x'')'` in a DO block creates the enum (measured) while the body's clean text — which keeps comments INSIDE literals, a literal being data — showed no contiguous `create type`, and the command was accepted as a readable literal: CI would have blessed a catalogue missing a live enum. An EXECUTE'd command is code, not data, so every literal in the command expression (the command, a `format()` template, its arguments — `%s` and `%L` are not told apart) is read as a migration fragment of its own now: the top-level scanner runs over it, a nested DO or function body inside it is checked as a body, a dollar-quoted value inside it is scanned, and its own clean text is held to the body rules — recursively, since a fragment can carry a fragment (`execute 'do $q$ … execute ''create -- gap⏎type …'' … $q$'` creates the enum too, measured). The sibling was the same trick around a call: `set_config/*c*/(''role'', …)` inside an EXECUTE'd literal switched the role (measured) past a scan that wanted the parenthesis adjacent, and the fragment reading closes it in the same motion. Round thirty-four, one more, a miss: `alter schema public rename to old_public; create schema public;` moves every catalogued enum into `old_public` (measured — `pg_type` shows them there and none left in `public`) and the generator only armed round twenty-five's qualification guard, so with no later enum statement CI would have blessed a catalogue of types that are no longer in `public`. Renaming `public` is refused now, and so is dropping it — `drop schema public cascade` takes every enum with it (measured), the sibling the review did not name — at top level and inside a body alike, while any other schema may still be created, renamed or dropped, since only `public` enums are catalogued. Round thirty-five, one more, round thirty-four's reader one alphabet short: PostgreSQL also spells an identifier as `U&"…"` with code-point escapes, so `drop schema U&"public" cascade` and `alter schema U&"public" rename to old_public` — and `U&"\0070ublic"`, the same word by code point, with or without a UESCAPE clause — reach the same schema (measured: the enums go, or move), and a reader that knew only bare and quoted names called each of them another schema, passing the drop and merely shadowing the rename. Refused rather than decoded, on the stopping rule: a rename or drop whose schema name the generator cannot read is refused by name, whatever the schema — decoding code-point escapes is more parser than fifteen enums earn — while a `create schema` it cannot read already falls to the conservative branch (a shadow), which needs no decoding to be safe. Round thirty-six, two more, both a gate red on a healthy tree and both in round thirty-five's reader, which read schema names off the INTACT statement: `drop schema "tenant,archive" cascade` was split at the comma inside the quoted name and `alter schema "tenant rename archive" rename to archived` was cut at the word inside it — legal names both (measured) — so each was refused as unreadable. The names are read off the skeleton now, where a quoted identifier is one masked token that can hold no comma, no space and no keyword, and resolved from the intact text at the same spans (the `alter_set_target` technique from round eleven, which this reader should have used from the start), with a quoted name exact and a bare one folded. Round thirty-seven, one more, a gate red on a healthy tree in the same reader: the optional `cascade`/`restrict` at the end of a `drop schema` was matched with nothing between it and the name, so `drop schema publiccascade` — a legal name (measured) — lost its tail to the keyword and was refused as a drop of `public`. The keyword needs whitespace before it and an identifier boundary after it now, the rule round thirty-one settled for every keyword in the file and this regex had skipped. Round thirty-eight, one more, a miss: a routine body may be written as a Unicode-escape string — `DO U&'begin execute ''create type public.ghost …''; end'` creates the enum (measured) — and the reader knew only the `E` prefix, so the `U&` literal in body position was masked as data and the migration passed. The unnamed sibling was worse and had been accepted since round eight: an `E'…'` body was checked as read, but PostgreSQL decodes its escapes first, so `\x63reate type` inside one is `create type` to the server (measured, the enum appears) and nothing to the mention rule. Both escape-string forms are refused in body position now, by name, rather than decoded — no migration in the tree writes a body either way, and a body the generator cannot read is refused whether or not the server would run it, the round twenty-eight rule. Round thirty-nine, one more, a miss, and the round thirty-five alphabet applied to the session guards: a setting's name may also be spelled `U&"…"`, so `set U&"standard_conforming_strings" = off` turns the lexer's escape handling on under the gate (measured — the label `'line\nfeed'` stores a real newline, the round seventeen defect exactly) and `alter role … set U&"search_path" = private` stores the path (measured), while every SET reader, top level and ALTER alike, knew the bare and quoted spellings only and passed both. A `set` or `alter … set` whose name is spelled that way is refused by name now, for every setting, since the reader cannot tell which one it is; the body rule was already safe (it refuses the word inside the spelling), and `set_config` with a `U&'…'` name was already refused as unreadable. Round forty, two more, and the class the last five rounds were circling, named at last: a `U&"…"` identifier can spell ANY name by code point, so `set U&"\0073earch_path" = auth, public` inside a body and `U&"\0073et_config"('search_path', …)` at top level both move the path (measured — the next unqualified `create type` lands in `auth`) past readers that look for the words `search_path` and `set_config`, which neither spelling contains. Rounds thirty-five and thirty-nine had refused the spelling at two sites — schema names, then setting names — and this is the third and fourth, which is the per-site disease this log keeps recording. One rule now, in the lexer, where every site passes: an identifier spelled `U&"…"` anywhere the generator reads code — a top-level statement, a body, an EXECUTE'd command — is refused by name, since it cannot be decoded and can spell `public`, a guarded setting, `set_config` or a schema; inside a string or a dollar-quoted value it is text and stays inert. That subsumes both earlier special cases, which are deleted, and flips the three spellings the earlier rounds had let through — `create schema U&"aux"`, and `reset U&"search_path"` in both its forms — to refused, stated rather than hidden: no migration in the tree writes one. Two of my own proofs went red on the way for a reason worth keeping: a body form I had called a miss was already refused by round eight's mention rule, because the literal `'search_path'` inside it IS a mention — so the shipped generator passed only the variant that spells the argument by code point too, and that is the one the proof now names. Round forty-one, one more, a gate red on a healthy tree: a quoted schema qualifier may carry a doubled quote — `"evil""pg_catalog".set_config('role', …)` calls a function in the schema `evil"pg_catalog` and switches nothing (measured, at top level and inside a body) — and the qualifier reader walked back to the PREVIOUS quote, which is the second half of the pair, so it read `pg_catalog`, called the custom function the built-in and refused the migration; one reader serves both sites, so both were wrong. It walks back through doubled pairs to the real opening quote now. Kept on the intact text rather than moved to the skeleton (round thirty-six's technique), because the body scan reads INSIDE an EXECUTE'd literal on purpose, where the skeleton is all mask — so this is the one name reader in the file that must parse a quoted identifier itself, and it was written after the round that settled how the others read one. Round forty-two, one more, a miss, and the boundary a fourth time: the `E'…'` prefix test asked whether the character before the `E` was alphanumeric or `_`, which is not the lexer's rule, so after `create domain foo$e as text` the typed literal `foo$e'x\'` — an identifier and then an ORDINARY string, to PostgreSQL — was read as an escape string whose `\'` carried it through the `;` and swallowed the `create type public.ghost` that followed (measured: the enum exists, and the catalogue was blessed without it, the existing fifteen keeping the empty-catalogue guard quiet); `foo€e` the same. The `U&` prefix on the very next line already asked `IDENT_CHAR`, and round thirty-one had made that the one boundary for every scan in the file — this test was the one it missed, which is the per-site disease surviving inside the fix written to end it. The lexer's rule for the `E` prefix too, now, and nothing else in the file asks `isalnum()`. Round forty-three, one more, a miss: `set` is a non-reserved word, so a role or a database may be named `set` unquoted — measured, `create role set` and `alter role set set search_path = private` both run and `rolconfig` stores the path — and the ALTER reader SEARCHED its statement for the first `set` followed by a name, which was the role's name followed by the keyword, read as a setting called `set`; the real `search_path` clause was never looked at and the statement passed, `alter database set …` and the `in database` form the same. It matters here because `db push` applies each file on a fresh connection, so a role default stored by one file governs the next. The reader parses the statement's HEAD now — the object kind, its target, an optional `in database` clause — and reads the `set` or `reset` clause only after it, refusing a head it cannot parse; a role named `reset` was already refused, by the accident of first-match rather than by design, and is by design now. Forty-three rounds, fifty-nine findings, fifty-seven of them in one file, every code finding proven red against the previous head before its fix. Limits of the audit, stated: the clone is shallow at 50 commits, so "wrong when written" for older entries rests on migration text and comments rather than blame; no PostgREST was run; the local server was Postgres 16 against a configured 17. 718 vitest, 419 deno, 86 smoke DO blocks, 20 validate gates, 15 enum types catalogued.
- money(create-plan): PR C of the spec-drift audit, and the smallest money-path fix in this log — one comparison operator — with the seam that made it provable. `create-plan` refused an overage rate `< 0` while 0026's `plans_overage_rate_positive` is `> 0` (the owner's decision: an overage rate of 0 is invalid, because a walk credits cannot cover is charged in full at that rate, and "in full at nothing" is a free walk, not a price). So a zero-rate body passed validation, minted the Stripe Price on the operator's connected account, and THEN died on the CHECK at insert: the operator saw "plan could not be saved" with no rule named, and every attempt left another orphaned Price. The Settings button enabled on any non-empty text, so "0" was one click away. The check is `<= 0` now, with a sentence naming the rule, and — the part that needed a seam — it lands BEFORE the first Stripe call, asserted on a deps recorder: `create-plan` was one of the three `index.ts`-only functions the backlog listed as untestable (importing it binds a port), so its decision logic moved to `handler.ts` behind injected `createPrice`/`insertPlan`, the house pattern from operator-billing, with `index.ts` reduced to wiring. Ten tests, and the headline one was proven red against the shipped predicate before the operator was changed (`expected promise to reject` — the zero-rate body reached the recorder). The frontend mirrors the rule rather than restating it: `lib/plan-form.ts` computes the cents the submit would send and gates the button on them, so "0" and "0.004" never leave the browser, and `Settings.plans.test.tsx` renders the real section and pins that the button consults it (red against the shipped `!overage` expression: `expect(element).toBeDisabled()`), because a guard correct in `lib/` and ignored by the screen is the shape this log keeps recording. Spec 04 states the ordering and the rule. Deliberately NOT done: any change to `plans` or the CHECK (the database was right; the caller was not), and no archive of the orphan Price on a failed insert (an orphan is inert and free, and archiving on a transient error strands a retry into a second one — the existing decision, kept). Backlog item 1 shrinks to `billing-portal` and `connect-onboarding`. 725 vitest (7 new), 429 deno (10 new).
- qa(enum-proofs): the proof set behind the forty-three review rounds on PR #88 becomes a committed gate, 10f. Those rounds proved every fix red against the previous head, and the harness that did it lived in this session's scratchpad — 591 scratch directories and forty-three derived proof scripts — so on the day the container was reclaimed the generator would have been a thousand-line parser with no test in the repository, and gate 10e, which diffs a catalogue that is byte-identical by construction, could not have told anyone that one of those rules had been reopened. A rule written down and connected to nothing, the defect this file records most often, produced by the very work that recorded it forty-three times. `scripts/gen-enum-catalog-proofs.py` carries 582 probes: each writes a migration into a named scratch copy of the real set and asserts the generator either renders the expected catalogue or refuses with the sentence the rule names — never a bare exit, which any crash satisfies. Only the fixed-behaviour half of each proof carries; the "shipped generator passed this" half compared against the previous commit's body and cannot exist without it, so those stay in the `docs(spec-drift)` entry as prose. Mechanics worth stating: `strip_sql` is memoised on its arguments for the run (a pure function of its text), so the 51 real migrations are parsed once rather than 582 times — 26 s instead of ten minutes — and the control (the real tree renders the committed block) runs BEFORE the cache is installed and again after it, so the cache is itself proven to change nothing; sixteen probes whose scratch copies had been created by earlier rounds' scripts and were only referred to by name were recovered from those copies, with their real files asserted byte-identical to the tree before the probe was trusted. Proven as a gate in both directions: reinstating round forty-two's `isalnum()` boundary turns exactly the two `foo$e` and `foo€e` probes red, reinstating round forty-three's searched ALTER clause turns its seven red, and the real tree is green — each sabotage applied to a snapshot-backed file, the diff asserted non-empty, and the restore asserted byte-identical before the next run was believed. In `ci.yml`, `SKILL.md` and `validate.sh` together, and `session-notes.md` says how to add a probe. Found on the way: the local Postgres had stopped over two idle days, so the first validate run went red on every database gate at once — read off the connection-refused line rather than diagnosed, and `scripts/db-start.sh` per `local-stack.md` brought it back. 22 gates green (21 labels; gate 8 runs twice), 582 probes, 725 vitest unchanged. Codex on the PR then found the probe versions hard-coded at `0052`/`0053`, and the consequence was worse than a stale number: a real `0053` landing later sorts AFTER the probe, so a probe that creates a schema and expects no enum DDL to follow meets the real migration's unqualified `create type` instead — and on a copy of the tree carrying a `0053_future_enum.sql` the shipped test did not go red on the thirteen probes that displaces, it DIED, on a `KeyError` inside the first of them that took the other 580 with it: a broken harness reading as a broken rule, in the suite whose one job is to name which rule broke. The versions derive from the highest real migration now, and every check runs through a helper that reports a raising probe as a named FAIL and moves on. Measured on that copy: 582 of 582 with the derived versions, 569 of 582 with the hard-coded ones reinstated, the `KeyError` named beside its probe and the suite alive to its last line. Rewriting 582 lines to route them through that helper found two defects of my own before they shipped, both the class this log keeps recording: the one line in the file that carried a `d = scratch("r1", …)` assignment beside its check lost the assignment (the real tree went 581 of 582 and named it), and the header-count probe hard-coded `16 enum types` — Codex's finding one line over, red on any healthy tree that adds an enum — so it reads the count off the committed header now. Both generator sabotages re-run against the rewritten harness: two red, seven red, real tree 582 of 582. Round two then found the harness passing for the wrong reason in its own absence proofs: `run()` turned a refusal into an EMPTY result, so `"payment_status" not in run(d)[2]` — the shape of six proofs, among them `cascade drop removes` and `drop type with cascade still read` — was satisfied by a generator that wrongly REFUSED a valid drop, the parser-that-sees-nothing defect reproduced inside the gate written to catch it. Measured on a copy whose strict DROP regex had lost `cascade|restrict`: the shipped test reported 582 of 582 with the three cascade-drop proofs green, the fixed one 579 of 582 with the three named and each carrying the generator's own sentence. A refusal RAISES now (`Refused`), so every proof that renders fails by construction when the render is refused — one rule in `run()` rather than a precondition added at six sites — and `check()` reports it as a named FAIL. Real tree 582 of 582. Round three found one proof that could not fail at all: the `xu&'…'` boundary check was written as `unchanged(probe) or run(<clean control>)[0] == committed`, and the control is a `select 1;` that renders the committed block by construction — so a generator that rendered a WRONG catalogue for that probe passed it. Measured on a copy whose generator appends a value to `payment_status` whenever a migration mentions `xu&`: the shipped test reported that proof `ok` at 581 of 582 (the sibling `xu&"col"` proof, which carries no fallback, went red on its own), the fixed one 580 of 582 with both named. The fallback is gone; it was the only one — the other twenty ` or ` in the file sit inside the needle `renames or drops`. Real tree 582 of 582. Round four found the worst shape available, and it was structural: every probe was appended to a copy of the FULL real migration set, so the probes inherited the baseline's STATE — and the generator's shadow guard (round twenty-five of #88) is state, armed for every later unqualified enum statement by any real migration that creates or renames a non-public schema. Reproduced exactly as the reviewer described: one `0052_future_schema.sql` containing only `create schema aux_future;` left the generator green on the real tree and turned 64 proofs red, 518 of 582 — a gate red on a healthy tree, blocking whoever ships the schema. Neither of the reviewer's remedies alone was right: qualifying the probes' DDL leaves the probes about unqualified behaviour exactly as fragile, and a precondition pin is still a red on healthy work. The probes now run on an ENUM-ONLY copy of the real migrations — every `create/alter/drop type` statement verbatim under its own file name, found by the generator's own loose scanners on the skeleton and copied from the clean text at the same spans, so creation order and the versions the catalogue prints are the real ones, and nothing else survives: no schema DDL, no session settings, no bodies, so no state. A control asserts that baseline renders the committed block BEFORE any probe runs, which is where a future migration whose enum DDL the filter did not carry across fails, by name, rather than as sixty unexplained reds below it — proven: with `alter type` dropped from the scanners the control is the first red at 379 of 583. Measured after: the real tree 583 of 583, the future-schema copy 583 of 583, the future-enum copy 583 of 583, and both generator sabotages still exactly two red and exactly seven red on the enum-only baseline, so the probes lost none of their teeth. Round five, a P3, and the class this log records most: the control added in round four raised the suite to 583 and `session-notes.md` still said 582 — a count written into prose, connected to nothing. The prose names no count now and says why; the footer of the run is the count. Round six, two P2s of the same class — a proof that goes red on a HEALTHY generator change — and a sibling the review did not name. The round-thirty-one guard against Python's `\b` (which stops at `$` and non-ASCII letters where PostgreSQL's lexer continues an identifier) was a ban over every non-comment source line, so a `\b` in an error message or a docstring would have failed gate 10f on a correct change; it is scoped now to the PATTERNS the generator compiles, walked out of the source with `ast` through concatenation, f-strings and the module-level building blocks (`IDENT_CHARS`, `LIT`), with a precondition that the scan saw patterns at all. A second proof called the private `alter_set_target` directly to assert its return value, so a rename or an inline would have raised `AttributeError` into a named FAIL while the behavioural probes beside it — `alter role deploy set search_path.custom = 'value'` renders, `… set search_path = private` refuses — already pin the behaviour; the direct call is deleted. The sibling: round four's baseline builder read three private scanner names (`CREATE_ANY`, `ALTER_ANY`, `DROP_ANY`) at module level, so renaming one would not have gone red, it would have KILLED the suite at the baseline build — the round-one class, one round after its fix. The generator now exposes `enum_statement_spans(skel)`, which `collect()` itself iterates and the baseline builder reads, so the two cannot disagree about what an enum statement is, and the catalogue it renders is byte-identical. Proven on a copy, shipped harness against fixed: a `\b` added to an error message went 582 of 583 against 583 of 583; a `\b` added to a compiled pattern is red under both, by the scoped check; `alter_set_target` renamed went 582 of 583 (`raised AttributeError`) against 583 of 583; `CREATE_ANY` renamed died with a traceback and no footer against 583 of 583. Round seven, two P2s, both in round six's own fixes. `enum_statement_spans` promised position order and sorted TUPLES, so kind came first: a healthy future migration `drop type if exists mood; create type mood as enum ('x');` — a net create — was copied into the probe baseline as create-then-drop, and the control went red (379 of 583) while the generator stayed green; the key is the source offset now, with a probe that filters exactly that migration and expects `mood`. And the scoped `\b` scan still covered EVERY regex the generator compiles, including the Markdown-label `re.findall` and the block-replacement `re.sub`, which read no SQL — a healthy `\b` in either would have failed the gate. The rule lives in the generator now: every SQL-reading pattern is built by `sql_re`, which refuses `\b` at construction, so the proofs pin the factory (refuses, naming IDENT_START; compiles an ordinary pattern; and the scanners go through it, 46 of them) and nothing scans source text at all; a regex over Markdown or file text uses `re` directly and is outside the rule by construction. The harness also names a generator that cannot be loaded as a `FAIL:` sentence instead of a traceback. Proven on copies, shipped harness against fixed: the drop-then-create migration 379 of 583 against 585 of 585; a no-op `\b` in the Markdown regex 581 of 583 against 585 of 585; a `\b` in a SQL scanner refused at import with the rule's own sentence; the kind-first sort reinstated 584 of 585 naming the order probe; and the round-42 and round-43 generator sabotages still exactly two and seven red. Catalogue byte-identical. Round eight, one P2, and it was the round-seven fix's own floor: `sql_re` calls ≥ 40 said nothing about the scanners that did NOT go through it — the reviewer changed `IS_DEFAULT` to a bare `re.compile` carrying `\b` and all 585 proofs passed. A floor is a vacuous assertion without a precondition, which `session-notes.md` already says. The rule is exact now: every pattern in the generator is built by one of two factories, `sql_re` (reads SQL; refuses `\b`) or `text_re` (reads Markdown or file text; no rule but the declaration), the three inline `re.finditer`/`findall`/`sub` went through them too, and the proof walks the source with `ast` and refuses any `re.<op>` outside the factories' own two `re.compile` (escape, the flags and the type names allowed), naming the line — with a precondition that the walker sees those two, so a walker that sees nothing cannot pass. Proven on a copy, shipped harness against fixed: the reviewer's exact bypass 585 of 585 against 586 of 587 naming `(684, 'compile')`, a bare inline `re.finditer` on SQL the same, and a healthy `\b` inside a declared `text_re` pattern green at 587 of 587. Catalogue byte-identical. Round nine, one P2, the third round on this guard and the one where its boundary got written down: the walker watches attributes of the NAME `re`, so `from re import compile as compile_re` handed a pattern constructor to a name nothing watched — reproduced, 587 of 587 with a `\b` scanner back in the file. The regex module may enter the generator only as a bare `import re` now: no alias, no `from re import …`, no other regex engine, walked from the `Import`/`ImportFrom` nodes with its own eyesight precondition. Stated rather than chased, in the walker's docstring: dynamic construction — `getattr`, `importlib`, `eval` — is outside this guard, as SQL assembled to evade the generator is outside the generator's (round thirteen of #88); both catch the mistake, not the adversary. Proven on a copy, shipped against fixed: the reviewer's exact bypass 587 of 587 against 588 of 589 naming `(65, 'from re import compile')`, and `import re as r` with a scanner built through `r` the same, naming the alias. Real tree 589 of 589. Round ten, one P2, and it was the round-nine rule's own scope disagreeing with what it guards — the shape the #85 rounds settled on fixing rather than declining: the import guard compared module names for EXACT membership, so `from re._compiler import compile as compile_re` — a static import of the engine behind `re.compile`, by name — let the package in by a side door, and a `\b` scanner built through it passed 589 of 589. A package is not entered only at its top: `re`, its submodules and the other engines with theirs are one predicate now, `is_regex_module`, and the fix made the rule simpler. Proven on a copy, shipped against fixed: the reviewer's exact bypass 589 of 589 against 588 of 589 naming `(65, 'from re._compiler import compile')`, and `import re._compiler as rc` the same. The stated boundary stands: dynamic construction is outside this guard. Round eleven, one P2, a gate red on a healthy tree: the walker judged attribute NAMES against a whitelist, so `re.ASCII` on a pattern that still went through `text_re` was refused as an illegal operation — an enumeration of flags, connected to nothing. A pattern is built by a CALL, so only calls are judged now: `re.escape` and `re.purge` build none, `re.compile` is allowed inside a factory, every other call on `re` is refused by name, and a flag, a type name or an exception class is an attribute and is free. Proven on a copy, shipped against fixed: the reviewer's `re.ASCII` 588 of 589 naming `(171, 'ASCII')` against 590 of 590; a bare `re.finditer` on SQL and the round-eight bare `re.compile` still red under both, naming their lines. Found by the sabotage round rather than the green run before it: the edit had deleted `named_failure`, the helper every structural proof reports through, and the real tree stayed 589 of 589 because the helper runs only when a guard FIRES — so the two bypasses went red as `NameError`, a red for the wrong reason. Restored, and the reporting path is a proof on the real tree now, red against the helper deleted. Round twelve, one P2, and it was round eleven's own relaxation: judging only CALLS let `compile_re = re.compile` — a reference, not a call — hand the constructor to a name nothing watched, and a `\b` scanner built through it passed 590 of 590. The rule judges REFERENCES now, with the `re` module's own convention as the line between function and constant: a lowercase attribute is a function, and every function but `escape`, `purge` and the exception `error` builds or uses a pattern, so a reference to one outside a factory — called or rebound — is refused by name; a capitalised attribute is a flag, a type or an exception class and is free, except `Scanner`, which compiles. Proven on a copy, shipped against fixed: the reviewer's rebinding 590 of 590 against 589 of 590 naming `(684, 'compile')`, with `re.ASCII` on a factory-built pattern and `except re.error:` both still green. Round thirteen, one P2, and it is round twelve's rule one level up: judging references to `re.<function>` let `regex_module = re` — the whole MODULE rebound, not one function — hand every constructor to a name nothing watched, and a `\b` scanner built as `regex_module.compile` passed 590 of 590. The bare name `re` may appear only as the base of an attribute access now, `re.<attr>` (`bare_module_aliases`), which also refuses `re` passed as an argument, stored in a container or returned — `getattr(re, "compile")` falls to the same rule, since the module is handed over bare before the dynamic call is made — while every remaining door (`globals()`, `sys.modules`, `importlib`, `eval`) is dynamic and outside the guard by the boundary the walker's docstring states. Proven on a copy, shipped against fixed: the reviewer's alias 590 of 590 against 590 of 591 naming `(684, 'Load')`, the `getattr` form the same, and the healthy uses — `re.ASCII` on a factory-built pattern, `except re.error:`, a function-local `import re` — green at 591 of 591. That last one was red on the SHIPPED harness at 589 of 590, because the import walker's eyesight precondition demanded exactly one bare `import re` and a function-local one is a second, so the precondition is at-least-one now: a gate that would have gone red on a healthy generator, the worst shape available, caught by the healthy-uses proof rather than by review. Round fourteen, one P2, and it is the boundary between the two factories with nothing behind it: the guard proved every pattern goes through `sql_re` OR `text_re`, and `text_re` permits `\b` by design, so a SQL scanner moved to `text_re` — the reviewer's `IS_DEFAULT = text_re(r"^\s*default\b\s*$", re.I)` — passed 591 of 591 with the boundary back in a SQL scanner. A walker cannot tell a SQL scanner from a Markdown one by reading the pattern; only the person declaring it can, so the exemptions are pinned BY NAME, the `no-raw-hex.test.ts` shape: `TEXT_RE_EXEMPTIONS` records the two sites that exist (`BACKTICK_RUNS` at module level, the inline block replacement in `main`) as (scope, bound name), `text_re_sites` walks every use of the name — a bound call, an inline call, or a bare REFERENCE, since `_t = text_re` would hide every site built through it (rounds twelve and thirteen, one factory over) — and the proof fails in BOTH directions, because a stale exemption excuses a real site forever. A walker that sees nothing reports both exemptions missing, so blindness cannot pass. Proven on a copy, shipped against fixed: the reviewer's change 591 of 591 against 591 of 592 naming `(684, '<module>', 'IS_DEFAULT')`; the alias 591 of 591 against 591 of 592 naming `<reference>`; `BACKTICK_RUNS` renamed without the record 591 of 592 naming both the new site and the stale entry; and a genuine new non-SQL pattern refused until recorded, then green at 592 of 592 once the record carries it. Real tree 592 of 592. Stated rather than hidden: the record is an allowlist, and an allowlist is an enumeration — the acceptable kind, of the EXCEPTIONS rather than the rule, editable only in this file and in the same commit as the pattern it excuses. Round fifteen, one P2, in round fourteen's own record: identity is (scope, bound name), so a second inline `text_re` call inside `main` — a SQL scanner moved to the permissive factory — shares the identity of the recorded one, and membership in a SET let it in unrecorded: measured, 592 of 592 on the shipped harness. The record is a LIST compared as a multiset now — each entry is spent on exactly one site in source order, so a duplicate identity is an extra and an entry with no site is reported by name — and the arithmetic has its own proofs on a synthetic module, so a refactor back to set membership is red on the real tree rather than only under a sabotage nobody re-runs. Proven on a copy, shipped against fixed: the reviewer's second inline site 592 of 592 against 593 of 594 naming `(1286, 'main', '<inline>')`, and set membership reinstated in the fixed harness 593 of 594 with the duplicate-identity proof named. Real tree 594 of 594. Round sixteen, one P2, a gate red on a healthy change, and in the generator rather than the harness: `sql_re` refused the SUBSTRING `\b`, so `sql_re(r"\\b")` — an escaped backslash and then a literal `b`, which matches the two-character text `\b` and asserts no boundary at all — was refused with the boundary sentence; measured on the shipped factory, which is the reviewer's exact call. `has_word_boundary` walks escapes in pairs now, so only an odd backslash before a `b` is the token, and `\B`, the complement with the same defect, is refused alongside it — the sibling the review did not name. Inside a character class `\b` is a backspace and is refused all the same, stated as the safe direction rather than modelled. Three proofs, the plausible wrong fix among them: with the shipped substring test the fixed harness reports 595 of 597 (the acceptance proof red with the factory's own sentence, `\B` red), a not-preceded-by-a-backslash rule — which accepts `\\\b`, three backslashes, an escaped backslash and THEN a boundary — reports 596 of 597 naming exactly that proof, and the real tree 597 of 597 with the catalogue byte-identical. Round seventeen, one P2, DECLINED with its limit made explicit — the second decline of this PR family, on the stopping rule: the factory also refuses a `\b` written inside a `(?#…)` or a verbose-mode `#` comment, where the engine sees no boundary (measured: both compile and match `foo`, and `re._parser` reports no AT node in either). Modelling that means inferring verbose mode from flags, inline and scoped groups, and telling a `#` in a class or behind an escape from one that opens a comment — a lexer for Python's regex language inside a fifteen-enum catalogue generator, for a refusal whose remedy is to reword a comment; the exact alternative, asking `re._parser` for AT_BOUNDARY nodes, rests on a private module a Python upgrade may rename, which is a gate red on a healthy tree, the worst shape. What makes the decline honest rather than convenient is the direction the heuristic fails: every boundary token the engine sees is written as an odd backslash before `b` or `B`, so the text scan never fails open, and no pattern in the generator uses verbose mode or a regex comment at all. The limit is stated in the factory's docstring and PINNED — a proof asserts the comment case is refused by name, so reading comments becomes a deliberate act that edits that line — and shown live: a factory that starts skipping `(?#…)` turns exactly that proof red at 597 of 598. Real tree 598 of 598, catalogue byte-identical.
