- fix(send-lookups)+ci(discarded-errors): the send-lookups backlog item (deleted here), and the rule it needed. `send-notification`'s `getClient` destructured `{ data }` and dropped `error` — and supabase-js reports a failed query in the RESOLVED value, never by rejecting — so a statement timeout, a paused database or a reset connection read as "no such client", `sendClaimed` wrote the TERMINAL skip "client has no email address", and a `payment_failed` email was cancelled for good over a blip. `getOperator` had the identical shape in the same deps object (the email went out as "Your walker" — quieter, same defect). Both bind and throw `HttpError(500, "db_error", …, cause, { client_id | operator_id })` now, and `{ data: null, error: null }` still resolves null: a client who genuinely has no row is still the terminal skip, and that direction has its own tests because a fix that throws on everything passes the first ones. **The rule was written down and connected to nothing** — stated in this log by `feat(push)`'s fifth and sixteenth Codex rounds (`dropSubscription`, `noteFailure`) and by `money(send-once)`'s deferral of this very item, and enforced by nothing — so `app/scripts/discarded-errors.test.ts` is the connection: it PARSES every non-test `.ts` under `supabase/functions/` with the TypeScript compiler API (a grep cannot tell `const { data } = await` from `const { data, error } = await` across a line break, and cannot follow `let q = db.from(…)` to the statement that awaits it), finds every `.from(` / `.rpc(` / `.auth.` chain, and classifies the statement that consumes the envelope: OK (`error` bound AND referenced afterwards in the same function, or an envelope variable whose `.error` is read later, or a deferred builder followed to the await that binds it), PASSED_ON (returned whole to a caller — printed, not failed, the stated blind spot), DISCARDED (a bare `await`, `data` bound without `error`, or — Codex's first-round finding on the PR, fixed the same day — `error` bound and never read, which is the defect wearing the fix's clothes and passed the first version's mere-presence rule; the tree had no such site, and a planted one is now named through the scan), UNCLASSIFIED (`.then(`, `Promise.all([…])`, a rest element, an unrecognised receiver — a check that cannot classify says so rather than passing by seeing nothing). Run against the pre-fix tree it named FOUR sites, two of them outside the backlog's text: `connect-onboarding`'s post-race re-read discarded its error and fell through to minting an onboarding link for the account it had just created — an operator finishing Stripe's forms on the orphan — and is a 500 with the cause now; and `change-plan`'s `current_period_end` write was a bare `await`, which is fixed DIFFERENTLY and on purpose: it runs after Stripe has already moved the subscription and the intent is committed, so a throw there tells the operator the change failed when it did not (the retry itself would be safe — a same-target repeat replays the same Stripe idempotency key, 0018, and an applied intent is refused as `already_on_plan` before Stripe is touched — so the harm is the misreport) — the error is bound and logged with `safeCause`, never thrown, and the webhook's checked write of the same column is the source of truth. Spec 04 states the three accepted shapes and what the gate proves — that the error is LOOKED AT (`if (error) return null` passes it), so what is DONE with it is pinned per site by `send_deps_test.ts`, which drives the real `makeSendDeps` against a scripted PostgREST double and makes the `deps.ts` header comment true (it had named that file since PR #86 and no such file existed — and its attribution of the outcome-write fence was wrong too, corrected a round later). Also corrected while there: spec 04 said `change-plan` answers `{ new_balance, plan }` where the code has returned `{ pending, new_balance, plan, intent_id }` since 0015 — a spec disagreeing with shipped behaviour is a bug in the spec — and an orphaned doc comment at the end of `send-notification/index.ts`. Preconditions, because an assertion with a floor is vacuous: the scan must reach every directory `repo-functions.sh` names plus `_lib`, and must classify the known-good deferred builder in `deps.ts` as OK, so a classifier that sees nothing fails by name rather than reporting a clean tree. Receivers are the load-bearing rule and are refused loudly rather than skipped quietly: a lowercase identifier or `adminClient()`/`createClient(…)` is a client, a CAPITALISED one is a global (`Uint8Array.from` in `_lib/crypto.ts`), anything else is UNCLASSIFIED. Built in an isolated worktree by an agent instructed to prove it before handing it over, and the proof order matters: the gate went red against HEAD FIRST, naming exactly `change-plan/index.ts:169`, `connect-onboarding/index.ts:96`, `send-notification/deps.ts:94` and `:125` and nothing else (101 sites classified: 96 OK, 4 DISCARDED, 1 PASSED_ON — matching a multiline receiver census of the tree exactly), and its first draft named two MORE, which were the classifier's own defect rather than the tree's: `webpush.ts` and `push_deps.ts` read a field called `auth` — the push encryption secret, a column on `push_subscriptions` — and a rule keyed on the word matched a bare property read; every real GoTrue call in the tree continues into a member (`.auth.admin.…`, `.auth.getUser()`), so the rule is `.auth` followed by a member access, both leaf shapes are pinned as non-queries, and the residual (`const a = db.auth` handed off whole) is stated in the header. Nine more sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: four against the deno tests (`getClient` and `getOperator` each reverted to the shipped `{ data }`, an over-correction that throws on absence, and the cause dropped from the throw), four against the gate (the `connect-onboarding` re-read and the `change-plan` write each reverted to their shipped text and named by line, `getOperator` reverted and named, the capitalised-receiver rule removed), and an empty `FUNCTIONS_DIR`, where the main assertion goes green on nothing and both preconditions fail by name. One sabotage taught the honest scope of another: with the receiver rule removed, `Uint8Array.from` in `_lib/crypto.ts` surfaces as PASSED_ON (it is `return Uint8Array.from(…)`) — printed, not red — so the detector that makes that rule load-bearing is the receiver FIXTURE, which is red, and not the tree scan, which is not. Residuals, stated: `getOperator`'s throw is a behaviour change (a retry instead of one wrongly-branded email — a `db_error` on `operators` beside a working `clients` read is not a real state, and the sibling rule is worth more); `connect-onboarding` has no behavioural test until its seam lands in the next PR, so its only proof here is the gate; `change-plan` has no seam and no test, so its proof is the gate and one log line; and PASSED_ON envelopes (`unsubscribe` hands its whole result to `handler.ts`) are not followed across functions. **Then an adversarial pass over the diff, and the honest account of it**: a 24-agent workflow (five lenses, two refuters per finding) hit the session's rate limit after its first two lenses had reported, so the nine findings they produced were verified by fixture directly rather than by the refuters that never ran — each is a scratch file the gate was pointed at, red before the fix and green after, which is the same evidence a refuter would have gathered. Seven were the gate passing for the wrong reason, and the class is the one this entry's own headline names: a rule that sees less than it claims. An envelope variable OVERWRITTEN by a second query before its `.error` was read passed for the first query (`let r = await a; r = await b; if (r.error) …`); a same-named variable in a nested callback (`others.forEach((r) => { if (r.error) … })`) satisfied the outer envelope, because use-search was by name across scopes; an arrow body that is a call's ARGUMENT (`Promise.all(ids.map((id) => db.from(…)…))` — the array nothing reads) was printed as PASSED_ON while the array-literal spelling of the same discard failed, so `.map` was the quiet route round the rule; an envelope handed to ANY call (`console.log(r)` beside `return r.data`) was PASSED_ON, so a debug print turned a discard into a pass; `db["from"](…)` was invisible outright; and `.auth.<member>` fired on any lowercase receiver, so `sub.auth.length` — a healthy read of the push encryption secret, which is a FIELD named `auth` — would have turned the gate red on a correct change, the worst shape available. That last one is fixed by asking how the receiver is DECLARED (a factory call, a variable initialised from one, a parameter typed as one) rather than by the word, and a receiver with no visible declaration is refused rather than skipped. The seventh was the spec claiming the gate proves the error is "looked at" while it only proved BOUND — Codex's finding, fixed the same morning. The two edge findings were mine too: the drain's per-row `catch { failed += 1 }` swallowed the very `HttpError` this PR taught the lookups to throw — on the ONLY path where a persistent failure recurs, with the single-row path's log line living in `handleRequest`'s catch, which a per-row throw inside the drain never reaches — so a paused database left nothing on the row and nothing in the log until the row aged out; and the change-plan log line used `msg` at top level where every thrown failure is recorded under `message` and `context.client_id`, so a search keyed on the standard fields could not find it. `logHandledError` is the one non-throwing shape now (`fn`, `message`, `cause` and `context` under `logServerError`'s names, `handled: true`, no `request_id` because a handler cannot reach it and no `status`/`code` because nothing was thrown — stated rather than faked), used by the change-plan cache write, the claim-release failure and both drain catches. Nine more sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores, one per rule — and two of them first printed nothing at all because the sabotage text did not parse, which is a red for the wrong reason; re-run as a plain deletion, both name their test. **Codex's second round then found the scope model half-built, three ways, all real**: the nested-scope skip covered FUNCTION scopes only, so a block-local `const r` (or a `for (const r of …)` loop variable, or a `catch (r)`) shadowing the outer envelope still had its `.error` read counted for the outer one; a destructured `error` that was REASSIGNED before its read (`let { data, error } = …; error = null; if (error) …`) passed the new bound-and-read rule, because the cutoff at the next write had been applied to whole-envelope variables and not to the local `bindsError` returns; and `r.error = null` — a WRITE to the property — satisfied the `.error`-read predicate, since it never asked which side of an assignment the access sat on. One lexical-scope model replaces the function-scope one: the search starts from the scope that DECLARES the variable (so a query declared inside an `if` block and read there stays OK — the first draft searched from the function and skipped its own block), skips any nested scope that redeclares the name, treats `var` as function-scoped, and closes the window at the next assignment to the variable OR to its `.error` (any assignment operator, `delete` included); a one-level alias (`const res = r`) is followed as the same envelope, which a healthy fixture demanded before the rule existed. Four more sabotages red-first behind verified diffs and restores — function-scopes-only, the destructured cutoff dropped, a write counted as a read, aliases not followed — and the healthy shapes (declared in a block, read in a non-shadowing block, a `var` alias, a differently-named catch) pinned green so the model cannot quietly become a gate red on ordinary code. **Round three found two holes in that fix, both real**: `for (var r of rows)` is not a nested declaration but a WRITE to the function's `r` on every iteration (the loop rule counted any loop variable as the loop's own, so the shadow skip hid the overwrite and the query passed on a read of the loop's row), and an alias started a FRESH `.error` window, so `const copy = r; r.error = null; if (copy.error) …` — one object, two names — passed on a read that observed the replacement. `var` is function-scoped everywhere now: a `var` loop variable, a `var` re-declaration in a nested block and a bare loop target (`for (r of rows)`) are all writes that close the window, and the envelope GROUP — the variable and every alias reached from it, transitively — shares one `.error` write window while each member keeps its own overwrite cutoff. Two more sabotages red-first (the loop rule reverted, the window per alias). **Round four found three more of the same class — an `.auth` receiver resolved to the FIRST same-named declaration in the function rather than the lexical one, `r["error"] = null` invisible to a property-access-only reader, and `({ r } = other)` not counted as a write — and four rounds of one class is the point at which the class gets fixed rather than the instance**: the classifier now resolves every identifier through the TypeScript checker's symbol (`ts.createProgram` over the scanned files with `noResolve`, which is all a per-file scan needs), so a shadow in any scope is a different binding by construction and the hand-written scope model — `isScopeNode`, `declaresHere`, `declaringScope`, `varDeclaredIn`, the nested-scope skips — is deleted rather than extended; member access is read one way for queries and `.error` alike (`memberAccess`, both spellings); assignment targets are walked through object and array patterns; and the `.auth` receiver is judged by the declaration its symbol reaches — a factory call, a variable initialised from one at declaration or by a later assignment, a parameter or variable typed as one, one type alias deep — with an import, a class member or a destructured parameter refused loudly. The review workflow's own confirmed gate findings landed in the same rewrite: `(await q).error`, `((await q) as T)` and `(await q)!` are read through the wrapper rather than reported as an unknown consumer, and a chain ending in `.throwOnError()` was blessed as OK — reversed one round later, below. Six sabotages red-first behind verified diffs and restores — identity by name instead of symbol (which is the whole class, and turns two shadow fixtures red at once), the bracketed `.error` reader, pattern targets, the `.throwOnError()` rule, the transparent-wrapper rule — and Codex's lexical case proven against the SHIPPED gate rather than a sabotage: the 0b10946 file, run from `git show` against a planted tree, passes it silently, and the rewrite names `probe/index.ts:5`. No `ci.yml`/`SKILL.md`/`validate.sh` change: the gate is a `scripts/**/*.test.ts` file in the node vitest project and runs under gate 3, and `session-notes.md` now says that a scan OVER edge code lives in vitest while deno tests DRIVE it. **Codex's fifth round and the review workflow's three remaining lenses (gate robustness, deno-test fidelity, docs accuracy — 38 agents, two refuters per finding) landed together, and two of the workflow's four P2s were defects in the round-four rewrite: a fix that sees less than it claims, the class this file keeps recording.** The `.auth` receiver rule answered "value" for anything that was not visibly a client, so an untyped alias of a real client (`const authDb = db` — Codex's case), `const db = deps.db`, `const db = makeClient()`, an untyped parameter and `db: any` all produced no site and no red; measured on the shipped gate, the alias case is `(no sites)`. "Value" needs POSITIVE evidence now — a type annotation that names something (`any`, `unknown`, `{}` and `Deps["db"]` say nothing), a literal, or an alias of one — a client is followed through aliases and casts transitively, and on a receiver that is neither, a `.auth.<member>` chain that reaches a CALL is refused by name, since a GoTrue call is always a call, while a bare read (`payload.auth?.token`) stays a field read on any receiver. The `.error`-read-directly rule blessed a read that reads nothing: `const e = (await q).error; return data;` — the round-one discard with more parentheses — and a bare `(await q).error;` statement were both OK; the bound local goes through the same read-after rule a destructured `error` does now, and the no-op statement is DISCARDED. And `.throwOnError()` went both ways: Codex found the bare REFERENCE (`.delete().throwOnError`, never invoked, so the delete never runs) counted as a throwing chain, and the workflow's second refuter showed that blessing the invoked form at all was wrong — postgrest-js then rejects with a raw `PostgrestError` that nothing decides, which lands in `handleRequest`'s catch as an unhandled error with no context: the H14 shape, one step around the CI check on `HttpError(5xx, …)` arity, and none of spec 04's three shapes. So the CALL, not the name, makes a chain link (a member referenced and never called is refused: nothing runs), an invoked `.throwOnError()` is refused with a reason naming the accepted shape, and `_lib/admin.ts` records why the auth-only `throwOnError` client option must not be set. The namespace hop (`.auth.admin.createUser(…)`) was the first draft of that rule going red on the healthy tree, caught by the tree test before it shipped. Deno: both drain-log tests threw a bare `Error`, so the `HttpError` context spread on the drain line — the `client_id` a person searches by — was pinned by nothing (the verifier removed both spreads and all 442 stayed green); the fixtures throw the shape the real wiring throws, and `getNotification`, `backlogIds` and the M1 tenant scope are driven through the real `makeSendDeps` for the first time — coverage the `deps.ts` header had credited to the other file. The docs lens's findings were each checked against the tree before a word moved, and four were false rather than stale: "their retry would mint a second update" (a same-target repeat replays the 0018 idempotency key and an applied intent is refused `already_on_plan` — the harm is the misreport), "which the drain never reaches" (the drain runs INSIDE `handleRequest`; the per-row throw is what never reaches its catch), the `logHandledError` field overlap (no `status`/`code` either), and the rule's attribution to `fix(edge-errors)`, which records a THROWN error swallowed by a catch — the opposite mechanism. Thirteen sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: eight against the gate (the blessed modifier, the walked-past reference, the always-OK direct read, an alias as a value, a value without evidence, a bare read made loud, the cast not unwrapped, and the hop rule — the gate-red-on-a-healthy-tree direction) and five against the deno suite (each context spread, the M1 scope, and each lookup's discarded error). The deno run prints no stray log line. **Round six, two P2s, both in the round-five fixes**: `const db: Db = adminClient()` — a factory initialiser under an annotation naming something else — returned on the annotation before the initialiser was looked at, so a receiver that was visibly a client was a "value" and its discarded `db.auth.getUser()` produced no site (measured on the shipped gate: `(no sites)`); client evidence wins now — at declaration, as a parameter default, by a later assignment — and the annotation decides only when nothing visible says client. And `return void (await q).error` — a discard that leaves nothing a linter would flag — was OK, because the direct-read rule knew only a bare statement as a discard; the read now walks up through what FORWARDS the value (wrappers, `await`, a comma's right side) to what holds it, and `void`, a comma's left side and a bare statement all discard. Two sabotages red-first behind verified diffs and `cmp`-verified restores, and both of Codex's cases proven silent against the SHIPPED gate rather than a sabotage. **Round seven, one P2, and it was the round-six fix applied to one site and not its siblings** — the shape this file keeps recording: `void (await q).error` had been closed for the DIRECT read, while `const { data, error } = await q; void error;`, `(error, 1)` and `void r.error;` on an envelope variable were still reads, because `isReadAfter` and the `.error`-read search counted any reference at all (all five shapes measured OK on the shipped gate). One rule now, in one helper: a reference is a read only if something CONSUMES it, and a bare statement, `void` or a comma's left side does not — applied to the bound local, the envelope's `.error` and the direct read alike, so the three sites cannot disagree again. Two sabotages red-first behind verified diffs and `cmp`-verified restores (every reference a read; nothing discards — which turns three tests red at once, the direct-read one included, because they share the helper). **Round eight, one P2, and it is the round-two overwrite rule applied to the envelope and never to the BUILDER**: `followBuilder` skipped every assignment target, so `let q = db.from("abandoned").select("id"); q = replacement; const { error } = await q; if (error) throw error;` was OK — the later `await` read as the original builder's consumer, for a query that was never executed, an accidental replacement silently omitting an operation while the gate passed. Measured on the shipped gate before the fix: Codex's case `["OK"]`, and the healthy sibling — a replacement AFTER the await (`const { error } = await q; …; q = other; await q;`) — `["OK","DISCARDED"]`, a red on ordinary code, because the replacement's await was attributed to the builder too. A write whose right side does not root at `q` now ENDS the follow (`q = other`, `({ q } = other)`, `[q] = other`, `for (q of xs)`, `var q = other` — the same pattern and loop targets round four taught the envelope), while assignment to itself (`q = q.eq(…)`, through a conditional too) grows the same builder; a builder replaced before anything consumed it is UNCLASSIFIED naming the line it never ran from, one continued on one branch of a conditional and replaced on the other is refused as not visible, and `q = wrap(q)` lets the right side classify itself (the call is the consumer, loudly) rather than reporting an abandonment that is not one. Four sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: every write treated as growth (the shipped rule), a partial continuation counted as growth, the follow never ending at a replacement, and a `var` re-declaration not seen as a write. **Round nine, two P2s, one in the gate and one in the drain line the PR added.** The gate's read window was SOURCE order and closures do not run in source order: `let { data, error } = await q; const check = () => error; error = null; if (check()) throw check();` was OK, because the closure's reference sits before the overwrite in the file and executes after it (measured on the shipped gate: `["OK"]`, and the envelope form `const check = () => r.error; r = other; …` the same). A write now counts from where it can EXECUTE — its own site in straight-line code, the closure's creation for an arrow or function expression, and the binding itself for a hoisted function declaration, which can be called before any read — and a read inside a closure counts only when no write can follow the binding at all, since the closure runs whenever it is called; the healthy shapes (a closure read with no later write, a straight-line read before a closure that writes is created) stay OK, and the conservative direction on a hoisted declaration is a false red rather than a miss. The drain's per-row line, added one round earlier, spread the thrown `HttpError`'s context LAST — so `isSuppressed`'s failure context, which carried `notification_id: null`, overwrote the row id the line exists to carry and `logHandledError` then dropped the null field: a generic line with no id, on the one path where a persistent failure recurs. The drain's own facts win now in both arms, the thrown context's other fields still ride along, and `isSuppressed` carries the operator and the type instead of a null. Five sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: a closure read counted whatever follows it, a closure write positioned where it sits, a hoisted declaration treated like an arrow, and each arm's spread order reverted to the shipped one. 770 vitest (45 new), 447 deno (18 new). **Round ten, one P2, and it is round nine's rule with its other half missing**: a closure runs when it is called — which can be after any later write (round nine) and can be NEVER, so `const { data, error } = await q; const check = () => error; return data;` was OK with `check` never invoked, a discarded error wearing a closure's clothes (measured `["OK"]` on the shipped gate, along with a callback handed to `forEach`, a closure returned on an object, and an inner closure never called). A reference inside a closure counts as a read now only when every closure around it is VISIBLY invoked — an IIFE, or a named function called in the same body — and execution the gate cannot see is refused for a read and assumed for a write, both the false-red direction: a `.error` write inside a closure nothing calls still closes the window. Own error worth recording, caught by the sabotage round rather than by review: the healthy fixtures pinning the IIFE and the hoisted declaration threw `error` itself beside the closure, a straight-line read that made them OK whatever the invocation rule said — a sabotage of the IIFE rule stayed green, so they throw a fresh error now and rest on the rule alone; and a second sabotage (a closure counted as called on a bare mention) stayed green because no fixture mentioned a closure without calling it, so one does. Five sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: every closure counted as invoked (the round-nine rule), an IIFE not recognised, a called hoisted declaration not recognised, only the outermost closure checked, and a bare mention counted as a call. 771 vitest (46 new), 447 deno (18 new). **Round eleven, one P2, and it is round ten's rule one level up**: a named closure counted as invoked wherever its call site sat, so `const check = () => error; const never = () => check(); return data;` was OK — a call that exists and never executes (measured `["OK"]` on the shipped gate, along with two closures that only call each other). A call site now executes only if every closure around IT is visibly invoked, recursively, with a `seen` set breaking cycles so two closures calling each other reach straight-line code nowhere; a reader called through an invoked wrapper and a self-recursive closure with one call from straight-line code stay OK. Two sabotages red-first: a call site counted wherever it sits (the round-ten rule), and the cycle guard removed — which is not an assertion going red but the mutual-recursion fixture overflowing the stack, the failure the guard exists for. **Round twelve, two P2s, both a rule with a door beside it.** The discard positions closed in round seven left a COPY open: `const copy = error; void copy; return data;` names the error twice and inspects it never, because an initializer is a consumer — and `const copy = r.error` the same (both measured `["OK"]` on the shipped gate). A copy carries the value, so it is followed to a real consumer now, transitively and under the copy's own window (`let copy = error; copy = null; if (copy) …` consumed nothing), for the bound local, the envelope's `.error` and the direct read alike, with a plain or logical assignment (`copy = error`, `copy ??= error`) an alias too. And the round-eleven call-site rule resolved the callee by SYMBOL alone, so `let check = () => error; check = () => null; if (check()) …` counted the call as the reader's when it runs the replacement; a call site gets the window a read gets — straight-line before the binding's next write (a hoisted declaration is live from the top, so every write bounds it), inside a closure only when no write can follow at all — and a replacement written inside a closure counts from that closure's creation. Three sabotages red-first behind verified diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: copies not followed, call sites counted wherever they sit, and a closure call site ignoring the later write; the healthy shapes (a copy that is read, a call before the replacement, arrow and hoisted) pinned OK. **Round thirteen, three P2s, and the third names the class the first twelve rounds had been circling.** The round-twelve alias rule followed `copy ??= error` as a copy, and a logical assignment evaluates its right side only conditionally — `let copy = new Error("other"); copy ??= error; throw copy;` never looks at the query's error (measured `["OK"]` on the shipped gate). `const box = { error }; return data;` was OK because a shorthand property is neither a discard position nor an identifier copy; the literal is followed to a consumer by the same rules now (`logHandledError({ cause: error })` is consumed, a boxed error bound to a local nothing reads is not). And `if (data) console.error(error); return data;` was OK because any syntactic use in the window counted, while supabase-js supplies `data: null` on failure — so the one branch that reads the error runs exactly when there is none. That is the class: a read the FAILURE PATH can skip, and the closure rules of rounds nine to twelve were its first instance (a closure that may never run). One rule now, `established`: a reference is a read only on every path — not inside a branch that excludes the binding (an `if` body, either arm of `?:`, the right of `&&`/`||`/`??` and of a logical assignment, a `case`, a loop body, a `catch`, an optional-chain call's arguments) and not after a conditional `return`/`continue`/`break` — applied to straight-line reads, to reads inside a closure relative to that closure, and to the call site that would establish a closure; a conditional `throw` is deliberately not an exit, since it aborts rather than completing as an absence. Stated as the conservative direction rather than hidden: the gate does not know which condition is the failure path, so `if (!data && !error) return null; if (error) throw error;` is refused too, and the remedy is `if (error) throw error;` first. The real tree stayed green — every site checks `error` in an unconditional `if` before anything else — and two fixtures of my own went red for the right reason and were rewritten: a "healthy" read inside `if (c) { … }` from round two, which is Codex's case with the condition renamed, and round eleven's self-recursive reader, whose read sits on its base case, a branch by construction. Five sabotages red-first behind verified diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the logical assignment as a copy, aggregates not followed, every position established, a conditional return not an exit, and call sites not held to every path. **Round fourteen, two P2s, each a site the round-thirteen rules did not reach.** A DEFAULT initializer runs only when the value it defaults is undefined — `function check(x = error) { return x; } check(null)` never evaluates the reference — and `isBranchEdge` did not know a default as a branch, so the call established a read that never executes (measured `["OK"]` on the shipped gate, with the arrow form, the envelope form, and a binding element's and a destructuring assignment's defaults beside it). All three default positions are branch edges now; a read in the BODY of a function with a defaulted parameter is still a read. And the direct read followed a local binding and nothing else, so `const box = { cause: (await q).error }; void box;` was OK one round after the bound and envelope forms learnt to follow a literal — the per-site disease, one round later; the direct read goes through `consumes` now, with the reason naming which of the three discards it was. Three sabotages red-first behind verified diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: defaults not branches, parameter defaults only (the binding and pattern siblings still reads), and the direct read not routed through `consumes`. **Round fifteen, two P2s.** A class body is not function-like, so `closuresAround` saw straight-line code in `class Never { field = error }` — an initializer that runs per construction, with `Never` never built (measured `["OK"]` on the shipped gate); a class field's initializer is a branch edge now, static or not, since the value lands on an object the gate does not follow, while a static block runs with the class statement and stays straight-line. And following a literal to its binding let ANY use of the binding count — `const box = { error, data }; return box.data;` was OK one round after the literal rule landed, the round-thirteen fix seeing less than it claimed. The key the error sits under travels with the value now (`Path`): a member read counts only along it, one hop or two, by name or by literal index; a destructuring only through the element that takes it, a rest element carrying it on; an object spread carries the keys through and an array spread scatters them to UNKNOWN; and a value that merely CARRIES the error is read only when handed on whole — a call, a return, a throw — not when its truthiness is what the consumer wants. Twenty-two cases probed on the shipped gate, all `OK`; nine are DISCARDED now and the thirteen healthy shapes stay OK. Four sabotages red-first behind verified diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: class fields straight-line, the path not tracked, a destructuring counting whatever element it takes, and a carrier consumed by anything. **Round sixteen, two P2s, both a rule reaching the error and stopping there.** `{ ...error }` copies the ERROR's own fields under names the gate cannot enumerate, and round fifteen passed the path through unchanged — so the literal was read as holding the error at the EMPTY path, which made every member of it a read: `const box = { ...error, data }; return box.data;` was `OK` (measured on the shipped gate). An object spread of a CARRIER still passes its keys through, since there the error genuinely survives under its own name; spreading the error itself scatters them to UNKNOWN, so only handing the literal on whole counts. And the member branch returned OK the moment an access existed, so `void error?.message` — reaching a field and throwing the answer away — was OK, along with `void r.error?.message`, `void (await q).error?.message` and two siblings Codex did not name (a bare `error?.message;` statement, and a copy of it that is then discarded). The access is followed by the same rules now, for a bound local, an envelope variable and a direct read alike, which is the round-seven discard positions one hop in; a value derived from the error is the error for this question, so the hop resets the path. Four sabotages red-first behind verified diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the spread passing the path through and the member's early return (both the shipped behaviour), and both over-corrections — every object spread scattering (which turns the round-fifteen carrier case red too) and a member of the error never counting. Checking the SIBLING rather than the site the reviewer named then found the same hole one function over, in `patternReads`: with the path empty it answered "a read by construction", so `const { message } = error; void message;` took the error apart, discarded every piece and reported OK (measured on the head Codex had just reviewed). Each binding carries a piece of the error, so the read is whichever piece is READ; two more sabotages red-first, the shipped rule and the over-correction where taking it apart never reads it. **Round seventeen, one P2, and it is the key travelling with nothing asking whether it survives**: `const carrier = { error }; const box = { ...carrier, error: fallback }; if (box.error) throw box.error;` carried `error` through the spread while the literal's own later property replaced it, so the read could only ever observe the fallback — `OK` on the shipped gate, with a later computed key and a later spread beside it. The last definition wins, so a carried key that any LATER element of the same object literal may define (the same name, a computed key, a spread of anything) is UNKNOWN now; an array literal needs no such rule, since a later element shifts no earlier index. The sibling one statement on, which the review did not name: `box.error = fallback` and `delete box.cause` replace the member the path names, and the read window closed only on writes to the BINDING — it closes on a write to the path's head key too, matched by key so a write to another member leaves it alone. Four sabotages red-first: no later element redefining and a property write never closing the window (both the shipped behaviour), and both over-corrections — every later element redefining (which turns the round-fifteen and round-sixteen literal cases red as well) and a write to ANY member closing it. **Round eighteen, three P2s, all in round seventeen's write rule seeing less than it claimed**: it compared `path[0]` only, so a write BELOW the head (`box.nested.cause = fallback`) was missed; it demanded a bare identifier receiver, so `(box).error = …` and `(box as any).error = …` were invisible; and it computed the window per binding, so a write through one name for an object left another name's window open, which is the round-three envelope group in the carrier's clothes. One rule replaces the three: a write REPLACES the error when its key chain — read outward from the base, unwrapping a transparent node at every level — is a PREFIX of the carried path, and the base is any name in the object's alias group (followed through `const alias = box` in both directions, transitively). A write DEEPER than the path mutates a field of the error and leaves the error itself, and one that diverges at any key touches another member. Writing the test for it found a defect of my own in round fifteen: `keyOf` returns an identifier's TEXT, which is right for `{ error: … }` where the identifier IS the name and wrong for `box[k]` where it is a variable — so a computed write that may hit the carried key was missed, and a computed READ answered DISCARDED for the wrong reason; `keyOfIndex` is the separate reader and only a literal names a key. Two of the five sabotages first stayed GREEN and both were broken proofs rather than passing code: one unwrapped the outer target where the inner receiver is what Codex's case needs (so both are sabotaged now, and a legal `(box.error) = fallback` earns the outer one its place), and the deeper-write rule had TWO enforcers — the length check and a comparison against an out-of-range key — so the loop now stops at the path's end and the length check alone decides, which is what makes its sabotage honest. **Round nineteen, two P2s, and together they say the alias GROUP was the wrong model**: a set of names with no sense of time or depth. `const inner = box.nested` never joined it, so a write through `inner` was missed; and `let alias = box; alias = other;` never left it, so a write through the REPLACEMENT closed the window on correct code — a gate red on a healthy tree, which this log calls the worst shape there is, and the first such red these nineteen rounds have produced. Replaced rather than patched twice: a name ROOTS at what it was initialised from, through member chains and transitively, and the read side and the write side are each said from that root and compared as absolute paths — so a write through any name for the object is seen by a read through any other, `inner.cause` included, while a binding reassigned before the write roots at itself and is a different object by then. `tsc` then caught what the passing suite did not, for the fourth time this PR: the symbol parameter the old model needed was dead. Three sabotages red-first: a name being its origin forever and only a bare identifier rooting elsewhere (both the shipped behaviour), and the over-correction where any root matches, which turns the round-eighteen case red as well. **Round twenty, one P2, and the case was passing for a second reason the finding did not name.** The finding is right: `let alias: T; alias = box;` is provenance as much as an initialiser, so what a name refers to is decided by the LAST write before the point in question — an assignment back restores it, a write of any other shape leaves it unknown, and the upstream name is resolved AS OF the copy, so a name that took the reference before the name it copied moved on still holds the object it copied. But instrumenting the matcher rather than reasoning about it showed the write WAS being found and the verdict was OK anyway, from a second path: `alias.error = fallback` was counted as a READ of the error, because a member on the carried path is consumed by whatever holds it and nothing asked which SIDE of the assignment it sat on. A target is written, not read — every assignment kind and `delete` alike, the compound ones included, because the write window closes at that same position so calling `??=` a read decides nothing, and a distinction no test can hold is a rule with nothing behind it (found by a sabotage of that very clause staying green). Four sabotages red-first: an assignment target counted as a read, provenance only from a declaration, the upstream resolved at the read rather than at the copy, and — after the clause was made uniform — the target rule removed entirely. **Round twenty-one, two P2s, both in round twenty's own provenance reader.** `(alias) = box` is the same assignment wearing the wrappers `memberChain` already climbs on the other side — the per-site disease, in the fix for a round that had just fixed it elsewhere. And a write inside a CLOSURE was taken as definite provenance, because `executesAt` places such a write at the closure's creation: right for "this may have happened by now", wrong for "this decided what the name is", and it reported a genuine read DISCARDED — the second gate-red-on-a-healthy-tree of this PR, both found by the reviewer. Only a write whose execution is certain — straight-line and established — can say what a name refers to; anything else leaves the name rooted at itself. Two shapes MOVED to the refusing direction as a result and are pinned as such: where a closure or a branch could have moved the name away, the gate cannot say whether the member write landed on the error, so it refuses, which is the same conservative direction the rest of the gate takes. Three sabotages red-first: the target not unwrapped and a closure write counted as provenance (both the shipped behaviour), and a conditional write counted, which makes `established` load-bearing beside `closureOf`. **Round twenty-two is round twenty-one's mirror, and the pair is only reconcilable by asking whether a write can run at all.** Filtering the uncertain writes OUT let the declaration answer as though a branch could never be taken: `let alias = other; if (c) alias = box; alias.error = fallback;` read OK while a taken branch replaces the query error. But simply treating every uncertain write as unknown would have restored round twenty-one's false red, since the two shapes are structurally identical and differ only in whether the closure is ever invoked — which the gate can ask, and already does elsewhere. So: a closure the gate cannot see INVOKED cannot run, and neither gives provenance nor takes it away; a branch, or an invoked closure, MIGHT, which leaves the name unknown — and a write through an unknown name is treated as possibly landing on the error rather than assumed away, which is the refusing direction the rest of the gate takes. Three sabotages red-first: an unknown base assumed not to be this object, a closure that cannot run still poisoning provenance (which restores the round-twenty-one false red), and the uncertain writes filtered out rather than refused. **Round twenty-three, two P2s, both halves of round twenty-two's own rule — one a false red, one a miss.** An uncertain write SUPERSEDED by a definite one decides nothing: `let alias = box; if (c) alias = other; alias = other2;` ends certain, and calling the name unknown made a genuine read DISCARDED (measured on the shipped gate; the third healthy-tree red of this PR, all three found by the reviewer). Uncertainty is now counted only after the last definite write — except for a live CLOSURE write, which stays pessimistic because its position is where it was CREATED and not where it is called, so nothing orders it against anything. The miss is the mirror: an unknown name was matched at the bound object's depth only, so one that may hold the NESTED carrier slipped past — `let inner = other; if (c) inner = box.nested; inner.cause = fallback;` is the error when the branch is taken — and an unresolved base is now checked against every depth along the carried path. Three sabotages red-first: any uncertain write invalidating and an unknown name matched at one depth (both the shipped behaviour), and the over-correction where uncertainty after the definite write is ignored, which turns the round-twenty-two case red too. **Round twenty-four, one P2, narrow and exact**: `alias ||= other2` passes `established`, because the STATEMENT is on every path, while the assignment inside it is not — so a logical assignment was counted as the definite write that supersedes earlier uncertainty and the name read as the value it may never have taken. `||=`, `&&=` and `??=` are uncertain now, as provenance and as supersession alike. Two sabotages red-first, and the second was a broken proof first: treating EVERY compound assignment as conditional stayed green, because nothing distinguished `+=` from `||=` — a rule with nothing behind it — so a case pins that `alias += ""` does settle the name (it always assigns, and what it assigns is not the carrier). **Round twenty-five was DECLINED**, the first decline of this PR and the second of this family. It is correct — `let a: any = {}; a ||= {};` cannot assign, because the initialiser is truthy, so refusing the shape is a red on a healthy function — but closing it means a constant-truthiness evaluator inside a gate about supabase envelopes, and the shape occurs nowhere in the code this gate scans: zero logical assignments under `supabase/functions`, measured rather than assumed. That is the epicycle the #85 stopping rule names, so the refusal is PINNED as a test and stated in spec 04 and the gate's own header rather than left to be rediscovered, with the remedy every other conservative refusal here carries — `if (error) throw error;` first. **Round twenty-six**: calling a GENERATOR returns an iterator and runs nothing, so `function* check() { return error; } check();` read the error never while `visiblyInvoked` claimed it did — the one claim in that function that was simply false. A generator is never established now; recognising that one is ITERATED instead is machinery for a shape that occurs nowhere under `supabase/functions` (zero generators, measured), so a read inside an iterated generator is a documented conservative refusal rather than an oversight, pinned as a case beside the round-twenty-five decline. An async function is unaffected — calling it runs its body to the first await — and that is pinned too. One sabotage red-first. 792 vitest (68 new), 447 deno (18 new), 43 e2e, 86 smoke DO blocks, 12 concurrency cases, 22 validate gates green.
- fix(walk-cost)+edge(seams): backlog item 1, both halves, each built by an agent in its own worktree on its own Postgres cluster and rebased onto PR #92's head without a textual conflict. **The walk cost.** Booking's committed sum (H12) ran the live weekend formula over every persisted scheduled walk while `walks.cost_credits` — the figure `fn_debit_walk` will actually take, stamped at creation since 0043 — sat unread in the same rows: the callback `committedCredits` accepted was typed `(walk: { status: string })`, so the screen cast its way to `service_type_id` and `scheduled_date` and never reached the snapshot, and a service re-priced after a walk was booked moved the sum away from the server's figure — the sum that decides whether the overage disclosure is shown at all. `committedCredits` is generic over the row and, as first built, Booking priced each booked walk `w.cost_credits ?? live`, the coalesce `fn_walk_cost` performs — moved INTO the function in the review round below; the walk being composed has no row yet and stays live on purpose. `api.ts`'s `walkCost()` wrapper is deleted — zero importers, and the one screen pricing a persisted walk already holds the column — while `fn_walk_cost` and its `authenticated` grant stay, the decision recorded in spec 03. The arithmetic moves to a zero-import leaf, `app/src/lib/walk-cost.ts`, so a deno script can load it, which is what gate 8d needs: `scripts/check-walk-cost-parity.sh` runs one case list through three implementations — the TS leaf, `fn_walk_cost`'s live fallback (0043) and the `fn_snapshot_walk_price` trigger (0044) — and compares three ways across implementations, trigger against function, function against TypeScript, and all against the expectation (a case list drifted away from every implementation passes the first two), plus the TypeScript runs against each other. The SQL side reads the trigger's stamp and NULLS it before asking the function — not to catch a trigger drift, which the TypeScript comparison catches regardless, but because the function COALESCES the snapshot first and its own expression, the figure `fn_debit_walk` charges a pre-0043 row, would otherwise be tied to nothing — and reads the function's answers through a join on the null having taken. The TypeScript side runs FOUR times: under `Etc/GMT+12` and `Etc/GMT-14` (fixed offsets, twelve hours west and fourteen east of Greenwich, either side of the day boundary) against a constant-offset local read, and under `America/Chicago` and `Australia/Sydney` against a DST-dependent one — two DST zones whose daylight seasons are complementary, for the reason the third review pass below establishes, with the season READ and refused by name if neither is on daylight time — every run required to agree with the others and with SQL, and the answers script REQUIRING the zone it was told and refusing a runtime that ignored `TZ`. Sixteen cases: weekdays, Saturday, Sunday, a Friday (so `isodow in (5, 6)` fails in both directions), the US DST-transition weekends, a leap day, a year boundary, a zero surcharge, 2 + 3; wired into `ci.yml`, `SKILL.md` and `validate.sh` together, skipped by name when deno is missing and inside the `7-8. database` skip when there is no database. Ten sabotages red-first, each behind an asserted single-match edit of a snapshotted fixed file and a `cmp`-verified restore: the live formula, the fallback dropped, the generic narrowed (tsc red on the pin and on Booking), `fn_walk_cost` at `in (5, 6)` inside the gate's own transaction (TRG≠FN and DISAGREE on six cases, the live definition untouched after), the trigger alone (TRG≠FN, the line that names which SQL copy moved), the leaf at `day === 5`, a drifted expectation, an empty case list, a dead database and a SQL error — both the named count sentence and exit 2, and the first version died on `trg: unbound variable`, because bash 5.2 reports an empty associative array as unset under `set -u`. **The seams.** `billing-portal` and `connect-onboarding` were the two `index.ts`-only functions — importing an `index.ts` runs `serveFunction` and binds a port, so nothing in either could be driven by a test, the blind spot that hid two defects in send-notification's deps and one in `overage_deps`. Both follow the house pattern now, BOTH halves of it: a `handler.ts` carrying the decisions behind a deps object, and a `deps.ts` carrying the wiring with every `Deno.env` read hoisted into `index.ts` — the first draft of this PR split only the handler, and the review named the untested half by its consequences: drop the per-request options from the portal call in `index.ts` and every session is created on the PLATFORM account (the B5 404) with all nineteen recorder tests green. `handleBillingPortal(user, deps)` carries the three refusals and the `accountOf`-not-`requireAccount` rule verbatim — this path takes no money, so a client whose walker Stripe is still reviewing must still be able to update a card or cancel, a rule carried until now only by a comment beside the call — and `makeBillingPortalDeps` takes the Stripe client as a THUNK resolved inside `createPortalSession`, so a missing key still cannot turn `not_client` or `no_billing` into a 500; the `as unknown as ConnectFields` cast did NOT disappear as planned, because the admin client is untyped and supabase-js infers the many-to-one `operator` embed as an ARRAY, so it lives at the wiring with the reason. `handleConnectOnboarding(operatorId, body, deps)` carries the order and the loser-adopts-winner rule, and `makeConnectOnboardingDeps` carries the conditional UPDATE, PR #92's re-read with its `readErr` throw and the `?? accountId` verbatim, with `stripeClient()` still eager (the operator-billing precedent), so `status` still 500s without `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` exactly as it did — recorded, not changed. One BEHAVIOUR change, decided here: `action` must be exactly `start` or `status`, and anything else is `400 bad_action`, refused before the handler's own operator lookup (`requireOperator` has already read the row once, to authenticate the caller) — the shipped code defaulted a missing action to `status` but tested only `=== "status"`, so `{ action: "foo" }` fell through to `start`, which for an operator not yet connected MINTS a Stripe Connect account (an already-connected operator's typo minted a single-use link); a typo creating an account is worse than a 400, spec 04 names exactly two values and the frontend sends only those. Thirty-seven tests: twenty on the handler recorders — every refusal before the first Stripe call, the charges-disabled walker still yielding a portal session, every recorded `billingPortal.*` call carrying the CONNECTED account (the loop iterates the whole recorder, since a name filter beside an unfiltered floor let a call recorded under another name slip past, the `customers.retrieve` shape from `fix(connect-routing)`), no connect-onboarding call carrying `stripeAccount`, the race loser adopting the winner's id in the link AND the response, a failed claim propagating as its 500 and minting NO link (one dep throw — the recorder cannot tell the write from the re-read and need not), a failed operator lookup propagating as its 500 rather than as `not_operator`, a status fixture in which `connected` and `charges_enabled` DIFFER (with them co-varying, a handler deriving one from the other's column passed every test), and a raw Stripe error propagating un-wrapped, pinned by identity because the repository's `_tests/asserts.ts` `assertEquals` is JSON-stringify equality and on two Errors compares `{}` to `{}` — and seventeen on the real wiring against a scripted PostgREST double (`_tests/scripted_db.ts`, shared with `send_deps_test.ts` rather than copied a third time): the `.select` strings and their filters, the embed handed through as one object, each lookup's throw with its cause and context, the Stripe thunk resolved AFTER the refusals, the per-request options forwarded to the real `create`, the CONDITIONAL claim (`is("stripe_account_id", null)` — drop it and two concurrent starts overwrite each other), the re-read whose answer wins, a failed write rejecting with no re-read, and a failed re-read rejecting — PR #92's fix for this function, driven for the first time — minting no link end to end. Twenty-six sabotages red-first with the same discipline, three of which first went red on TYPECHECK rather than on the test (deleting a refusal leaves the row possibly null) and were rewritten to compile, because a compile error is red for the wrong reason. The discarded-errors gate classifies the envelope reads in the two `deps.ts` files OK; the handlers carry no supabase query for it to see. **The review round, before the PR opened.** An adversarial pass over the diff — two lenses reported before the session's usage limit stopped the other three and every verifier, so each finding was verified by hand rather than by a refuter — found seven things, and the serious one was mine: gate 8d's timezone half was VACUOUS. The TypeScript side ran once, in the caller's zone, which is UTC in CI — where a leaf reading the LOCAL day, `new Date(isoDate).getDay()`, the exact trap the leaf's own comment warned about, answered all sixteen cases correctly, and the four DST rows the case file said "pin the `T12:00:00Z` choice" pinned nothing, because a DATE has no zone and `getUTCDay` cannot see one. Measured on the shipped gate: that leaf PASS under UTC and red only under America/Chicago; the noon-plus-`getDay()` variant PASS under UTC and Chicago and red only east of Greenwich; a bare date under `getUTCDay` correct everywhere, which is why no case could or should distinguish the suffix. Hence the two fixed-offset runs, which turn both local-read leaves red (`TZ-DEPENDENT`, naming the case and each answer). Second, the NULL that makes `fn_walk_cost` compute was guarded by nothing: deleting it stayed green, and with the function sabotaged to `isodow in (5, 6)` beside it the shipped gate still printed `WALK COST PARITY PASS` — the join on `cost_credits is null` is the fix, and the same pair now reads `0 function answers`. Third, the header, the commit message and both drafts had the null's MECHANISM inverted — "without the null a sabotaged trigger reads as a function that agrees with it", when a trigger sabotage is red without it through the TypeScript comparison (measured: the null kept gives `TRG≠FN`, the null removed gives the count sentence, and the trigger-only sabotage was red in both), so what the null makes checkable is the function's own expression — and said "four comparisons" where the loop then made three (it makes four now, the fourth being the TypeScript runs against each other, and the header, `SKILL.md`, `ci.yml` and spec 02 say so alike); spec 02 had the mechanism right all along. Fourth, a second superuser was refused as a walk-status error, because the `walks` update guard admits only a service session or the walk's own operator through `auth.uid()`, a psql connection is neither, and a superuser bypasses RLS and not triggers; it is refused BY NAME first now (`gate 8d must connect as the postgres role (session_user is parity_su)`). In the lib: `committedCredits` left the snapshot-first coalesce to Booking's callback and typed the row `{ status }`, so a second caller passing rows without `cost_credits` would have compiled and run the live formula over every one — the original defect one new caller away; the coalesce lives in the function now, its row type REQUIRES the column (a `@ts-expect-error` pin that is red in both directions, since an unused directive is itself a type error), and the callback is the fallback only, never asked for a snapshotted row. And nothing pinned that `effectiveWalkCost` delegates to the leaf the gate reads — with the formula re-inlined in `credits.ts` the gate and every test stayed green, a fourth copy untied at the seam the gate cannot see — so `credits.delegation.test.ts` mocks the leaf to a sentinel. Eleven more proofs red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the local-day leaf both ways, `day === 5` under the two-zone run, the null removed (count sentence), the null removed with the function sabotaged (shipped PASS against fixed FAIL), the second superuser, the trigger sabotaged with the null kept and removed, the lib handing every row to the callback, the row type widened (tsc), and the formula re-inlined. **The second review pass, after the usage limit reset, ran all four finder lenses**, and its findings were the first pass's method applied to the first pass's fixes. The two fixed-offset zones have no DST, so a leaf whose error is DST-DEPENDENT — UTC midnight shifted by the offset in force NOW, the natural "make it right for my zone" mistake — passed both runs and was a day out in the product's own zone on two cases already in the file (measured: `TZ-DEPENDENT … ts(Chicago)=1` on `2026-03-07` and `2027-01-02`, zero reds under either fixed offset); the gate runs `America/Chicago` as a third zone, which is what makes the DST rows load-bearing, and deno carries its own zone data so the run needs no host tzdata. The answers script's zone argument was OPTIONAL, so a shell edit that dropped it kept the gate green with the refusal inert — measured `WALK COST PARITY PASS` with the argument gone — and it is required now, the same edit reading `usage: … the zone is required` and the count sentence. The remaining findings were sentences disagreeing with the tree, each checked before it moved: the header said three comparisons while its own loop made four; the guard admits the walk's operator too; the session-notes remedy `${arr[$k]-}` addresses a missing ENTRY and does not make `${#arr[@]}` safe; `refused before the operator row is even read` was false at the endpoint, since `requireOperator` reads it to authenticate; "MINTED a Stripe Connect account" was true only for an operator with no account on the row; the body still described Booking doing the coalesce, a state the PR no longer ships; "skips by name" was true only of the missing-deno branch; `assertEquals … cannot fail` named no implementation, and `@std/assert`'s would fail; the case file attributed the `T12:00:00Z` rule to `credits.ts` and dated it to a phase this shallow clone cannot read; `concurrency.sh` still pointed case 10 at "Backlog item 1", which after the renumbering is the suppressed-address item; and "a failed claim — write OR re-read" described one dep throw as two exercised paths, which the deps split above turns from an overstatement into a driven fact. Thirteen more proofs red-first behind verified diffs and `cmp`-verified restores — six on the wiring (the options dropped from the portal call, Stripe resolved at construction, the conditional claim dropped, the re-read error discarded, the re-read's answer ignored, the lookup error discarded), three on the handler tests (`connected` derived from `charges_enabled`, a swallowed lookup, the account dropped with the loop unfiltered), and four on the gate (the DST-dependent leaf, the argument dropped with the zone required, the constant-offset leaf still red under the fixed offsets, and the argument dropped with the zone optional again — the shipped behaviour, which PASSES, the finding itself). **A third pass over this PR's own gate then refuted the sentence the second pass had just written into it.** The header claimed the `America/Chicago` run catches a DST-dependent leaf on "the DST-weekend cases already in the file"; measured with deno's own zone data over the real case list, that leaf is 2 BAD at Chicago's DAYLIGHT offset (2026-03-07 and 2027-01-02) and **0 BAD at its standard offset** — so for the five months Chicago is on standard time gate 8d would have reported the leaf green in all three zones while describing itself as the thing that catches it, which is a check reporting success having verified nothing, this repository's most-repeated defect, inside the round that added the check. The rows named were wrong too: of the four DST-weekend cases only 2026-03-07 is caught, the Sundays being invisible to a leaf that reads a weekend either way. A DST zone pins that leaf only while the zone is ON daylight time, on rows dated in its STANDARD time, so one zone can never cover the year. `Australia/Sydney`'s daylight season is the complement of Chicago's — measured, no day of 2026-2028 has both zones on standard time — so the gate runs FOUR TS zones and the pair covers every day (Sydney is 2 BAD at its daylight offset, 2026-07-04 and 2026-07-06, and 0 BAD at its standard one). The precondition is ASSERTED rather than assumed, which is the part that makes the pair a rule instead of a hope: the run reads which zone is on daylight time today (today's offset against the larger of this year's January and July offsets; a zone with no DST answers 0, and anything but a 0 or a 1 is the probe failing and is reported as that) and REFUSES BY NAME if neither is, because a run that pins nothing must not print PASS — and the PASS line itself now carries the season it measured. Three more sentences corrected, each read against the tree first: the gate's SQL comment and its refusal message still said the `walks` update guard "admits only a service session" where the header already said "or the walk's own operator via `auth.uid()`"; the `bad_action` test said the fall-through "created a Stripe Connect account" unqualified, true only for an operator not yet connected (every other site was already qualified); and the failed-claim test title named two paths where one dep throw is driven, which the deps split drives as two. Four more proofs red-first behind verified diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the DST-dependent leaf under each DST zone at its own standard offset (green, the finding), the same leaf caught by whichever zone is on daylight time, the precondition refusing when told both zones are on standard time, and the probe's non-numeric answer reported as a probe failure rather than as a season. Also folded in, found while verifying PR #92's merge rather than created by this work: `SUPABASE_ACCESS_TOKEN` has lapsed a second time (deploy-staging run 101 on `c34df1e`, `Link project`, `{"message":"Unauthorized"}`; last green run 100 on 2026-09-05), and `ops(staging-auth)` had given that failure an `::error` annotation and a Troubleshooting section but no line in `owner-actions.md` — the one list this repository designates for things no file here can do — so a recurring owner-only blocker lived only in a runbook nobody opens until a deploy is already red. It is §2a now, with a row in the table that says what tells you it is still undone. 797 vitest, 484 deno, 43 e2e, 86 smoke DO blocks, 12 concurrency cases, 23 validate gates green (22 labels; gate 8 runs twice).
- ci(gates): PR A of the spec-drift follow-ups — eight gates that passed for the wrong reason, and the lockstep `CLAUDE.md` has claimed since the phase plan ended with nothing behind it. Every defect was measured against the SHIPPED gate before anything changed, which is the only way to tell a gate that is weak from one a reader merely mistrusts. **The service worker's security boundary was proven by nothing.** Every Supabase fixture in `service-worker.test.ts` was cross-origin, and `sw.js`'s fetch handler returns early for ANY cross-origin request before `isNeverCache` is consulted — so the origin check alone satisfied all five never-cache assertions, on the file whose own header says what matters "is not that the paths are named; it is that `respondWith` is never called for them". Measured: replacing `isNeverCache`'s body with `return false` left all 31 tests green. Five same-origin fixtures added and the same sabotage now turns exactly those five red. Supabase IS cross-origin in the deployment shipped today, which is why the same-origin case has to be ASSERTED rather than observed — `isNeverCache` is the rule that still holds the day the API sits behind the app's own origin, and the `qc(1-4)` leak it exists to prevent is not a bug about foreign hosts. **The FormError grep matched one literal class name**, so a bare `<span className="onboard__error" role="alert">` — the exact shape the rule forbids — slipped past under any other name, measured by planting it. The remedy the audit proposed (fail any `__error` class outside `fields.tsx`) would have gone RED ON A HEALTHY TREE, the worst shape this log records: `<FormError className="claim-invite__error" />` is correct usage, and all NINE `role="alert"` sites outside `fields.tsx` are props handed to `StateField`, never attributes on a raw element. That count is a correction — the commit says eight, which was recalled rather than counted, and nine is the number `grep` returns (LoadError, ErrorBoundary, NotificationInbox, SignIn, Onboard, WalkMode, ResetPassword, Signup, ClaimInvite, every one of them a `<StateField`). The rule is about the TAG, which a grep cannot see and a parser can: `app/scripts/form-error.test.ts` reads TSX with the TypeScript compiler API and asks whether the element is intrinsic, because a lowercase tag is a raw DOM element while a capitalised one is a component that owns its own live region. The `ci.yml` step is DELETED rather than kept beside it — one implementation, the rule PR #85's rounds 24 and 25 settled — and both allowlists I first wrote are gone, because measuring showed they excused nothing and an entry that excuses nothing is an enumeration with nothing behind it. **Invariant 1's catalogue regex missed two ordinary spellings**: probed against a live database, `update public.clients set …` and `update clients c set …` each wrote `credit_balance` outside `fn_ledger_apply` and each was invisible to the check that exists to make invariant 1 true. It allows an optional schema qualifier and an optional alias now, catches five spellings, and still says nothing about a write to another table. **The walk-channel gate could not count**: `grep -rln` yields FILE names, so a second `supabase.channel(`shadow:${walkId}`)` planted beside the real one left one filename and the step reported PASS, while the `-Eq` below it was satisfied by the one private call; deleting the server's `private: true` outright passed too, because the check only ever looked for the literal `false` and `private` DEFAULTS to false — which is the whole of review H1, so the gate was blind to the exact regression it was written after. My first fix counted calls against a `supabase\.channel\([^)]*config: *\{ *private: true` match, and I then asked my own PR whether that could go red on healthy code. It could, twice: `supabase.channel(topicFor(walkId), { config: { private: true } })` stops the `[^)]*` at the inner call's `)`, and `{ config: { private:true } }` has no space after the colon — both correct code, both measured RED, and nothing in this repository enforces either spelling (no prettier, no oxlint spacing rule). So the counting fix traded a gate that passed for the wrong reason for one red on a healthy tree, which this log calls the worse of the two. A regex over an argument list is the wrong instrument for a question about an argument list: `app/scripts/realtime-channel.test.ts` asks the compiler instead — every call whose callee is `supabase.channel`, its second argument's `config.private`, and the server's `{ topic, …, private }` message literal — and it runs under gate 3, so a local run covers what was CI-only. Five rules red-first (no options argument, `private: false`, a second channel in the same file, and the server publishing public or omitting the flag) and both healthy spellings green where the deleted grep is red. Writing it, my own precondition caught the parser's one blind spot: `broadcast.ts` writes `{ topic, event, payload, private: true }` with three properties SHORTHAND, so the literal could not be found at all — presence accepts shorthand now while the VALUE still has to be a real assignment, because a shorthand `private` is a reference this check cannot resolve and "cannot say" is not "is private". **The lockstep is enforced at last** — `scripts/check-gate-lockstep.py`, gate 10g, classifying every named `ci.yml` run-step as SETUP, as a `validate.sh` gate label, or as CI_ONLY, with a CI_ONLY step required to be named VERBATIM in SKILL.md §13, which is what a person reads to learn what a green local run did not cover. It fired on live drift the moment it existed: the secret-leak step called itself "validate gate 7" when gate 7 is the database reset and it is gate 11, and `session-notes.md` told a fresh session that TWO gates exist only in CI while the audit that found that said seven — measured against the tree there are FIFTEEN, so the note was wrong AND the proposed correction would have been wrong, which is the argument for counting it here rather than writing a third number into prose. The map is an allowlist of exceptions in one place (the `TEXT_RE_EXEMPTIONS` shape) and the check fails on an unclassified step, a stale map entry, a `validate.sh` label that no longer exists, a §13 entry with no step behind it, or a count that disagrees — a stale entry excuses a real check forever, which is what `verify-deployment.sh`'s `contract_for` was given the same treatment for. **Three more, each built in its own worktree and verified against the tree rather than taken from its report.** `verify-deployment.test.ts` derives the read-only argument instead of enumerating it: a function behind `serveFunction` answers 405 before its handler runs — one wrapper checked once — while a function with its own `Deno.serve` has no such wrapper, so its read-only behaviour is a fact somebody established by reading it and `contract_for` is where that reading is recorded. That second half lived in the script's header as a sentence naming two functions, so a third bypasser would silently take the DEFAULT contract and the probe would find out by making the request, against production. Both sides are PARSED now, not grepped, for a reason in the tree rather than hypothetical: `platform-webhook/index.ts:6` says "Same bare Deno.serve shape as stripe-webhook", so a grep counts a COMMENT. The gate is deliberately one-directional — a bypasser must have a case, a case need not be a bypasser — because `unsubscribe` has a legitimate bespoke contract and the converse rule would have been red on this healthy tree. `staging-smoke.yml`'s `onboard-repro` was the sibling that never got the cure: `Replay invite-claim` was fixed three times (the single-page `user_id_for`, then a stop rule that inferred the last page from the size REQUESTED rather than from what the server returned, then cleanup that reports a refused delete instead of swallowing it) while `Replay onboard`, directly above it in the same job, kept all three verbatim — dead today only because its fixture address is run-scoped, which is a property of the FIXTURE and not of the step. The fix is a deletion rather than a second copy: a shell function cannot cross a `run:` boundary, so one definition means a file both steps source (`scripts/staging-fixtures.sh`, with the job now checking the repository out to reach it), which is also what makes the rules testable at all. Against the SHIPPED step with a leftover on page 3 of 350 users, the whole diagnosis was `FAIL: could not create fixture user`. And `gen-definer-catalog.py` still read SQL with the naive comment-stripping regex pair `gen-enum-catalog.py` needed forty-three review rounds to replace, so it shares that lexer now and carries sixteen probes of its own in gate 10f — which is why that gate's one-line description moved in all three files plus the lockstep map, in one edit, proven by doing the edit HALFWAY first and watching the map name it three ways. The rendered catalogue is BYTE-IDENTICAL across that change (86 lines, 72 definer functions), so this was a latent gate defect and not a wrong spec; verified independently by reinstating the old generator, which turns 11 named probes red at 603 of 614. Its dead `strip_sql_comments` wrapper went too — zero callers, and a convenience wrapper that discards the skeleton, under exactly the name the broken stripper had, is the shape that re-creates the defect. Two residuals of that generator are measured and recorded as backlog item 4 rather than fixed here, because neither occurs in the tree today (checked, not assumed): a final `grant execute … to authenticated` with no terminating semicolon at end-of-file, and a grant to a QUOTED role, each render **none** — and **none** in a security spec means "no API role can call it", which is the wrong way round. **Then the PR's own questions were aimed at the PR, and Codex's at it after that, and between them they found seven more — every one in this commit's own work, and every one of the seven the SAME direction of failure the five findings above are about.** Mine, all three red on healthy code: `form-error` asked `/^[a-z]/` of the whole tag TEXT, so after `import * as ui from "./fields"` the approved `<ui.FormError role="alert" />` was flagged as a bare live region (JSX resolves a tag three ways and only a lowercase IDENTIFIER and a namespaced name are raw elements — a property access is a component whatever its case); `validate_labels()` used `re.search` where a second computed label needs `finditer`, so a step mapped to one would have been reported as claiming a gate `validate.sh` does not declare; and invariant 1 could not see `update only clients set …`, probed across eleven spellings, of which it read nine. The eleventh — a comment between the table and SET — is STATED rather than chased, because reading it needs a SQL comment lexer and PostgreSQL's block comments nest, and the looser alternative was measured and rejected rather than argued away: a rule matching any `update … clients … credit_balance` before the next semicolon also matches `update walks set … where client_id in (select id from clients where credit_balance > 0)`, red on healthy code again. The honest scope is written into the step now: `has_column_privilege` says `anon` and `authenticated` hold NO update on `clients.credit_balance`, so the GRANT is the enforcement and the grep is a backstop against a definer function written in good faith. Codex's SEVEN, across two rounds, all the opposite direction — a gate blessing what it forbids: `<span className={"signin__error"} role={"alert"}>` passed, because a braced value is a `JsxExpression` and the reader gave up on any expression, where a string literal inside braces is the same attribute with braces round it; `broadcast.ts` publishing a PUBLIC message and then a private one passed, because each match overwrote the single result and only the last was asserted; a gate added to `validate.sh` with no `ci.yml` step behind it passed, because the lockstep only ever iterated the map into the labels and never the other way (the invariant inverted); and a commented-out `# run "…"` read as a declared gate, which was not latent — the unanchored regex was at that moment carrying `the same gates in the same order` as a gate label, swallowed out of the prose in `validate.sh`'s own gate-7b comment, the mention-versus-use distinction this repository has paid for before. Round two found three more, each measured against the shipped gate first. A `skip_gate` label counted as a runnable one, so commenting out `run "8c. push endpoint parity"` while leaving its no-deno fallback reported the three lists in lockstep on a `validate.sh` that could no longer run 8c at all — the two are read separately now, and the failure says WHICH, because "renamed" and "can never run" have different fixes. An UNNAMED `run:` step was dropped by the reader's own filter, so `- run: python3 scripts/new-check.py` is a real CI check classified by nobody and mirrored by nothing; the invariant is about checks, not about checks whose author remembered a display name.
