And the server-message rule required `topic` AND `private`, which excluded exactly the literal that matters: `messages: [{ topic, event, payload }, { topic, event, payload, private: true }]` shipped a PUBLIC message while the sibling satisfied the assertion — review H1's whole point is that `private` DEFAULTS to false, so an omission is not a smaller mistake than `false`, and an absent `private` is recorded and failed like any other non-`true` value now. Splitting `run` from `skip_gate` also emptied `LOCAL_ONLY`: the two umbrella skip labels it held were only ever there because a skip label counted as a gate, so the allowlist is empty, still carries its stale check, and says so rather than looking like an oversight. Each fix red-first, and the ones with a healthy direction proven in both: a genuinely dynamic `role={role}` stays invisible, a namespace-imported component stays unflagged, and the anchored label reader names the commented-out gate where the unanchored one misses it and invents a spurious one instead. Round three found two more, and the second corrected a sentence of mine in this very entry. `ci_steps()` classified a step by its display NAME, and a name does not identify a step — this workflow already proves it, with an `Install` in both `frontend` and `e2e-today` — so a genuinely new check named `Tests` in the `database` job silently inherited `edge-functions`' mapping and the gate reported `PASS: 47 ci.yml run-steps classified` having classified nothing (measured). Keyed on `(job, name)` now, and §13's entries are `job / name` for the same reason; rejecting duplicate names outright was the other option offered and would have been RED ON A HEALTHY TREE, since both `Install` steps are legitimate and both SETUP. And invariant 1's alias group accepted only a bare identifier, so `update clients as "c" set …` — valid PostgreSQL, with `prosrc` keeping the quotes — reported PASS against a definer function that really did write the balance (measured: the quoted probe unseen, the plain one seen). Eleven spellings read now, twelve probed. The correction is the part worth keeping: the step's own comment called the column grant "the enforcement" and this check "a backstop", and that is wrong in the one direction that matters, because a SECURITY DEFINER function runs as its OWNER — so the grant that stops `anon` and `authenticated` writing `credit_balance` does not constrain the population this query scans at all. For a definer function the text search IS the only automated check there is. It still catches the mistake rather than the adversary, but it is not standing behind a stronger control, and writing that it was is exactly the false assurance this PR is about. **Round four found three more, and the first was round three's own fix one scope in.** Keying on `(job, name)` fixed a name reused ACROSS jobs and left a name reused WITHIN one: two `run:` steps called `Tests` in `edge-functions` produce the same tuple, so the second satisfied the classification check, satisfied the stale-map check, inherited `6b. deno test`, and the gate reported `PASS: 47` again (measured). A duplicate tuple is REFUSED now rather than given a further discriminator, because the only one available is POSITIONAL and re-points at a different step the moment somebody inserts one above it — the stale-exception shape this file guards against twice — and unlike a name shared across jobs, zero duplicates exist within a job today, so refusing is not red on a healthy tree. **The second inverted a rule rather than widening it.** `verify-deployment.test.ts` asked whether a function had its own `Deno.serve` — enumerating the ways of BYPASSING the house wrapper instead of requiring it — so a function serving itself by any other ordinary spelling took the DEFAULT contract and nobody ever established that a production GET against it is read-only. Measured with a planted `probe-serve/index.ts` importing std's `serve`: 14 of 14 green, and the `addEventListener("fetch")` spelling the same. Enumerating the ways something can be wrong is how the next one is missed — `verify-photo-integrity.sh` took three rounds to learn it — so the question is now "is this function behind `_lib/http.ts`'s `serveFunction`?", which has one answer and no list, and a function that is not must carry a `contract_for` case whatever the reason. Strictly stronger, and confirmed so rather than assumed: the original `Deno.serve` bypass is still caught. The detector requires a CALL of the name IMPORTED from `_lib/http.ts`, and both halves are load-bearing — four `deps.ts` files in this tree name `serveFunction` in a comment explaining why importing an `index.ts` binds a port, which a grep would read as housed, and a local function of the same name proves nothing about what is in front of the request. **The third was a receiver read as a spelling.** `realtime-channel.test.ts` required the receiver to be the identifier `supabase`, so `const realtime = supabase; realtime.channel(…)` — a second, PUBLIC topic — left all five tests green, and a renamed import (`import { supabase as db }`) would have been equally invisible. The client is resolved now from its IMPORT under whatever local name, then by fixpoint over plain aliases through casts and parentheses (the `discarded-errors.test.ts` receiver rule, for the same reason: a receiver is a value, not a word), and every `.channel(` call is classified rather than filtered — one this file cannot resolve to the client is REPORTED, because "cannot say" is not "not a channel", and measured on the healthy tree there is exactly one `.channel(` in `app/src`, so refusing the unresolvable is not red on healthy code. The resolver is pinned on fixtures as well as by the real call, in BOTH directions, because a resolver admitting nothing would report every call unresolvable and pass every alias sabotage for the wrong reason. Nine proofs red-first this round behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the duplicate tuple, an imported-`serve` function, a fetch listener, the plain `Deno.serve` regression, three alias spellings, an unresolvable receiver, and the resolver reduced to the shipped spelling rule. **Round five found three more, and all three were the ROUND-FOUR fixes read one level down — a rule correct about the thing it names and blind to the composition around it.** `housed()` accepted a `serveFunction` call anywhere in the function's DIRECTORY, so an `index.ts` serving itself through an imported `serve(…)` beside an unused helper that calls the wrapper read as housed: 14 of 14 green, measured. A directory is not a request path, so the scan is the ENTRYPOINT (`index.ts`, what Supabase evaluates) and the call must sit outside any function body, because a call that never executes serves nothing; a module-scope call inside an `if` still runs and still counts, since refusing it would be red on a healthy tree. Checked rather than assumed: all fourteen real calls are top-level statements in an `index.ts`. The message scan keyed on a DIRECT `topic` property, so `const message = { topic, …, private: true }` spread into `messages: [{ ...message, private: false }]` left the public literal invisible while the base supplied a reassuring `true` from a line that is not what gets sent — 6 of 6 green while the server published publicly. Literals are read through spreads in SOURCE ORDER now (later wins, exactly as the language does it), following a spread of a locally declared object one hop; a spread it cannot follow OVERWRITES the two names this file asks about rather than merely noting itself, because `{ private: true, ...unknown }` could have the flag replaced and a `true` left standing would pass on a value that may not survive. And the JSX attribute reader skipped spread attributes, so `<span {...{ className: "signin__error", role: "alert" }}>` — the same element to React and to a screen reader — passed 3 of 3; an object-literal spread is unwrapped now, later still wins in both directions, a genuinely dynamic `{...props}` is still invisible, and an unreadable spread does not ERASE a literal `role="alert"` already found, since refusing to un-flag is the safe direction. Each new rule is pinned on FIXTURES as well as by the one real file it reads, because `serverPrivate()` and the tree scan each read exactly one input and every ordering rule would otherwise be proven only under sabotage. Two own errors worth recording, both caught by insisting a sabotage go red for its NAMED reason: a "dynamic spread stays invisible" fixture used `className="signin__hint"` only after the first attempt used `signin__error`, which is itself the forbidden shape, so the green I was looking for would have been a red for an unrelated reason; and the entrypoint rule had NO fixture isolating it — the unused-helper case wrapped its call in a function, so the module-scope rule caught it too and a sabotage restoring the whole-directory scan stayed GREEN. A sabotage that does not go red is a broken proof, so a sibling file calling the wrapper at TOP level was added and the sabotage then named it. Twelve proofs this round — eleven red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores (the three findings, both ordering rules, and five sabotages: spreads ignored, an unresolvable spread merely noted, JSX spreads skipped, function bodies descended into, and the whole-directory scan restored) and one GREEN in the healthy direction, that a dynamic spread on a benign element stays invisible. **Round six found three more, and the useful thing about it is that all three are the same finding in three places, so the response was one reader per file rather than three more special cases.** `if (false) serveFunction(handle); serve(handle);` read as housed, because round five's rule counted any call outside a function body — and I had ALLOWED that, on an argument I never measured, that refusing module-scope conditionals would be red on a healthy tree. Measured since: all fourteen real calls are plain expression statements, so requiring exactly that is green, and "it is at module scope" is simply not "it runs". Only an unconditional statement of the module counts now (an expression statement, a `const` binding, an `export default`, each through `await`, parentheses, `as` and `!`), and a function that genuinely needs a conditional wrapper is exactly one somebody should read and record a `contract_for` case for. The CLIENT's channel options were read by a direct-property helper while the SERVER's messages resolved spreads, so `config: { private: true, ...{ private: false } }` reported private on a client joining a PUBLIC topic — one rule, two scopes, the disagreement this repository keeps paying for, and this time I had built the second scope myself one round earlier by fixing the message side alone. `effectiveProps` is the only reader in that file now; the helper it replaced is deleted, so there is no sibling to forget. And the JSX reader opened the outer spread and then looked for direct properties, so `{...{ ...{ role: "alert" } }}` — one more layer of the same element — passed 4 of 4; the object reader is recursive there too, depth-capped, with the same later-wins ordering and the same distinction between a property that is ABSENT (leaves an earlier answer standing) and one that is present but dynamic (replaces it with no answer). Own error worth recording: a fixture expectation I wrote for the composed message case was simply wrong — every object literal in the file is read, so a composition reports once for itself and once for each nested literal carrying a `topic`, which is the conservative direction and the reason the `const m = …` case reports two values. The code was right and the expectation was not, which is worth noticing before "fixing" a reader to match a number one has assumed. Six proofs this round: the three findings red-first against the shipped gate, and three new fixture groups covering nested spreads, in-place spreads, and the two unconditional spellings that must still count. **Round seven found four, and the most valuable was not about a gate at all — it was a live bug in this PR's own new shell library.** A GitHub Actions `run:` step is `bash -e`, so `code=$(admin …)` exits the WHOLE STEP at the assignment when curl cannot connect: the `case` that would have warned never runs, the deletes after it never run, and the `::error` annotation that is the entire point of the H14 fix in that file can never be printed. Reproduced against an unreachable origin — exit 7, no warning, nothing after it. Codex named `del`; all THREE capture sites had the identical shape, so there is one `admin_status` helper now that prints the status and returns curl's exit, and each caller decides: cleanup warns and carries on (it is housekeeping), a lookup that could not be sent is a FAILED lookup and never an absence (two security assertions in the claim replay read that distinction), and a create says "staging was unreachable, not refusing". Three new tests drive the real library against a dead port, red against the shipped shape. The three gate findings were the same class as the rounds before: a file-global name map let an unused nested `const channelConfig` overwrite the outer binding the call actually spreads, so the WRONG literal answered confidently — worse than answering none — and a name declared twice is unresolvable now rather than resolved by symbol, which is the stopping rule applied to a 30-line file where renaming is the cheaper remedy. Reading LITERALS is sound only while the literal is what gets sent, and `const message = { …, private: true }; message.private = false;` left a stale `true` on a line the POST no longer used — mutation is REFUSED as a precondition rather than modelled, the answer the enum-catalogue generator settled on for the same class of question, and `broadcast.ts` contains none today so it costs nothing. And `role={"alert" as const}` read as dynamic, because `as`, `satisfies`, parentheses and `!` are transparent to React and were not to the reader. Own error worth recording, the second of its kind in two rounds: a fixture expectation for the shadowed-name case predicted two reported values where the reader reports one, because the shadowing literal carries no `topic` and is therefore not a message. The code was right and my arithmetic about it was not — worth catching before "fixing" a reader to match a number one has assumed. Seven proofs this round: four findings red-first against the shipped code, and three new fixture groups. **Round eight found three, and the first is the one I had declined to build a round earlier.** `messages: [{ …, private: true }, publicMessage(topic, event, payload)]` passed 8 of 8 while the second message went to a public topic: the scan answered "is any `{ topic, … }` LITERAL in this file public?", which says nothing about an element that is not a literal at all. In round seven I took the refuse-mutation half of Codex's remedy and left the trace-the-array half, reasoning that tracing was a dataflow analysis — and that was wrong about what the remedy required. Reading the `messages:` array is not dataflow; it is reading the expression that is actually POSTED, which is what the rest of the file already does one level in. Every element must resolve to an object literal written in place or an identifier naming a module-scope one, and a call, a conditional, a spread of an array, an identifier naming nothing, a `messages` that is not an array literal and an EMPTY array are each refused BY NAME. The literal scan stays as the backstop and the two are unioned, so a public message built somewhere the array reader does not look is still caught. Second, `{ ["role"]: "alert" }` — a computed key whose expression is a string literal — names exactly one property and is fully static, and both object readers treated it as unreadable, so the JSX one passed the forbidden shape (4 of 4 green) while the channel one would have failed a legitimate `["private"]: true` on a healthy tree. Both read it now; a key that is genuinely an expression still marks the answer unreadable, since it could be the name being asked about. Third, and the one that matters most operationally: `user_id_for` exhausting its 50-page bound returned 0 WITH EMPTY STDOUT — "no such account" about a project holding more than 5,000 auth users — which is precisely the answer the claim replay's dead-token assertion must never be given wrongly, since it would pass while checking nothing. That is the function's own documented distinction between an absence and an incomplete lookup, contradicted by its last line. It returns 9 now, with a test driving 5,200 users through the real library. Four proofs this round: three findings red-first, and two new fixture groups. **Round nine found three, each an incompleteness in one of my own earlier fixes, and the first is the FIFTH instance in one shell function of the same defect.** `user_id_for`'s repeated-page guard — the one that catches an endpoint ignoring `page` — returned 0 with empty stdout, which the claim replay reads as "no account": the same page twice proves pagination is broken, not that the address is absent. Stopping early was right; calling it ABSENCE was not. That function has now turned "I could not find out" into "it is not there" five separate ways — the single-page lookup, the size-requested stop rule, a transport failure, the 50-page bound and this — which is worth recording as a shape rather than as five bugs. Second, `validate_labels()` collapsed its matches straight into a set, so a second `run` accidentally reusing a label inherited the first's `ci.yml` mapping and was mirrored by nobody while the lockstep reported PASS: the `ci_steps()` duplicate, one function over, in the same file, added in the same PR. Occurrences are counted before the set now and a duplicate is refused. Third, and the sharpest: round seven's shadow-refusal rule counted VARIABLE declarations alone, so a function PARAMETER named `message` did not shadow a module-scope `const message` and the array reader resolved an element to the wrong object — the defect that rule exists to close, one binding form over. Every binding form counts now (parameter, `function`, `class`, import, catch clause, destructured element), and only a variable declaration can carry a literal, so everything else binds null and makes a colliding name unresolvable. Three proofs red-first, and four new fixture cases covering the binding forms. **Round ten found three, and two of them are the same rule living in one of my two readers and not the other — in OPPOSITE directions, which is what finally made the asymmetry worth deleting rather than patching.** The JSX reader did not follow a spread of a locally declared object (`const errorAttrs = { role: "alert" }; <span {...errorAttrs} />` — the ordinary attribute-composition pattern, 4 of 4 green), while the channel reader had followed one since round seven; and the channel reader tested `private` by exact node kind, so `private: true as const` — the ordinary way to preserve a literal type — read as NOT private, which is a gate RED ON A HEALTHY TREE and blocks a behaviour-preserving refactor, while the JSX reader had unwrapped transparent wrappers since round seven. Each file now carries both rules, the shadow-refusal included, so the two cannot disagree about what a name or a value means; the wrappers are unwrapped once in the channel file, in `privateValue`, so the message side and the client side cannot diverge either. Third: `validate_labels()` read only DOUBLE-quoted labels, so `run '13. new check'` — valid shell, and nothing here enforces a quote style — was invisible and the reverse check never required a CI counterpart for a real local gate. Both styles with a backreference now, the `gen-enum-catalog.py` round-twenty-five lesson in a different language. Own error worth recording: the first version of that pattern reached the file as `\\1`, a literal backslash rather than a backreference, so it matched nothing — and what caught it was the gate's own "read no skip_gate labels — this check is blind" precondition firing, which is that guard earning its place. Four proofs this round, three findings red-first plus the `false as const` direction green, and three new fixture groups. **Round eleven found three, the THIRD consecutive round of the same sibling asymmetry, so the response is a shared module rather than a fourth patch.** A `let` binding reassigned before use resolved to its stale initializer in both readers — `let attrs = { role: "status" }; attrs = { role: "alert" }` answered `"status"`, and the mirror answers `"alert"` about an element that no longer carries it, so the old rule was wrong in BOTH directions rather than merely incomplete — and a `config: { ...({ private: true } as const) }` was read as an unresolvable spread, red on healthy code, in the one reader that had not yet learnt what the other learnt a round earlier. `app/scripts/lib/static-object.ts` is the one implementation now: `unwrapTransparent`, `literalText`, `propertyKey`, `declaredObjects` and the assignment-operator test, with each gate keeping only the part that genuinely differs — the JSX reader distinguishes absent from dynamic so an earlier answer survives, the channel reader carries nodes and markers so it can recurse into `config` and print what it could not read. Those are different questions; the primitives underneath them were not, and that is what kept diverging. The binding rule is now about the ASSIGNMENT rather than the keyword — a `const` that is shadowed is refused, a `let` nothing assigns to still resolves — so it refuses on evidence rather than on a style. Honest about what the first of the three fixes does and does not do: for Codex's exact element the outcome is a MISS either way, since an unresolvable spread cannot create evidence any more than `{...props}` can; what the fix removes is the CONFIDENTLY WRONG answer, which the fixture proves (sabotaging the assignment tracking turns `expected 'alert' to be null` red in one file and `expected ['true'] to deeply equal ['<unresolvable spread `c`>']` in the other). Own error worth recording, the third of its kind: a fixture expectation again predicted two reported values where the reader reports one, because the inner literal of `...({ private: true } as const)` carries no `topic` and is therefore not a message. Read the reader, do not predict it. Five proofs this round: two findings red-first, the wrapped-spread direction green in two spellings, and one sabotage of the shared module turning both files red. **Round twelve found three, and the shared module paid for itself immediately: two of the three were one edit in one file rather than two edits in two.** "Assigned" meant rebinding the NAME and not mutating what it HOLDS, so `const channelConfig = { private: true }; channelConfig.private = false;` spread into the client's `config` read as private on a channel joining a public topic — and a loop variable is assigned on every iteration while producing no assignment expression at all, so `for (channelConfig of [{ private: false }])` did the same. Both are now part of one rule in `static-object.ts`: a binding is unresolvable if the name is rebound (assignment, compound, `++`, a destructuring target, a bare `for…of`/`for…in` variable) OR if the object is mutated (`x.y =`, `x["y"] =`, `delete x.y`, `Object.assign(x, …)`), with a loop that DECLARES its own variable left alone so the rule is not red on healthy code. The property-mutation half is the same rule `mutations()` already applied to `broadcast.ts` — applied to the bindings a spread resolves, where it was missing. Third, the deployment gate's own unwrap did not know `void`, so `void serveFunction(handle)` — an unconditional call, and the ordinary way to say "deliberately not awaited" — demanded a bespoke production contract for a function that is behind the wrapper: red on a healthy tree again, and the one unwrap in this PR that did not move into the shared module because it unwraps `await` too, which the others must not. Six proofs this round: two findings red-first, the `void` direction green in two spellings, and seven new fixture cases across mutation, loops and the declared-loop-variable exemption. **Round thirteen found two — both RED ON A HEALTHY TREE, which is the direction worth ending on.** `Object.assign` was matched on the METHOD NAME alone, so an unrelated `registry.assign(channelConfig)` invalidated an immutable literal and `metrics.assign({…})` in `broadcast.ts` was reported as a mutation; the receiver decides now (`Object` or `globalThis.Object`), through one predicate both sites share rather than a copy of the test in each. And the 50-page pagination case had no explicit timeout: it is 1.1 s on this container but Codex measured ~5.04 s on a slower one, against vitest's 5 s default — a gate that fails for machine speed rather than for a regression. The ceiling is stated (30 s) and the case says why the work cannot be made smaller: proving the bound is REACHED requires reaching it. **Own error worth recording, and it is the NINTH of the restore family**: this round's snapshots were all taken at the top of the round, before its fix existed, under names ending `.FIXED.ts` — so a sabotage restore quietly un-fixed the file and three later "proofs" ran against pre-fix code, one of them reporting a pass that meant nothing. The command was right and the file was wrong. Name a snapshot for WHEN it was taken, re-take it after every fix, and check the fix is still present rather than trusting the name. A second own error in the same round, the red-for-the-wrong-reason trap: the probe for the `metrics.assign` case passed `{ topic }`, which IS a message literal to the backstop scan, so the run went red for a reason unrelated to the rule under test — the payload is `{ label: event }` now. Six proofs this round: one finding red-first, three healthy-tree directions green, one sabotage of the shared predicate turning two fixture groups red, and three new fixture cases. **Round fourteen found ONE, which is where this converged.** A mutation through an ALIAS — `const alias = config; alias.private = false;` — recorded only the name it was written through, so the original kept its stale literal and a client joining a public topic read as private. The invalidation is closed over an alias graph now (`const b = a` makes the two names one object, undirected and transitive), which is Codex's precise remedy rather than its conservative one: refusing to resolve anything that is ever aliased would have been broader than the defect, and there are real aliases in the scanned tree. Aliasing by itself is therefore not a refusal — only a mutation through any name for the same object is — and that direction is pinned. Three proofs: the finding red-first, the unmutated-alias direction green, and the alias edges sabotaged. **A tenth own error, found while writing this entry**: the round-thirteen paragraph was in this file TWICE, because a combined edit script had two independent `python3` blocks and I re-ran both after the first block's second assertion failed — the block that had already written. A script that writes before a later assertion can fail is not idempotent, and re-running it is not free. The duplicate is removed; the lesson is to make an edit script assert everything BEFORE it writes anything, which the ones here now do. **Round fifteen found one, and it is round fourteen one binding form short** — the same shape as round nine's finding about the shadow rule. An alias can be formed by an ASSIGNMENT as well as a declaration, so `let alias; alias = config; alias.private = false;` linked nothing and `config` kept its stale literal. Fixing it exposed a latent over-refusal in my own round-fourteen closure, which the fix separates rather than papering over: REBINDING a name and MUTATING an object are different events, and closing the first over the alias graph would refuse `config` for `let a = config; a = other;`, where nothing has touched it. Two sets now — rebinding costs one name its literal, mutation costs it to every name for that object — with both directions pinned, and the sabotage that merges them turns the over-refusal case red. Three proofs. Fifteen rounds, forty-one findings. The shape shifted as it went: the first ten were mostly a gate BLESSING what it forbids, the last five mostly a gate RED ON A HEALTHY TREE — the direction hardest to see from inside one's own work — and the answer each time was structural rather than another case: one reader per file, then one module for the primitives the two readers kept diverging on, then refusal-as-precondition wherever modelling would have been a dataflow analysis. **Round sixteen found two, one of which was FIXED and one DECLINED — the first decline of this PR, and the reasoning is the point.** The fix: an alias is also formed by a DESTRUCTURING assignment, so `[alias] = [config]; alias.private = false;` linked nothing and `config` kept its stale literal (measured: the reader still answered `config`). The link is made conservatively — every assignment target to every identifier the right side mentions — rather than by matching positions, because the right side can be any expression; that over-links, which can refuse a name nothing touched and can never miss one that was mutated. The decline: an alias REBOUND before being mutated (`let a = config; a = other; a.private = false;`) still invalidates `config`, and Codex is right that this is a false red. Removing the stale edge requires knowing which assignment ran first — flow sensitivity — and the trade is not free in either direction, because the mirror (`a.private = false;` THEN `a = other;`) is a real mutation a lifetime-aware graph would MISS. For a gate deciding whether a walk's live position reaches a public topic, an over-refusal is legible and its remedy is obvious while a miss is silent, so the refusal stays — PINNED as a test with the reasoning beside it, so changing it is a decision somebody makes rather than a regression, and stated as costing nothing today since neither scanned file aliases anything. Three proofs: the destructuring link sabotaged, the same case end-to-end through the real hook, and the refusal pinned. Sixteen rounds, forty-three findings, forty-two fixed and one declined on a stated trade-off. **Round seventeen found three, two of them red on a healthy tree.** A destructuring DECLARATION forms an alias exactly as the assignment form does — `const [alias] = [config]` — and round sixteen had covered only the assignment: the same one-form-short shape three rounds running, so both now go through the same conservative target-to-source link. `{ "messages": [...] }` — a quoted key — failed the message reader's own PRECONDITION, so a behaviour-preserving refactor turned the gate red while the private message sat there fully readable; it goes through `propertyKey` now, the helper that already existed one module over for exactly this. And a `run` split across lines for readability was invisible to a same-line regex, so a real local gate could be absent from CI with the reverse check reporting lockstep: continuations are joined first, and — the more useful half — a `run` or `skip_gate` whose label cannot be read is now REFUSED BY NAME rather than skipped, the enum-generator's rule in a third language. That refusal went red on the healthy tree on its first run, because `run() {` and `skip_gate() { … }` are DEFINITIONS rather than invocations — caught immediately, and the exclusion is one `(` — which is the shape of nearly every own-error in these rounds: a rule correct about its subject and one form too wide or one form too narrow. Four proofs. Seventeen rounds, forty-six findings. **Round eighteen found three, all "one more form" again, so this round stopped adding forms and enumerated the SETS instead.** The three were a parameter default (`function m(alias = config)`), a `for…of` that declares its own variable (`for (const alias of [config])`), and a shell `run` behind a control prefix (`if run "…" cmd; then`). Four consecutive rounds of the same shape is the argument for a completion claim rather than a fifth patch, so: on the TypeScript side there is now ONE helper answering "what can this name come to hold?", listing the complete set of constructs that bind a name together with a value expression — variable declaration, parameter, binding element, `for…of`/`for…in`, and assignment — with the note that nothing else in the language does (a class field, a catch clause and an import bind a name with no in-file source to link it to). On the shell side the reader no longer anchors at line start: a command word is matched wherever a command can START — after a newline, a separator `; & | ( ) { }`, or any of bash's reserved words, taken from its grammar rather than from the cases in front of me — with comments stripped quote-aware first, so a `# run "x"` is a mention and not a gate. Both went red on the healthy tree on the first attempt and both were caught immediately: the TS enumeration compiled clean, but the shell one matched the reserved word INTO the command text, so eighteen real gates read as unreadable — the prefix is outside the captured group now. Nine proofs: three findings red-first, four control-prefix spellings read, a comment NOT read, and an unreadable form still refused by name. Eighteen rounds, forty-nine findings, forty-eight fixed and one declined on a stated trade-off. **Round nineteen found four, two of which falsified round eighteen's own completion claims — which is the more useful result than the fixes.** On the TypeScript side the "complete set of constructs that bind a name" omitted an ENUM and a namespace (both introduce a value binding, so either shadows an outer object and makes the map answer confidently and wrongly), and the alias edge covered `=` while a LOGICAL assignment (`a ??= config`) assigns the same object when it runs. Both are in now, with the asymmetry stated: a logical assignment FORMS an alias and is deliberately not a definite REBINDING, because it may not run — two questions about one statement with different answers. On the shell side the command-position rule missed an assignment or redirection prefix (`MODE=ci run "…"`, which bash allows before the command word) and a reserved word at offset 0 of the text. Both are in; the second cannot occur in a real script, whose first line is a shebang, and was fixed anyway because it is one alternation. **The honest disposition after nineteen rounds is to stop claiming completeness for the shell reader:** the `unreadable` refusal cannot be widened to every occurrence of the word, because `npm --prefix app run lint` legitimately contains one, so telling an invocation from an argument NEEDS command position and a regex over bash will always have an edge. The robust answer is a `--list-gates` mode in `validate.sh` itself — the shell parsing the shell — and that is written into `docs/dev/backlog.md` with its design rather than claimed again here. Ten proofs: four findings red-first, and a spelling matrix of ten shell forms covering every prefix, a comment that must NOT be read, and `npm run lint` as an argument that must not be either. **Round twenty found three, and the first is the sharpest kind: my round-nineteen COMMENT and my round-nineteen CODE disagreed.** The comment said a logical assignment forms an alias and is deliberately not a definite rebinding; the predicate feeding `rebound` was `isAssignmentOperator`, which includes all three, so `let attrs = { role: "alert" }; attrs ??= { role: "status" };` dropped a literal the program necessarily still holds. The resolution is not a guess about control flow but a PROPERTY of the map: a name is only ever in it when its declaration initializer is an object literal, which is non-nullish and truthy — so `??=` and `||=` cannot assign to such a name and are not rebindings, while `&&=` always can and is. Second: the NAME of a function or class EXPRESSION binds inside its own body, so `const C = function attrs() { … attrs … }` resolved to an unrelated outer literal — the confident-wrong-answer hazard again, in the two forms the declaration list did not cover. Third: a NAMESPACE import of the house wrapper (`import * as http …; http.serveFunction(handle)`) read as unhoused and demanded a bespoke production contract — red on a healthy tree. Own error worth recording, and the fixtures caught it rather than review: excluding `??=` from rebinding also excluded it from the ALIAS graph, because the two rules were nested one inside the other, so `let a; a ??= config; a.private = false;` stopped invalidating `config`. They are separate blocks now, which is what they always were conceptually. Six proofs: three findings red-first, the namespace direction green, and three sabotages of the new rules. **Round twenty-one found three, and the useful part is what my own fix for the third one then failed at.** An ALIAS of a literal — `const base = { role: "alert" }; const attrs = base; <span {...attrs} />` is the composition pattern one hop longer, and it bound `attrs` to null, so the JSX gate skipped the spread as unresolvable and MISSED the raw element entirely (measured: `declaredObjects` answers `['base']`). The alias edge was already recorded for mutation; the LITERAL travels it now, transitively and through the transparent wrappers, and REFUSES when the source is not resolvable in its own right — `let base = {a}; base = {b}; const attrs = base;` gives `attrs` the second object while the map still holds the first, so following a rebound source would be confidently wrong rather than merely incomplete, which is the hazard the whole map exists to avoid. Second, `(supabase).channel(…)` leaves the receiver a `ParenthesizedExpression`, and an identifier-only test reported a correctly private call as unresolvable — a gate RED ON A HEALTHY TREE, measured against the one real call by planting the parentheses. The receiver goes through `unwrapTransparent` now and `clientNames`' local copy of that unwrapper is deleted (it knew one wrapper fewer — the sibling divergence the shared module exists to end), but the half that matters more is that `channelCallsIn` is split out so the CLASSIFICATION is drivable from fixtures at all: it read the real tree only, where there is exactly one call, so every rule about receivers and options was proven by sabotage and by nothing in the committed suite. Third, a bash assignment prefix may carry a quoted value containing whitespace, so `MODE="ci mode" run "13. new check"` was invisible in BOTH directions — neither runnable nor unreadable (measured: `[]` and `[]`) — and a local gate could lack a CI counterpart while lockstep reported success. The value is read as a shell WORD now, bare characters plus quoted sections, the three branches disjoint by their first character so the nesting cannot backtrack. **And that third fix arrived with exactly the defect this PR is about**: `validate_labels()` reads one real file, so the reader's spelling matrix lived only in sabotages nobody re-runs. `_SPELLINGS` is driven on every invocation now, fifteen rows in both directions — and its FIRST version reported PASS over an empty matrix, measured by emptying it, so the precondition counts INSIDE the loop and demands at least two rows of each direction. Counting the list instead would have missed a loop iterating something else, which is precisely what the sabotage did. When `validate.sh --list-gates` lands (backlog item 1) the shell parses the shell and this reader goes away; the matrix becomes the test of the lister's output rather than being deleted. Ten proofs: three findings red-first, and seven sabotages — alias resolution disabled, the rebound-source guard dropped, the receiver unwrap reverted, a receiver rule admitting everything, the prefix word reverted (naming its five quoted rows), the matrix loop emptied, and the matrix reduced to one direction. One own error worth recording: a sabotage that removed the matrix's negative rows by literal text failed its own assertion on an escaping mistake and wrote nothing, so the run that followed was the healthy file — the "SABOTAGE DID NOT APPLY" discipline catching a broken proof rather than a passing one. **Round twenty-two found three, and the first is the round-four inversion one level in.** `housed()` required the house wrapper and not what MAKES the wrapper read-only: the argument is that `handleRequest` answers 405 before the handler, and `ServeOptions.methods` widens exactly that — so a `serveFunction(handle, { methods: ["GET", "POST"] })` took the DEFAULT contract while `verify-deployment.sh` fires an unauthenticated GET at PRODUCTION, which would have executed its body. Measured by planting one: 14 of 14 green, silent. That is the enumerate-instead-of-require shape round four was itself about, surviving one level down in its own fix. `refusesGet` reads the options literal now and REFUSES anything it cannot resolve — a spread, a computed key, a non-literal `methods` — because an unreadable list is not evidence of POST-only; `unsubscribe`, which genuinely admits GET, moves to unhoused and passes on the reviewed `contract_for` case it already carries, which is the rule working rather than an exception. Second, an ACCESSOR is an object-literal member that defines a property and answers a function body, and both object readers fell through every branch for one — so `{ ...base, get private() { return false; } }` reported the SPREAD's `true` for a key the object no longer carries, a confidently wrong answer rather than an absent one. `definesWithoutValue` lives in the shared module and both gates consume it, so the two cannot disagree about it; one sabotage turns both files red, which is that module earning its place. Third, a reserved word begins at a command position too, so `cmd;if run "13. new check"` and `(if run "…"` were invisible in BOTH directions (measured: `[]` and `[]`) — the same set of places that can precede a command now precedes a reserved word. Seven proofs: three findings red-first (the third as a LIVE probe against the real function tree, which named `probe-get` where the shipped gate was silent), and four sabotages — `refusesGet` always true (`upsilon`), the accessor predicate answering false (both readers at once), and the boundary reverted (naming its three separator rows). Twenty-two rounds, sixty-two findings. **Round twenty-three found one, and it is round twenty-two's own fix red on a healthy tree.** `serveFunction(handle, undefined)` is byte-for-byte the POST-only default, because the parameter carries a default initializer (`_lib/http.ts:258`, `options: ServeOptions = {}`), and `{ methods: undefined }` is the default list, because `handleRequest` reads `options.methods ?? DEFAULT_METHODS` (`:294`) — both verified in the source before anything moved. Round twenty-two's `refusesGet` refuses whatever it cannot read, which was right for a spread and wrong for these two: measured against the shipped gate with both planted, `expected [ 'probe-undef', 'probe-undefmethods' ] to deeply equal []`, i.e. each demanded a bespoke production `contract_for` case for a call that already refuses GET. `isExplicitUndefined` goes in the shared module and both positions consult it, `void <anything>` included since the VALUE is static however the operand behaves. **The shadow question was measured rather than guarded**: `undefined` is a legal binding name in a module and plain JavaScript honours it (a module-scope `let undefined = {…}` makes a defaulted parameter take that object — run, not assumed), which would be the gate BLESSING what it forbids; but TypeScript refuses the binding outright (`TS2397: Declaration name conflicts with built-in global identifier`) and every file this gate reads is typechecked, so the state cannot occur. That is the #85 round-23 stopping rule — grow nothing for an unreachable state, and PIN the precondition rather than leaving it implicit — so a test runs the repo's own `tsc` against the shadow and fails if it is ever accepted, naming the decision that would then be due. Five proofs red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: each position reverted separately (`psi`/`psi2`, then `omega`), the shared predicate wired to false (both at once), the over-correction where `null` counts as undefined (`omega2`, since a default initializer does not fire for null), and the precondition test against a compiler that accepts the shadow. Own error worth recording: the first attempt at that last one had a wrong Python anchor, wrote nothing, and the run beneath it was the HEALTHY file reporting 15 passed — the "SABOTAGE DID NOT APPLY" assertion caught it, which is the only reason a green run was not mistaken for a proof. Twenty-three rounds, sixty-three findings. **Round twenty-four falsified round twenty-three's precondition, which is the more useful result than the fix.** I had declined a shadow walk on the measured ground that TypeScript refuses to bind `undefined`, and pinned that as a test. It is FALSE. Codex's own example — a destructuring binding — is refused (measured, TS2397), but the general point held, and enumerating every module-scope form found the one that matters: an IMPORT ALIAS is ACCEPTED. `import { wideOpen as undefined } from "./opts.ts"; serveFunction(handle, undefined);` typechecks, and a `.ts` specifier is exactly how these Deno functions import, so the form is reachable in the very files this gate reads. Measured against the shipped gate with such a function planted: **15 passed, silent** — a handler admitting GET classified as the POST-only default, so `verify-deployment.sh` would fire an unauthenticated production GET at a body that runs. The gate BLESSING what it forbids, which is the worse direction, produced by a decline I had called measured. The identifier is RESOLVED now rather than matched by name: a file that binds it anywhere gets no answer, which sends its function to a reviewed `contract_for` case — conservative by construction, since a false refusal costs one recorded reading and a false acceptance costs a live GET. The refused declaration forms (`let`/`var`/`const`, both destructurings, a defaulted binding element, `function`, `class`, `enum`, `namespace`) and the accepted import form are BOTH pinned now, in one test that fails in either direction, because the old one asserted a claim rather than a boundary. Four proofs red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the shipped name-only rule (both new fixtures flip), the import half of the enumeration dropped (only the import fixture flips), the declaration half dropped (only the loop-binding fixture flips — so each half is individually load-bearing rather than incidentally covered), and a compiler that refuses the import form. Twenty-four rounds, sixty-four findings. **Round twenty-five found one, and three of my own sabotages for it stayed green — each a broken proof that named a real gap in the matrix rather than a passing test.** `#` opens a shell comment only at the START OF A WORD, and `_strip_shell_comments` blanked every unquoted occurrence to the end of the line: `MODE=ci#local run "13. new check" true` invokes `run` (measured against bash), while the reader saw nothing, so the gate was in neither the runnable nor the unreadable set — invisible in both directions, which is how a local gate lacks a CI counterpart while lockstep reports success. Broader than the reviewer's example: an ordinary word carrying a `#` anywhere earlier on the line (`echo a#b; run "…"`) did the same to every gate after it. The word-boundary set is bash's METACHARACTERS read from the grammar and each measured — space, tab, newline, `|&;()<>` — with `{}` deliberately absent, because they are reserved WORDS and `echo x}#y` is the single word `x}#y`, so including them would blank a real gate on any line mentioning a brace. A word also CONTINUES through a closing quote (`echo "a"#b` prints `a#b`) and through a backslash escape (`echo \\ #x` prints ` #x`), both measured rather than reasoned about. Byte-for-byte no change to what is stripped from the real `validate.sh`, so this closes a hole without moving any present expectation. The valuable part was the proving: sabotage B (never strip a comment at all) PASSED, because a bare space is not a command position and `# run "x"` is refused by the boundary alone — so comment stripping was load-bearing only for a `;` or a reserved word INSIDE the comment text, and the matrix had no such row; sabotage C (a closing quote opens a new word) PASSED, because the negative row it was aimed at is satisfied either way, and needed a row where a gate FOLLOWS the quoted hash; sabotage D (drop the backslash rule) needed the escaped-SPACE row, since the escaped-hash row is satisfied by the word-start rule one branch up. Each gap is a row now. Five proofs red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the shipped strip-every-hash rule, comment stripping disabled, a closing quote opening a word, the backslash rule dropped, and braces added to the break set. Twenty-five rounds, sixty-five findings. **Round twenty-six found the sibling of round twenty-five, one grammar over — the per-site disease inside the round that had just taught the rule.** An unquoted BACKSLASH escapes the next character, so `MODE=ci\\ mode run "13. new check" true` is one assignment prefix and one command to bash (measured, `MODE=[ci mode]`), and the `word` pattern's bare branch merely excluded whitespace: it stopped at the escaped space, the prefix never matched, and the gate was in neither the runnable nor the unreadable set — invisible in both directions, the same failure round twenty-five fixed in the comment stripper one round earlier while this grammar, ten lines away and needing the identical rule, was never looked at. Measured across four spellings against the shipped gate, all `runnable=[] unreadable=[]`: an escaped space, an escaped separator (`MODE=a\\;b`), an escaped redirection target (`>my\\ file`) and an escaped quote. The fix adds an escape branch and excludes `\\` from the bare branch so the four branches stay disjoint by their first character; the real `validate.sh` reads identically before and after (24 runnable, 7 skipped, none unreadable), so this closes a hole without moving any present expectation. **One sabotage stayed green and taught the honest scope of the ordering claim**: with the escape branch LAST and `\\` left in the bare branch every spelling still reads, because Python's engine backtracks into it — so the order is a BACKTRACKING property and not a matching one, no matrix row can pin it, and the comment now says so rather than implying the rows prove it. What the rows pin is that the branch exists. A second own error, the round-twenty-five lesson arriving immediately: the first `MODE=a\\#b` row I wrote was satisfied under BOTH grammars (a `#` is an ordinary bare character either way) and under the stripper sabotage too — a row that cannot fail — so it is `MODE=a\\ #b` now, which is red under a sabotage of either rule and is what ties the two together. Four proofs red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the shipped word grammar (four rows red, the tying row among them) and the stripper's backslash branch (two rows red, one of them round twenty-five's own). Twenty-six rounds, sixty-six findings. **Round twenty-seven found a separator inside a QUOTED word read as a command boundary, and the round is worth recording for the two sabotages that stayed green rather than for the fix.** `echo 'diagnostic; run "13. fake" true'` runs only `echo` (measured), while the reader scanned the raw text and reported `13. fake` as runnable — a PHANTOM local gate, which satisfies a ci.yml mapping and lets quoted diagnostic text keep the reverse check happy after the real gate has been deleted. Measured across three spellings against the shipped gate, all reporting a phantom label. The lexer emits a same-length SKELETON now, with the CONTENTS of every quoted span masked and the quote DELIMITERS kept, so one pattern finds a real gate's own label and the label is read back out of the clean text at the same span — the technique `gen-enum-catalog.py` settled on in its rounds six and seven, in a third language. The real `validate.sh` reads identically before and after (24 runnable, 7 skipped, none unreadable). **The first sabotage — the shipped raw-text scan — stayed GREEN, and that was the more useful finding**: `_SPELLINGS` drove the PATTERN directly while `validate_labels` did its own scanning, so the matrix spoke for a reader the real run did not use and a sabotage of the real one proved nothing. One `shell_gate_labels` now, called by both, which is the round-twenty-one move (`channelCallsIn` split out so the classification is drivable from fixtures at all) arriving in the sibling file. The same asymmetry held for the UNREADABLE scan, which had no matrix at all and whose failure direction is the worse one: off the raw text `echo 'note; run'` is reported as an unreadable gate (measured) — a gate RED ON A HEALTHY TREE. It is `shell_unreadable_calls` now with its own two-row matrix and its own both-directions precondition. **The second green sabotage is recorded as a limit rather than papered over**: masking a newline inside a quoted span changes no verdict I can construct, because the boundary before a real gate is a newline OUTSIDE the quotes and is never masked either way — the only behaviour that moves is a label spanning lines, refused by name one way and read as a multi-line label the other, both ending in a red. The preservation stays as the conservative reading and the comment says outright that no row pins it, which is the honest form of the round-twenty-six ordering note one round on. Four proofs red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the raw-text label scan (three rows, the multi-line one among them), the label read off the mask (twenty-six rows), the raw-text unreadable scan, and the unreadable matrix emptied. Twenty-seven rounds, sixty-seven findings. **Round twenty-eight found one, and it is the difference between "preceded by whitespace" and "at a command position" — a distinction bash makes and the reader did not.** A reserved word is reserved only WHERE A COMMAND CAN START, so in an argument it is an ordinary word: `echo if run "13. fake" true` runs only `echo` (measured, and the same for `while`, `done` and `then`), while the reserved-word branch admitted one after ANY whitespace and reported `13. fake` as a runnable gate — a PHANTOM label, which satisfies a ci.yml mapping and keeps the reverse check happy after the real local gate has been deleted, so the lockstep would report success over a gate that no longer exists. Round twenty-two had widened that branch to admit a reserved word after a separator, correctly, and taken arbitrary whitespace with it. A reserved word is anchored at a genuine separator now, and a CHAIN of them is allowed because a reserved word is itself a command position — `if ! run`, `cmd; then run` — which also replaces the `(?<=\s!)` lookbehind that handled `!` separately and had NO ROW in the matrix: `! run "…"` at the start of a line was invisible to it (measured), so that spelling is pinned for the first time here. The real `validate.sh` reads identically before and after (23 runnable, 10 skipped, none unreadable), so this closes a hole without moving any present expectation. **One existing row stopped distinguishing and the comment beside it says so rather than implying otherwise**: `# if run "…"` was red under a sabotage of the comment stripper only because the old boundary read the `if` inside the comment text, and the tightened one refuses it whether or not the comment is stripped — the stripper is still load-bearing, via the `# cmd; run "…"` row beside it, which is red under that sabotage and was the one that found the rule in the first place. Four sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the shipped whitespace rule (three new negative rows, plus the line-start `!`), the `!` branch dropped from the chain (both `!` rows), the chain dropped entirely (all six reserved-word positives), and comment stripping disabled (the separator row alone). Twenty-eight rounds, sixty-eight findings. **Round twenty-nine found the same phantom one construct over, and two siblings it did not name.** A `)` is a metacharacter and a command boundary — except when it CLOSES a substitution: bash runs only `echo` for `echo $(printf x) run "13. fake" true`, and the same for `$((…))` and `<(…)` (measured for all three), while the reader returned `13. fake` — the round-twenty-seven phantom in a construct the quoted-span mask cannot reach, and a phantom label satisfies a ci.yml mapping after the real local gate is deleted. One stack answers it: each `(` records whether `$`, `<` or `>` opened it, so only a substitution's closer stops being a boundary while `(run "…" x)` and `case a in a) run "…" x;; esac` — both real command positions (measured) — keep theirs, and a `)` with nothing on the stack is a case pattern's. The same stack settles a rule the reviewer did not name: a word CONTINUES through a substitution closer (`echo $(printf a)#b` prints `a#b`) and ENDS at a subshell's (`(printf b)#c` prints `b`), so the shipped reader blanked the rest of a line carrying a real gate after the first — both directions measured and both pinned. And the sibling CONSTRUCT: an unquoted BACKTICK is `$( )` spelled another way, so a command position opens after it — `` echo `run "13. x" y` `` runs `run` (measured) while the reader found NOTHING, invisible in both directions, which is the failure rounds twenty-five and twenty-six were about. The real `validate.sh` reads identically before and after (23 runnable, 10 skipped, none unreadable), and its twenty backticks all sit inside comments, which are stripped first. Own error worth recording, the third of its kind on this PR: I wrote the `(printf b)#c; run "…" x` fixture as a POSITIVE and bash refuted it — the `#c` opens a comment that swallows the gate — so the code was right and my prediction was not, again, and the row is a negative that pins the subshell half of the word rule. Seven sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the paren branches bypassed (the shipped behaviour — three phantoms and the substitution-hash row), the opener never recognising a substitution, an empty stack read as one (the case pattern), the closer ending a word, NO closer ending a word (which is what makes the subshell row load-bearing), the backtick branch bypassed, and a closing backtick ending a word. Twenty-nine rounds, sixty-nine findings. **Round thirty found the round-twenty-nine stack consuming an entry it never opened.** A `case` pattern's `)` closes no `(`, so inside a substitution it POPPED the substitution: `echo $(case a in a) run "1. real" true;; esac)` runs `run` (measured) while the reader answered nothing — a real gate invisible — and the substitution's own closer, now unpaired, stayed a boundary, so `echo $(case a in a) :;; esac) run "1. fake" true` reported a PHANTOM for a word bash passes to `echo` (measured too, both halves reproduced against the shipped gate). The stack carries a `case` marker now, pushed at the reserved word and popped at `esac`, and a `)` above one is a pattern closer that consumes nothing — so a SECOND pattern in the same `case` still reads, which is the row that makes the guard load-bearing and which I did not have until a sabotage of it stayed green. **`case` counts only AT a command position**, or an argument would invent the very phantom this closes: `echo $(echo case) run "…" true` runs no `run` (measured), so the lexer tracks one flag whose ONLY consumer is this marker — an imprecision in it can add or drop a `case` and can never move a boundary. Own error worth recording, and the trace found it rather than review: the first version evaluated that flag at every WORD START including whitespace, which matched no bare word and cleared the flag one character before the word it was meant to read, so `esac` was never recognised and the phantom survived the fix. The real `validate.sh` reads identically before and after (23 runnable, 10 skipped, none unreadable) and contains no `case` at all. Eight rows added, each measured against bash as written; five sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the shipped pop-on-every-closer (the two-pattern rows), the marker never pushed, `esac` never popping, the whitespace guard removed, and every word treated as reserved (the argument row). **Round thirty-one found the same phantom in the one boundary this reader had matched as a CHARACTER rather than as a word.** A brace is a reserved WORD in bash and is recognised only where it stands alone at a command position, so `echo x{ run "13. fake" true` runs only `echo` (measured) while the reader returned `13. fake` — a PHANTOM local gate, which satisfies a ci.yml mapping and keeps the reverse check happy after the real local gate has been deleted. Broader than the reviewer's example, and that is what measuring the general claim rather than the case in front of me showed: `echo x} run …`, `echo a{b,c} run …` (a brace expansion) and `echo ${HOME} run …` (every parameter expansion in the file) each produced the same phantom, four spellings from one lookbehind. The brace moves into the reserved-word CHAIN, where it must be preceded by a separator and followed by whitespace — which is bash's own rule, measured: `{run "…" true; }` is a syntax error — and `}` leaves the boundary machinery outright, because it never precedes a command at all (`{ :; } run` is a syntax error too, so a separator always stands between a group's closer and the next command). The two SIBLINGS carried the same claim and were fixed in the same edit rather than left for a later round: the lexer's own command-position flag treated every brace as a separator, so `echo $(echo x{ case) run "1. lexer-brace" true` read `case` out of an argument, pushed a `case` marker, stopped the substitution's closer being a boundary and invented the round-thirty phantom from the other side; and `_BARE_WORD` ended a word at a brace, so `case{` — the ordinary word `case{`, which bash tries to RUN (measured) — was read as the reserved word and did the same. The real `validate.sh` reads identically before and after: 23 runnable, 10 skipped, none unreadable. Fourteen rows added, each measured against bash as written; five sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the character lookbehind (four rows), the bare-word terminator (the `case{` row alone, so each half is individually load-bearing rather than incidentally covered), the brace dropped from the chain (all seven group rows), and the lexer rule in BOTH directions — every brace opening a command position, which also turns round twenty-five's `echo x}#y` row red because a brace that ends a word lets the `#` after it open a comment, and no brace opening one, which loses the `case` inside a group. **Round thirty-two found the one construct the quoted-span mask hides from itself.** Bash evaluates a command substitution INSIDE a double-quoted word, so `result="$(run \"13. hidden gate\" true)"` executes `run` (measured) while the lexer masked the whole quoted region and both readers answered nothing — invisible in BOTH directions, neither runnable nor unreadable, which is how a real local gate lacks a CI counterpart while lockstep reports success. The backtick spelling and an argument position (`echo "$(run …)"`) do the same. A double quote is SUSPENDED for the substitution's duration now and resumes at its closer, tracked two ways because the two spellings are: a `$(` lands on the paren stack and its quote comes back when that entry is popped, a backtick only flips `in_backtick` and its quote comes back when that does. The BOUNDARIES were measured rather than assumed, and four of them are rows: a single quote suspends nothing, an escaped `\\$(` or ``\\` `` opens nothing, a `$` not followed by `(` is an expansion rather than a substitution, and the quote genuinely RESUMES — so `"$(printf a); run '…' true"` is literal text after the closer and not a phantom gate. Codex named the first; the other two came from measuring the general claim, which is the same move rounds twenty-five to thirty-one kept needing. The grammar is bash's now: an optional descriptor (digits or `{name}`, attached with NO space — `2 >|g.a` runs a command called `2`), one of `<<<`/`<<-`/`<<`/`<>`/`<&`/`>|`/`>>`/`>&`/`<`/`>` longest-first, or `&>`/`&>>` which take no descriptor at all (`2&>f` and `{fd}&>f` are both command-not-found), then optional whitespace and the target. **Requiring the target to be NON-EMPTY closed a PHANTOM the old grammar had**: `> run "1. fake" true` read as a redirection with no target followed by a gate, while bash redirects stdout to a file NAMED `run` and then tries to execute the label — no gate runs, and reporting one satisfies a ci.yml mapping for a gate that does not exist. **My own sabotage round then found two more phantoms, both pre-existing on the previous head and both closed here** (measured against `afec76d` rather than assumed): `>| run "1. fake" true` survived the non-empty-target rule by a different route, the `|` being read as a command boundary, so the lexer now emits the `|` of a `>|` as its own inert marker — its OWN marker and not `_MASK`, because the redirection grammar still has to recognise the operator, and masked as an ordinary character the phantom came back through the target word instead; and `echo $(>|case) run "1. fake" true` reported a gate because a bare word could contain the substitution CLOSER, so a target word that began inside a substitution ran past its end — a word OUTSIDE one continues through the closer (round twenty-nine) and a word that began inside it cannot, which is one character removed from a character class. **Two sabotages stayed green and each was answered rather than shrugged at.** The `skel[-2] != ">"` guard I first wrote on the noclobber rule — `>>|` and `<|` are bash syntax errors, so the `|` after those keeps its boundary — is DELETED: no row could pin it, and on the one input it changed it gave the worse answer, reporting a phantom for `echo x >>| run "1. fake" true`, a line bash refuses outright, where treating it as the operator reports nothing. The `at_cmd` clause looked equally unpinnable until the `$(>|case)` row existed, and then pinned it exactly: with `at_cmd` left true the lexer reads `case` as reserved, pushes a marker, and the substitution's closer stops popping — the round-thirty phantom, reached through a redirection. Nine sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the shipped grammar, the `>|` operator dropped, the `{varname}` descriptor dropped, no space before the target, an empty target allowed, the lexer rule off, the `>>` guard REINSTATED (the healthy-tree direction), the marker replaced by `_MASK`, and the substitution closer put back in the bare word class. A tenth refused to apply at all — its anchor was the multi-line `noclobber = (…)` the `>>`-guard deletion had since collapsed to one line — and the harness's own `SABOTAGE … DID NOT APPLY` assertion is what stopped a stale proof being believed. One own error worth recording, and it invalidated the first measurement round outright: the probe's `run()` printed to STDERR, and `2>|`, `&>` and `2>&1` redirect stderr — so five forms reported `ran=[]` and read exactly like a gate bash had not run. A probe whose evidence channel is the thing under test measures nothing; it appends to a file now. Codex named the `;`; measuring the general claim rather than the case in front of me found every character in `_BOUNDARY_CHARS` (`|`, `&`, `(`, `)` too), the LEADING spelling (`\; run …`, where bash reports `;: command not found`), three constructs the review did not name — inside a substitution, inside a backtick, and as a redirection TARGET word, each reached by the same escape branch — and the SIBLING READER, where the direction is worse: `echo x\; run` is an ordinary line invoking no gate and was reported as an unreadable gate INVOCATION, a gate RED ON A HEALTHY TREE. **The interesting half is what the sabotage round then refuted about my own fix.** The first version masked only the escaped CHARACTER and kept the backslash, with a comment arguing the backslash was load-bearing for `word_piece`'s `\\[\s\S]` branch; sabotage B — masking both — left the whole behavioural matrix green, so the claim was a rule with nothing behind it. Measured rather than argued: 408 constructed inputs, 24 escaped characters across 17 contexts (argument, assignment value, redirection target, substitution, backtick, expansion, heredoc, quoted word, arithmetic and label positions), gave the IDENTICAL answer from both readers either way. So the fix is the simpler one — one branch, both characters masked, wherever the pair is written — which also makes the rule the same sentence as the expansion, heredoc and arithmetic masks rather than a fourth shape, and lets `_ESCAPE_MASK` be read by the SAME checker rather than a sibling of its own. That invariant is what the behavioural rows cannot supply: they pin the five boundary characters and nothing else, so a `>`, a `"`, a `$` or a `#` reaching the skeleton raw is a rule no row can speak for, and enumerating the characters that must be masked is how the next one is missed. Its `False` rows are what stop it becoming "an escape hides everything" — an escaped BACKSLASH is a complete pair and the separator after it is real, and a backslash inside SINGLE quotes is no escape at all (both measured, bash runs the gate). **Two more defects found in my own work rather than by review**, both the shape this file keeps recording. The lexer's escape branch consumed the earlier pairs and the nested branch then fired on whatever single backslash was left, which is "every odd N": right at 1 and 5, wrong at 3 and 7. SECOND, the round-46 live-region exemption read the NEAREST named declaration, and a nearest-name rule reads a reusable name as an identity: `const helpers = { FormError: () => <span role="alert" /> };` in the file that owns the approved component was classified as `FormError` and inherited its exemption, so a forbidden raw live region passed the gate that exists to forbid it — measured by planting Codex's exact example, 6 passed. Three more spellings shared the hole: `register({ FormError: … })`, a class method of that name, and a local `const FormError` nested inside another component. The question is which DECLARATION the element sits in, not which name is closest to it, so the answer is the name bound by the element's TOP-LEVEL STATEMENT — a function or class declaration's own name, a variable statement's declarator ON THE PATH (`const a = 1, FormError = …` is the second one), and "" for a statement that binds nothing, which is never exempt. That makes the answer module-scope by construction rather than by a second test, and it deletes the property-key branch rather than adding a guard beside it. Two fixture expectations from round forty-six MOVED and that is the finding rather than churn: the object-property spelling now answers its statement's name, and a class method answers its class. **Sabotages B and D stayed green, and both are limits rather than gaps.** `_BANG`'s delimiter reaches only the lexer's command-position flag, and `_WORD_BREAK` excludes `!`, so a character glued to one is never examined either way; what the lookahead changes is whether the flag survives a word like `!xy`, and for that to matter the next word must be `case` at a command position — impossible once `!xy` is the command word, since `case a in a)` in an argument position is a syntax error. Measured over 45 constructed inputs: the twelve that answer differently all exit 2. The `time_opt` reset at `!` is the same shape — `_command`'s chain already refuses `time -p ! -p run`, so only the `case` marker can see it, and the two differing inputs of sixteen are syntax errors too. Both are kept because without them the lexer and the chain would mean different things by "the `!` reserved word" and by "an option", which is the one-rule-two-scopes disagreement these rounds keep finding, and both now say in their own comments that no row pins them. The real `validate.sh` reads identically to the previous head: 23 `run` and 10 `skip_gate` invocations, 24 and 7 distinct labels, 0 unreadable, gate 12 found. 298 spelling rows, 41 unreadable. Six sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores — the shipped lexer (five rows, the miss and the phantom among them), the shipped chain (both readers), and four on the exemption, each naming a different row: the shipped nearest-name rule (`helpers`), the nearest declaration without the property branch (`Input`, which isolates the module-scope half from the property half), the statement's first declarator instead of the one on the path, and the variable-statement branch dropped — plus the shipped exemption demonstrated PASSING on Codex's planted offender. 847 vitest, 484 deno, 43 e2e, 24 validate gates green with none skipped. **Round forty-eight found the one reserved word that is followed by a NAME rather than a command, and the same defect in the lexer beside it.** `function NAME { … }` is a supported bash spelling (`help function` documents it), and `function` sat in the plain reserved-word set — so the chain stopped at the NAME, and the body's `{` was then reachable from no boundary at all, a `{` being a reserved word rather than a metacharacter and therefore absent from `_BOUNDARY_CHARS`. `function checks { run "13. new check" true; }; checks` runs the gate (measured) and was read by NEITHER reader — invisible in BOTH directions, so a local gate written that way could have no CI counterpart while lockstep reported success. Codex named one spelling; measuring the general claim rather than the case in front of me found six, including the extra-blank and tab forms, `skip_gate`, inside a substitution and after a separator — while `function NAME() {`, the POSIX `NAME() {`, a newline before the brace and a `( … )` body all read already, because each puts a `(` or a newline between the name and the body. **Checking the sibling rather than the site the reviewer named found the LEXER failing the same way in both directions**, and the worse one: with `function` granting a command position to the next word, `function case { :; }` — legal bash, measured, where the keyword suppresses reserved-word recognition and `case() { :; }` is a syntax error — pushed a `case` marker that was never popped, so the substitution's own closer stopped being one and `echo $(function case { :; }; printf x) run "1. fake" true` reported a PHANTOM for a word bash passes to `echo`; and the NAME then cleared the flag, so the body's `{` opened no command position, a `case` inside it went unrecognised and its pattern's `)` popped the enclosing substitution — the same phantom by the opposite route. Round thirty's defect, reached through `function` as round forty-six reached it through `time` and forty-seven through `!`. One rule closes both: the word after `function` is a name, and the command position survives it. The name's own grammar was SWEPT off the shell rather than guessed — every printable ASCII character tried inside one, where bash accepts `!#%+,-./:?@]^_{}~`, the digits and the letters and refuses `$`, a backslash and a quoted name outright as "not a valid identifier" — so those three are excluded, because a definition bash refuses never runs and reporting its body is a phantom. **Attribution was measured rather than asserted**: the same scan against the previous head and against the fix gives 135 divergences and 45, with ZERO introduced — the 45 that remain are pre-existing, every one a name bash refuses reached through a `(` or a newline boundary rather than through this rule, and stated as a residual. **One sabotage stayed green and is recorded as a limit rather than implied tested**: the blank required after the name is bash's own rule (`function f {echo x; }` is a syntax error, pinned by a row), but `+` versus `*` is pinned by nothing — 2080 constructed inputs, 143 answer differently and bash refuses to PARSE all 143, so nothing runs on any input that could distinguish them. The other stated residual runs the other way: a `{` is legal inside a name (`function f{g { run "…" true; }; f{g` runs the gate, measured, and has a row), so a name ending in one reads a gate in a file bash refuses outright — excluding `{` would trade that phantom for a MISS on a legal spelling, which is the defect the whole rule is about, and the sabotage that makes the trade is red on that row. A `for` name is not a `function` name: what follows it is a word list, not a body, so the honest rule is that no command position opens at all, which is one `SHELL_WORD_TAKING` tuple read by both readers rather than a state machine in one of them. `coproc` is deliberately NOT in it and that is measured rather than assumed — `coproc run "1. x" true` really does invoke the gate — so its OTHER form, `coproc run { :; }`, still reads as an unreadable call; identical before and after, stated as the residual it is rather than closed by enumerating which words begin a compound command. A case therefore carries WHICH HALF of a clause it is in (`case-pat`/`case-body`), because a clause body is ordinary shell and the distinction is load-bearing rather than tidy: `case a in a) (run "…" true);; esac` and `case a in a) :; (run "…" true);; esac` both invoke the gate (measured), so only `;;`, `;&` and `;;&` flip back to the pattern half and never a single `;`. **Then the backtick spelling needed the same rule and could not have it**: the lexer never opened a command position after a backtick — the skeleton has spelled one `(` since round twenty-nine, so the two disagreed — and `case` inside one was therefore never recognised at all, leaving ``echo `case a in a|run) run "…" true;; esac` `` red on a healthy tree exactly as the `$( … )` form was.
