Three spellings of one question, three different answers. The fix is one `memberAccess` in `static-object.ts` that all three consume, with the key read through `literalText` so both literal forms and the transparent wrappers are handled in one place, and the `globalThis.Object` receiver read by the same helper rather than by a fourth inline test. **The healthy direction is what keeps it from becoming "any subscript is the built-in"**: a genuinely computed key answers null rather than guessing, so a bound `Object`, a bound `globalThis`, somebody else's `registry["assign"]` and `Object[k]` all stay silent. Six sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores, each naming a different subset: the shared reader reverted to property-only (3 tests, all three files at once — the shared module doing the work), the element branch narrowed to a string literal (2, the template rows), the `globalThis` receiver property-only (1), a dynamic key read as a name (1), and each sibling put back on its own reader (1 each). **The dynamic-key sabotage stayed GREEN on its first run and named a missing row rather than a passing rule**: the control's key variable was `k`, which never reaches the `assign` comparison, so the row that distinguishes the rule is one whose key VARIABLE is itself called `assign` — where reading the subscript's source text reports the built-in while the program calls whatever the variable holds. 848 vitest, 24 validate gates green with none skipped. **Round fifty-six found a call's CALLEE read without unwrapping, and the same root in three sibling readers and two neighbouring rules.** `(Object.assign)(m, { private: false })` invokes the built-in exactly as the bare spelling does, and `isObjectAssignCall` tested `call.expression` raw — so the call was not a direct mutation, and the inner reference was not ESCAPED either, because `calleeCall` correctly climbs the wrapper and finds the call. Neither branch fired and the stale `private: true` survived. Codex named the paren and `as` spellings; measuring the general claim found six more — `satisfies`, `!`, `<T>x`, a nested paren, and a wrapper combined with each of the computed and `globalThis` receivers — all eight reading `direct=0 escaped=0 literal-kept=true`. **Checking the siblings is where it stopped being one site.** `discarded-errors.test.ts`'s query reader had the identical hole in the identical direction: `(db.from)("walks")` with a discarded error left **68 of 68 green**, with `as`, `!` and `.rpc` beside it. Its two FACTORY readers had it in BOTH directions — `const db = (adminClient)();` and an inline `(adminClient)().from(…)` read as neither a client nor a value, so a HANDLED `.auth` error on one was REPORTED (a gate red on a healthy tree) while an escaped `db.from` on it was not reported at all. And `mutations()` in `realtime-channel.test.ts` read a write TARGET the same way, so `(m.private) = false`, `(m["private"]) = false` and `(m.private)--` went unreported — that list is the PRECONDITION which makes reading `broadcast.ts`'s literals sound, so a write it cannot see is one where the gate calls a public message private. Six local copies of the wrapper SET in `discarded-errors.test.ts` each omitted the `<T>x` assertion too, and two of those were live gates red on a healthy tree (`(<SupabaseClient>db).from(…)` with its error handled, and the same assertion around a whole chain). The fix is the shared module, as it was the last three rounds: one `isTransparentWrapper` that `unwrapTransparent` and `calleeCall` are now built on, `calleeOf` as `calleeCall`'s exact inverse, and `memberTarget` for a write target — with all six local copies, the three callee reads and the two write-target branches routed through them, so there is no sibling left to forget. `verify-deployment.test.ts` was measured and was ALREADY correct (its own unwrap knew all five, plus the `await` and `void` this question needs and the shared one must not have); it is rewritten in terms of the shared predicate so it cannot diverge later, which is a de-duplication and not a fix. The healthy direction is pinned at every site — the receiver is still RESOLVED through the wrapper, so a bound `Object`, somebody else's `registry.assign` and a dynamic `Object[k]` stay silent, and `await` stays out of the shared set because `(await f)(x)` is a call whose callee is the await. Nine sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores, each naming a different fixture: the shipped callee read, the shared `memberTarget` raw (which moves BOTH write-target consumers at once — measured through the probe, where each consumer's own sabotage moves only its own row), the query reader raw, each factory reader reverted separately (naming the inline row and the `const db =` row respectively), the shared predicate dropping `<T>x` (which turns TWO files red, so the sharing is proven rather than asserted), each write-target branch, and `await` added to the shared set, which turns **59** tests red. One own error worth recording, and it is the TENTH instance of the restore family in a new spelling: the measurement harness that drives `mutations()` restores the file it temporarily edits, and it was handed the HEAD snapshot rather than the FIXED one — so the measurement was right and the fix was silently gone afterwards, caught by `git diff --stat` rather than by the harness. The rule is about any harness that writes a tracked file, not only a sabotage, and `session-notes.md` says so now. Three of this round's new rows also had to be corrected before they meant anything: they asserted `classifySource(…) === []` where that helper returns every classified site, OK ones included, so they were red for the wrong reason until they asserted the verdict. 849 vitest, 484 deno, 24 validate gates green with none skipped. **Round fifty-seven found the SAME rule facing the other way — a reader that asks about `.parent` without climbing the wrappers.** Round fifty-six fixed the DOWNWARD reads (`calleeOf`, `unwrapTransparent`); `outermost` then asked whether a builder method was invoked by comparing `p.parent` to a call, so a transparent wrapper between the member and its call made an INVOKED method read as uncalled: `db.from("walks").select!("id")` runs and handles its error, and the gate failed the healthy file — red on a healthy tree, the worst shape this log records. Codex named the `!` spelling; measuring the general claim found all five wrappers and a link DEEPER in the chain, six in all. **Checking the siblings found two more, in the two other directions.** `errorAccess` asked the syntactic parent too, so `(r).error` and `r!.error` — genuine reads — reported the envelope as one whose error is never read, red on a healthy tree again; and the `.auth` namespace hop asked it as well, so `(db.auth).getUser(token)` with its error THROWN AWAY left **69 of 69 green** — a miss on the auth path, the gate blessing what it forbids. Codex's own sentence named the wrapped hop inside `outermost`, and `(db.auth.admin).getUserById(…)` was red there for the same reason. The fix is the shared module, as it was the last four rounds: `outward` is the UPWARD inverse of `unwrapTransparent`, `calleeCall` is rebuilt on it (and got simpler), and every parent reader in `discarded-errors.test.ts` goes through it — the five that ask a question plus the one local copy of the climb, which is deleted. The other two gates were measured and have no parent reader of this shape: `form-error`'s walks EVERY parent to the enclosing statement, which is a different question. **Two things are recorded as limits rather than claimed as fixes**, because measuring refuted what I had predicted for each. `passedToCall` is red either way, so what the syntactic parent cost there is the REASON — `log((r))` reported "`.error` is never read" for an envelope whose consumer is simply not visible, and a red that misdescribes itself is its own defect; that has its own row. And `inlineCallback` changes its own answer while no constructed input changes a VERDICT, so it goes through the helper for consistency and the comment says exactly that rather than implying a fix. The `parent.expression === cur` clause inside `outward` is defence in depth pinned by no row, and that is measured rather than asserted: walking every node of the five wrapper kinds, the only child for which it is false is the TypeNode of an `as`, `satisfies` or `<T>x`, which none of these readers ever holds — stated in the docstring, the round-41 precedent. Six sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores, each naming a DIFFERENT fixture: the shipped `invoked` test (`select!`), the shipped `hop` test (the wrapped `.admin` hop), the shipped `errorAccess` (`(r).error`), the shipped `.auth` hop (the missed discard, which fails as "expected exactly one site" — nothing reported at all), the shipped `passedToCall` (the misdescribing red), and `outward` reduced to the identity, which turns THREE tests red across TWO files, so the sharing is proven rather than asserted. A seventh stayed green and was answered rather than shrugged at — it is the clause the probe above showed no row can pin. The healthy direction is pinned at every site: a reference that really is never invoked still runs nothing, an ordinary discard is still a discard, and an envelope nothing reads is still discarded. One own error worth recording: the local Postgres refused to start and the first validate run went red on every database gate at once — read off the log rather than diagnosed, where `could not bind IPv4 address "127.0.0.1": Address already in use` says the previous cluster's socket had not been released, so the retry after it started cleanly. 850 vitest, 484 deno, 24 validate gates green with none skipped. **Round fifty-eight found the round-fifty-seven fix carrying a COUNT where the `while` loop it replaced had none — and the count was the class, not the instance.** `outward` climbed at most eight wrappers, so a named closure invoked through nine — Codex's `(((((((((check)))))))))()` — reported its envelope as never read: a gate red on a healthy tree, in the helper that had replaced a loop which climbed every wrapper. Measured against the shipped gate first: eight OK, nine DISCARDED, for every wrapper kind, mixed, and at forty; and every other parent reader at nine — the builder link "referenced and never called", the `.error` read "never read", the call argument misdescribed again, and the `.auth` hop, where a DISCARDED error was not reported at all. **Checking the siblings turned one count into six.** `unwrapTransparent`, the downward inverse, carried the same eight — a receiver nine deep read "unrecognised" (red on a healthy tree), a callee nine deep read as no call at all (a MISS, the discarded error behind it blessed), a factory nine deep "neither a client nor a value" in both directions; `aliasLiteral` stopped at sixteen hops behind a guard set that already terminated it; `resolveProperty` followed a spread eight deep and refused the ninth (a refusal in `verify-deployment`, a MISS in `form-error`); `effectiveProps` in `realtime-channel.test.ts` — the stated residual walk — had its own `depth < 8`; and `verify-deployment`'s local unwrap, the one that also climbs `await` and `void`, had its own eight. Six loops, four files, one shape: a fixed iteration count standing where a termination argument belongs. **The fix is the argument, in each place.** A tree walk needs none — a finite tree has a finite depth, so `.parent` ends at the source file and `.expression` at a leaf — and both helpers are unbounded now (measured to five hundred). The alias walk's guard set was always its argument, so it carries no count. The two spread recursions are GRAPH walks, and there the count was doing a job by accident: `var a: any = { ...a, methods: ["POST"] }` is legal and runs (measured in node — the hoisted name is still `undefined` when the literal is evaluated, so the spread contributes nothing), and without the count the reader recursed until the stack ran out. Each carries the set of literals it is inside now, and a spread that re-enters one is unresolvable — the answer an unfollowable spread already gets, in source order, so a later `methods` wins over the cycle and a cycle after a readable `methods` erases it. **Pinned at every consumer, in all four files, from measured values**: Codex's row and every wrapper kind at nine, the six parent readers, the three downward readers in both directions, nine-deep spreads and seventeen-hop aliases in the three files that read them, and a cyclic spread in each of the three that must not hang — plus the healthy direction, that forty wrappers around a closure handed AWAY still runs nothing. Eight sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores, each naming a different fixture: the `outward` count (Codex's row), the `unwrapTransparent` count (FOUR files red at once, so the sharing is proven), the alias count (three files — `a17`, `m17`, `phi12`), the spread count (`phi11` and the nine-deep `role`), the spread guard removed (`RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded` in two files, on the cycle rows — which is the finding), the channel walk's count (nine values where ten literals carry `topic`), its guard removed (the same overflow), and the local unwrap's count (`lambda4`). 851 vitest, 24 validate gates green with none skipped. **Round fifty-nine, a body-only finding, moved from the TypeScript readers to the SHELL one, and it is the grammar lesson of the `time` and `function` rounds applied to the one reserved word the chain still read as if it took a command directly.** `coproc [NAME] command`: the word after `coproc` is a NAME whenever a compound command follows it, and the body runs — `coproc checks { run "13. new check" true; }` invokes `run` — while both readers returned nothing, because the chain read `checks` as the command and stopped, leaving the body's `{` reachable from no boundary: a local gate with no CI counterpart while lockstep reports success. Measured first against bash 5.2 over fifty-three spellings (with the stub reporting on STDERR, because a coprocess's stdout IS the pipe and the first harness read every body as silent), then forty-eight of them against the shipped reader: Codex's row, a tab before the brace, a redirection after the group, an identifier with `_` and a digit, a quoted name, a `$NAME`, the glued `checks(`, and a gate at the HEAD of a named `until` or `if` were all missed; and the other direction one word over, which the finding did not name — `coproc run { run "…" true; }` names the coprocess `run`, and `shell_unreadable_calls` refused that name as an unlabelled invocation, a gate red on a healthy tree. The boundary is bash's own: a word followed by anything but a compound command is the COMMAND (`coproc checks run "…" true` runs a command called `checks`, and so do the `time` and assignment-prefix spellings), a newline ends the question (`coproc checks`⏎`{ … }` is a coproc of `checks` and a group in the main shell), and a reserved word in the name position stays reserved (`coproc if …` is unnamed, `coproc fi {` is a syntax error, and `coproc time {` reads the body either way). **The fix is the `function NAME` rule one reserved word over, in both halves**: one `_COPROC_NAME` lookahead — a shell word, quotes, masks and `$` included since bash validates the identifier only at run time, followed across blanks and never a newline by `{`, `(`, `[[` or a loop or conditional keyword — consulted by the chain (`coprocspec`, with `coproc` out of the plain reserved set as `time` and `function` are) and by the lexer, where the command position SURVIVES the name so the body's `{` opens one and a `case` inside it is recognised. That second half has exactly one row that can see it, and it is round thirty's phantom reached through `coproc`: `echo $(coproc checks { case a in a) :;; esac; }) run "1. fake" true`, where an unrecognised `case` lets the pattern's `)` pop the enclosing substitution and the tail reports a gate for a word bash passes to `echo`. Twenty spelling rows and five unreadable rows, every expectation measured against bash. Five sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores, each naming a different row: the lexer's name branch off (ONE row, the case-marker phantom), the chain's name off (thirteen, Codex's among them, and `coproc run {` refused again), every word after `coproc` taken as a name (the four `checks`-is-the-command rows), the lookahead crossing a newline (one — `coproc run`⏎ must be refused as the unlabelled call it is), and the name class narrowed to a function's (the quoted and `$NAME` rows). 428 spelling rows, 79 unreadable, 851 vitest, 24 validate gates green with none skipped. **Round sixty is round fifty-nine's name class one word wider, and the fix moved the question from a regex at the word's START to the lexer's own END of the word.** `coproc $(printf checks) { run "13. dynamic" true; }` expands to the identifier `checks` and runs the body — and so do the backtick, the double-quoted, the glued `checks$(printf 2)` and the nested spellings, a substitution carrying a `;`, and one whose body invokes a gate of its own (twenty-two spellings measured against bash 5.2, twenty-one of them then against the shipped reader) — while `_COPROC_NAME`'s character class stopped at the substitution's `(`, so nine answered wrong: eight bodies read by neither reader, and an unreadable call inside one invisible. There is no name regex now. The chain reads the name as a skeleton WORD — a substitution region, an escape, a bare non-metacharacter or a quoted run, the grammar it already used for an assignment's value — and the lexer marks the word after `coproc` as a name in progress and asks the question at its END, where its own tokenisation has carried it through the substitution, the backtick or the quote: the first word break at the SAME nesting, outside any quote, and only there. What the two halves share is the lookahead asked after the word, `_COPROC_OPENER`, so they cannot disagree about which opener makes a name. The first version of that end rule was refuted by a trace rather than by review: `(` is a word break, so the question was answered at the `(` of `$(printf checks)` — before the branch below had pushed the substitution's entry, where the depth alone cannot tell it from a subshell's `(` — and the real end after the `)` never asked it, so the body's `case` went unrecognised and its pattern's `)` popped the enclosing substitution: round thirty's phantom, through a substitution-spelled name. A `(` after `$` is the word continuing. Seventeen spelling rows and three unreadable rows, every expectation measured against bash; the lexer's half is pinned by four phantom rows, one per way a name can carry a blank or a quote inside it. Five sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores: the chain's name grammar back to a bare class (nine rows), the lexer's start rule narrowed to bare words (the four phantoms), the `$(` exclusion removed and the depth guard removed (the same three `$(` phantoms — two clauses of one rule, and the backtick row stays green under both because a backtick pushes nothing), and the backtick guard removed (that one row). The quote, expansion, arithmetic and heredoc guards are pinned by no row and say so: a blank inside any of them makes a name bash refuses at run time. 445 spelling rows, 82 unreadable, 851 vitest, 24 validate gates green with none skipped. **Round sixty-one is round fifty-eight's class in the chain: a bounded depth standing where a structure belongs.** The name grammar round sixty gave the chain rested on `_SUBST_REGION`, a regular expression balanced to a fixed depth of two, so `coproc $(echo $(echo $(echo $(printf checks)))) { run "G" true; }` — which bash runs — was skipped with its body: measured, names two and three substitutions deep read and four and six did not, an unreadable call inside such a body was invisible, and the assignment-value grammar beside it carries the same bound in the miss direction (documented at `_subst_region` as losing the prefix rather than inventing a command; a four-deep value was measured lost, and it is stated here rather than chased — moving the prefix grammar into the lexer is a round of its own, since the redirection grammar shares it). Codex's remedy was the right shape and the fix is SIMPLER than what it replaces: a regular expression cannot balance nesting, the lexer already reads every word to its end with balanced regions of any depth and already decides the name there, so the chain reads no name at all. Where the lexer finds a name, the blank that ends it becomes `_NAME_BREAK` in the skeleton, a boundary the body is reached from, and `coproc` goes back into the plain reserved set; `name_piece` and `coprocspec` are deleted. That also closes the false-refusal direction round fifty-nine found — `coproc run { … }` is then `run` followed by the marker rather than by a blank, and both readers admit an invocation only before a blank or a separator, while a name ended by `(` is `run(`, which they already exclude as a definition's shape. A mask of the name's first character was written for that direction and DELETED, because no row could pin it: the sabotage removing it stayed green, and a second row built to reach it (`coproc run( run "…" true )`, measured legal) stayed green too — code no row can reach is code a reader trusts for nothing. Nine spelling rows and three unreadable rows at depth four and six, every expectation measured against bash. Two sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores, and they differ by exactly the two false-refusal rows: the marker not emitted (twenty-eight rows red, `coproc run { …}` and `coproc run ( … )` refused again among them) and the marker emitted but not a boundary (twenty-six — the bodies lost, the names still not read). 454 spelling rows, 85 unreadable, 851 vitest, 24 validate gates green with none skipped. Sixty-one rounds, a hundred and thirty-five findings. **Round sixty-two: a `[[ … ]]` conditional is a REGION, and the sibling it exposed was older than the finding.** Codex named `[[ -z x || run ]]`: bash runs no `run` for it — `run` is the string operand of the `||` — while `shell_unreadable_calls` read the `||` as a list operator and refused `run ]]` by name, a gate red on a healthy tree. Measuring the general claim first, 13 of 22 constructed conditionals differed from bash on the shipped reader — `&&`, a grouping `( … )`, a newline after the operator, `! [[`, inside `if`, after a pipe, in a case body, in a backtick, and a `=~` regex whose `(a|run)` alternation the reader took for a subshell and a pipe — and the mirror was a PHANTOM: `echo $([[ -z x || case ]]) run "1. fake" true` passes every word to `echo` (measured) and the reader answered `1. fake`, because the `case` after the `||` stood at what it took for a command position, pushed a marker, and the substitution's own closer became a boundary. Round thirty's defect, reached through `[[` as it was reached through `time`, `!`, `function` and `coproc`. The grammar was read off bash rather than the manual, some sixty shapes: `&&`, `||`, `(` and `)` are expression operators; a newline is whitespace after an operator, after `[[` and before `]]`; a bare `;` inside is a syntax error; `#` still opens a comment; `)]]` closes and `x]]` does not; and the reserved word counts only AT a command position and only as the whole word — `echo [[ -z x || run "…" true ]]` passes `[[` to `echo` and the `||` is real, `X=1 [[ …` and `>/dev/null [[ …` make `[[` an ordinary command bash cannot find after which `|| run ]]` really invokes `run`, `case a in [[)` is a pattern, `function [[ {` is a name, inside a heredoc body it is text, and `[[x` at a command position is to bash ONE word (the subscript-shaped lexing the `run]=2` rule already records). So "cond" joins `parens`: `[[` at a command position pushes it and no resume entry (it suspends nothing), `]]` at a word start pops it, a bare `(` or `)` at that level is masked and pushes nothing, and the separators are masked by the rule the extglob group already had. What a substitution inside it does is untouched — `[[ $(run "…" true >&2; printf x) == x ]]` invokes the gate (measured), and so do the process-substitution and backtick spellings. **The sibling**: writing the backtick row found that a backtick pushes nothing onto `parens`, so while one is open INSIDE a region the region is still `parens[-1]` and every rule keyed on that fired on the backtick's contents — real shell at a command position. `` echo @(`true; run "…" true`) ``, `` echo @(`printf x | run "…" true`) ``, `` case a in `true | run "…" true; printf a`) :;; esac `` and its subshell spelling all invoke the gate (measured) and the shipped reader found none of the four: a MISS in both directions, pre-existing since the extglob and case-pattern rounds, and the cond region had it by construction. One helper, `innermost(*kinds)`: the entry must be on top of the stack AND the backtick state must be what it was at the push, recorded by index in `region_bt` through a `push()` that every stack push now goes through. A third, smaller: the unreadable reader's word-end set named `)` and not `_SUBST_CLOSE`, which is how the skeleton spells a substitution's closer — so `echo $(run)` and `` x=`run` ``, each an unlabelled invocation bash runs (measured), were refused by neither reader; found writing `[[ $(run) == x ]]`. Three guards were written and DELETED because no input could pin them, each measured by a variant that left every row and probe green: `at_word_start` on `[[` (`at_cmd` holds only at a word start), `exp_depth`/`arith_depth` on `[[` (`at_cmd` is never true inside either), and `hd` on `]]` (`<<` inside `[[` is a syntax error). Fifty-four spelling rows and forty unreadable rows, every expectation measured against bash — among them the argument, prefix, pattern, function-name, heredoc and expansion spellings that must NOT open the region, `[[ ]]x == ]]x || run ]]` and `[[ x == a]] || run ]]` (legal, measured) for the closer's two guards, and the four backtick shapes. Fourteen sabotages behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores, each witnessed by a row the others leave green — the region never opened (25 red), separators live inside it (22, with `[[ ( run ) ]]` still green), the grouping's `(` live (4, with `[[ -n x && run ]]` still green), its `)` popping the region (1), the closer never popping (28), the region opened in argument position (6), in a heredoc body (1), for a function's name (1), in a case pattern (1), for `[[x` (2), the closer's lookahead (1) and its word-start guard (1), `innermost` ignoring the backtick state (12), and the closer dropped from the word-end set (5). **Two of them stayed GREEN on the first run, and both were the proof's fault**: the rows for the function-name and `[[x` guards each carried a later `]]`, so the wrongly-opened region closed there and the answer came out right by luck — `function [[ { :; }; run "…" true` and `[[x == x || run "…" true` (both legal, measured) leave it open, and are red. 508 spelling rows, 125 unreadable, 851 vitest, 24 validate gates green with none skipped. Sixty-two rounds, a hundred and thirty-six findings. **Round sixty-three found a member this family of readers could not NAME being read as no member at all, and the sibling readers had the same hole in the same direction.** `memberAccess` answers null for a computed key — `supabase[key]`, `supabase[pick()]`, a template, a concatenation — and every consumer read that null as "not the member I am asking about": `const key: "channel" = "channel"; supabase[key]("walk:public")` type-checks, opens a second PUBLIC topic, and planted in the real hook left all 11 realtime-channel tests green (Codex, PR #94), with eight more spellings measured green beside it — the escaped `const opener = supabase[key]`, the wrapped `(supabase as never)[key]`, an alias of the client, and the namespace hop `supabase.realtime[key]`. Checking the siblings rather than the site the reviewer named found the class: `db[key]("walks")` with its error discarded produced NO site in `discarded-errors.test.ts`, and neither did `db[pick()]`, the escaped and wrapped spellings, `db.auth[key]` or `db["auth"][key]` (measured against the shipped gate, every one "(no sites)"); and in the shared module `Object[k](m, { private: false })` with `k: "assign"` mutates exactly as the direct call does while `declaredObjects` kept `m`'s literal readable and stale and `mutations()` reported nothing — 11 of 11 green with it planted in `broadcast.ts`, and `form-error`'s reader, which is the same `declaredObjects`, read `alert` off a declaration the program had overwritten. Not guessing which member and reading it as no member are different rules, and the readers had the first and not the second. One shared `computedAccess` (an element access whose key is not a literal) and one `memberRoot` (the expression a chain is rooted at, unwrapped at every hop) now, consumed by every gate: a computed member is REPORTED BY NAME when the chain's root is a receiver the gate has resolved — the realtime client (itself, an alias, a wrapper, or a namespace reached from it), a client with positive evidence in the query reader, the built-in `Object` in the shared module — and left alone on any other receiver, because `handlers[type](payload)` is ordinary code and reporting it would be red on a healthy tree. The `Object` half REVERSES round fifty-five's pin: that round was right that a computed key is not a NAME (reading the subscript's text would call `Object[assign]` the built-in while the program calls whatever the variable holds) and wrong that it is therefore no member — the row whose key variable is called `assign` is reported as a COMPUTED member now, which keeps the first rule and adds the second. `verify-deployment` was checked as the fourth sibling and found already refusing — `http[k](handle)` through a namespace import is not housed and needs a `contract_for` case, the conservative direction — so its new fixture pins that rather than changing it. The healthy direction is what makes the narrowing load-bearing, and the sabotage that drops it is the strongest proof this round has: with every computed member reported, the REAL TREE goes red in both gates — 84 computed accesses in `app/src`, and the `supabase/functions` scan red on its own — so the rule is not "report every subscript"; and with the narrowing kept there is no computed access on the client in `app/src`, none on a client in `supabase/functions`, and no computed access on `Object` in either (measured), so the conservative reading costs nothing today. A LITERAL key stays what it was (`supabase["channel"]` is `supabase.channel`, pinned in both directions), and a HANDLED error behind a computed member is refused too, stated as conservative: the refusal is about what the gate cannot read, the remedy is to name the member, and it is red on nothing today. Ten sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores, each naming a different fixture: the realtime branch removed (Codex's row), the root walk reduced to the immediate receiver (the namespace row alone), `isComputedObjectAccess` wired false (the mutations rows AND the form-error row, two files at once — the shared module doing the work), `declaredObjects` no longer invalidated (the form-error row ALONE, so the two halves are individually load-bearing), the query reader's branch removed (its new test), each gate's client narrowing dropped (the healthy rows, and the real tree in both), a literal key read as computed (the literal-control rows in BOTH files — I had predicted the realtime rows only, and the query reader reported `db["from"]` twice, a prediction wrong in the safe direction), the deployment gate counting a computed member of the namespace as the wrapper (`theta2`), and `memberRoot` not unwrapping its start (the wrapped row in both consumers). Own error worth recording: the first `broadcast.ts` plant landed INSIDE the request literal, went red with `<computed accessor, unreadable>`, and read as a finding — red for the wrong reason, the trap this file already names — so it was re-planted above the request, where the shipped gate is green and the fixed one red. 853 vitest, 24 validate gates green with none skipped. Sixty-three rounds, a hundred and thirty-seven findings. **Round sixty-four found the channel gate's alias resolver carrying a PARTIAL copy of the shared module's binding-form list, and the two sibling readers each missing a different slice of the same list.** `clientNames()` followed a declaration initializer and nothing else, so an alias formed by ASSIGNMENT was invisible: `let db: typeof supabase; db = supabase; db[key]("walk:public")` opens a public topic and, planted in the real hook, left all 12 realtime-channel tests green (Codex, PR #94) — and so did the logical-assignment `db ??= supabase`, a parameter default, a binding-element default, and a declaration alias OF an assignment alias, every one measured green on the shipped gate. The finding named the computed branch; the escaped reference (`const opener = db.channel; opener(t)`) and the destructured method (`const { channel: opener } = db`) through the same alias were misses too, while the NAMED call was caught only as an unresolvable receiver. Checking the siblings rather than the site the reviewer named found each missing a different form: `declaredAsClient` in `discarded-errors.test.ts` read `EqualsToken` alone, so `db2 ??= db` followed by a computed call produced no site and a discarded `db2.auth.getUser()` was refused as declared "neither a client nor a value" — a refusal misdescribing a receiver that IS one; and `objectBuiltinMember` in the shared module matched the SPELLING `Object`, so `const O = Object; O.assign(m, { private: false })` — and the assignment alias, the computed, the escaped and `const g = globalThis; g.Object.assign(…)` spellings — reported no mutation and kept the literal readable and stale, `assignCalls 0, escaped 0` on every one. Round eighteen had enumerated the complete set of constructs that bind a name to a value and called it the one place that answers "what can this name come to hold?"; it was one place with three partial readers beside it. `bindingSources(sf)` is that enumeration now, lifted out of `declaredObjects`' visitor (the inline links and `makeLinkBinding` are deleted; the same targets are linked to the same identifiers after the walk, and every existing alias fixture holds), and it has two consumers with two readings: `declaredObjects` links every target to every identifier the source mentions, conservative because its safe direction is refusal; `holdersOf(sf, seeds)` is DIRECTED and PRECISE, because its consumers report on a positive answer and a false positive there is a gate red on healthy code — a name holds the seed when a whole binding (a declaration, an assignment in any alias-forming spelling, a parameter or binding-element default) binds it to the seed's identifier through the transparent wrappers, transitively, and a pattern's names or a loop's variable only where the part is VISIBLE, a literal aggregate written in place and matched by position or key, one level deep. A part taken from anything else — `const { data } = await supabase.from(…)` — is not the client, and that clause is load-bearing rather than tidy: the sabotage that follows a pattern's names from ANY source mentioning the client turns the REAL TREE red, three channel sites where the healthy tree has one. `clientNames` is the import seeds plus `holdersOf`; `isAliasFormingAssignment` is exported and `declaredAsClient` reads it with the left side unwrapped; `isGlobal` asks whether an identifier is the global `Object` or `globalThis` by spelling OR by holding it, cached per file. Name-based and file-wide, so a name later rebound away from the client still counts — the reporting direction, stated in every consumer. Measured on the trees rather than assumed: no `for…of` over a literal mentioning `supabase`, no destructuring from one, no alias of `Object` in either tree, and every `= supabase.` in `app/src` is a call result, which holds no client. Against the fixed gate every planted spelling is red on the real files — the nine hook plants and the `Object` alias in `broadcast.ts` — and the query gate classifies every logical-assignment alias while an assignment from a non-client stays silent. Ten sabotages red-first behind verified non-empty diffs and `cmp`-verified restores, each naming a different fixture: the shipped declaration-only fixpoint (Codex's row), the operator set narrowed to `=` (the `??=` rows in BOTH consumers and the existing spread-alias fixture — I had predicted three files and it was two, because `form-error` carries no logical-assignment fixture), the defaults dropped from the enumeration (the parameter-default row and the existing default-alias fixture), the literal-aggregate matching removed (the `[db] = [supabase]` row), `isGlobal` matching the spelling alone (the form-error row and the mutation rows), `declaredAsClient` back to `=` (its `??=` row), its target not unwrapped (the `(db2) = db` row alone), `declaredObjects` consuming only whole bindings (the existing destructuring-alias fixture — so the refactor kept the conservative links), `holdersOf` not unwrapping the source (the wrapped-source row and the existing alias-resolution test), and the over-correction above (the real tree). 855 vitest, 24 validate gates green with none skipped. Sixty-four rounds, a hundred and thirty-eight findings. **And one more, reached the wrong way round, which is the part worth recording.** I believed the CI run for this PR had wedged — the `frontend` job reading `in_progress` in `Behavioural tests still execute` — and bounded every ci.yml job in response. **The incident did not happen.** The job record says run 35005156983 finished at 18:05:29 with `conclusion: success`, 76 seconds end to end, with the step I called hung taking 18; what I was reading was a STALE `check-runs` snapshot, and I then asserted "the better part of an hour" from a sense of elapsed time rather than from the timestamps, which is precisely the error `ci(apt)` records — "elapsed time read off intuition instead of the job record, then explained with a theory the evidence never required". Third instance of that in this log; the container's own clock settles it in one command and I did not run it until after I had written the claim into a commit message, two code comments, this entry and a PR comment. **The change survives its own false motivation, and that is the test it had to pass**: `ci(concurrency)` genuinely bounded `e2e-today` and genuinely left the other four on GitHub's six-hour default, a wedged job genuinely is indistinguishable from a slow one until a ceiling says so, this session genuinely cannot clear one (the token 403s on Actions writes), and on `main` a six-hour job genuinely holds the deploy gate and produces the misleading red `ops(gate-noise)` is about. So all five carry a ceiling at roughly eight times their measured runtime on run 35004363610 — headroom, not a target, since a ceiling that trips on an ordinary slow day is itself a gate red on a healthy tree — and `verify-workflows.py` gains a rule 4 so the next job added is bounded without anyone remembering. Scoped to `ci.yml` ON PURPOSE and the exemption argued rather than assumed: the deploy workflows stay unbounded for the same reason `ci(concurrency)` refused `cancel-in-progress` on them — killing `supabase db push` halfway leaves an environment nobody can describe, which is worse than a job that runs long. Every version of the false story is corrected in the tree; only the commit message it shipped in still carries it, which is what this sentence is for. Own errors worth recording. The first sabotage of `isNeverCache` was `return false || (…)`, which is a no-op — a broken proof, caught before it was believed. The EIGHTH instance of this repository's restore-discipline family happened here, and it is a new spelling: not `git restore` or `git checkout <file>`, the two `session-notes.md` names, but `git checkout-index -f -a`, reached for reflexively to undo a sabotage — which overwrote every unstaged file from the index and destroyed two finished edits and their proof. The rule is about the CLASS, not those two names, and says so now. Four COVERAGE classifications were guesses until measured (`Exactly one <main>`, `The walk channel is private`, `Every 5xx throw carries its cause`, `DEV fixtures absent from the production bundle` have no local counterpart at all), which is how the CI-only count went from eleven to fifteen — `discarded-errors.test.ts` merely MENTIONS the 5xx check in a comment, the mention-versus-use distinction this repository has paid for before. The form-error precondition named two sites that do not exist, because `StateField`'s `role={role}` is an expression and this scan cannot see it; it names the one real raw live region now. Both new preconditions first pinned a LINE NUMBER (`components/fields.tsx:37`), which is red the first time somebody edits a comment above it — measured, by adding one line to `fields.tsx` — so each names the file and the shape and leaves the line where it helps, in the failure message. Two `validate.sh` labels in the map were wrong because the SQL suites take their label from `$(basename)` over a glob, so the map's reader expands the glob exactly as the shell does rather than the glob being replaced — the glob is deliberate, and a suite added there should appear on its own. And `git diff --numstat` shows NOTHING for an untracked new file, so a sabotage on one has to be verified by `cmp` against a snapshot; that is in `session-notes.md` now. 851 vitest, 484 deno, 43 e2e, 86 smoke DO blocks, 12 concurrency cases, 614 catalogue-generator proofs, 24 validate gates green with NONE skipped (23 labels; gate 8 runs twice), 46 `ci.yml` run-steps classified, 14 of them CI-only — one fewer of each than when this entry was first written, because the walk-channel check moved out of `ci.yml` and into gate 3, and gate 10g refused every half of that move until all four files agreed.
